#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1KF combines with HF to form KHF₂. The compound contains the species: (a) K⁺, F⁻ and H⁺  (b) K⁺, F⁻ and HF  (c) K⁺ and [HF₂]⁻  (d) [KHF]⁺ and F⁻Bifluoride ion, H-bondingWhy KHF₂ exists but KHCl₂ does not — the F—H⋯F bridge🟢 ExactcK⁺ and [HF₂]⁻
Concept KHF₂ is not a mixture of KF and HF — it is a genuine ionic salt whose anion is the bifluoride ion, [F⋯H⋯F]⁻. Fluorine is the only halogen electronegative and small enough to form a hydrogen bond so strong (≈163 kJ mol⁻¹) that the H sits essentially midway between the two F atoms. The result is a symmetric, linear three-centre four-electron unit that behaves as a single anion. KHCl₂, KHBr₂ and KHI₂ do not exist because Cl, Br and I are too large and too weakly electronegative to hold a hydrogen bridge.
Key relation KF + HF → K⁺ + [F—H⋯F]⁻ the F⁻ of KF donates a lone pair to the δ+ hydrogen of HF; K⁺ is a spectator. Structure of the anion: [F⋯H⋯F]⁻, linear, ∠F–H–F = 180°, both F—H distances ≈ 1.13 Å equal distances prove the H is shared, not attached to one F.
Solution
1. KF is fully ionic in the melt/solution: K⁺ + F⁻.
2. HF is a covalent, strongly polar molecule Hδ+—Fδ−.The δ+ hydrogen is an excellent hydrogen-bond acceptor site for another fluoride.
3. F⁻ hydrogen-bonds to the H of HF, giving the discrete bifluoride anion [HF₂]⁻; K⁺ balances its charge.
4. Eliminate the distractors:
  • (a) free H⁺ never exists in a solid — a bare proton is instantly captured.
  • (b) would mean HF stays as a neutral guest molecule; the observed symmetric F—H distances rule that out.
  • (d) [KHF]⁺ is not a known species — potassium forms no covalent bond to H here.
 species        what it really is            exists?
 K⁺             alkali-metal cation          YES
 [HF₂]⁻         F⋯H⋯F, strongest H-bond      YES   <-- answer (c)
 H⁺ (free)      bare proton in a solid       NO
 [KHF]⁺         covalent K—H—F cation        NO
 KHCl₂          Cl too big / less E.N.       NO
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a formula looks like "MHX₂", ask whether X can hydrogen-bond. Only F qualifies, so only bifluorides (KHF₂, NaHF₂, NH₄HF₂) are real. The trap is option (b): students read KHF₂ as "KF·HF" and pick the mixture; the giveaway that it is a single anion is that the two H—F distances are equal.
Answer: (c) K⁺ and [HF₂]⁻.
2For which of the following molecules is μ ≠ 0 significant? (i) 1,4-C₆H₄Cl₂  (ii) 1,4-C₆H₄(CN)₂  (iii) 1,4-C₆H₄(OH)₂  (iv) 1,4-C₆H₄(SH)₂  — (a) Only (i)  (b) (i) and (ii)  (c) Only (iii)  (d) (iii) and (iv)Dipole moment, para-substitutionWhich shows a dipole moment — 1,4-dichlorobenzene and related isomers🟡 Similard(iii) and (iv)
Concept In a para-disubstituted benzene the two substituents sit at 180° to each other, so if each group's dipole lies exactly along the ring C—X axis the two vectors are equal and opposite and cancel to zero. The whole question therefore reduces to one test: is the group dipole collinear with the C—X bond? For a linear or axially symmetric substituent (—Cl, —C≡N) it is. For a bent substituent (—O—H, —S—H) the group dipole is tilted away from the ring axis by the C—O—H (or C—S—H) angle, and the two tilted vectors no longer cancel.
Key relation μnet = |μ₁ + μ₂| ; for para groups at 180°, μnet = 0 only if both μ lie along the ring axis a bent group contributes a perpendicular component that survives even when the axial components cancel. For —OH the C—O—H angle is ≈109°, for —SH ≈96° — both far from 180°.
Solution
1. (i) p-dichlorobenzene: C—Cl is a simple σ bond, dipole strictly along the axis. Two equal, opposite vectors ⇒ μ = 0.
2. (ii) p-dicyanobenzene: —C≡N is linear, so its dipole is again axial. ⇒ μ = 0.
3. (iii) hydroquinone: each —OH is bent; the O—H bond moment points off-axis. Except in one artificially chosen anti conformation the perpendicular components add, giving a real, measurable μ ⇒ μ ≠ 0.
4. (iv) benzene-1,4-dithiol: same argument with the even more sharply bent C—S—H (≈96°) ⇒ μ ≠ 0.Sulfur uses almost pure p orbitals (Drago), so the angle is closer to 90° and the off-axis component is larger still.
5. Only (iii) and (iv) survive ⇒ option (d). Options (a) and (b) wrongly keep the axially symmetric halide/nitrile; (c) forgets that —SH behaves exactly like —OH.
 molecule            substituent shape     dipole along ring axis?   μ
 (i)  p-C6H4Cl2      C—Cl axial            YES  → cancels            0
 (ii) p-C6H4(CN)2    —C≡N linear, axial    YES  → cancels            0
 (iii) p-C6H4(OH)2   C—O—H bent ≈109°      NO   → residual          ≠0  <--
 (iv) p-C6H4(SH)2    C—S—H bent ≈ 96°      NO   → residual          ≠0  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the rule: para + axially symmetric group = zero dipole; para + bent group (OH, SH, NH₂, OR) = non-zero. The same logic explains why p-dinitrobenzene and p-benzoquinone are non-polar but hydroquinone is polar. Trap: students see "para" and reflexively answer "all zero" — the shape of the substituent, not just its position, decides.
Answer: (d) (iii) and (iv).
3Incorrect statement is: (a) AlF₃ > MgO > MgF₂ : lattice energy  (b) Li > Na > Al > Mg : electron affinity  (c) SF₆ > PF₅ > SiF₄ : Lewis acidic character  (d) SiCl₄ > SiBr₄ > SiI₄ : decreasing order of electronegativity of SiLewis acidity, lattice energyHypervalent fluorides and their Lewis acid/base behaviour🟡 SimilarcSF₆ > PF₅ > SiF₄ : Lewis acidic character
Concept A Lewis acid must be able to accept a lone pair, which needs (a) a vacant low-lying orbital and (b) enough room around the central atom for an extra ligand. Across SiF₄ → PF₅ → SF₆ the coordination number rises 4 → 5 → 6 while the central atom shrinks. SF₆ is therefore coordinatively saturated and sterically sheathed by six F atoms — it is famously inert, not acidic. SiF₄, with only four ligands round a comparatively large Si, readily adds two more F⁻ to give [SiF₆]²⁻. So the true order is exactly the reverse of what (c) claims.
Key relation Lewis acidity: SiF₄ > PF₅ > SF₆ SiF₄ + 2F⁻ → SiF₆²⁻ and PF₅ + F⁻ → PF₆⁻ both occur readily; SF₆ + F⁻ gives nothing. Lattice energy U ∝ (z₊·z₋)/(r₊ + r₋).
Solution
1. Test (a): AlF₃ (Al³⁺, U ≈ 5492) > MgO (2+/2−, U ≈ 3795) > MgF₂ (2+/1−, U ≈ 2957). The stated order is correct.Al³⁺ is tiny and triply charged, which beats even the 2+/2− pair of MgO.
2. Test (b): electron affinity Li (60) > Na (53) > Al (42) > Mg (≈ −40, essentially zero because 3s² is full). Correct.
3. Test (c): as argued, acidity falls Si → P → S; SF₆ is not a Lewis acid at all. The statement is INCORRECT — this is the answer.
4. Test (d): the electronegativity a central atom displays rises when it carries more electronegative substituents. Cl > Br > I, so Si is most electronegative in SiCl₄. Correct.
 statement                                       verdict
 (a) AlF3 > MgO > MgF2 lattice energy            TRUE
 (b) Li > Na > Al > Mg electron affinity         TRUE
 (c) SF6 > PF5 > SiF4 Lewis acidity              FALSE  <-- answer (c)
     real order: SiF4 > PF5 > SF6
 (d) Si most E.N. in SiCl4                       TRUE
⚡ JEE Trick Learn SF₆ as the textbook example of kinetic inertness: thermodynamically it should hydrolyse, but the six F atoms block any approach, so it neither hydrolyses nor accepts F⁻. Contrast SF₄, which is reactive. Trap: option (c) reads plausibly because "more F atoms ⇒ more electron-withdrawing ⇒ more acidic" — that argument fails once the octet expansion is already maximal.
Answer: (c) SF₆ > PF₅ > SiF₄ : Lewis acidic character.
4(i)Passage (VSEPR): molecules adopt geometries placing valence electron pairs as far apart as possible; multiple bonds repel slightly more than single bonds, lone pairs most of all. — Which of the following statements is false? (a) SbF₄⁻ and SF₄ are isostructural (b) In IOF₅ the hybridisation of the central atom is sp³d² (c) The double bond(s) in SOF₄ and XeO₃F₂ occupy equatorial position(s) (d) None of theseVSEPR, isostructural speciesShapes from lone pairs — VSEPR practice questions🟡 SimilardNone of these
Concept Every part of this item is settled by one count — the steric number SN = (σ bonds) + (lone pairs) — plus one VSEPR rule: in a trigonal bipyramid the equatorial positions are the roomy ones, so whatever repels most (a lone pair, or the fat π cloud of a double bond) goes equatorial. "Isostructural" means same number of σ bonds and same number of lone pairs, hence the same shape, regardless of which elements are involved.
Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs; lp = ½[V + (monovalent atoms) − (charge, signed) ] − σ count the central atom's valence electrons V, add one per monovalent ligand, subtract the charge, halve, then subtract the σ bonds. π bonds never change SN.
Solution
1. (a) SbF₄⁻ : Sb has 5 e⁻, +1 from the charge = 6; 4 σ bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair ⇒ SN 5 ⇒ see-saw. SF₄ : S has 6 e⁻, 4 σ ⇒ 1 lone pair ⇒ SN 5 ⇒ see-saw. Isostructural ⇒ statement TRUE.
2. (b) IOF₅ : iodine forms 5 σ to F plus 1 σ to O (the second I=O linkage is π) ⇒ SN 6, no lone pair ⇒ sp³d², octahedral. TRUE.
3. (c) SOF₄ : 4 F + 1 O = 5 σ, SN 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal; the S=O π cloud is bulkiest so O sits equatorial. XeO₃F₂ : 3 O + 2 F = 5 σ, SN 5 ⇒ TBP; the three Xe=O go equatorial and the two F axial. TRUE.
4. None of (a)–(c) is false, so the false statement is "none of these" ⇒ (d).
 species    σ    lp   SN   hybrid    shape              statement
 SbF4-      4    1     5   sp3d      see-saw            (a) TRUE
 SF4        4    1     5   sp3d      see-saw
 IOF5       6    0     6   sp3d2     octahedral         (b) TRUE
 SOF4       5    0     5   sp3d      TBP, O equatorial  (c) TRUE
 XeO3F2     5    0     5   sp3d      TBP, 3 O equat.
 ⇒ nothing is false                                     <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Never count π bonds when working out hybridisation — only σ bonds and lone pairs. And remember the equatorial priority list for a TBP: lone pair > double bond > single bond to the least electronegative ligand. That one line answers XeO₃F₂, SOF₄, SF₄, ClF₃ and PCl₃F₂ in a second each.
Answer: (d) None of these — all three statements are true.
4(ii)Which of the following does not represent an isostructural pair? (a) SF₅⁻ and IF₅  (b) ClO₂F₃ and SOF₄  (c) SeF₃⁺ and XeO₃  (d) None of theseIsostructural pairsTrick to find the shape and geometry of a molecule using VSEPR🟡 SimilardNone of these
Concept Two species are isostructural when they have the same number of σ bonds and the same number of lone pairs on the central atom — the identity of the atoms is irrelevant. So the whole problem is three quick electron counts. Charge matters: a negative charge adds one electron to the central atom's pool, a positive charge removes one.
Key relation lp = ½ (V + M − c) − σ where V = valence electrons of central atom, M = number of monovalent ligands, c = charge with sign for SF₅⁻: V = 6, M = 5, c = −1 ⇒ lp = ½(6+5+1) − 5 = 6 − 5 = 1.
Solution
1. (a) SF₅⁻ : σ = 5, lp = 1 ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ sp³d² ⇒ square pyramidal. IF₅ : I has 7 e⁻, 5 σ ⇒ lp = 1 ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ square pyramidal. Isostructural ✓
2. (b) ClO₂F₃ : Cl makes 3 σ to F and 2 σ to O (the Cl=O π bonds don't count) ⇒ σ = 5, lp = 0 ⇒ SN 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal. SOF₄ : σ = 5, lp = 0 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal. Isostructural ✓
3. (c) SeF₃⁺ : V = 6, M = 3, c = +1 ⇒ lp = ½(6+3−1) − 3 = 4 − 3 = 1 ⇒ SN 4 ⇒ sp³ ⇒ trigonal pyramidal. XeO₃ : σ = 3, Xe has 8 e⁻ ⇒ lp = 1 ⇒ SN 4 ⇒ trigonal pyramidal. Isostructural ✓
4. Every listed pair is isostructural, so the answer is (d) None of these.
 pair                σ   lp   SN   shape                isostructural?
 SF5- / IF5          5    1    6   square pyramidal     YES
 ClO2F3 / SOF4       5    0    5   trigonal bipyramidal YES
 SeF3+ / XeO3        3    1    4   trigonal pyramidal   YES
 ⇒ none of the pairs fails                              <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Isoelectronic ⇒ isostructural, but the converse question is best done with the (σ, lp) pair rather than by counting total electrons — it is faster and immune to charge-sign slips. Trap: candidates see the oxygens in ClO₂F₃ and XeO₃ and try to count them as two electron domains each; a double bond is still one domain.
Answer: (d) None of these — all three pairs are isostructural.
4(iii)Select the incorrect statement with respect to the SO₂Cl₂ molecule: (a) it gives H₂SO₄ and HCl on hydrolysis at room temperature (b) it has two dπ–pπ bonds between S and the bonded O atoms (c) it is a polar molecule (d) None of theseSO₂Cl₂ structure and hydrolysisSulfuryl chloride SO₂Cl₂ — structure and hybridisation🟡 SimilardNone of these
Concept Sulfuryl chloride is O₂SCl₂ — sulfur at the centre with two S=O and two S—Cl bonds, σ count 4, lone pairs 0, so it is sp³, roughly tetrahedral but distorted because the two bulky S=O π clouds push the Cl atoms together. Every one of the three statements probes a different standard fact about it, and all three happen to be true.
Key relation Hydrolysis: SO₂Cl₂ + 2H₂O → H₂SO₄ + 2HCl water attacks the electron-poor S; each Cl leaves as HCl and is replaced by OH. Bonding: S=O is one σ (sp³–p) + one π made by 2p(O) → 3d(S), i.e. a dπ–pπ bond sulfur is a third-period element with empty 3d orbitals, so it uses dπ–pπ, unlike carbon which can only use pπ–pπ.
Solution
1. (a) SO₂Cl₂ is a classic fuming liquid that hydrolyses readily even at room temperature to sulfuric acid and hydrogen chloride. TRUE.Compare SF₆, which does not hydrolyse — the difference is that Cl is a far better leaving group than F and S is more exposed here.
2. (b) Two S=O linkages ⇒ two π bonds, each formed by sideways overlap of a filled O 2p with an empty S 3d ⇒ two dπ–pπ bonds. TRUE.
3. (c) The four substituents are not identical (2 O, 2 Cl), so the bond moments cannot cancel: the resultant of the two strong S=O moments does not balance the resultant of the two S—Cl moments. Net μ ≠ 0, the molecule is polar (μ ≈ 1.8 D). TRUE.
4. Nothing is incorrect, so the answer is (d).
 statement                                    verdict
 (a) SO2Cl2 + 2H2O → H2SO4 + 2HCl             TRUE
 (b) two S=O π bonds are dπ–pπ                TRUE
 (c) 2 O + 2 Cl on sp3 S ⇒ μ ≠ 0 (polar)      TRUE
 ⇒ no incorrect statement                     <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Any tetrahedral centre carrying two different kinds of ligand (SO₂Cl₂, SOCl₂, CH₂Cl₂, POCl₃) is polar — cancellation needs four identical ligands. And remember the pairing: second-period centre ⇒ pπ–pπ; third period or below ⇒ dπ–pπ. Trap: "None of these" options make students hunt for a flaw that isn't there.
Answer: (d) None of these — every statement about SO₂Cl₂ is correct.
5(i)Passage (Fajans' rules): a cation deforms (polarises) the electron cloud of an anion; the greater the polarisation, the more covalent the bond. — Considering BeCl₂, MgCl₂, CaCl₂ and BaCl₂, which statement is true? (a) BeCl₂ is the least ionic of the given chlorides (b) covalent character increases as the atomic number of the metal increases (c) BeCl₂ has the highest melting point (d) all are highly ionic compoundsFajans' rules, covalent characterLiCl, RbCl, BeCl₂, MgCl₂ — greatest and least ionic character🟡 SimilaraBeCl₂ is least ionic out of the given chlorides
Concept Down group 2 the cation gets bigger (Be²⁺ 31 pm → Ba²⁺ 135 pm) while the charge stays +2. Polarising power is essentially charge density = z/r², so it falls steeply down the group. Be²⁺ therefore distorts Cl⁻ hardest, drags the most electron density into the internuclear region, and gives the most covalent — i.e. the least ionic — chloride. Everything else about the series follows from that one trend.
Key relation polarising power ∝ z / r² for a fixed +2 charge, smaller r ⇒ larger power; Be²⁺ (31 pm) beats Ba²⁺ (135 pm) by a factor of about 19. covalent character ↑ ⇒ lattice energy contribution ↓ ⇒ melting point ↓
Solution
1. Rank cation size: Be²⁺ < Mg²⁺ < Ca²⁺ < Ba²⁺.
2. Rank polarising power (inverse of size): Be²⁺ > Mg²⁺ > Ca²⁺ > Ba²⁺.
3. So covalent character: BeCl₂ > MgCl₂ > CaCl₂ > BaCl₂, i.e. ionic character is lowest in BeCl₂ ⇒ statement (a) is TRUE.
4. Kill the others: (b) reverses the trend — covalent character decreases with increasing atomic number here. (c) is false: BeCl₂ melts at 405 °C while BaCl₂ melts at 962 °C, exactly because BeCl₂ is the most covalent. (d) is false: BeCl₂ is a covalent, polymeric chain solid that even dissolves in organic solvents.
 chloride   cation r(pm)  polarising power  character     m.p.(°C)
 BeCl2      Be2+  31       highest           most covalent   405
 MgCl2      Mg2+  72       high              covalent-ish    714
 CaCl2      Ca2+ 100       lower             ionic           772
 BaCl2      Ba2+ 135       lowest            most ionic      962
 ⇒ BeCl2 = least ionic                                   <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Beryllium is the group-2 anomaly you can bet on: BeCl₂ is covalent, polymeric and a Lewis acid, while the rest of the group gives ordinary ionic salts. Use the diagonal relationship Be↔Al as a memory hook — AlCl₃ is covalent too. Trap: option (c) exploits the reflex "ionic ⇒ high m.p." while forgetting to ask which one is ionic.
Answer: (a) BeCl₂ is the least ionic of the given chlorides.
5(ii)In which of the halides is there maximum polarisation? (a) AlF₃  (b) AlCl₃  (c) AlBr₃  (d) AlI₃Polarisability of the anionFajans' rule — polarisation, polarising power and polarisability🟡 SimilardAlI₃
Concept Polarisation is a two-sided affair: the cation's polarising power and the anion's polarisability. Here the cation is the same (Al³⁺) in all four options, so the contest is decided entirely by the anion. Polarisability measures how loosely the outer electron cloud is held — it grows steeply with anion size because the outermost electrons are further from the nucleus and more poorly shielded.
Key relation polarisability ∝ (ionic size) ; F⁻ < Cl⁻ < Br⁻ < I⁻ radii 133, 181, 196 and 220 pm respectively — I⁻ has its valence electrons in the 5p shell, far from the nucleus. covalent character ∝ (polarising power of cation) × (polarisability of anion)
Solution
1. Cation is Al³⁺ throughout — a small, triply charged ion with very high polarising power. It is a constant, so ignore it.
2. Compare the anions by size: F⁻ (133 pm) < Cl⁻ (181) < Br⁻ (196) < I⁻ (220).
3. Polarisability follows size, so I⁻ is the most easily deformed ⇒ AlI₃ shows maximum polarisation.
4. Cross-check with a physical property: AlF₃ is a high-melting ionic solid (1290 °C) whereas AlI₃ is a low-melting, dimeric, essentially covalent solid (191 °C) — exactly what maximum polarisation predicts.AlCl₃ and AlBr₃ sit in between and both exist as covalent Al₂X₆ dimers.
 halide   anion   r(anion)/pm   polarisability   covalent character
 AlF3     F-        133          lowest           lowest (ionic)
 AlCl3    Cl-       181          higher           high
 AlBr3    Br-       196          higher still     higher
 AlI3     I-        220          highest          highest    <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick When the cation is fixed, go straight to anion size (bigger = more covalent); when the anion is fixed, go to cation charge density (smaller and more charged = more covalent). That two-line rule handles almost every Fajans MCQ. Trap: some students argue "F is most electronegative ⇒ it pulls hardest ⇒ most polarisation" — electronegativity governs bond polarity, not polarisability.
Answer: (d) AlI₃.
5(iii)Which is most covalent in nature? (a) NaCl  (b) MgCl₂  (c) AlCl₃  (d) CaCl₂Fajans' rules, cation chargePredicting covalent character with Fajans' rule — NaCl, MgCl₂, AlCl₃🟢 ExactcAlCl₃
Concept All four chlorides share the same anion, Cl⁻, so once again the anion drops out and the cation's charge density decides. Going Na⁺ → Mg²⁺ → Al³⁺ the charge rises 1 → 2 → 3 while the radius shrinks 102 → 72 → 53 pm — the two effects reinforce each other, so Al³⁺ has by far the greatest polarising power. Ca²⁺ (100 pm) is large and only doubly charged, so it sits near the ionic end.
Key relation polarising power = z / r² Na⁺: 1/102² ≈ 0.96×10⁻⁴; Mg²⁺: 2/72² ≈ 3.9×10⁻⁴; Al³⁺: 3/53² ≈ 10.7×10⁻⁴; Ca²⁺: 2/100² = 2.0×10⁻⁴ — Al³⁺ wins by a wide margin.
Solution
1. List cation charge and radius: Na⁺ (+1, 102 pm), Mg²⁺ (+2, 72 pm), Al³⁺ (+3, 53 pm), Ca²⁺ (+2, 100 pm).
2. Compute z/r² (relative units): 0.96, 3.9, 10.7, 2.0.
3. Highest value ⇒ strongest polarisation of Cl⁻ ⇒ greatest covalent character ⇒ AlCl₃.
4. Confirm experimentally: AlCl₃ sublimes at 178 °C as covalent Al₂Cl₆ dimers and dissolves in benzene, while NaCl melts at 801 °C and CaCl₂ at 772 °C as ionic lattices.A low melting point plus solubility in a non-polar solvent is the fingerprint of covalency.
 chloride  cation  z   r(pm)   z/r² (rel.)  m.p.(°C)   character
 NaCl      Na+     1   102       0.96         801      ionic
 MgCl2     Mg2+    2    72       3.9          714      partly covalent
 CaCl2     Ca2+    2   100       2.0          772      ionic
 AlCl3     Al3+    3    53      10.7          178 s.   most covalent  <-- (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the standard covalent-character ladder NaCl < MgCl₂ < AlCl₃ across a period — it is asked verbatim year after year. Add the diagonal partners BeCl₂ and AlCl₃ (both covalent dimers/polymers) and you can answer this family instantly. Trap: CaCl₂ looks "more charged than NaCl", but its cation is nearly as large as Na⁺, so it never beats Al³⁺.
Answer: (c) AlCl₃.
6(i)Passage (Drago's rule): if the central atom is in the third row or below, its lone pair occupies a stereochemically inactive s-orbital and bonding uses almost pure p-orbitals, giving bond angles near 90°, provided the substituent's electronegativity ≤ 2.5. — In which option does the covalent bond have maximum s % character? (a) S—H in H₂S (b) P—H in PH₃ (c) N—H in NH₃ (d) all have equal s % characterDrago's rule, s-characterDrago's rule — why H₂S and PH₃ have almost no s-character🟢 ExactcN — H bond in NH₃
Concept Hybridisation costs energy: the s and p orbitals must first be promoted to a common energy. For second-period atoms (N, O) the 2s and 2p levels are close together, so mixing is cheap and hybridisation happens — NH₃ is sp³. For third-period and heavier atoms (P, S) the ns–np gap is much larger, and with a weakly electronegative substituent like H there is nothing to pay for it, so the atom simply keeps its lone pair in the pure s orbital and bonds with pure p. That is Drago's rule, and it is why PH₃ (93.5°) and H₂S (92°) have almost exactly 90° angles.
Key relation cos θ = − s/(1 − s) for an equivalent hybrid s = 0.25 (sp³) ⇒ θ = 109.5°; s → 0 (pure p) ⇒ θ → 90°. Drago applies when central atom is period ≥ 3 and substituent electronegativity ≤ 2.5 H is 2.1, so both PH₃ and H₂S qualify.
Solution
1. NH₃ — nitrogen is period 2, so Drago does not apply; N hybridises sp³, giving each N—H bond ≈ 25 % s-character and an angle of 107°.
2. PH₃ — phosphorus is period 3 and H has EN 2.1 ≤ 2.5, so Drago applies: pure p bonding, ≈ 0 % s-character, angle 93.5°.
3. H₂S — sulfur is period 3, H again below 2.5: pure p bonding, ≈ 0 % s-character, angle 92°.
4. Maximum s-character therefore belongs to the N—H bond of NH₃ ⇒ option (c). Option (d) fails because the three central atoms are not from the same period.
 molecule  central atom  period  Drago applies?  bonding orbitals  s%     angle
 NH3       N               2       no            sp3               25 %   107°   <-- (c)
 PH3       P               3       yes            ~pure p            ~0 %   93.5°
 H2S       S               3       yes            ~pure p            ~0 %   92°
⚡ JEE Trick The whole hydride bond-angle series NH₃ 107° > PH₃ 93.5° > AsH₃ 92° > SbH₃ 91.5° is one application of Drago, not four separate facts. The instant you see a period-3-or-below central atom bonded to H, answer "≈90°, pure p, lone pair in s". Trap: the rule fails when the substituent is very electronegative — PF₃ has an angle of 97°, not 90°, because F (4.0) exceeds 2.5 and forces hybridisation.
Answer: (c) the N—H bond in NH₃.
6(ii)Select the incorrect statement regarding the P₄ molecule: (a) each P atom is joined to three P atoms (b) P₄ contains a total of 12 bond angles (c) the lone pair of each P atom is present in an almost pure s-orbital (d) the lone pair of each P atom is present in a hybrid orbitalP₄ tetrahedron, Drago's ruleDrago's rule across the group-15 and group-16 hydrides🟡 SimilardLone pair of each P atom present in hybrid orbital
Concept White phosphorus P₄ is a perfect tetrahedron of four P atoms with a P atom at each vertex and a P—P bond along each of the six edges. Every P is bonded to the other three and keeps one lone pair. Because P is a period-3 atom bonded to another weakly electronegative atom (P itself, EN 2.1), Drago's rule applies: the lone pair stays in the pure 3s orbital and the three bonds use nearly pure 3p orbitals — which is exactly why the ∠P–P–P is a strained 60° instead of anything near 109°.
Key relation Number of bond angles at one atom with n bonds = ⁿC₂ = n(n−1)/2 choose any two of the three bonds; 3 angles per P. Total for P₄ = 4 × ³C₂ = 4 × 3 = 12. Edges (bonds) = ⁴C₂ = 6.
Solution
1. (a) In a tetrahedron each vertex connects to the other three vertices ⇒ each P is bonded to three P atoms. TRUE.
2. (b) Each P contributes ³C₂ = 3 angles; 4 P atoms give 4 × 3 = 12 bond angles. TRUE.
3. (c) By Drago (period-3 central atom, low-EN substituent) the lone pair sits in the almost pure 3s orbital — this is precisely what allows the 60° ring strain to be tolerated. TRUE.
4. (d) This directly contradicts (c): if the lone pair were in a hybrid orbital the atom would be sp³ and the angles would tend to 109.5°, which they emphatically are not. INCORRECT ⇒ answer (d).
 feature of P4                       value           statement
 vertices (P atoms)                    4
 edges  = P—P bonds  = 4C2             6
 bonds per P                           3             (a) TRUE
 angles per P = 3C2                    3
 total bond angles = 4 × 3            12             (b) TRUE
 ∠P–P–P                               60°
 lone pair orbital            almost pure 3s         (c) TRUE
                              NOT a hybrid           (d) FALSE <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick P₄ is worth memorising as a package: 4 atoms, 6 bonds, 12 bond angles of 60°, lone pair in pure s, huge ring strain ⇒ high reactivity (it ignites in air, unlike red phosphorus). The 60° angle is itself the clue that no sp³ hybridisation is present. Trap: (c) and (d) are direct opposites — spotting a contradictory pair tells you one of them must be the answer before you read anything else.
Answer: (d) the lone pair of each P atom is present in a hybrid orbital — it is in an almost pure s orbital.
6(iii)The hybridisation of the atomic orbitals of central atom "Xe" in XeO₄, XeO₂F₂ and XeOF₄ respectively is: (a) sp³, sp³d², sp³d²  (b) sp³d, sp³d, sp³d²  (c) sp³, sp³d², sp³d  (d) sp³, sp³d, sp³d²Hybridisation of xenon compoundsHybridisation run through PCl₃, PCl₅, SF₄, SF₆, BF₃, XeF₄, XeF₆🟡 Similardsp³, sp³d, sp³d²
Concept Xenon brings eight valence electrons. Each Xe—F bond consumes one of them; each Xe=O consumes two (one σ + one π) but still counts as only one electron domain. Whatever electrons are left over pair up as lone pairs, and steric number = σ bonds + lone pairs then fixes the hybridisation. This one bookkeeping rule handles the entire xenon-compound family.
Key relation lone pairs = ½ [8 − (1 per F) − (2 per O)] ; SN = σ + lp ; SN 4 → sp³, SN 5 → sp³d, SN 6 → sp³d², SN 7 → sp³d³.
Solution
1. XeO₄: four O use 4 × 2 = 8 electrons — all of xenon's supply. lp = 0, σ = 4 ⇒ SN 4 ⇒ sp³, tetrahedral.
2. XeO₂F₂: two O use 4 e⁻, two F use 2 e⁻, total 6; leftover = 8 − 6 = 2 ⇒ 1 lone pair. σ = 4 ⇒ SN 5 ⇒ sp³d, see-saw (the lone pair and both O go equatorial, the two F axial).
3. XeOF₄: one O uses 2 e⁻, four F use 4 e⁻, total 6; leftover 2 ⇒ 1 lone pair. σ = 5 ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ sp³d², square pyramidal.
4. Sequence sp³, sp³d, sp³d² ⇒ option (d). (a) wrongly gives XeO₂F₂ six domains; (b) wrongly hybridises XeO₄ as sp³d; (c) swaps the last two.
 species    e- used by O   e- used by F   left   lp   σ   SN   hybrid    shape
 XeO4         4×2 = 8         0            0     0    4    4   sp3       tetrahedral
 XeO2F2       2×2 = 4         2×1 = 2      2     1    4    5   sp3d      see-saw
 XeOF4        1×2 = 2         4×1 = 4      2     1    5    6   sp3d2     square pyramidal
 ⇒ sp3, sp3d, sp3d2                                                <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick For xenon compounds use the shortcut lp = (8 − F − 2·O)/2 and never count the π of Xe=O as a domain. Learn the four benchmarks — XeF₂ linear (sp³d), XeF₄ square planar (sp³d²), XeF₆ distorted octahedral (sp³d³), XeO₃ pyramidal (sp³) — and every other member of the family falls out of the same arithmetic. Trap: treating Xe=O as two domains inflates SN and gives option (a) or (b).
Answer: (d) sp³, sp³d, sp³d².
7Which of the following elucidate molecular orbital theory rightly? (a) the number of MOs equals the number of AOs from which they are formed (b) BMOs increase the electron density between the nuclei (c) ABMOs have a region of zero electron density (d) the number of electrons in BMOs is more than in ABMOs  [one or more correct]MOT postulatesMolecular Orbital Theory — core concepts and postulates🟡 Similara, b, c(a), (b) and (c)
Concept MO theory is built on LCAO: n atomic orbitals combine to give exactly n molecular orbitals — half bonding (in-phase, constructive interference) and half antibonding (out-of-phase, destructive interference). Constructive combination piles electron density between the nuclei and lowers the energy; destructive combination creates a nodal plane between them where ψ = 0, hence zero electron density, and raises the energy. What MO theory does not assert is that bonding orbitals must always hold more electrons than antibonding ones — that is a property of a stable molecule, not a postulate.
Key relation ψbonding = ψA + ψB ⇒ density ∝ ψ² is enhanced between nuclei; ψantibonding = ψA − ψB ⇒ ψ = 0 at the midpoint, a node. Bond order = (Nb − Na)/2.
Solution
1. (a) Conservation of orbitals: 2 AOs → 2 MOs, 8 AOs → 8 MOs. This is a fundamental LCAO result. CORRECT.
2. (b) The in-phase sum reinforces ψ between the nuclei, so ψ² there is larger than the sum of the separate atomic densities — the electron "glue" that holds the nuclei together. CORRECT.
3. (c) The out-of-phase difference makes ψ vanish on a plane between the nuclei; ψ² = 0 there. CORRECT.
4. (d) Not a rule at all. He₂ would have Nb = Na = 2, and O₂²⁻ has plenty of antibonding electrons; species with Na ≥ Nb simply do not exist as stable molecules, but MO theory happily describes them. INCORRECT as a general statement.
 statement                                              MOT?
 (a) no. of MOs = no. of AOs combined                   YES  <--
 (b) BMO piles density between the nuclei               YES  <--
 (c) ABMO has a nodal plane (zero density)              YES  <-- answer (a,b,c)
 (d) Nb always > Na                                     NO   (He2: Nb = Na = 2)
⚡ JEE Trick Distinguish a postulate from a consequence. "MOs = AOs" and "BMO/ABMO from in-phase/out-of-phase overlap" are postulates; "Nb > Na" is merely the condition for a stable species. Whenever an option contains the words "always" or "must", test it on He₂ or Be₂ — the two molecules that do not exist.
Answer: (a), (b) and (c).
8Select the correct statement(s) regarding σ and π bonds: (a) a σ-bond lies on the line joining the nuclei of the bonded atoms (b) the π-electron cloud lies on either side of the line joining the nuclei (c) a (2pπ–3dπ) pi-bond is stronger than a (2pπ–3pπ) pi-bond (d) the σ-bond has the primary effect in deciding the direction of the covalent bond, while the π-bond has no primary effect  [one or more correct]σ vs π bondingOrbital overlap concept — sigma, pi and delta bonds compared🟡 Similara, b, c, dAll four statements are correct
Concept A σ bond is formed by head-on (axial) overlap, so its electron cloud is cylindrically symmetric about the internuclear line — the bond literally lies along that line and therefore fixes the direction of the bond. A π bond comes from sideways (lateral) overlap of parallel p (or p and d) orbitals, so its cloud sits in two lobes above and below the internuclear axis, with the axis itself a nodal line. Because the π overlap is added on to an already-formed σ framework, it cannot re-orient the bond; it only shortens and stiffens it.
Key relation σ: axial overlap, free rotation, one nodal plane containing… none through the axis; π: lateral overlap, restricted rotation, one nodal plane containing the internuclear axis. Overlap efficiency for π bonds involving period-3 atoms: 2p–3d > 2p–3p the diffuse, correctly oriented 3d lobe reaches the compact 2p far better than the mismatched, node-bearing 3p does.
Solution
1. (a) By definition of axial overlap, the σ electron density is concentrated on the internuclear line. CORRECT.
2. (b) Lateral overlap puts the π density in two lobes flanking that line, with zero density on the line itself. CORRECT.
3. (c) This is the standard size/energy-matching argument: 3p is too diffuse and too high in energy to overlap well with a compact 2p, whereas an empty 3d orbital contracts under the pull of an electronegative neighbour and gives better lateral overlap. Hence S=O (2p–3d) is stronger than the hypothetical 2p–3p π. CORRECT.It is exactly why SO₂ and SO₃ have short, strong S=O bonds while P=P and Si=Si π bonds are notoriously weak.
4. (d) Molecular geometry is set by the σ framework and the lone pairs (VSEPR); the π bonds sit wherever the σ skeleton already points them. CORRECT.
 statement                                          verdict
 (a) σ cloud on the internuclear line               TRUE
 (b) π cloud above and below that line              TRUE
 (c) 2pπ–3dπ stronger than 2pπ–3pπ                  TRUE   answer = a,b,c,d
 (d) σ decides bond direction, π does not           TRUE
⚡ JEE Trick Convert "which decides shape?" questions into "count σ bonds and lone pairs" — π bonds are invisible to VSEPR and to hybridisation. And keep the pπ–dπ fact handy: it explains the shortened S—O, P—O and Cl—O bonds and the trigonal-planar (not pyramidal) shape of N(SiH₃)₃.
Answer: (a), (b), (c) and (d) — all are correct.
9Which of the following statements is correct? (a) ClF₃ is a bent T-shaped molecule (b) in SF₄ the equatorial F—S—F angle is 103° due to lp–lp repulsion (c) in the [ICl₄]⁻ ion the Cl—I—Cl bond angle is 90° (d) in OBr₂ the bond angle is less than in OCl₂  [one or more correct]VSEPR shapes and bond anglessp³d, sp³d², sp³d³ — PCl₅, SF₄, BrF₃, SF₆, XeF₄, IF₇ shapes🟡 Similara, c(a) and (c)
Concept Four independent VSEPR facts are on trial. Two hinge on getting the number of lone pairs right (ClF₃ has two, SF₄ has one), one on recognising an AB₄E₂ square-planar ion, and the last on the "surrounding-atom electronegativity" rule: as the attached atoms become less electronegative, the bonding pairs move closer to the central atom, repel each other more, and the bond angle opens up.
Key relation lp = ½(V + M − c) − σ; AB₃E₂ ⇒ T-shape, AB₄E ⇒ see-saw, AB₄E₂ ⇒ square planar. Angle rule: EN(ligand) ↓ ⇒ bond pair closer to centre ⇒ bp–bp repulsion ↑ ⇒ angle ↑.
Solution
1. (a) ClF₃: Cl has 7 e⁻, 3 σ ⇒ 2 lone pairs ⇒ AB₃E₂ ⇒ trigonal bipyramid with both lone pairs equatorial ⇒ the three F form a slightly bent T-shape (∠ ≈ 87.5°). CORRECT.
2. (b) SF₄ has only one lone pair, so the 101–103° equatorial contraction is caused by lp–bp repulsion, not lp–lp. The number quoted is right but the reason is wrong ⇒ INCORRECT.
3. (c) [ICl₄]⁻: I has 7 e⁻ + 1 charge = 8; 4 σ ⇒ 2 lone pairs ⇒ AB₄E₂ ⇒ octahedral electron geometry with the lone pairs trans ⇒ square planar, ∠Cl–I–Cl = 90°. CORRECT.
4. (d) Br (EN 2.8) is less electronegative than Cl (3.0), so in OBr₂ the bonding pairs lie nearer to O and repel more ⇒ ∠BrOBr ≈ 112° > ∠ClOCl ≈ 111°. The statement claims the opposite ⇒ INCORRECT.Bromine's larger size adds steric repulsion in the same direction, reinforcing the conclusion.
 statement                              analysis                     verdict
 (a) ClF3 bent T-shape                  AB3E2, 2 lp equatorial       TRUE  <--
 (b) SF4 equat. 103° from lp–lp         only ONE lp ⇒ lp–bp          FALSE
 (c) [ICl4]- angle 90°                  AB4E2 ⇒ square planar        TRUE  <--
 (d) OBr2 angle < OCl2                  112° vs 111° ⇒ OBr2 LARGER   FALSE
⚡ JEE Trick Repulsion hierarchy — lp–lp > lp–bp > bp–bp — but also check how many lone pairs there actually are before invoking lp–lp; option (b) is a classic "right number, wrong reason" trap. For angle comparisons use the pair of rules: less electronegative ligand ⇒ bigger angle; less electronegative central atom ⇒ smaller angle.
Answer: (a) and (c).
10Consider three orbitals: (i) a p orbital with lobes along z, (ii) a p orbital with lobes along y, (iii) a four-lobed d orbital in the xz plane. Correct statement(s): (a) orbitals (i) and (ii) can never form any type of covalent bond (b) if the internuclear axis is 'x', combination of (ii) and (iii) can form a π bond (c) orbital (iii) can form a δ-bond with another orbital of the same lobe orientation (d) if the internuclear axis is 'x', combination of (i) and (iii) can form a π bond  [one or more correct]Orbital overlap, σ/π/δ bondsDelta bond formation by d-orbitals and how σ/π/δ overlaps differ🟡 Similara, c, d(a), (c) and (d)
Concept Two orbitals overlap productively only if they have matching symmetry about the internuclear axis. If every region of positive overlap is exactly cancelled by an equal region of negative overlap, the net overlap integral is zero and no bond of any kind can form. Classifying by how many nodal planes contain the internuclear axis: 0 ⇒ σ, 1 ⇒ π, 2 ⇒ δ. Reading the three pictures: (i) = pz, (ii) = py, (iii) = dxz.
Key relation S = ∫ ψA ψB dτ ; S = 0 ⇒ no bond mutually perpendicular orbitals always give S = 0 by symmetry, whatever the internuclear axis. δ bond: two four-lobed d orbitals, both perpendicular to the internuclear axis, overlapping face-to-face in all four lobes.
Solution
1. (a) pz and py are mutually perpendicular. Whatever axis you choose, one lobe overlaps in phase exactly as much as the other overlaps out of phase ⇒ S = 0 ⇒ no bond ever. CORRECT.
2. (b) Internuclear axis = x. py lies wholly in the xy plane; dxz lies wholly in the xz plane. The two planes meet only along x, where both have nodes ⇒ zero overlap ⇒ no π bond. INCORRECT.
3. (c) Two identically oriented four-lobed d orbitals brought together face-to-face give a δ bond — two nodal planes contain the internuclear axis. This is the bonding in [Re₂Cl₈]²⁻. CORRECT.
4. (d) Internuclear axis = x again. pz and dxz both have lobes in the xz plane, arranged side-by-side about x ⇒ lateral, in-phase overlap ⇒ a genuine pπ–dπ bond. CORRECT.
 combination              axis   orientation                overlap   bond
 pz + py                  any    mutually perpendicular       0       none   (a) TRUE
 py + dxz                  x     xy-plane vs xz-plane         0       none   (b) FALSE
 dxz + dxz (same orient.)  —     four-lobed, face to face   large     δ      (c) TRUE
 pz + dxz                  x     both lobes in xz plane     large     π      (d) TRUE
 ⇒ answer (a), (c), (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Test any overlap in two seconds: sketch the two orbitals with signs and ask "does every + · + region have a matching + · − region?" If yes, the answer is zero overlap. Then count nodal planes through the internuclear axis to name the bond: 0 = σ, 1 = π, 2 = δ. Trap: option (b) looks reasonable because both orbitals "contain x" — but they occupy perpendicular planes, which kills the overlap.
Answer: (a), (c) and (d).

Level 8

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1(i)Passage (VBT): atoms bond by pairing unpaired electrons (ground or excited state) through overlap of orbitals each carrying one unpaired electron of opposite spin. — Which orbital combination does not form a π-bond? (a) px–px sideways (b) dx²−y² + py sideways (c) dxy + dxy sideways (d) dyz + py sidewaysπ-bond symmetryWhich orbitals can overlap to form σ and which to form π bonds🟡 Similarbdx²−y² + py sideways overlapping
Concept A π bond needs both partners to be π-symmetric about the internuclear axis — i.e. each must have exactly one nodal plane that contains that axis, and the two must share the same nodal plane so their lobes lie in the same plane and can overlap laterally. Orbitals whose lobes point along the axis are σ-type; orbitals with two nodal planes through the axis are δ-type. Pair a π-type with a σ-type or a δ-type and the positive and negative overlaps cancel exactly, giving zero.
Key relation Nodal planes containing the internuclear axis: 0 → σ, 1 → π, 2 → δ. π bond forms ⟺ both orbitals are π-type in the SAME plane a p orbital can only ever be σ or π; dx²−y² and dz² are the two that behave badly.
Solution
1. (a) px + px with the axis along z: both are perpendicular to z and lie in the same (xz) plane ⇒ classic lateral overlap ⇒ π bond forms.
2. (c) dxy + dxy with the axis along x: dxy has the xz plane as a node containing x ⇒ π-type; identical partners ⇒ π bond forms.
3. (d) dyz + py with the axis along z: dyz is π-type in the yz plane and py is π-type in the same yz plane ⇒ π bond forms (this is the standard pπ–dπ bond of SO₂, SO₃, POCl₃).
4. (b) dx²−y² has its four lobes lying along the x and y axes. Choose the axis along z: dx²−y² then has two nodal planes through z ⇒ δ-type, while py is π-type — mismatch, zero overlap. Choose the axis along x or y instead: dx²−y² now points straight at the partner ⇒ σ-type, still not π. No orientation gives a π bond ⇒ answer (b).
 combination         axis   symmetry of each         net overlap   bond
 px + px               z     π  and  π  (xz plane)     large        π
 dx²−y² + py           z     δ  and  π                   0          none  <-- (b)
                    x or y   σ  and  σ                 (σ, not π)
 dxy + dxy             x     π  and  π  (xy plane)     large        π
 dyz + py              z     π  and  π  (yz plane)     large        π (pπ–dπ)
⚡ JEE Trick Sort every d orbital once and for all: dz² is always σ; dxz, dyz, dxy can be π; dx²−y² and dxy can be δ (which one depends on the axis you choose). The two "axis-hugging" orbitals, dz² and dx²−y², are the ones that break π bonding. Trap: option (d) looks exotic but is exactly the pπ–dπ bond you already know from SO₂.
Answer: (b) dx²−y² + py.
1(ii)Which of the following orbitals cannot form a δ-bond? (a) dx²−y²  (b) dxy  (c) dz²  (d) dzxδ-bond formationWhy dz² overlap gives only a σ bond and never π or δ🟢 Exactcdz² orbital
Concept A δ bond is the "face-to-face" overlap of two four-lobed d orbitals that both lie in a plane perpendicular to the internuclear axis, so that all four lobes of one meet all four lobes of the other. Its signature is two nodal planes containing the internuclear axis. Four of the five d orbitals (dxy, dyz, dzx, dx²−y²) have the required four-lobed cloverleaf shape. The fifth, dz², does not — it is two lobes along z plus a doughnut, and it is cylindrically symmetric about z, so it has no nodal plane containing z at all.
Key relation δ bond ⇒ 2 nodal planes through the internuclear axis; dz² has 0 nodal planes through z (it has two nodal cones instead) cylindrical symmetry about the axis is the definition of σ symmetry — so dz² can only ever give a σ overlap.
Solution
1. Ask of each orbital: is it four-lobed and can it be oriented perpendicular to the internuclear axis?
2. dx²−y² — four lobes in the xy plane. Take the axis along z ⇒ perpendicular ⇒ δ possible.
3. dxy — four lobes in the xy plane. Axis along z ⇒ δ possible.
4. dzx — four lobes in the zx plane. Axis along y ⇒ perpendicular ⇒ δ possible.
5. dz² — its unique axis is z and it is rotationally symmetric about z; along any other axis it still presents a lobe/collar head-on. It can never present four lobes face-on ⇒ δ impossible ⇒ answer (c).This is exactly why in the quadruple bond of [Re₂Cl₈]²⁻ the δ component comes from dx²−y², never from dz², which supplies the σ.
 orbital     shape                 can lie ⟂ to an axis?   δ bond?
 dx²−y²      4 lobes on x,y axes         yes (axis z)        YES
 dxy         4 lobes between x,y         yes (axis z)        YES
 dzx         4 lobes between z,x         yes (axis y)        YES
 dz²         2 lobes + collar,           NO — cylindrical     NO   <-- answer (c)
             symmetric about z                                (σ only)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the one-line summary of the Re₂Cl₈²⁻ quadruple bond: σ (dz²) + 2π (dxz, dyz) + δ (dx²−y²). It simultaneously tells you which orbital does what and proves that a bond order of 4 is possible without a third π bond. Trap: students eliminate dx²−y² because "its lobes lie on the axes" — that is fine for δ, it is only fatal for π.
Answer: (c) the dz² orbital.
1(iii)Which combination of orbitals does not form any type of covalent bond (z-axis is the molecular axis)? (a) pz + pz  (b) py + py  (c) s + py  (d) s + sZero-overlap combinations2s overlapping 2px or 2py with z as the internuclear axis — why nothing happens🟢 Exactcs + py
Concept An s orbital is a sphere: it is positive everywhere and has no directional preference. A py orbital placed on the neighbouring atom with z as the internuclear axis lies perpendicular to that axis, with a + lobe above the yz mid-plane and an identical − lobe below. The sphere therefore overlaps the + lobe and the − lobe by exactly equal amounts, and the two contributions cancel term for term. The overlap integral is rigorously zero — no bonding MO, no antibonding MO, nothing at all.
Key relation S = ∫ ψs ψp_y dτ = 0 by symmetry the integrand is odd in y about the plane y = 0 while the s orbital is even, so the integral vanishes exactly, not approximately. Rule of thumb: an s orbital can only bond with an orbital that points at it.
Solution
1. (a) pz + pz along z: both point head-on at each other ⇒ axial overlap ⇒ σ bond.
2. (b) py + py along z: both perpendicular to z, in the same yz plane ⇒ lateral overlap ⇒ π bond.
3. (d) s + s: two spheres always overlap constructively ⇒ σ bond (this is H₂).
4. (c) s + py along z: the sphere sees + and − lobes symmetrically ⇒ S = 0 ⇒ no bond of any type ⇒ answer (c).Compare s + pz, which does bond — that is the σ bond in HCl. Orientation, not identity, is what decides.
 combination   orientation w.r.t. z      overlap        result
 pz + pz       both along z              constructive   σ bond
 py + py       both ⟂ to z, same plane   constructive   π bond
 s  + s        spherical                 constructive   σ bond
 s  + py       sphere sees + and − equal  + and − cancel  ZERO  <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the three zero-overlap classics: s + p⟂, p∥ + p⟂, and s + dxy-type. Every "which pair forms no bond" question is one of these three in disguise. Draw the two orbitals with signs and look for a mirror plane that flips one orbital's sign but not the other's — that mirror plane is a guarantee of zero overlap.
Answer: (c) s + py.
2(i)Passage (σ and π bonds): a covalent bond forms by overlap of atomic orbitals each holding one electron of opposite spin; axial overlap gives σ, sideways overlap gives π, and σ is the stronger. — The correct order of increasing C—O bond length of CO, CO₃²⁻, CO₂ is: (a) CO₃²⁻ < CO₂ < CO (b) CO₂ < CO₃²⁻ < CO (c) CO < CO₃²⁻ < CO₂ (d) CO < CO₂ < CO₃²⁻Bond order vs bond lengthHow bond order controls bond length and bond dissociation energy🟡 SimilardCO < CO₂ < CO₃²⁻
Concept Bond length is controlled by bond order: the more shared electron pairs holding two nuclei together, the shorter and stronger the bond. For a species with resonance the relevant quantity is the average (fractional) bond order — total bonds between the two kinds of atom divided by the number of equivalent linkages. Ranking C—O in these three species is therefore purely an arithmetic exercise.
Formula average bond order = (total number of bonds between X and Y) ÷ (number of X—Y linkages) for CO₃²⁻: 4 bonds shared over 3 C—O linkages. And bond order ↑ ⇒ bond length ↓.
Solution
1. CO: C≡O with one dative π — bond order 3, length 113 pm.MO count: Nb = 10, Na = 4 ⇒ (10−4)/2 = 3, the same as isoelectronic N₂.
2. CO₂: O=C=O, two independent double bonds — bond order 2, length 116 pm.
3. CO₃²⁻: three resonance structures share one C=O and two C—O ⇒ 4 bonds over 3 linkages ⇒ bond order 1.33, length 129 pm.
4. Increasing length is decreasing bond order: 3 > 2 > 1.33 ⇒ CO < CO₂ < CO₃²⁻ ⇒ option (d). Option (a) is exactly reversed; (b) and (c) misplace CO₂.
 species     structure           bonds / linkages   bond order   length (pm)
 CO          C≡O                     3 / 1             3.00         113   shortest
 CO2         O=C=O                   4 / 2             2.00         116
 CO3^2-      resonance hybrid        4 / 3             1.33         129   longest
 increasing length ⇒ CO < CO2 < CO3^2-                          <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the fractional-bond-order shortcut for oxyanions: NO₃⁻ = 1.33, CO₃²⁻ = 1.33, NO₂⁻ = 1.5, SO₄²⁻ = 1.5, ClO₄⁻ = 1.75. Also remember that CO has the shortest and strongest bond of any diatomic (1072 kJ mol⁻¹), beating even N₂. Trap: reading "increasing" as "decreasing" — the two are adjacent options here on purpose.
Answer: (d) CO < CO₂ < CO₃²⁻.
2(ii)The compound having maximum bond angle is: (a) BBr₃  (b) BCl₃  (c) BF₃  (d) None of theseBond angle in boron trihalidesBCl₃ molecular geometry — trigonal planar, 120°, no lone pair🟡 SimilardNone of these — all three are 120°
Concept The usual "electronegativity of the surrounding atom" rule (bond angle rises as the ligand becomes less electronegative) only bites when the central atom carries a lone pair or when the geometry is otherwise flexible. In BX₃ the boron has just three bond pairs, no lone pair, and by symmetry the only arrangement that keeps three identical pairs as far apart as possible in a plane is exactly 120°. Symmetry, not electronegativity, fixes the angle — so all three halides have the same bond angle and no one of them can be "maximum".
Key relation AB₃ with 0 lone pairs ⇒ sp², trigonal planar, ∠ = 360°/3 = 120° exactly three equivalent ligands round a planar centre must be symmetry-equivalent; there is no way to distort without making them inequivalent.
Solution
1. Boron has 3 valence electrons; in BX₃ all three form σ bonds ⇒ σ = 3, lp = 0 ⇒ SN 3 ⇒ sp², trigonal planar for BF₃, BCl₃ and BBr₃ alike.
2. Three identical ligands at maximum separation in a plane ⇒ ∠X–B–X = 120° in every case.
3. Halogen electronegativity (F 4.0 > Cl 3.0 > Br 2.8) would normally shift the angle, but here it cannot: any deviation would break the three-fold symmetry.What the halogen does change is B—X bond length and back-bonding strength (F best), which is why Lewis acidity runs BF₃ < BCl₃ < BBr₃ — but not the angle.
4. All equal ⇒ no single option is "maximum" ⇒ answer (d) None of these.
 molecule   σ   lp   SN   hybrid   shape             bond angle
 BF3        3    0    3   sp2      trigonal planar     120°
 BCl3       3    0    3   sp2      trigonal planar     120°
 BBr3       3    0    3   sp2      trigonal planar     120°
 ⇒ all identical, none is maximum                     <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Apply the electronegativity–bond-angle rule only when a lone pair is present (NH₃ vs NF₃, PF₃ vs PCl₃, OF₂ vs OCl₂). For lone-pair-free symmetric shapes — BX₃ 120°, CX₄ 109.5°, SF₆ 90°, PCl₅ 120°/90° — the angle is locked by symmetry. Trap: this question deliberately mimics the PF₃ < PCl₃ < PBr₃ series, where the rule does apply because P has a lone pair.
Answer: (d) None of these — BF₃, BCl₃ and BBr₃ all have 120°.
2(iii)The strength of bonds formed by 2s–2s, 2p–2p and 2p–2s overlap has the order: (a) s–s > p–p > p–s  (b) s–s > p–s > p–p  (c) p–p > p–s > s–s  (d) p–p > s–s > p–sExtent of orbital overlaps–s, s–p and p–p overlapping compared — which gives the strongest bond🟢 Exactcp–p > p–s > s–s
Concept Bond strength tracks the extent of overlap, and extent of overlap tracks directionality. An s orbital is a sphere, so when two s orbitals meet only a small slice of each cloud lies in the bonding region — the rest points uselessly outwards in all directions. A p orbital is a directed dumb-bell: essentially the whole of one lobe can be aimed straight at the partner. So two p orbitals concentrate the most charge between the nuclei, an s–p pair is intermediate, and s–s is the weakest.
Key relation bond strength ∝ overlap integral S; directionality ranking p (directed) > s (spherical) both partners directed > one directed > neither directed ⇒ p–p > p–s > s–s.
Solution
1. p–p axial overlap: two dumb-bells nose to nose — the entire front lobe of each sits in the internuclear region ⇒ largest S ⇒ strongest.
2. p–s overlap: one directed lobe meets a sphere — good, but half the s density is wasted ⇒ intermediate S.
3. s–s overlap: two spheres — only a thin lens-shaped region is shared ⇒ smallest S ⇒ weakest.
4. Hence p–p > p–s > s–s ⇒ option (c). Options (a) and (b) put the non-directional s–s on top, which contradicts the whole basis of directed valence; (d) misorders the middle term.Consistency check: H₂ (s–s) has D = 436 kJ mol⁻¹ while N₂'s σ component (p–p) is far stronger — and this ranking is also why hybrid orbitals, which have p character, out-perform pure s.
 overlap    orbitals            directed?      overlap region     strength
 p–p        dumb-bell/dumb-bell  both           largest            1st  <-- answer (c)
 p–s        dumb-bell/sphere     one            medium             2nd
 s–s        sphere/sphere        neither        smallest           3rd
⚡ JEE Trick The same "more directional ⇒ better overlap ⇒ stronger bond" logic explains why hybrid orbitals beat pure orbitals and why bond strength within a hybrid family rises with p-character in the σ-framework sense. Trap: students confuse this with the energy ordering (s lower than p) and conclude s–s must be more stable — overlap, not orbital energy, is what is being ranked.
Answer: (c) p–p > p–s > s–s.
3(i)Passage (extent of overlap): the extent of overlapping depends on the types of orbitals involved and the nature of the overlap; more overlap ⇒ higher bond energy. — The incorrect order of bond dissociation energy is: (a) H—H > Cl—Cl > Br—Br (b) Si—Si > P—P > Cl—Cl (c) C—C > N—N > O—O (d) H—Cl > H—Br > H—IBond dissociation energiesBond energy, bond dissociation enthalpy and mean bond enthalpy🟡 SimilarbSi—Si > P—P > Cl—Cl
Concept Two opposing effects govern single-bond strength. Going down a group, atoms get bigger, overlap gets poorer and the bond weakens (H—H > ... , H—Cl > H—Br > H—I). Going across period 2, however, lone-pair/lone-pair repulsion on the small atoms takes over: N—N and O—O are anomalously weak because their lone pairs are forced close together. Period-3 elements are large enough that this repulsion is relieved, so within period 3 the trend is the ordinary one — Cl—Cl is stronger than P—P and Si—Si, the reverse of period 2.
Key relation Reference values (kJ mol⁻¹): H—H 436, Cl—Cl 242, Br—Br 193 · Si—Si 222, P—P 201, Cl—Cl 242 · C—C 348, N—N 163, O—O 146 · H—Cl 431, H—Br 366, H—I 298
Solution
1. (a) 436 > 242 > 193 ✓ — correct as written (H is tiny, so H—H is exceptionally strong).
2. (c) 348 > 163 > 146 ✓ — correct; the collapse from C to N is the lone-pair-repulsion anomaly of period 2.
3. (d) 431 > 366 > 298 ✓ — correct; down the halogen group the overlap with H's small 1s worsens.
4. (b) Actual values are Cl—Cl 242 > Si—Si 222 > P—P 201, i.e. the true order is Cl—Cl > Si—Si > P—P. The stated Si > P > Cl is INCORRECT ⇒ answer (b).In period 3 the atoms are big enough that lone-pair repulsion no longer dominates, so Cl—Cl behaves normally — unlike F—F (158), which is the period-2 anomaly.
 order given                       actual values (kJ/mol)          verdict
 (a) H–H > Cl–Cl > Br–Br           436 > 242 > 193                 TRUE
 (b) Si–Si > P–P > Cl–Cl           222 , 201 , 242 → Cl is largest  FALSE  <-- (b)
 (c) C–C > N–N > O–O               348 > 163 > 146                 TRUE
 (d) H–Cl > H–Br > H–I             431 > 366 > 298                 TRUE
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the two famous weak-bond anomalies of period 2: N—N and O—O (and F—F) are weaker than their period-3 counterparts P—P, S—S and Cl—Cl, because of lone-pair repulsion in the compact second shell. Combined with "down a group, bonds weaken", that single fact settles almost every bond-energy ordering asked.
Answer: (b) Si—Si > P—P > Cl—Cl is the incorrect order.
3(ii)Which combination of orbitals does not form a covalent bond (x-axis is the internuclear axis)? (a) s + py  (b) py + py  (c) dyz + dyz  (d) dxy + dxyZero-overlap combinationsWhich overlap gives a bond when x is the internuclear axis🟡 Similaras + py
Concept With the internuclear axis fixed along x, classify each orbital by how it looks along x. An s orbital is spherically symmetric ⇒ σ-type. py is perpendicular to x ⇒ π-type (nodal plane xz). dyz has its four lobes in the yz plane, entirely perpendicular to x ⇒ δ-type. dxy has lobes between x and y ⇒ π-type (nodal plane xz). A bond forms only when the two partners belong to the same symmetry class.
Key relation With axis = x: s, px, dx²−y², dz² → σ · py, pz, dxy, dxz → π · dyz → δ count nodal planes containing x: 0 = σ, 1 = π, 2 = δ.
Solution
1. (b) py + py: π with π, same plane ⇒ π bond forms.
2. (c) dyz + dyz: δ with δ, four lobes face to face ⇒ δ bond forms.
3. (d) dxy + dxy: π with π ⇒ π bond forms.
4. (a) s (σ-type) + py (π-type): symmetry mismatch. The sphere overlaps the + and − lobes of py equally, so S = 0 ⇒ no bond ⇒ answer (a).Change py to px and the pair immediately bonds — the orbital is not the problem, its orientation relative to x is.
 pair (axis = x)   symmetry class each      match?   result
 s + py            σ  and  π                 NO      zero overlap   <-- answer (a)
 py + py           π  and  π                 YES     π bond
 dyz + dyz         δ  and  δ                 YES     δ bond
 dxy + dxy         π  and  π                 YES     π bond
⚡ JEE Trick Write the symmetry table for your chosen axis once at the top of the rough sheet, then just read off matches. With x as axis: σ = {s, px, dx²−y², dz²}, π = {py, pz, dxy, dxz}, δ = {dyz}. Trap: the exam switches the axis between sub-questions (here x, in 1(iii) z) precisely to catch memorised answers.
Answer: (a) s + py.
3(iii)Which compound does not form a pπ–pπ bond? (a) SO₃  (b) NO₃⁻  (c) SO₄²⁻  (d) CO₃²⁻pπ–pπ vs dπ–pπ bondingCounting pπ–dπ bonds in SO₄²⁻, NO₃⁻ and the SO₃ trimer🟢 ExactcSO₄²⁻
Concept A pπ–pπ bond needs an unhybridised p orbital left over on the central atom. A second-period centre (C, N) is sp² in these oxyanions and always keeps one pure p, so CO₃²⁻ and NO₃⁻ are pure pπ–pπ systems. Sulfur is different: it can use either a leftover 3p or its empty 3d. In SO₃ the sp² sulfur still has one unhybridised 3p, so one of the three π bonds is pπ–pπ and the other two are dπ–pπ. In SO₄²⁻ the sulfur is sp³ — all four of its s and p orbitals are consumed by the four σ bonds — so nothing is left for pπ–pπ and both π bonds must be dπ–pπ.
Key relation unhybridised p available? sp² → yes (one) · sp³ → no (none) sp² uses s + px + py, leaving pz; sp³ uses s + all three p.
Solution
1. CO₃²⁻: C is sp², leftover 2pz overlaps the O 2p ⇒ pπ–pπ (carbon has no d orbitals at all, so it can do nothing else).
2. NO₃⁻: N is sp², leftover 2pz ⇒ pπ–pπ.
3. SO₃: S is sp² with three σ bonds; leftover 3pz gives one pπ–pπ, and two empty 3d orbitals give two dπ–pπ. So SO₃ does form a pπ–pπ bond.
4. SO₄²⁻: S is sp³ with four σ bonds — every s and p orbital is used. The two π bonds therefore use 3d only ⇒ two dπ–pπ, zero pπ–pπ ⇒ answer (c).
 species    central atom   hybrid   leftover p?   π bonds
 SO3        S              sp2      yes (3pz)     1 pπ–pπ  + 2 dπ–pπ
 NO3-       N              sp2      yes (2pz)     1 pπ–pπ  (delocalised)
 CO3^2-     C              sp2      yes (2pz)     1 pπ–pπ  (delocalised)
 SO4^2-     S              sp3      NO            2 dπ–pπ only   <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick One question settles it every time: is the central atom sp³? If yes, there is no leftover p orbital and any π bond it makes must be dπ–pπ (SO₄²⁻, ClO₄⁻, PO₄³⁻). If it is sp² or sp, a pπ–pπ bond is available. Trap: option (a) — students remember "SO₃ has dπ–pπ" and forget that it has one pπ–pπ as well.
Answer: (c) SO₄²⁻.
4(i)Passage: elements P (period 2, 4 valence e⁻), Q (2, 6), R (3, 7), S (3, 3), T (3, 6), U (3, 4). — Choose the incorrect statement: (a) P exhibits maximum covalency among the elements given (b) Q does not exhibit variable covalency (c) R exhibits minimum covalency among the elements given (d) R and S combine to form an SR₅ type compoundCovalency from positionReading valency and bonding from an electronic configuration🟡 SimilardR and S combine to form SR₅
Concept Decode each label first — period number plus valence-electron count fixes the element uniquely. Then apply the covalency rule: a period-2 element can never exceed a covalency of 4 (no d orbitals, only four orbitals in the L shell), whereas period-3 elements can promote electrons into empty 3d orbitals and show variable covalency rising in steps of two.
Key relation P (2, 4e) = C · Q (2, 6e) = O · R (3, 7e) = Cl · S (3, 3e) = Al · T (3, 6e) = S · U (3, 4e) = Si; maximum covalency (period 2) = 4 ; (period 3) = up to 6, 7 with promotion
Solution
1. Identify: P = carbon, Q = oxygen, R = chlorine, S = aluminium, T = sulfur, U = silicon.
2. (b) Oxygen is period 2 with no d orbitals and cannot promote ⇒ covalency fixed at 2 ⇒ "does not exhibit variable covalency" is TRUE.
3. (c) Chlorine's ordinary covalency is 1 (as in HCl, Cl₂), the lowest of the whole set — C is 4, O is 2, Al is 3, S is 2, Si is 4 ⇒ TRUE.
4. (d) R + S means Cl + Al. Aluminium has only three valence electrons and no way to reach a covalency of five, so AlCl₅ does not exist — the real compound is AlCl₃ (dimerising to Al₂Cl₆). INCORRECT ⇒ answer (d).
5. (a) is stated in the sense that carbon shows the maximum covalency that a period-2 element can (4) and is the only element here whose covalency is fixed at that maximum.Read strictly, Cl can reach 7 in Cl₂O₇, so (a) is loosely worded — but only (d) describes a compound that cannot exist at all, so (d) remains the intended and safest answer.
 label  period  val.e-   element   usual covalency   max covalency
 P        2       4         C            4                4
 Q        2       6         O            2                2  (no variable)
 R        3       7         Cl           1                7 (Cl2O7)
 S        3       3         Al           3                4 ([AlCl4]-)
 T        3       6         S            2                6 (SF6)
 U        3       4         Si           4                6 ([SiF6]2-)
 ⇒ "AlCl5" is impossible                              <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Decode letter-element puzzles by writing the configuration immediately: "period 3, 7 valence e⁻" is 3s²3p⁵ = Cl, no guessing needed. Then remember the period-2 ceiling of 4: no NF₅, no OF₆, no AlCl₅ (Al is period 3 but has only 3 valence electrons, which caps it at 4 even with an empty orbital).
Answer: (d) R and S combine to form an SR₅ type compound — AlCl₅ does not exist.
4(ii)Choose the correct statement: (a) Q has the maximum value of electron affinity (b) R has the maximum value of electronegativity (c) S has the maximum atomic size (d) T and U are same-group elementsPeriodic trendsIonisation enthalpy, electron gain enthalpy and electronegativity for JEE🟡 SimilarcS has maximum atomic size
Concept With the six elements identified (C, O, Cl, Al, S, Si) the question becomes four routine periodic-trend checks. Two of them hinge on the famous period-2 anomalies: oxygen's electron affinity is lower than sulfur's and far lower than chlorine's, because the incoming electron is crammed into a small 2p shell and suffers severe repulsion. Atomic size, meanwhile, decreases left to right and increases down — so the leftmost period-3 element in the list wins.
Key relation Electron affinity (kJ mol⁻¹): Cl 349 > S 200 > O 141 > C 122 > Si 134 > Al 42. Electronegativity (Pauling): O 3.5 > Cl 3.0 > C 2.5 = S 2.5 > Si 1.8 > Al 1.5. Atomic radius (pm): Al 143 > Si 118 > S 104 > Cl 99 > C 77 > O 66.
Solution
1. (a) Q = O, EA 141. But R = Cl has 349 — the highest of the whole periodic table's common elements. FALSE.The small 2p shell of O makes the added electron unusually repelled — this is why Cl, not F, holds the EA record.
2. (b) R = Cl, EN 3.0. But Q = O has 3.5. FALSE.
3. (c) S = Al, radius 143 pm — larger than Si (118), S (104), Cl (99), C (77) and O (66). TRUE ⇒ answer (c).
4. (d) T = sulfur (group 16), U = silicon (group 14) — two groups apart. FALSE.
 statement                       check                                verdict
 (a) O has max EA                Cl 349 > S 200 > O 141               FALSE
 (b) Cl has max EN               O 3.5 > Cl 3.0                       FALSE
 (c) Al has max atomic size      Al 143 pm, largest of the six        TRUE   <--
 (d) S and Si in the same group  group 16 vs group 14                 FALSE
⚡ JEE Trick Two anomalies pay for themselves every year: EA: Cl > F and EA: S > O (small period-2 atoms repel the incoming electron). For size, remember that within a period the group-13 element is the biggest p-block atom, so Al beats Si, S and Cl comfortably.
Answer: (c) S (aluminium) has the maximum atomic size.
4(iii)Choose the incorrect statement: (a) SR₃ is a hypovalent compound (b) UR₄ can act as a Lewis acid (c) PQ₂ cannot act as a Lewis acid (d) UR₄ > SR₃ : Lewis acidic characterLewis acidity, hypovalencyCoordinate covalent bonds — how to spot the donor and the acceptor🟡 SimilarcPQ₂ (CO₂) cannot act as a Lewis acid
Concept Substituting the identities from the passage: SR₃ = AlCl₃, UR₄ = SiCl₄, PQ₂ = CO₂. A Lewis acid is anything that can accept a lone pair — either because it has an incomplete octet (AlCl₃, six electrons ⇒ hypovalent) or because it has a vacant low-lying orbital and room to expand (SiCl₄, which goes to [SiCl₆]²⁻), or because it has an electrophilic multiply-bonded carbon whose π* can accept density (CO₂).
Key relation CO₂ + OH⁻ → HCO₃⁻ and CO₂ + 2OH⁻ → CO₃²⁻ + H₂O the hydroxide lone pair attacks the carbon — textbook Lewis acid behaviour, which is also why CO₂ dissolves in alkali. SiCl₄ + 2Cl⁻ → [SiCl₆]²⁻ silicon uses its empty 3d orbitals to reach coordination number 6; carbon cannot do this, which is why CCl₄ is inert to hydrolysis.
Solution
1. (a) AlCl₃ has only 6 electrons around Al — an incomplete octet, the definition of a hypovalent species. TRUE.
2. (b) SiCl₄ readily accepts lone pairs (hydrolyses instantly in water, forms [SiCl₆]²⁻). TRUE.
3. (c) CO₂ is a well-known Lewis acid: it is attacked by OH⁻, by NH₃ (giving carbamate) and by Grignard reagents. Saying it "cannot act as a Lewis acid" is INCORRECT ⇒ answer (c).
4. (d) Statement of the book's ordering: silicon can expand its coordination number to 6 whereas aluminium is limited to 4, so SiCl₄ is credited with the greater Lewis acidity here. Accepted as TRUE.Note this ordering is convention-dependent — against hard bases AlCl₃ is famously the stronger acid — but only (c) is unambiguously false, so it remains the answer.
 statement                              species        verdict
 (a) SR3 hypovalent                     AlCl3, 6 e-     TRUE
 (b) UR4 is a Lewis acid                SiCl4 → SiCl6²- TRUE
 (c) PQ2 cannot be a Lewis acid         CO2 + OH- → HCO3-   FALSE  <-- answer (c)
 (d) UR4 > SR3 Lewis acidity            Si expands to 6     TRUE (as printed)
⚡ JEE Trick Three families of Lewis acid to recognise instantly: incomplete octet (BF₃, AlCl₃, BeCl₂), expandable octet (SiCl₄, PCl₅, SF₄), and electrophilic multiple bond (CO₂, SO₂, SO₃). Trap: candidates dismiss CO₂ because it "has a complete octet" — the acceptor orbital there is the π*, not an empty σ orbital.
Answer: (c) PQ₂ (CO₂) cannot act as a Lewis acid — it can.
5(i)Passage (hybridisation): hybridisation mixes orbitals of comparable energy on the same atom; hybrid orbitals overlap more efficiently than pure s or p orbitals. — Which is not correctly matched between the species and the type of overlapping? (a) XeO₃ : three (dπ–pπ) bonds (b) H₂SO₄ : two (dπ–pπ) bonds (c) SO₃ : three (dπ–pπ) bonds (d) HClO₄ : three (dπ–pπ) bondsdπ–pπ bond countingpπ–pπ and pπ–dπ bonding inside the SO₂ molecule🟡 SimilarcSO₃ : three (dπ–pπ) bonds
Concept Count π bonds first (one per X=O double bond), then ask which of them can be pπ–pπ. If the central atom is sp³ it has no spare p orbital, so every π bond is dπ–pπ. If it is sp², exactly one spare p remains, so one π bond is pπ–pπ and the rest are dπ–pπ. That single distinction is what makes SO₃ the odd one out.
Key relation number of dπ–pπ bonds = (total π bonds) − (1 if the centre is sp², 0 if sp³) carbon and nitrogen have no d orbitals, so this bookkeeping applies only to period-3-and-below centres.
Solution
1. XeO₃: 3 σ + 1 lone pair ⇒ SN 4 ⇒ sp³; three Xe=O ⇒ three π, no spare p ⇒ 3 dπ–pπ. Matched correctly.
2. H₂SO₄: S has 4 σ (2 to =O, 2 to —OH) ⇒ SN 4 ⇒ sp³; two S=O ⇒ 2 dπ–pπ. Matched correctly.
3. HClO₄: Cl has 4 σ (3 to =O, 1 to —OH) ⇒ SN 4 ⇒ sp³; three Cl=O ⇒ 3 dπ–pπ. Matched correctly.
4. SO₃: S has only 3 σ and no lone pair ⇒ SN 3 ⇒ sp², so one 3p orbital survives. Of the three S=O π bonds, one is pπ–pπ and only two are dπ–pπ. The stated "three dπ–pπ" is wrong ⇒ answer (c).
 species    σ   lp   SN   hybrid   π bonds   pπ–pπ   dπ–pπ   printed   ok?
 XeO3       3    1    4   sp3        3         0       3       3       YES
 H2SO4      4    0    4   sp3        2         0       2       2       YES
 SO3        3    0    3   sp2        3         1       2       3       NO   <-- (c)
 HClO4      4    0    4   sp3        3         0       3       3       YES
⚡ JEE Trick Use the one-liner: sp³ centre ⇒ all π bonds are dπ–pπ; sp² centre ⇒ exactly one pπ–pπ, the rest dπ–pπ. It instantly gives SO₂ (1 pπ–pπ + 1 dπ–pπ), SO₃ (1 + 2), SO₄²⁻ (0 + 2), ClO₄⁻ (0 + 3) and POCl₃ (0 + 1). Trap: XeO₃ and SO₃ look like twins on paper, but their steric numbers differ because XeO₃ carries a lone pair.
Answer: (c) SO₃ has two dπ–pπ bonds and one pπ–pπ, not three dπ–pπ.
5(ii)Consider NH₃, PH₃, H₂S, SO₂, SO₃, BF₃, PCl₃, IF₇, P₄, H₂ and select the incorrect statement: (a) six molecules involve hybridisation (b) three molecules are hypervalent compounds (c) six molecules are non-planar in structure (d) two molecules involve dπ–pπ bonding as well as pπ–pπ bondingHybridisation and planarity countingIn which molecule are all atoms coplanar — CH₄, BF₃, PF₃, NH₃🟡 SimilarcSix molecules are non-planar in structure
Concept Four separate counts across the same ten species. Two rules do the heavy lifting: Drago's rule (a period-3-or-below central atom bonded to a low-electronegativity ligand does not hybridise — PH₃, H₂S, P₄) and the "any three atoms are automatically coplanar" geometry fact, which rescues H₂S, SO₂ and H₂ from the non-planar list.
Key relation Drago applies when central atom period ≥ 3 AND ligand EN ≤ 2.5. Hypervalent ⇒ more than 8 electrons round the central atom. Planarity ⇒ a molecule of ≤ 3 atoms is always planar; 4+ atoms need a flat arrangement.
Solution
1. Hybridisation (a): NH₃ sp³, SO₂ sp², SO₃ sp², BF₃ sp², PCl₃ sp³ (Cl EN 3.0 > 2.5, so Drago fails and P must hybridise), IF₇ sp³d³ ⇒ six. PH₃, H₂S, P₄ are pure-p (Drago) and H₂ is plain s–s ⇒ statement TRUE.
2. Hypervalent (b): SO₂ (10 e⁻), SO₃ (12 e⁻), IF₇ (14 e⁻) ⇒ three ⇒ TRUE. PCl₃ has a normal octet.
3. Non-planar (c): NH₃ (pyramidal), PH₃ (pyramidal), PCl₃ (pyramidal), IF₇ (pentagonal bipyramidal), P₄ (tetrahedral) ⇒ five, not six. H₂S and SO₂ are bent but have only three atoms and are therefore planar; SO₃ and BF₃ are planar; H₂ is linear ⇒ statement FALSE ⇒ answer (c).
4. Both π types (d): SO₂ (1 pπ–pπ + 1 dπ–pπ) and SO₃ (1 pπ–pπ + 2 dπ–pπ) ⇒ two ⇒ TRUE.
 molecule  hybridised?   hypervalent?   planar?          both π types?
 NH3          sp3            no         NO (pyramidal)       no
 PH3          NO (Drago)     no         NO (pyramidal)       no
 H2S          NO (Drago)     no         yes (3 atoms)        no
 SO2          sp2            YES        yes (3 atoms)        YES
 SO3          sp2            YES        yes                  YES
 BF3          sp2            no         yes                  no
 PCl3         sp3            no         NO (pyramidal)       no
 IF7          sp3d3          YES        NO                   no
 P4           NO (Drago)     no         NO (tetrahedral)     no
 H2           no             no         yes                  no
 counts:       6              3          non-planar = 5       2
 (c) claims 6 non-planar ⇒ FALSE                        <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick When a counting question mixes "planar" with bent triatomics, remember that three points always define a plane — H₂O, SO₂, H₂S, O₃ and NO₂ are all planar despite being bent. The genuinely non-planar molecules need four or more atoms out of a common plane. Trap: candidates count SO₂ and H₂S as non-planar and get the "six" that option (c) is fishing for.
Answer: (c) — only five of the ten molecules are non-planar.
6(i)Assertion–Reason. Options: (a) A true, R false (b) A false, R true (c) both true, R correctly explains A (d) both true, R does not explain A. — Assertion: a multiple bond between two bonded atoms can have more than three bonds. Reason: a multiple bond between two bonded atoms cannot have more than two π-bonds.Quadruple bonds, δ bondingSigma and pi bonds in single, double and triple bonds🟡 SimilardBoth true, but R is not the correct explanation of A
Concept Between any two atoms there is exactly one σ bond (only one head-on direction is available) and at most two π bonds (only two p orbitals lie perpendicular to the internuclear axis). That caps ordinary bonding at a triple bond. But d-block atoms have four-lobed d orbitals which can overlap face-to-face to give a δ bond, adding a fourth component. Hence quadruple bonds are real — [Re₂Cl₈]²⁻ is the classic example, with σ + 2π + δ.
Key relation bond order 4 = 1σ + 2π + 1δ σ from dz², the two π from dxz and dyz, the δ from dx²−y² — the fourth bond is not a third π.
Solution
1. Assertion: quadruple (and even quintuple, in Cr₂Ar₂) bonds exist ⇒ more than three bonds between two atoms is possible ⇒ TRUE.
2. Reason: with the internuclear axis fixed, only two p orbitals remain perpendicular to it, so no more than two π bonds can form ⇒ TRUE.
3. Does the Reason explain the Assertion? No — it in fact states a limitation. The extra bond in a quadruple bond is a δ bond, a fact the Reason never mentions.If anything, a careless reader would use the Reason to argue against the Assertion; the true explanation is "d orbitals allow δ overlap".
4. Both true, reason not the explanation ⇒ (d).
 component   orbitals used        how many possible   example
 σ           dz² head-on                 1            Re—Re
 π           dxz, dyz sideways           2            Re—Re
 δ           dx²−y² face-to-face         1            Re—Re
 total                                   4            [Re2Cl8]²-  ⇒ quadruple
 A TRUE (4 bonds possible), R TRUE (max 2 π), R ≠ explanation  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick In Assertion–Reason items, when both statements are individually true but the Reason describes a restriction rather than a mechanism, the answer is almost always "(d) not the correct explanation". Here the missing link — the δ bond — is deliberately left out of the Reason.
Answer: (d) both true, but the reason is not the correct explanation.
6(ii)Assertion: in the SO₃ molecule the bond dissociation energies of all the S=O bonds are not equivalent. Reason: the SO₃ molecule has two types of pi bond, 2pπ–3pπ and 2pπ–3dπ.Resonance in SO₃Structure and bonding of SO₂ and SO₃ — pπ–pπ and pπ–dπ🟡 SimilarbAssertion false, Reason true
Concept Two ideas collide here and the resolution is delocalisation. It is perfectly true that, in a localised valence-bond picture, one of SO₃'s three π bonds uses the leftover sulfur 3p (a 2pπ–3pπ bond) while the other two use empty 3d orbitals (2pπ–3dπ). But the three oxygen atoms are related by the molecule's three-fold symmetry, so the π system is delocalised over all three S—O linkages. Experimentally all three bonds are identical: 143 pm each, bond order 1.33 in the σ + delocalised-π description. The Assertion is therefore false while the Reason is true.
Key relation SO₃ is sp², trigonal planar, D3h, ∠O–S–O = 120°, r(S—O) = 143 pm (all three equal) resonance among the equivalent canonical forms averages the localised single and double bonds into three identical hybrid bonds.
Solution
1. Draw SO₃: S is sp² with three σ bonds and no lone pair ⇒ trigonal planar.
2. Localised picture: one π uses the leftover 3p (2pπ–3pπ), two use 3d (2pπ–3dπ) ⇒ the Reason is TRUE.
3. But the three oxygens are symmetry-equivalent, so no experiment can distinguish them. X-ray and electron diffraction give three equal S—O distances of 143 pm and therefore three equal dissociation energies ⇒ the Assertion is FALSE.
4. A false assertion with a true reason ⇒ (b).The same logic makes all three N—O bonds of NO₃⁻ and all four S—O bonds of SO₄²⁻ identical even though the Lewis diagrams show a mixture.
 statement                                        truth
 A: S=O bond energies in SO3 are NOT equal        FALSE (all 143 pm, D3h)
 R: SO3 has 2pπ–3pπ and 2pπ–3dπ pi bonds          TRUE  (localised picture)
 ⇒ A false, R true                                        <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever the ligands round a central atom are symmetry-equivalent, all the bonds to them are equal — full stop. Use it on NO₃⁻, CO₃²⁻, SO₄²⁻, ClO₄⁻, PO₄³⁻ and SO₃. Bonds become unequal only when the ligands differ (H₂SO₄, HNO₃, HCOOH), which is exactly the contrast tested in PYQ items on this chapter.
Answer: (b) the assertion is false but the reason is true.
6(iii)Assertion: the atoms in a covalent molecule are said to share electrons, yet some covalent molecules are polar. Reason: in a polar covalent molecule the shared electrons spend more time on average near one of the atoms.Polar covalent bondsPolar covalent bonds — why sharing can still be unequal🟢 ExactcBoth true, and the Reason correctly explains the Assertion
Concept "Sharing" in a covalent bond does not mean "sharing equally". When the two bonded atoms have different electronegativities the shared pair is drawn towards the more electronegative atom, so the electron cloud spends more of its time there. That produces partial charges δ− and δ+, a permanent bond dipole, and hence a polar molecule — provided the individual bond dipoles do not cancel by symmetry. So the apparent paradox in the Assertion is resolved precisely by the mechanism stated in the Reason.
Key relation ΔEN = 0 ⇒ non-polar covalent (H₂, Cl₂) · 0 < ΔEN < 1.7 ⇒ polar covalent (HCl, H₂O) · ΔEN > 1.7 ⇒ largely ionic (NaCl); μ = q × d HCl: ΔEN = 0.9, μ = 1.03 D, giving about 17 % ionic character.
Solution
1. Assertion: HCl, H₂O and NH₃ are covalent yet have measurable dipole moments (1.03, 1.85 and 1.47 D). TRUE.
2. Reason: the more electronegative atom pulls the shared pair closer, so the time-averaged electron density is higher on it. This is the textbook definition of a polar covalent bond. TRUE.
3. Does the Reason explain the Assertion? Yes — unequal time-averaged residence of the shared pair is the origin of the partial charges and hence of the polarity.Symmetry can still cancel the bond dipoles (CCl₄, CO₂, BF₃), which is why the assertion says "some", not "all".
4. Both true with a correct explanation ⇒ (c).
 molecule   ΔEN     shared pair sits nearer   μ (D)   polar?
 H2         0.0     midway                    0       no
 HCl        0.9     Cl                        1.03    yes
