 H2O        1.4     O                         1.85    yes
 CCl4       0.5     each Cl, but symmetric    0       no (cancels)
 ⇒ A true, R true, R explains A                     <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Separate bond polarity from molecular polarity: every C—Cl bond in CCl₄ is polar, yet the molecule is not, because the four vectors cancel. Ask two questions in order — "are the bonds polar?" then "does the geometry cancel them?" That handles CO₂, BF₃, SF₆, XeF₄ and CCl₄ in one sweep.
Answer: (c) both true, and the reason correctly explains the assertion.
7Among B₂H₆, B₃N₃H₆, N₂O, N₂O₄, H₂S₂O₈, the total number of molecules containing a covalent bond between two atoms of the same kind is ______ [integer]Homonuclear bonds, peroxy linkageStructure of peroxydisulphuric acid H₂S₂O₈ — the O—O linkage🟡 Similar33
Concept The question asks for a direct bond between two identical atoms. Two traps are built in: B₂H₆ looks as if it should have a B—B bond but does not (the two borons are held together only through the two hydrogen bridges), and B₃N₃H₆ (borazine) alternates B and N round the ring so it has only B—N bonds. The three genuine cases each hide a homonuclear linkage inside a formula that does not advertise it.
Key relation N₂O = N≡N⁺—O⁻ (an N—N bond) · N₂O₄ = O₂N—NO₂ (an N—N bond, 175 pm and weak) · H₂S₂O₈ = HO₃S—O—O—SO₃H (a peroxy O—O bond) Marshall's acid is the one sulfur oxyacid with a genuine peroxide linkage — H₂S₂O₇ (oleum) has an S—O—S bridge instead and does not qualify.
Solution
1. B₂H₆: two BH₂ units joined by two 3-centre-2-electron B—H—B bridges. The B⋯B distance is long and there is no B—B bond; no H—H bond either ⇒ NOT counted.
2. B₃N₃H₆ (borazine, "inorganic benzene"): a six-membered ring of alternating B and N ⇒ every ring bond is B—N ⇒ NOT counted.
3. N₂O: linear N—N—O with a nitrogen–nitrogen linkage ⇒ counted.
4. N₂O₄: two NO₂ units joined by a long, weak N—N σ bond (which is why it dissociates so easily to 2NO₂) ⇒ counted.
5. H₂S₂O₈: peroxydisulphuric (Marshall's) acid contains a —O—O— peroxy bridge ⇒ counted.
6. Total = 3.
 molecule      skeleton                      same-kind bond?    count
 B2H6          H2B(μ-H)2BH2, no B—B          NO                  –
 B3N3H6        alternating B—N ring          NO                  –
 N2O           N≡N—O                         N—N  YES            1
 N2O4          O2N—NO2                       N—N  YES            2
 H2S2O8        HO3S—O—O—SO3H                 O—O  YES            3
 ⇒ total = 3                                             <-- answer 3
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the three sulfur oxyacids that look alike: H₂S₂O₇ (pyrosulphuric/oleum) has an S—O—S bridge and no peroxide; H₂S₂O₈ (Marshall's) has one O—O; H₂SO₅ (Caro's) also has one O—O. And never draw a B—B bond in diborane — the two borons are bridged, not bonded.
Answer: 3 (N₂O, N₂O₄ and H₂S₂O₈).
8Match the column. Column-I: (P) B₃N₃H₆ (Q) I₃⁻ (R) B₂Cl₄ (solid) (S) SiF₄. Column-II: (i) planar geometry (ii) non-planar geometry (iii) compound having a coordinate bond (iv) compound having a back bond (v) non-polar compound. — (a) P–iv,v; Q–iii; R–i,iv,v; S–i,v  (b) P–i,iv,v; Q–i,iii,v; R–i,iv,v; S–ii,iv,v  (c) P–v; Q–ii; R–iv; S–iii  (d) P–ii,iv,v; Q–iii,iv,v; R–iv,v; S–ii,iv,vBack bonding, borazine, I₃⁻Back bonding — concept, problems and tricks for JEE and NEET🟡 SimilarbP–(i),(iv),(v); Q–(i),(iii),(v); R–(i),(iv),(v); S–(ii),(iv),(v)
Concept Three ideas run through all four entries. Back bonding is the donation of a filled p (or lone pair) on the more electronegative ligand into an empty orbital on the electron-deficient central atom — it appears wherever a hypovalent B or an empty-d Si sits next to N, Cl or F. Planarity follows from the resulting sp²/aromatic-like character. And a molecule is non-polar whenever its bond dipoles cancel by symmetry, which is true of all four species here.
Key relation Back bond types: N(2p) → B(2p) in borazine · Cl(3p) → B(2p) in B₂Cl₄ · F(2p) → Si(3d) in SiF₄. Coordinate bond in I₂ + I⁻ → I₃⁻ the iodide lone pair is donated into the σ* of I₂ — a genuine dative interaction.
Solution
1. (P) B₃N₃H₆ — borazine. Alternating B and N in a six-membered ring, each atom sp². Each N donates its lone pair into the empty 2p of the adjacent B ⇒ back bond (iv). The delocalised π system makes it planar (i), and the D3h symmetry cancels all dipoles ⇒ non-polar (v). ⇒ i, iv, v.
2. (Q) I₃⁻. Made by I⁻ donating a lone pair to I₂ ⇒ coordinate bond (iii). Central I has 3 lone pairs + 2 bond pairs ⇒ SN 5 ⇒ sp³d, linear, and any linear species is trivially planar (i); the two equal, opposite I—I moments cancel ⇒ non-polar (v). ⇒ i, iii, v.
3. (R) B₂Cl₄ (solid). In the solid it is planar (the two BCl₂ units coplanar, D2h) ⇒ (i). Each Cl back-donates 3p density into the empty 2p of its boron ⇒ (iv). Centrosymmetric ⇒ non-polar (v). ⇒ i, iv, v.
4. (S) SiF₄. Tetrahedral, so non-planar (ii). Fluorine lone pairs donate into the empty Si 3d orbitals — the classic pπ–dπ back bond (iv), which is why the Si—F bond is unusually short and strong. Tetrahedral symmetry cancels the four bond moments ⇒ non-polar (v). ⇒ ii, iv, v.
5. Reading off: P–i,iv,v; Q–i,iii,v; R–i,iv,v; S–ii,iv,v ⇒ option (b).
 entry            geometry           back bond       coordinate    polar?
 (P) B3N3H6       planar    (i)      N→B     (iv)        no         no (v)
 (Q) I3-          linear/planar (i)  no                 YES (iii)   no (v)
 (R) B2Cl4 (s)    planar    (i)      Cl→B    (iv)        no         no (v)
 (S) SiF4         tetrahedral (ii)   F→Si(3d)(iv)        no         no (v)
 ⇒ P–i,iv,v ; Q–i,iii,v ; R–i,iv,v ; S–ii,iv,v          <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Spot back bonding by the pattern "electron-deficient centre + ligand with a lone pair": BF₃, B₂Cl₄, borazine, SiF₄, N(SiH₃)₃, H₃Si—O—SiH₃, PF₃. Its two signatures are a shortened bond and a flattened geometry. In matching questions, first mark every entry "non-polar/polar" — that column alone usually eliminates two options.
Answer: (b) P–(i),(iv),(v); Q–(i),(iii),(v); R–(i),(iv),(v); S–(ii),(iv),(v).
9Which of the following combinations lead to the formation of gerade molecular orbital(s)? (a) pz(+ up) added to pz(− up), side by side (b) pz added to a four-lobed dxz (c) two identical dxy-type orbitals approaching along the x-axis (d) two identical four-lobed d orbitals in the xy plane approaching along the z-axis  [one or more correct]Gerade / ungerade symmetrySelect which of the given molecular orbitals are gerade and which are ungerade🟢 Exacta, b, c, dAll four combinations
Concept Gerade (g) means the molecular orbital is unchanged when every point is reflected through the centre of the molecule: ψ(−r) = +ψ(r). Ungerade (u) means it changes sign. Applying the inversion test to standard combinations gives a table you can memorise: σ from s+s is g and σ* is u; π from p+p is u and π* is g; δ from d+d is g and δ* is u. Note that the σ/π/δ label and the g/u label are independent — a bonding orbital is not automatically gerade.
Key relation i·ψ(r) = ψ(−r) ; = +ψ ⇒ gerade, = −ψ ⇒ ungerade. Parity of the building blocks: s even (g), p odd (u), d even (g) two odd functions combined out of phase across the centre give an even MO — that is exactly why π* is gerade.
Solution
1. (a) Lateral p + p with opposite phases (+ up on the left, − up on the right) is the out-of-phase combination ⇒ π* orbital ⇒ π* is gerade. Inversion swaps the two centres and flips the sign of each p orbital — the two sign changes cancel and ψ is reproduced. GERADE ✓
2. (b) p + d overlapping laterally with the p's lobes facing the d's out-of-phase lobes ⇒ again the antibonding π combination ⇒ gerade by the same argument. ✓
3. (c) Two identical dxy-type orbitals, in phase, meeting along x ⇒ a π-type MO built from two even (d) functions ⇒ inversion reproduces ψ exactly ⇒ gerade. ✓
4. (d) Two identical four-lobed d orbitals lying in the xy plane and approaching along z ⇒ face-to-face δ overlap, in phase ⇒ δ bonding MO ⇒ gerade. ✓
5. All four are gerade ⇒ answer (a), (b), (c), (d).
 combination                       type of MO      inversion test    label
 (a) pz + pz, out of phase          π*             ψ(−r) = +ψ(r)      g   ✓
 (b) pz + dxz, out of phase         π*             ψ(−r) = +ψ(r)      g   ✓
 (c) dxy + dxy in phase, axis x     π (from d)     d is even          g   ✓
 (d) d + d in phase, axis z         δ bonding      d is even          g   ✓
 memorise: σ(g) σ*(u) | π(u) π*(g) | δ(g) δ*(u)          <-- answer a,b,c,d
⚡ JEE Trick Do not memorise pictures — memorise the six-entry table σg, σ*u, πu, π*g, δg, δ*u, and remember that MOs made from d orbitals keep the parity of d (even). The counter-intuitive entry — bonding π is u while antibonding π* is g — is the one the examiner is testing.
Answer: (a), (b), (c) and (d) — all four give gerade molecular orbitals.
10Which of the following overlaps results in an ungerade bonding molecular orbital? (a) two p orbitals subtracted, drawn with opposite shading (net in-phase lateral overlap) (b) a p orbital added in phase to a four-lobed d orbital (c) an s orbital added to a p orbital whose facing lobe is out of phase (d) two s orbitals subtracted  [one or more correct]Gerade / ungerade, bonding MOsGerade and ungerade molecular orbitals explained🟡 Similara, b(a) and (b)
Concept Two independent tests must both be passed. Bonding requires that the lobes actually facing each other across the internuclear region have the same sign, so electron density is built up between the nuclei. Ungerade requires that inversion through the centre flips the sign of the whole MO. Reading the shading in the figures tells you the phases; the standard parity table then tells you g or u. Since π bonding is u and σ bonding is g, only laterally overlapping (π-type) combinations can be both bonding and ungerade.
Key relation bonding ⇔ facing lobes in phase; parity table σg, σ*u, πu, π*g so "ungerade AND bonding" ⇒ must be a π bonding orbital.
Solution
1. (a) The two p orbitals are drawn with reversed shading and combined by subtraction, so the net effect is the in-phase lateral combination ⇒ π bonding ⇒ π is ungerade. Both tests passed ✓
2. (b) The p lobe and the facing d lobe carry the same shading and are added ⇒ constructive lateral overlap ⇒ π bonding, again ungerade. Both tests passed ✓
3. (c) The shaded s orbital faces the unshaded lobe of the p orbital ⇒ destructive head-on overlap ⇒ this is σ* antibonding. It fails the "bonding" test ✗
4. (d) s − s is the out-of-phase combination ⇒ σ* antibonding. It is indeed ungerade, but it is not bonding ✗
5. Only (a) and (b) satisfy both conditions ⇒ answer (a), (b).
 option   overlap             facing lobes   bonding?   parity   both?
 (a)      p + p lateral        in phase       YES        u  (π)   YES  <--
 (b)      p + d lateral        in phase       YES        u  (π)   YES  <--
 (c)      s + p head-on        OUT of phase   NO         u  (σ*)  no
 (d)      s − s head-on        OUT of phase   NO         u  (σ*)  no
 ⇒ answer (a) and (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Read the picture in two passes: first ask "do the facing lobes match in shading?" (bonding or not), then apply the parity table (g or u). The pair that is both bonding and ungerade is always π; the pair that is both antibonding and gerade is always π*. Trap: options (c) and (d) are ungerade, so students who check only the parity pick them.
Answer: (a) and (b).

Level 9

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1Among the following molecules the N—Si bond length is shortest in: (a) N(SiH₃)₃ (b) NH(SiH₃)₂ (c) NH₂(SiH₃) (d) all have equal N—Si bond lengthBack bonding, N→Si pπ–dπWhy N(SiH₃)₃ is planar but N(CH₃)₃ is pyramidal — nitrogen→silicon back bonding🟡 SimilarcNH₂(SiH₃)
Concept Nitrogen carries exactly one lone pair. Silicon has empty 3d (and low-lying σ* Si—H) orbitals, so the N lone pair is delocalised backwards into silicon — a pπ–dπ back bond. Back bonding adds partial double-bond character to the N—Si linkage, which shortens and strengthens it. The crucial point is that the same single lone pair must be shared among all the silyl groups attached to that nitrogen, so the back-bond character per N—Si bond is diluted as the number of SiH₃ groups increases.
Key relation back-bond character per bond ∝ 1 / (number of acceptor Si atoms) One lone pair spread over n silicons gives each bond only 1/n of the π character; more π character ⇒ higher bond order ⇒ shorter bond.
Solution
1. NH₂(SiH₃): one Si acceptor. The whole N lone pair goes into that single Si ⇒ maximum back-bond character ⇒ highest N—Si bond order ⇒ shortest bond.
2. NH(SiH₃)₂: the same lone pair is now split between two Si atoms ⇒ each bond gets about half the π character ⇒ longer than in (c).
3. N(SiH₃)₃: the lone pair is split three ways ⇒ least π character per bond ⇒ longest N—Si bond. (This molecule is still planar, because even one-third of a lone pair delocalised is enough to force N into sp².)
4. Option (d) is wrong: the three molecules have different numbers of acceptors, so the bond lengths cannot be equal. The N—H bonds present in (b) and (c) do not compete for the lone pair at all — hydrogen has no vacant orbital of suitable energy.
 species        Si acceptors   lone-pair share per N—Si   N—Si bond order   length
 NH2(SiH3)          1               1  (full)                 highest       shortest <-- answer (c)
 NH(SiH3)2          2               1/2                       medium        medium
 N(SiH3)3           3               1/3                       lowest        longest
⚡ JEE Trick For back bonding always count donor lone pairs ÷ acceptor atoms. One donor, fewer acceptors ⇒ shorter bond. The same rule instantly gives B—F bond order BF₃ < BF₂H < BFH₂ (fewer fluorines sharing the boron's empty p ⇒ shorter B—F). Trap: students assume "more silyl groups ⇒ more back bonding ⇒ shorter", forgetting that the donor lone pair is a fixed, finite resource.
Answer: (c) NH₂(SiH₃).
2Consider the reaction MX₄ + X′₂ ⟶ MX₄X′₂. If the atomic number of M is 52 and X, X′ are halogens with X′ more electronegative than X, choose the correct statement: (a) both X′ atoms occupy axial positions formed by overlapping of p and d orbitals only (b) all M—X bond lengths are identical in both MX₄ and MX₄X′₂ (c) central atom M does not use any valence non-axial set of d-orbital in the hybridisation of the final product (d) hybridisation of M remains the same in both reactant and final productsp³d vs sp³d², which d-orbitalssp³d and sp³d² hybridisation — which d orbitals actually take part🟡 SimilarcCentral atom M does not use any non-axial d-orbital in the product's hybridisation
Concept Z = 52 is tellurium (group 16), so MX₄ = TeX₄ and MX₄X′₂ = TeX₄X′₂. TeX₄ has 4 bond pairs + 1 lone pair ⇒ steric number 5 ⇒ sp³d, trigonal-bipyramidal electron geometry, see-saw shape. Adding X′₂ oxidatively uses up the lone pair, giving 6 bond pairs + 0 lone pairs ⇒ steric number 6 ⇒ sp³d², regular octahedron. The whole question then turns on which d orbitals sp³d² uses.
Key relation sp³d² uses dz² and dx²−y² These are the two "axial" d orbitals — their lobes point along the x, y, z axes, exactly where the six octahedral ligands sit. The non-axial set (dxy, dyz, dzx) has lobes between the axes and is not used.
Solution
1. Reactant TeX₄ — sp³d, see-saw. Product TeX₄X′₂ — sp³d², octahedral. Hybridisation changes ⇒ (d) is false.
2. In a regular octahedron all six positions are equivalent, so the phrase "axial vs equatorial" no longer applies to the product; also the six hybrids are sp³d², not "p and d only" ⇒ (a) is false.
3. In the see-saw TeX₄ the two axial Te—X bonds are longer than the two equatorial ones (axial bonds suffer three 90° lone-pair repulsions). In octahedral TeX₄X′₂ all Te—X are equal. So the bond lengths are not identical in both compounds ⇒ (b) is false.
4. The product is sp³d², which draws on dz² and dx²−y² only. Neither dxy, dyz nor dzx (the non-axial set) is involved ⇒ (c) is true.
 stage        species        SN   lone pairs   hybrid    d-orbitals used        shape
 reactant     TeX4           5        1        sp3d      d(z2)                  see-saw
 product      TeX4X'2        6        0        sp3d2     d(z2), d(x2-y2)        octahedral   <-- answer (c)
 non-axial d set  = dxy, dyz, dzx  -> NEVER used in sp3d / sp3d2 / sp3d3
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the d-orbital roll-call once: sp³d → dz²; sp³d² → dz² + dx²−y²; sp³d³ → dz² + dx²−y² + dxy. Every "which d orbital is used" question collapses to this line. Trap: option (d) is designed for people who see two extra atoms added and assume the hybridisation is unchanged because "it is still a halide".
Answer: (c).
3Which of the following have planar structure? (a) I₃⁻ (b) ICl₃ (c) Cl₂O₆ (d) Be₂Cl₄ [more than one correct]Planarity, interhalogen dimersStructures of interhalogen compounds — ClF₃, BrF₃, IF₅, IF₇ and the ICl₃ dimer🟡 SimilarabdI₃⁻, ICl₃ and Be₂Cl₄
Concept "Planar" means every atom of the species can be placed in one plane. A linear species is trivially planar. For dimers you must draw the actual bridged structure, not the monomer. And a formula that looks molecular may in fact be an ion pair in the solid, in which case the two ions have their own geometries and the assembly is not planar.
Key relation 2 atoms = always linear · 3 atoms = always planar · 4+ atoms ⇒ check hybridisation and lone pairs Planarity is decided by the electron geometry at every heavy atom simultaneously; one sp³ centre with two out-of-plane substituents destroys it.
Solution
1. I₃⁻: central I has 3 lone pairs + 2 bond pairs ⇒ sp³d, lone pairs equatorial, bonds axial ⇒ linear ⇒ planar ⇒ counted.
2. ICl₃: the monomer is sp³d with 2 lone pairs ⇒ T-shaped, which is planar; in the solid it exists as the dimer I₂Cl₆ with two chlorine bridges, and that dimer is also completely planar ⇒ counted either way.
3. Cl₂O₆: this is not O₃Cl—O—ClO₃; it is the ion pair [ClO₂]⁺[ClO₄]⁻. The cation ClO₂⁺ is linear but the anion ClO₄⁻ is tetrahedral ⇒ the species as a whole is non-planar ⇒ not counted.
4. Be₂Cl₄: the dimer of BeCl₂ has two chlorine bridges; each Be becomes sp² (three σ bonds, trigonal planar) and the whole Be₂Cl₄ unit lies in one plane ⇒ counted. Contrast Al₂Cl₆, where each Al is sp³ and the bridging chlorines sit above and below the Al—Al plane ⇒ non-planar.
 species     real structure                  central-atom state    planar?
 I3-         linear (sp3d, 3 lp)             sp3d                  YES  <--
 ICl3        T-shaped monomer / I2Cl6 dimer  sp3d                  YES  <--
 Cl2O6       [ClO2]+ [ClO4]-  (ion pair)     sp2 + sp3             NO
 Be2Cl4      Cl2Be(mu-Cl)2BeCl2 bridged      sp2 at each Be        YES  <--
 ⇒ answer (a), (b) and (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the three "planar dimer / non-planar dimer" pairs: Be₂Cl₄ (sp², planar), I₂Cl₆ (sp³d, planar), Al₂Cl₆ (sp³, non-planar). And remember the disguised ion pairs — Cl₂O₆ = [ClO₂]⁺[ClO₄]⁻, PCl₅(s) = [PCl₄]⁺[PCl₆]⁻, N₂O₅(s) = [NO₂]⁺[NO₃]⁻ — every one of them is a favourite trap.
Answer: (a), (b) and (d).
4In the structure of H₂C=SF₄, which of the following is/are correct? (a) two C—H bonds are in the same plane as the axial S—F bonds (b) two C—H bonds are in the same plane as the equatorial S—F bonds (c) total six atoms are in the same plane (d) the equatorial S—F plane is perpendicular to the nodal plane of the π-bond [more than one correct]sp³d geometry + π nodal planesp³d, sp³d² and sp³d³ — trigonal bipyramidal geometry of PCl₅, SF₆ and IF₇🟡 Similaracd(a), (c) and (d)
Concept In H₂C=SF₄ sulfur carries four σ bonds to F plus one σ bond to C — steric number 5, no lone pair ⇒ sp³d, trigonal bipyramidal. The bulky =CH₂ group, like a lone pair or a multiple bond, prefers an equatorial site (only two 90° repulsions instead of three). The remaining question is the rotational orientation of the CH₂ plane, and that is fixed by the π bond: the C and S p-type orbitals must be parallel for maximum sideways overlap.
Key relation π overlap is maximum when the two π lobes are parallel ⇒ the CH₂ plane locks perpendicular to the π lobes The nodal plane of a π bond is the plane that contains both nuclei and the σ framework — here it is the CH₂ plane itself.
Solution
1. Put S at the origin, C along +x (an equatorial site). Then the equatorial plane is xy (it contains C and the two equatorial F) and the two axial F lie along ±z.
2. The π bond uses the p orbital on C and the matching orbital on S. Maximum overlap and minimum repulsion with the equatorial S—F bonds is obtained when those lobes point along ±y, i.e. the CH₂ group lies in the xz plane — the plane that contains the axial S—F bonds ⇒ (a) true, (b) false.
3. Atoms in that xz plane: S, C, the 2 H, and the 2 axial F ⇒ six atoms coplanar ⇒ (c) true.
4. The π lobes lie along ±y, so the nodal plane of the π bond is the xz plane. The equatorial S—F plane is xy. xz ⟂ xy ⇒ (d) true. Two planes sharing the x axis and containing the mutually perpendicular directions y and z are necessarily perpendicular.
 axis set:  S at origin, C along +x (equatorial), axial F along ±z, equatorial F in xy plane

 feature                         plane        contains
 CH2 group / axial F—S—F         xz           S, C, H, H, F(ax), F(ax)   -> 6 atoms  <-- (c)
 equatorial S—F bonds + C        xy           S, C, F(eq), F(eq)
 nodal plane of C=S  pi bond     xz           same as the CH2 plane      <-- (a)
 xz ⟂ xy                          -->  equatorial plane ⟂ nodal plane    <-- (d)
 ⇒ answer (a), (c) and (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Two rules settle every "orientation" question in a trigonal bipyramid: (i) lone pairs and multiple bonds go equatorial; (ii) the plane of an sp²-like substituent (=CH₂, —NH₂, —C₆H₅) rotates so that its π lobes avoid the crowded equatorial belt, which puts the substituent's own plane in line with the axial bonds. Trap: option (b) is what you get if you carelessly assume "equatorial group ⇒ equatorial plane".
Answer: (a), (c) and (d).
5Which of the following statements are not correct? (a) all C—O bonds in CO₃²⁻ are equal but not in H₂CO₃ (b) C—O bond lengths in HCO₂⁻ are equal but not in HCO₂H (c) the C—O bond length in HCO₂⁻ is longer than the C—O bond length in CO₃²⁻ (d) the C—O bond length in HCO₂⁻ and the C—O bond length in CO₃²⁻ are equal [more than one correct]Resonance, fractional bond orderBond order and resonance structures of the carbonate ion CO₃²⁻🟡 Similarcd(c) and (d)
Concept Equivalent resonance structures average the π electrons over the equivalent oxygens, giving every such C—O bond the same fractional bond order. Higher bond order always means shorter bond. In the neutral acids H₂CO₃ and HCO₂H one oxygen carries an H, so that C—O(H) bond is a pure single bond and cannot resonate with the C=O — the bonds become unequal.
Key relation bond order = (total σ + π bonds over the equivalent set) / (number of equivalent bonds) · CO₃²⁻ : 4/3 ≈ 1.33 · HCO₂⁻ : 3/2 = 1.50 Carbonate spreads one π bond over three C—O links; formate spreads one π bond over only two.
Solution
1. (a) CO₃²⁻ has three equivalent resonance forms ⇒ all three C—O equal (1.29 Å). H₂CO₃ has two C—O(H) single bonds and one C=O ⇒ unequal. Statement (a) is correct, so it is not an answer.
2. (b) HCO₂⁻ (formate) has two equivalent resonance forms ⇒ both C—O equal (1.27 Å). HCO₂H has one C=O (1.21 Å) and one C—OH (1.34 Å) ⇒ unequal. Statement (b) is correct, so it is not an answer.
3. (c) Formate bond order 1.50 > carbonate bond order 1.33 ⇒ the formate C—O is shorter, not longer. Statement (c) is incorrect ⇒ an answer.
4. (d) Different bond orders cannot give equal bond lengths. Statement (d) is incorrect ⇒ an answer. The question asks which statements are NOT correct, so the marked options are the two false ones.
 species    equivalent C—O   pi bonds shared   bond order   length (Å)
 HCO2-             2               1             1.50         1.27   (shorter)
 CO3^2-            3               1             1.33         1.29   (longer)
 ⇒ (c) "HCO2- longer than CO3^2-"  FALSE   <-- answer (c)
 ⇒ (d) "the two are equal"          FALSE   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Write the fractional bond order as a fraction, never as a decimal you have to recall: NO₃⁻ = 4/3, CO₃²⁻ = 4/3, NO₂⁻ = 3/2, HCO₂⁻ = 3/2, SO₄²⁻ = 6/4. Then "bigger fraction ⇒ shorter bond" answers every comparison instantly. Trap: the question says not correct — half the marks lost on this item come from ticking (a) and (b).
Answer: (c) and (d).
6Match the column. Column-I: (A) I₂Cl₆ (B) SiC (C) Al₂(CH₃)₆ (D) S₃O₉. Column-II: (P) all atoms are sp³ hybridised (Q) have non-planar structure (R) have 3c–4e bonds (S) have 3c–2e bonds. — (a) A–R, B–P, C–S, D–Q  (b) A–P, B–R, C–S, D–Q  (c) A–Q, B–R, C–S, D–P  (d) A–Q, B–S, C–R, D–P3c–2e vs 3c–4e bridgesWhere 3-centre-2-electron bonds really occur — B₂H₆, Al₂(CH₃)₆, BeH₂(s), BeCl₂🟡 SimilaraA–R, B–P, C–S, D–Q
Concept A bridge is 3c–2e (electron-deficient, "banana") when the bridging atom brings only one electron pair for three centres — typical of B and Al alkyl/hydride bridges. It is 3c–4e (hypervalent, "electron-rich") when the bridging arrangement is a linear three-centre system holding four electrons — typical of the halogen bridges in interhalogen dimers and of the axial bonds in trigonal-bipyramidal halides. Recognising which kind of bridge a dimer uses is the whole game here.
Key relation 3c–2e ⇒ electron deficient centre (B, Al, Be) · 3c–4e ⇒ electron rich, hypervalent centre (I, Cl, Xe, P) Count valence electrons at the bridged atom: fewer than an octet ⇒ 3c–2e; more than an octet ⇒ 3c–4e.
Solution
1. (A) I₂Cl₆: the planar dimer of ICl₃; each iodine is hypervalent and the two Cl bridges are the classic 3c–4e linear bonds ⇒ A–R.
2. (B) SiC (carborundum): a giant covalent solid with the diamond lattice — every Si and every C is tetrahedrally bonded ⇒ all atoms sp³ ⇒ B–P.
3. (C) Al₂(CH₃)₆: trimethylaluminium dimer, bridged by two methyl groups. Carbon has no lone pair to donate, so the bridge is electron deficient — a 3c–2e banana bond exactly like B₂H₆ ⇒ C–S.
4. (D) S₃O₉: the cyclic trimer of SO₃ — a six-membered (—S—O—)₃ ring in which every S is sp³ and the ring puckers ⇒ non-planar ⇒ D–Q. Do not confuse S₃O₉ with the planar monomer SO₃, where sulfur is sp².
5. Only option (a) carries A–R, B–P, C–S, D–Q.
 entry            structure                              distinguishing feature      match
 I2Cl6            planar Cl-bridged ICl3 dimer           3c-4e halogen bridges       R   <--
 SiC              diamond-type giant covalent lattice    every atom tetrahedral      P   <--
 Al2(CH3)6        CH3-bridged dimer, banana bonds        3c-2e electron deficient    S   <--
 S3O9             cyclic (SO3)3 six-membered ring        puckered, non-planar        Q   <--
 ⇒ answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Sort bridges by the bridging atom: H or CH₃ bridge ⇒ 3c–2e (B₂H₆, Al₂(CH₃)₆, BeH₂ chain); halogen bridge on a hypervalent centre ⇒ 3c–4e (I₂Cl₆); halogen bridge on an electron-deficient centre ⇒ ordinary dative bond (Al₂Cl₆). Trap: Al₂Cl₆ and Al₂(CH₃)₆ look like twins but the chloride dimer uses lone-pair donation while the methyl dimer must use a banana bond.
Answer: (a) A–R, B–P, C–S, D–Q.
7Match the column. Column-I: (A) ICl₃ (B) AlCl₃ (C) AlF₃ (D) NO₂. Column-II: (P) hybridisation of the central atom is similar in both dimer and monomer form (Q) both monomer and dimer forms are planar (R) in the dimer form all atoms are sp³ hybridised (S) does not exist in dimer form. — (a) A–Q, B–R, C–S, D–P  (b) A–R, B–P, C–S, D–Q  (c) A–Q, B–S, C–R, D–P  (d) A–P, B–S, C–R, D–QDimerisation and hybridisation changeBanana bonds and the dimeric structure of AlCl₃ (Al₂Cl₆)🟡 SimilaraA–Q, B–R, C–S, D–P
Concept When a monomer dimerises, ask two things: (i) does the central atom gain σ bonds — if yes, the steric number and hence the hybridisation change; (ii) does the new geometry force atoms out of the original plane. A species dimerises at all only if it is electron deficient (AlCl₃), hypervalent with usable lone pairs (ICl₃) or an odd-electron radical (NO₂). A strongly ionic lattice such as AlF₃ has no discrete molecule to dimerise.
Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs · sp² (SN 3) → sp³ (SN 4) on adding one dative bond Al in AlCl₃ has SN 3; accepting one Cl lone pair in the dimer raises it to SN 4 ⇒ tetrahedral, and the bridging chlorines then sit above and below the Al—Al line.
Solution
1. (A) ICl₃ — monomer sp³d, T-shaped and planar; dimer I₂Cl₆ is also completely planar ⇒ A–Q.
2. (B) AlCl₃ — monomer sp² trigonal planar; in Al₂Cl₆ each Al becomes sp³ and every Cl is also effectively sp³ ⇒ "in the dimer all atoms sp³" ⇒ B–R.
3. (C) AlF₃ — F is small and highly electronegative, so by Fajans' rules AlF₃ is essentially ionic: a high-melting giant lattice with 6-coordinate Al. There is no molecular dimer ⇒ C–S.
4. (D) NO₂ — a bent odd-electron radical with sp² nitrogen; on dimerising to O₂N—NO₂ (N₂O₄) the unpaired electron simply forms the N—N σ bond and nitrogen stays sp² ⇒ hybridisation unchanged ⇒ D–P.
5. Only option (a) matches A–Q, B–R, C–S, D–P.
 species   monomer state        dimer                     hybridisation change   match
 ICl3      sp3d, T-shaped       I2Cl6, planar             sp3d -> sp3d           Q  <--
 AlCl3     sp2, trigonal planar Al2Cl6, all atoms sp3      sp2  -> sp3            R  <--
 AlF3      ionic giant lattice  none                      —                      S  <--
 NO2       sp2 bent radical     N2O4, O2N—NO2             sp2  -> sp2            P  <--
 ⇒ answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Down group 13 the fluoride is always the odd one out: AlF₃ ionic and non-volatile, AlCl₃/AlBr₃/AlI₃ covalent dimers. The same Fajans logic explains BeF₂ (glassy polymer) vs BeCl₂ (chain), and why AlCl₃ sublimes at 180 °C while AlF₃ melts above 1290 °C. Trap: option (b) swaps A and B and catches anyone who remembers "Al₂Cl₆ is planar" — it is not; I₂Cl₆ is.
Answer: (a) A–Q, B–R, C–S, D–P.
8(i)Passage: Phosphorus forms different halide compounds with halogens; in the crystalline state some of them exist as ion pairs. — What is the hybridisation of the central atom of the anionic part of PBr₅ in the crystalline state? (a) sp² (b) sp³ (c) sp (d) not applicablePBr₅(s) = [PBr₄]⁺Br⁻Structure of solid PBr₅ — why the anion is a bare bromide🟡 Similardnot applicable
Concept Solid phosphorus pentahalides ionise, but not in the same way. PCl₅(s) becomes [PCl₄]⁺[PCl₆]⁻ because chlorine is small enough for six of them to fit round one phosphorus. Bromine is much bulkier, so [PBr₆]⁻ cannot form; instead PBr₅(s) is [PBr₄]⁺ Br⁻. The anion is a bare halide ion — a single atom with no bonds at all.
Key relation PCl₅(s) → [PCl₄]⁺ (sp³, tetrahedral) + [PCl₆]⁻ (sp³d², octahedral) · PBr₅(s) → [PBr₄]⁺ (sp³, tetrahedral) + Br⁻ (monatomic) Hybridisation is a property of a central atom surrounded by other atoms; a lone monatomic ion has no directional bonds to hybridise for.
Solution
1. Write the crystal structure: PBr₅(s) = [PBr₄]⁺ Br⁻.
2. Identify the anionic part: it is the free bromide ion Br⁻.
3. Br⁻ is a single atom with 8 valence electrons and no bond pairs. There is no central atom, no steric number, no directional bonding ⇒ hybridisation is not applicable ⇒ option (d).
4. Options (a), (b), (c) all presuppose a polyatomic anion such as [PBr₆]⁻ — which the size of bromine forbids. Steric crowding: six Br atoms (radius 114 pm) cannot be accommodated octahedrally round P (radius 100 pm), whereas six Cl (99 pm) can.
 solid halide     cation                anion                 anion hybridisation
 PCl5(s)          [PCl4]+  sp3          [PCl6]-  sp3d2        sp3d2
 PBr5(s)          [PBr4]+  sp3          Br-  (bare ion)       NOT APPLICABLE   <-- answer (d)
 PI5              does not exist even as [PI4]+ I- at RT
⚡ JEE Trick Store the solid-state ion pairs as a single line: PCl₅ → [PCl₄]⁺[PCl₆]⁻ · PBr₅ → [PBr₄]⁺Br⁻ · N₂O₅ → [NO₂]⁺[NO₃]⁻ · Cl₂O₆ → [ClO₂]⁺[ClO₄]⁻ · I₂Cl₆ stays covalent. Trap: candidates answer sp³d² out of habit, forgetting that the bromide is monatomic.
Answer: (d) not applicable.
8(ii)What is the difference between the bond angles in the cationic species of PCl₅ and PBr₅ in the solid state? (a) 60° (b) 109°28′ (c) 0° (d) 90°[PCl₄]⁺ vs [PBr₄]⁺Hybridisation in the solid state — PCl₅, PBr₅, N₂O₅, XeF₆, IF₅🟡 Similarc0°
Concept The two solids ionise differently in the anion but identically in the cation: both give a PX₄⁺ ion. PX₄⁺ has four bond pairs and zero lone pairs ⇒ steric number 4 ⇒ sp³ ⇒ a perfectly regular tetrahedron. Because there is no lone pair and all four substituents are identical, there is nothing to distort the ideal angle in either ion.
Key relation SN 4, 0 lone pairs ⇒ regular tetrahedron ⇒ ∠X—P—X = 109°28′ (109.5°) Bond-angle distortion needs either a lone pair or two different substituents; PCl₄⁺ and PBr₄⁺ have neither.
Solution
1. PCl₅(s) = [PCl₄]⁺[PCl₆]⁻ ⇒ cation is [PCl₄]⁺, sp³, tetrahedral, ∠Cl—P—Cl = 109°28′.
2. PBr₅(s) = [PBr₄]⁺Br⁻ ⇒ cation is [PBr₄]⁺, sp³, tetrahedral, ∠Br—P—Br = 109°28′.
3. Difference = 109°28′ − 109°28′ = 0° ⇒ option (c).
4. Option (b) is the trap for reading "bond angle" instead of "difference of bond angles"; (d) 90° belongs to the octahedral [PCl₆]⁻ anion, not to the cation. The size difference between Cl and Br changes bond lengths, never the ideal tetrahedral angle.
 solid        cation      lone pairs   hybrid   shape          bond angle
 PCl5(s)      [PCl4]+         0         sp3     tetrahedral    109°28'
 PBr5(s)      [PBr4]+         0         sp3     tetrahedral    109°28'
 difference                                                    0°       <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick An ion with SN 4 and no lone pair is locked at 109.5° whatever the halogen — NH₄⁺, PCl₄⁺, PBr₄⁺, BF₄⁻, SO₄²⁻ all share the same angle. Only lone pairs or unequal substituents move a bond angle. Trap: (b) is placed there precisely because the candidate has just computed 109°28′ and stops reading.
Answer: (c) 0°.
8(iii)All possible bond angles in the anionic part of PCl₅ (solid state) are: (a) 109°28′ (b) 90°, 180° (c) 90°, 120°, 180° (d) 72°, 90°, 180°[PCl₆]⁻ octahedral anglesSolid PCl₅ exists as an ionic compound — the [PCl₄]⁺[PCl₆]⁻ pair🟡 Similarb90°, 180°
Concept The anion of solid PCl₅ is [PCl₆]⁻: six bond pairs, no lone pair ⇒ steric number 6 ⇒ sp³d², a regular octahedron. In a regular octahedron there are only two distinct X—P—X relationships: cis (adjacent, 90°) and trans (opposite, 180°). No third angle exists, and there is no lone pair to bend anything.
Key relation regular octahedron ⇒ angles 90° (12 cis pairs) and 180° (3 trans pairs) only 120° belongs to the equatorial belt of a trigonal bipyramid and 72° to a pentagonal bipyramid — neither shape occurs here.
Solution
1. PCl₅(s) = [PCl₄]⁺[PCl₆]⁻ ⇒ the anionic part is [PCl₆]⁻.
2. Count: P contributes 5 valence e⁻, six Cl contribute 6, the negative charge adds 1 ⇒ 12 e⁻ = 6 bond pairs, 0 lone pairs ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ sp³d², octahedral.
3. Enumerate the angles: adjacent Cl atoms subtend 90°, opposite Cl atoms subtend 180° ⇒ option (b).
4. Eliminate: (a) 109°28′ is the tetrahedral [PCl₄]⁺ cation; (c) adds 120°, which needs a trigonal-bipyramidal or trigonal-planar centre (that is gaseous PCl₅, not the anion); (d) adds 72°, which needs sp³d³ / pentagonal bipyramidal (IF₇).
 ion            SN   lone pairs   hybrid    shape                 angles present
 [PCl4]+         4        0        sp3      tetrahedral           109°28'
 [PCl6]-         6        0        sp3d2    octahedral            90°, 180°     <-- answer (b)
 PCl5 (gas)      5        0        sp3d     trigonal bipyramidal  90°, 120°, 180°
 IF7             7        0        sp3d3    pentagonal bipyramid  72°, 90°, 180°
⚡ JEE Trick Read the angle list backwards to identify the shape: "90, 180" ⇒ octahedral; "90, 120, 180" ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal; "72, 90, 180" ⇒ pentagonal bipyramidal; "109.5" ⇒ tetrahedral. Option (c) here is the gas-phase PCl₅ answer, planted for anyone who forgets the question says solid state.
Answer: (b) 90°, 180°.
9Total number of molecules in which each covalent bond is comprised of a back bond: BF₃, N(SiH₃)₃, PF₃, POF₃, B(OH)₃, O(SiH₃)₂, NH(SiH₃)₂, BFH₂, BF₂(NH₂) ______ [integer]Back bonding — every bondBack bonding in disilyl ether (H₃Si—O—SiH₃) — which bonds actually back-bond🟡 Similar33
Concept The word each is doing all the work. A molecule qualifies only if every single covalent bond in it carries back-bonding character. Back bonding needs a filled orbital (lone pair) on one atom and a vacant orbital of comparable energy on its partner. Hydrogen has no lone pair and no accessible vacant orbital, so any X—H bond immediately disqualifies the whole molecule — and that single observation eliminates most of the list.
Key relation back bond ⇔ donor lone pair (F, O, N) + vacant orbital (B 2p, Si 3d/σ*, P 3d) B—H, Si—H, O—H and N—H bonds can never be back bonds; screen for them first.
Solution
1. BF₃ — three B—F bonds only. Each F lone pair donates into the vacant B 2p ⇒ every bond is a back bond ⇒ counted.
2. PF₃ — three P—F bonds only. F lone pair → vacant P 3d (pπ–dπ) ⇒ every bond is a back bond ⇒ counted. The lone pair on P is not a bond, so it does not break the "each covalent bond" condition.
3. POF₃ — three P—F back bonds plus the P—O linkage, which is itself a dπ–pπ back bond from an oxygen lone pair into P 3d ⇒ every bond qualifies ⇒ counted.
4. N(SiH₃)₃, O(SiH₃)₂, NH(SiH₃)₂ — the N—Si / O—Si bonds do back-bond, but each molecule also contains Si—H (and in one case N—H) bonds, which do not ⇒ rejected.
5. B(OH)₃, BFH₂, BF₂(NH₂) — all contain O—H, B—H or N—H bonds ⇒ rejected.
6. Total = 3 (BF₃, PF₃, POF₃).
 molecule        bonds present                    any non-back bond?      count
 BF3             3 × B—F                          none                     1
 PF3             3 × P—F                          none                     2
 POF3            3 × P—F  +  P=O                  none                     3   <-- answer 3
 N(SiH3)3        3 × N—Si + 9 × Si—H              Si—H                     –
 B(OH)3          3 × B—O  + 3 × O—H               O—H                      –
 O(SiH3)2        2 × O—Si + 6 × Si—H              Si—H                     –
 NH(SiH3)2       2 × N—Si + N—H + 6 × Si—H        N—H, Si—H                –
 BFH2            B—F + 2 × B—H                    B—H                      –
 BF2(NH2)        2 × B—F + B—N + 2 × N—H          N—H                      –
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a back-bonding count says "each" or "all" bonds, do a one-pass hydrogen scan first — cross out every molecule containing X—H — and only then check the remaining ones. Trap: the question deliberately loads the list with the famous back-bonding molecules N(SiH₃)₃, O(SiH₃)₂ and B(OH)₃, which everyone recognises and wrongly counts.
Answer: 3 (BF₃, PF₃ and POF₃).
10(i)Three-column match. Molecular Orbital: (1) σs (2) πp (3) σ*sp (4) δ*. Number of nodal planes: (P) 3 (Q) 2 (R) 1 (S) 0. Symmetry: (I) BMO gerade (II) ABMO ungerade (III) BMO ungerade (IV) ABMO gerade. — Which of the following matchings is INCORRECT? (a) (1), S, I (b) (2), R, I (c) (3), R, II (d) (4), P, IINodal planes, gerade/ungeradeNodal planes in orbitals and the gerade / ungerade classification of MOs🟡 Similarb(2), R, I
Concept Two independent labels are being tested. Bonding vs antibonding is decided by whether the atomic wavefunctions were added (in phase, electron density between the nuclei) or subtracted (out of phase, a node between the nuclei). Gerade vs ungerade is decided by inversion through the centre of the molecule: if ψ(−r) = +ψ(r) it is g, if ψ(−r) = −ψ(r) it is u. The two labels are independent — you must test both separately.
Key relation σ (from s): bonding = g, antibonding = u · π (from p): bonding = u, antibonding = g π MOs invert differently from σ MOs because a p orbital already changes sign on inversion, so the parity labels flip relative to σ.
Solution
1. (a) σs, S = 0 nodal planes, I = BMO gerade. A bonding σ built from two s orbitals has no node at all ⇒ 0 nodal planes ✓, and it is symmetric on inversion ⇒ gerade ✓ ⇒ correct matching.
2. (b) πp, R = 1 nodal plane, I = BMO gerade. The bonding π does have exactly one nodal plane (the plane containing the internuclear axis) ✓, but bonding π is ungerade (III), not gerade ⇒ INCORRECT ⇒ this is the answer.
3. (c) σ*sp, R = 1, II = ABMO ungerade. An antibonding σ has one nodal plane, perpendicular to the axis and between the nuclei ✓, and σ* is ungerade ✓ ⇒ correct.
4. (d) δ*, P = 3, II = ABMO ungerade. A δ orbital already has 2 nodal planes containing the axis; the antibonding combination adds one more perpendicular node ⇒ 3 ✓, and δ* is ungerade ✓ ⇒ correct. δ from d+d: δ bonding is gerade, so δ* must be ungerade.
 MO        nodal planes   bonding?   parity   matching offered   verdict
 sigma_s        0            BMO        g       (1) S  I          OK
 pi_p           1            BMO        u       (2) R  I          WRONG parity  <-- answer (b)
 sigma*_sp      1            ABMO       u       (3) R  II         OK
 delta*         3            ABMO       u       (4) P  II         OK
⚡ JEE Trick Chant the parity rule: "sigma bonding is g, pi bonding is u — and starring (*) flips it." So σ g / σ* u / π u / π* g / δ g / δ* u. Nodal planes containing the axis: σ 0, π 1, δ 2; an asterisk adds one perpendicular node. Trap: option (b) gives the correct nodal-plane count so it "feels" right, and the parity slips past unchecked.
Answer: (b) — πp is a bonding ungerade MO, not gerade.
10(ii)How many orbitals are occupied with the set (1)(S)(I) in F₂? (a) 0 (b) 1 (c) 2 (d) 3MO configuration of F₂Molecular orbital diagram of the F₂ molecule🟡 Similarc2
Concept The set (1)(S)(I) means: an MO of type σs (built from s atomic orbitals), with 0 nodal planes, that is a bonding gerade MO. So we are asked to count the occupied bonding σ MOs that come from s orbitals in F₂. The restriction "σs" is essential — σ(2pz) is also bonding, gerade and node-free along the axis, but it is built from p orbitals and therefore belongs to a different row of column 1.
Key relation F₂ (18 e⁻): σ1s² σ*1s² σ2s² σ*2s² σ2pz² π2px² π2py² π*2px² π*2py² Bond order = (10 − 8)/2 = 1, and every electron is paired ⇒ F₂ is diamagnetic, exactly as observed.
Solution
1. Fluorine has Z = 9 ⇒ F₂ has 18 electrons to place.
2. Scan the filled list for MOs formed from s atomic orbitals: σ1s, σ*1s, σ2s, σ*2s.
3. Keep only the bonding gerade ones: σ1s and σ2s. The starred σ*1s and σ*2s are antibonding and ungerade ⇒ rejected.
4. Both σ1s and σ2s are fully occupied in F₂ ⇒ count = 2 ⇒ option (c). σ2pz is bonding and gerade too, but it is a σp, not a σs, so it is outside the requested set.
 MO of F2      built from   bonding?   parity   nodal planes   in set (1)(S)(I)?
 sigma 1s          s          BMO        g          0             YES   (1)
 sigma* 1s         s          ABMO       u          1             no
 sigma 2s          s          BMO        g          0             YES   (2)
 sigma* 2s         s          ABMO       u          1             no
 sigma 2pz         p          BMO        g          0             no (sigma_p, not sigma_s)
 pi 2px, 2py       p          BMO        u          1             no
 pi* 2px, 2py      p          ABMO       g          2             no
 ⇒ occupied orbitals in the set = 2                       <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick When a three-column match asks "how many occupied orbitals of this type", always re-read column 1 — it usually restricts the parent atomic orbital (s vs p) and that restriction is what separates the right answer from the plausible one. Here forgetting it gives 3 instead of 2.
Answer: (c) 2.
10(iii)If the z axis is the molecular axis, the CORRECT matching for the dxy + dxy orbital is 4, P, II for the subtraction of wavefunctions. Which matching is CORRECT for the addition of wavefunctions for the same combination of orbitals? (a) P, IV (b) P, III (c) Q, II (d) Q, Iδ bonding MO from dxy–dxyMO diagrams, gerade and ungerade orbitals, and bond order — worked problems🟡 SimilardQ, I
Concept Two dxy orbitals on atoms lying along the z axis overlap face to face through all four lobes — this is a δ interaction. Adding the wavefunctions gives the bonding δ MO; subtracting gives the antibonding δ*. Each dxy orbital already carries two nodal planes that contain the z axis (the xz and yz planes), and those survive in both combinations. Subtraction adds one extra node perpendicular to the axis; addition does not.
Key relation δ (addition) : 2 nodal planes, BMO, gerade · δ* (subtraction) : 3 nodal planes, ABMO, ungerade d orbitals are themselves gerade; the in-phase combination preserves that symmetry, the out-of-phase one destroys it.
Solution
1. The question hands you the antibonding half: subtraction ⇒ (4) δ*, P = 3 nodal planes, II = ABMO ungerade. Use it as the calibration.
2. Addition removes the perpendicular node ⇒ nodal planes drop from 3 to 2 ⇒ column-2 entry Q.
3. Addition puts electron density between the nuclei ⇒ bonding; and reversing the sign of the antibonding ungerade partner gives gerade ⇒ column-3 entry I (BMO gerade).
4. So the addition matching is Q, I ⇒ option (d). Options (a) and (b) keep 3 nodal planes, which belongs to δ*; option (c) keeps the antibonding label, which belongs to the subtraction case already given.
 combination of dxy + dxy   (z = molecular axis)

 operation      MO      nodal planes                    type      parity   match
 subtraction    delta*  xz, yz  +  xy (perpendicular)=3  ABMO       u       4, P, II   (given)
 addition       delta   xz, yz                       =2  BMO        g       Q, I       <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Use the "one extra node" rule: the antibonding partner of any MO has exactly one more nodal plane than the bonding one, and the opposite parity. So the moment the paper gives you the σ*/π*/δ* data, you can read off the bonding entry by subtracting one node and flipping g↔u. Trap: candidates keep P = 3 because they remember "δ orbitals have lots of nodes" without noticing which node the asterisk contributed.
Answer: (d) Q, I.

Level 10

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1More than one may be correct. A homodiatomic molecule 'X₂' has bond order 1. Which of the following prediction(s) will be CORRECT for 'X₂'? (a) the difference between the total number of bonding and antibonding electrons is 2 (b) the molecule cannot be an odd-electron molecule (c) the total number of bonding electrons is more than the antibonding electrons (d) the last electron of 'X₂' must have entered a bonding molecular orbitalBond order arithmetic in MOTMolecular orbital theory — bond order and stability🟡 Similarabc(a), (b) and (c)
Concept Everything here follows from one definition, BO = (Nb − Na)/2, plus one structural fact: a homodiatomic X₂ contains two identical atoms, so its total electron count is 2Z — always even. The one genuinely subtle option is (d): a bond order of 1 fixes the difference Nb − Na, but it says nothing about where the last electron went. F₂ is the standing counter-example.
Key relation BO = (Nb − Na)/2 = 1 ⇒ Nb − Na = 2 · total e⁻ in X₂ = 2Z (even) Both statements are exact; the rest of the question is just reading them carefully.
Solution
1. (a) BO = 1 gives Nb − Na = 2 directly ⇒ correct.
2. (b) X₂ has 2Z electrons, and 2Z is even for every element ⇒ a homodiatomic molecule can never be an odd-electron species ⇒ correct. Odd-electron molecules such as NO, NO₂ and ClO₂ are all heteronuclear.
3. (c) Nb = Na + 2 > Na ⇒ correct.
4. (d) Test it on F₂ (18 e⁻): the configuration ends …σ2pz² π² π² π*² π*², so the last electrons enter the antibonding π* level, yet BO = (10 − 8)/2 = 1. Compare Li₂ (6 e⁻), where BO is also 1 but the last electron does enter the bonding σ2s ⇒ the statement is not necessarily true ⇒ incorrect.
 example of BO = 1      total e-    Nb    Na    Nb - Na    last electron entered
 Li2                        6        4     2       2       sigma 2s   (BONDING)
 B2^2-                     12        8     6       2       pi  2p     (BONDING)
 F2                        18       10     8       2       pi* 2p     (ANTIBONDING)   <-- kills (d)
 ⇒ (a), (b), (c) always true;  (d) not necessarily true
⚡ JEE Trick Keep F₂ and Li₂ as a matched pair in memory: same bond order 1, opposite answers to "where did the last electron go?". Any statement that tries to infer the identity of the highest occupied MO from the bond order alone is false, because only the difference Nb − Na is fixed.
Answer: (a), (b) and (c).
2In which of the following processes is/are the product(s) diamagnetic? (a) N₂²⁺ + e⁻ ⟶ N₂ (b) 2NO₂ ⟶ N₂O₄ (c) NO₂ + [ClO₂]⁺[ClO₄]⁻ ⟶ [NO₂]⁺[ClO₄]⁻ + ClO₂ (d) O₂²⁻ − e⁻ ⟶ O₂⁻ [more than one correct]Magnetic character of productsMolecular orbital theory — paramagnetism and diamagnetism🟡 Similarab(a) and (b)
Concept A species is diamagnetic only if every electron is paired. For diatomics fill the MO diagram and look for unpaired electrons; for polyatomic species just count total valence electrons — an odd total guarantees paramagnetism. Note the wording: the products, plural. If a reaction makes two products, both must be diamagnetic for the option to count.
Key relation odd total electrons ⇒ at least one unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic · O₂ⁿ series: O₂⁺ 15e⁻ (1 unpaired), O₂ 16e⁻ (2), O₂⁻ 17e⁻ (1), O₂²⁻ 18e⁻ (0) Only the peroxide ion O₂²⁻, with π* completely filled, is diamagnetic in that series.
Solution
1. (a) The product is N₂, 14 electrons: …σ2s² σ*2s² π2p⁴ σ2pz², everything paired ⇒ diamagnetic ⇒ counted.
2. (b) NO₂ is an odd-electron radical (paramagnetic), but the product N₂O₄ pairs the two odd electrons into the N—N σ bond ⇒ diamagnetic ⇒ counted. This is exactly why brown paramagnetic NO₂ dimerises to colourless diamagnetic N₂O₄ on cooling.
3. (c) Two products are formed: [NO₂]⁺[ClO₄]⁻ (both ions have even, paired electron counts ⇒ diamagnetic) and ClO₂, which is an odd-electron radical ⇒ paramagnetic ⇒ not all products diamagnetic ⇒ rejected.
4. (d) O₂²⁻ (18 e⁻, diamagnetic) loses an electron to give O₂⁻, the superoxide ion, 17 e⁻ with one electron alone in a π* orbital ⇒ paramagnetic ⇒ rejected.
 option   product(s)                    electrons   unpaired e-   verdict
 (a)      N2                               14            0        diamagnetic  <--
 (b)      N2O4                             46            0        diamagnetic  <--
 (c)      [NO2]+[ClO4]-  and  ClO2         even + ODD    0 and 1   ClO2 is paramagnetic
 (d)      O2-  (superoxide)                17            1        paramagnetic
 ⇒ answer (a) and (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the four famous odd-electron (always paramagnetic) species: NO, NO₂, ClO₂, O₂⁻. Then learn their diamagnetic partners: N₂O₄ (from 2NO₂), N₂O₂ (from 2NO), O₂²⁻ (from O₂⁻ + e). Dimerisation or electron transfer that pairs the odd electron always kills the paramagnetism. Trap: option (c) hides a radical in the second product.
Answer: (a) and (b).
3(i)Passage: O₂ is paramagnetic — a property it shares with B₂ — which cannot be explained by the traditional Lewis dot structure but is evident from the MO picture. — In the energy level diagram of O₂²⁺, the 13th electron is present in: (a) σ2px orbital (b) π2py or π2pz orbital (c) *π2py or *π2pz orbital (d) *σ2px orbitalFilling order for O₂²⁺MO diagram of the oxygen molecule — bond order and magnetic property🟡 Similarbπ2py or π2pz orbital
Concept For O₂ and every species beyond it (Z > 7) there is no s–p mixing, so σ2p lies below the π2p pair. The paper takes x as the molecular axis, so the head-on MO is σ2px and the two sideways MOs are π2py and π2pz. Locating "the 13th electron" is then just a matter of filling in strict order and counting.
Key relation Z > 7 order: σ1s < σ*1s < σ2s < σ*2s < σ2px < (π2py = π2pz) < (π*2py = π*2pz) < σ*2px The reversed order (π below σ) applies only to B₂, C₂ and N₂, where 2s–2p mixing pushes σ2p above π2p.
Solution
1. O₂ has 16 electrons, so O₂²⁺ has 14 electrons.
2. Fill in order: σ1s (e⁻ 1–2), σ*1s (3–4), σ2s (5–6), σ*2s (7–8), σ2px (9–10), then the degenerate π2py/π2pz pair takes electrons 11, 12, 13, 14.
3. Electron number 13 therefore lands in a π2py or π2pz orbital ⇒ option (b).
4. Check: with 14 electrons, Nb = 10, Na = 4 ⇒ BO = 3 and all electrons paired ⇒ O₂²⁺ is isoelectronic with N₂ and is diamagnetic. Option (c) would be right for O₂ itself (electrons 15 and 16), which is the trap.
 O2^2+ , 14 electrons, molecular axis = x

 MO             e- numbers filled
 sigma 1s          1, 2
 sigma* 1s         3, 4
 sigma 2s          5, 6
 sigma* 2s         7, 8
 sigma 2px         9, 10
 pi 2py / pi 2pz  11, 12, 13, 14      <-- 13th electron sits here : answer (b)
 pi* / sigma*      empty
 bond order = (10 - 4)/2 = 3 ; diamagnetic (isoelectronic with N2)
⚡ JEE Trick Skip the 1s shell whenever a question numbers electrons from the valence diagram only — read the options first to see which convention is in play. And fix the two orderings once: B₂, C₂, N₂ ⇒ π below σ; O₂, F₂, Ne₂ ⇒ σ below π. Trap: (c) is the answer for O₂, not for O₂²⁺.
Answer: (b) π2py or π2pz.
3(ii)Which of the following sets of orbitals results in an N.B.M.O. (non-bonding molecular orbital) if the z-axis is the bond formation axis? (a) pz + dxz (b) s + dyz (c) py + dxz (d) all of theseZero overlap by symmetryIf the z-axis is the internuclear axis, which overlaps are zero?🟡 Similardall of these
Concept Two atomic orbitals combine only if they have the same symmetry about the internuclear axis. Classify each orbital by how many nodal planes contain that axis: 0 ⇒ σ type, 1 ⇒ π type, 2 ⇒ δ type. A σ orbital and a π orbital have net zero overlap: the positive overlap on one side is exactly cancelled by an equal negative overlap on the other, so no bonding or antibonding MO forms and the electrons stay in a non-bonding MO.
Key relation S = ∫ψAψB dτ = 0 whenever ψA and ψB behave differently under a symmetry operation of the axis · with z as the axis: σ: s, pz, dz² · π: px, py, dxz, dyz · δ: dxy, dx²−y² Only same-class pairs (σ with σ, π with π of the same orientation, δ with δ of the same orientation) overlap.
Solution
1. (a) pz + dxz: pz is σ (0 nodal planes containing z), dxz is π (nodal planes xy and yz — one of them, yz, contains z) ⇒ different symmetry ⇒ zero overlap ⇒ NBMO.
2. (b) s + dyz: s is spherically symmetric ⇒ σ; dyz is π ⇒ the four lobes of dyz overlap the sphere in two positive and two negative regions that cancel exactly ⇒ zero overlap ⇒ NBMO.
3. (c) py + dxz: both are π type, but they are π in different planes. Reflect in the xz plane (y → −y): py ∝ y changes sign, while dxz ∝ xz is unchanged ⇒ opposite behaviour ⇒ zero overlap ⇒ NBMO.
4. All three combinations give zero net overlap ⇒ option (d) all of these.
 z = internuclear axis

 pair                symmetry classes      test                                 net overlap
 pz + dxz            sigma  vs  pi         different m  (0 vs 1)                 ZERO  <--
 s  + dyz            sigma  vs  pi         + and - lobes cancel over the sphere   ZERO  <--
 py + dxz            pi(yz-plane) vs pi(xz-plane)   y -> -y : one flips, one not  ZERO  <--
 ⇒ every listed set is non-bonding : answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Reduce every overlap question to a one-line symmetry test: count nodal planes containing the internuclear axis (σ = 0, π = 1, δ = 2) and, for π and δ, note which plane. Same number and same plane ⇒ overlap; anything else ⇒ zero. Trap: (c) is the hard one — two π orbitals still cancel if they are π about different planes.
Answer: (d) all of these.
3(iii)Which of the following sets of molecules/ions has equal bond order? (a) NO⁺, N₂, O₂²⁺ (b) O₂, N₂²⁻, NO⁻ (c) NO, O₂⁺, N₂⁻ (d) All of theseIsoelectronic species, bond orderBond order of N₂ and its ions — N₂⁺, N₂⁻, N₂²⁻ and more🟡 SimilardAll of these
Concept All the species listed are second-period diatomics, so a single MO diagram serves them all. For such species the bond order depends only on the total number of electrons — not on which elements supply them. Therefore any isoelectronic set automatically has identical bond order. The whole question reduces to counting electrons three times.
Key relation 14 e⁻ ⇒ BO 3 · 15 e⁻ ⇒ BO 2.5 · 16 e⁻ ⇒ BO 2 · 17 e⁻ ⇒ BO 1.5 · 18 e⁻ ⇒ BO 1 Beyond 14 electrons every additional electron enters a π* orbital and lowers the bond order by exactly 0.5.
Solution
1. Set (a): NO⁺ = 7 + 8 − 1 = 14; N₂ = 14; O₂²⁺ = 16 − 2 = 14 ⇒ all 14 e⁻ ⇒ all BO 3 ⇒ equal ✓.
2. Set (b): O₂ = 16; N₂²⁻ = 14 + 2 = 16; NO⁻ = 15 + 1 = 16 ⇒ all 16 e⁻ ⇒ all BO 2 ⇒ equal ✓.
3. Set (c): NO = 15; O₂⁺ = 16 − 1 = 15; N₂⁻ = 14 + 1 = 15 ⇒ all 15 e⁻ ⇒ all BO 2.5 ⇒ equal ✓.
4. Every set is internally isoelectronic ⇒ (d) All of these. Note the bond orders differ between sets (3, 2 and 2.5) — the question only asks for equality within each set.
 set   species                 electrons   Nb   Na   bond order   equal within set?
 (a)   NO+ , N2 , O2^2+           14       10    4      3.0            YES  <--
 (b)   O2  , N2^2- , NO-          16       10    6      2.0            YES  <--
 (c)   NO  , O2+ , N2-            15       10    5      2.5            YES  <--
 ⇒ all three sets qualify : answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Never build three MO diagrams — just add up electrons and read the ladder 14→3, 15→2.5, 16→2, 17→1.5, 18→1. Anion adds electrons, cation removes them. This single ladder answers most MOT questions in JEE Main in under ten seconds. Trap: "All of these" is often skipped because candidates check only set (a) and move on.
Answer: (d) All of these.
4Integer type. Which of the following molecule(s) or ion(s) is/are having bond order less than or equal to two: B₂, C₂, O₂²⁻, O₂⁺, H₂⁺ ______Bond order ≤ 2 countBond order of H₂, He₂, Li₂, Be₂, B₂, C₂, N₂, O₂, F₂ and Ne₂ explained🟡 Similar44
Concept Count total electrons for each species, place them in the correct MO order (remember the switch at N₂/O₂), and compute BO = (Nb − Na)/2. The condition is "less than or equal to two", so BO = 2 exactly still counts — a favourite place to drop a mark.
Key relation BO = (Nb − Na)/2 · B₂ 10 e⁻ ⇒ 1 · C₂ 12 e⁻ ⇒ 2 · O₂²⁻ 18 e⁻ ⇒ 1 · O₂⁺ 15 e⁻ ⇒ 2.5 · H₂⁺ 1 e⁻ ⇒ 0.5 H₂⁺ has a single electron in σ1s and nothing in σ*1s, giving the smallest possible non-zero bond order.
Solution
1. B₂ (10 e⁻): π2px¹ π2py¹ is the HOMO ⇒ Nb = 6, Na = 4 ⇒ BO = 1 ⇒ ≤ 2 ⇒ counted.
2. C₂ (12 e⁻): π2p⁴, σ2p empty ⇒ Nb = 8, Na = 4 ⇒ BO = 2 ⇒ ≤ 2 ⇒ counted.
3. O₂²⁻ (18 e⁻): π* completely filled ⇒ Nb = 10, Na = 8 ⇒ BO = 1 ⇒ counted.
4. O₂⁺ (15 e⁻): π*³ ⇒ Nb = 10, Na = 5 ⇒ BO = 2.5 ⇒ > 2 ⇒ rejected.
5. H₂⁺ (1 e⁻): σ1s¹ ⇒ Nb = 1, Na = 0 ⇒ BO = 0.5 ⇒ counted.
6. Total = 4.
 species    electrons    Nb    Na    bond order    BO <= 2 ?
 B2            10         6     4       1.0          YES   1
 C2            12         8     4       2.0          YES   2
 O2^2-         18        10     8       1.0          YES   3
 O2+           15        10     5       2.5          no
 H2+            1         1     0       0.5          YES   4    <-- answer 4
⚡ JEE Trick Two counts are worth memorising because they are always misplaced: C₂ has BO 2 and is diamagnetic (both π filled, σ2p empty — no double bond of the ordinary kind, it is two π bonds), and O₂²⁻ has BO 1, the same as F₂ since both have 18 electrons. Trap: reading "less than two" instead of "less than or equal to two" drops C₂ and gives 3.
Answer: 4 (B₂, C₂, O₂²⁻ and H₂⁺).
5Integer type. Find the number of non-bonding molecular orbitals from the following if the z-axis is the internuclear axis: (dx²−y² + s), (s + dyz), (s + px), (pz + py) ______Zero-overlap combinationsIf z is the main axis of a diatomic, what do px and py overlap to form?🟡 Similar4004
Concept Same symmetry test as before, applied four times. Classify each orbital about the z axis by the number of nodal planes containing z: σ (0) = s, pz, dz²; π (1) = px, py, dxz, dyz; δ (2) = dxy, dx²−y². A pair drawn from two different classes always has net zero overlap and therefore produces a non-bonding MO rather than a bonding/antibonding pair.
Key relation overlap ≠ 0 only if both orbitals belong to the same symmetry class (and the same plane, for π and δ) Otherwise the constructive and destructive lobe-overlaps are equal in magnitude and cancel exactly on integration.
Solution
1. dx²−y² + s: dx²−y² is δ (nodal planes at 45° to x and y, both containing z); s is σ ⇒ δ vs σ ⇒ zero overlap ⇒ NBMO.
2. s + dyz: σ vs π ⇒ zero overlap ⇒ NBMO.
3. s + px: σ vs π ⇒ the two lobes of px overlap the s sphere with opposite signs and cancel ⇒ zero overlap ⇒ NBMO.
4. pz + py: pz lies along the axis (σ) while py is perpendicular (π) ⇒ zero overlap ⇒ NBMO.
5. All four combinations are non-bonding ⇒ answer 4 (written 004 in the three-digit numerical format). Had the axis been x instead of z, (s + px) would have become a genuine σ overlap — the choice of axis is not decoration.
 z = internuclear axis

 combination            class of 1st    class of 2nd    same class?    result
 d(x2-y2) + s              delta            sigma            no        NBMO   1
 s + d(yz)                 sigma            pi               no        NBMO   2
 s + px                    sigma            pi               no        NBMO   3
 pz + py                   sigma            pi               no        NBMO   4
                                                                   <-- answer 4 (004)
⚡ JEE Trick Write the three classes on your rough sheet the instant a question names an internuclear axis — σ {s, pz, dz²}, π {px, py, dxz, dyz}, δ {dxy, dx²−y²} — then just check whether the two orbitals sit in the same bucket. Trap: the paper always slips in one pair that would overlap for a different axis choice.
Answer: 4 (all four combinations are non-bonding).

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1(2026 Level-5) Given below are two statements: Statement I: The number of species among SF4, NH4+, [NiCl4], XeF4, [PtCl4]2−, SeF4 and [Ni(CN)4]2−, that have tetrahedral geometry is 3. Statement II: In the set [NO2, BeH2, BF3, AlCl3], all the molecules have incomplete octet around central atom. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer: (a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are trueTetrahedral vs square planar; incomplete octetWhy some ML₄ species are tetrahedral (sp³) and others square planar (dsp²)🟡 SimilarcStatement I is false but Statement II is true
Concept Two independent ideas are being tested. For geometry, a four-coordinate centre is tetrahedral only when all four σ-pairs are equivalent and there is no stereochemically active lone pair and no dsp² mixing; a lone pair on a main-group centre (SF4, SeF4) pushes you to see-saw, and a d8 metal with a strong-field ligand (or a 5d metal at all) collapses to square planar. For the octet, "incomplete" means the central atom finishes with fewer than eight electrons — the classic electron-deficient family Be, B, Al, plus odd-electron NO2 where nitrogen is stuck on seven.
Key relation Steric number = σ bonds + lone pairs on the central atom SN 4 with 0 lp ⇒ tetrahedral; SN 5 with 1 lp ⇒ see-saw. For complexes: Ni2+(d8) + weak field ⇒ sp³ tetrahedral; d8 + strong field, or any Pt(II)/Pd(II) ⇒ dsp² square planar CN⁻ pairs the d electrons and frees an inner d orbital; Cl⁻ cannot.
Solution
1. SF4: S has 4 bp + 1 lp ⇒ SN 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal arrangement, see-saw shape. Not tetrahedral.
2. NH4+: N has 4 bp + 0 lp ⇒ sp³ ⇒ tetrahedral. ✔
3. [NiCl4]2−: Ni2+ is d8, Cl⁻ is a weak-field ligand and cannot force pairing ⇒ sp³ ⇒ tetrahedral (and paramagnetic, 2 unpaired). ✔
4. XeF4: Xe has 4 bp + 2 lp ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ octahedral arrangement, the two lone pairs go trans ⇒ square planar. Not tetrahedral.
5. [PtCl4]2−: Pt(II) is 5d8; the large 5d splitting makes it dsp² ⇒ square planar regardless of ligand strength. Not tetrahedral.
6. SeF4: identical to SF4 ⇒ see-saw. Not tetrahedral.
7. [Ni(CN)4]2−: CN⁻ is strong field ⇒ pairs the d8 electrons ⇒ dsp² ⇒ square planar (diamagnetic). Not tetrahedral.
8. Tetrahedral count = 2, not 3 ⇒ Statement I is FALSE. The trap is assuming both nickel complexes behave alike — the ligand decides.
9. Statement II: NO2 has 17 valence electrons, so N ends with 7; BeH2 gives Be 4; BF3 gives B 6; AlCl3 (monomer) gives Al 6. All four are below eight ⇒ Statement II is TRUE.
10. False + True ⇒ option (c). Eliminate (a) and (d) since I fails; eliminate (b) since II genuinely holds.
 species            SN / d-count   hybridisation   shape            tetrahedral?
 SF4                4bp + 1lp      sp3d            see-saw               no
 NH4+               4bp + 0lp      sp3             tetrahedral          YES
 [NiCl4]2-          d8, weak fld   sp3             tetrahedral          YES
 XeF4               4bp + 2lp      sp3d2           square planar         no
 [PtCl4]2-          5d8            dsp2            square planar         no
 SeF4               4bp + 1lp      sp3d            see-saw               no
 [Ni(CN)4]2-        d8, strong fld dsp2            square planar         no
                                                   count = 2 (not 3) <-- Stmt I FALSE

 central atom       electrons around it            octet?
 N in NO2           7 (odd electron)               incomplete
 Be in BeH2         4                              incomplete
 B in BF3           6                              incomplete
 Al in AlCl3        6                              incomplete   <-- Stmt II TRUE  ⇒ answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the three-line rule for MX4: Pt(II)/Pd(II)/Au(III) → always square planar; Ni(II) → ligand decides (CN⁻, CO strong ⇒ square planar, halides weak ⇒ tetrahedral); main group with a lone pair → never tetrahedral. The distractors are built on two traps — counting XeF4 as tetrahedral because it is "4-coordinate", and calling AlCl3 octet-complete because you pictured the Al2Cl6 dimer. The statement says "molecules" of AlCl3, i.e. the monomer, which is electron deficient.
Answer: (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true
2(2026 Level-5) Pair of species among the following having same bond order as well as paramagnetic character will be — (a) O2+, N22− (b) O2−, N2+ (c) O2+, N2− (d) O2−, N2−MOT — bond order and magnetic characterBond orders and stability across the whole N₂ and O₂ ion family🟡 SimilarcO2+, N2−
Concept Every one of these ions is built by adding or removing electrons from the antibonding π* level, so both bond order and magnetism follow mechanically from the electron count. N2 and O2 use different MO orderings (N2 has 2s–2p mixing, so π2p lies below σ2p; O2 does not, so σ2p is lowest), but for these ions the electrons being added or removed all land in π*2p, which is the highest level in both schemes — so the ordering question never actually bites.
Key relation B.O. = ½(Nb − Na); paramagnetic ⟺ at least one unpaired electron π* is a doubly degenerate level, so it fills by Hund's rule — one electron gives 1 unpaired, two give 2 unpaired, three give 1 unpaired, four give 0.
Solution
1. Reference points: N2 has 14 e⁻ (B.O. 3, diamagnetic), O2 has 16 e⁻ (B.O. 2, paramagnetic with π*x¹π*y¹).
2. O2+ = 15 e⁻: remove one π* electron ⇒ π*¹ ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 5) = 2.5, 1 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic.
3. O2− = 17 e⁻: π*³ ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 7) = 1.5, 1 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic.
4. N2+ = 13 e⁻: an electron leaves the bonding σ2p ⇒ B.O. = ½(9 − 4) = 2.5, 1 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic.
5. N2− = 15 e⁻: one electron enters π* ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 5) = 2.5, 1 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic.
6. N22− = 16 e⁻: π*x¹π*y¹ ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 6) = 2, 2 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic.
7. Now test the options. (a) 2.5 vs 2 ✘ bond orders differ. (b) 1.5 vs 2.5 ✘. (d) 1.5 vs 2.5 ✘. (c) O2+ and N2− are both B.O. 2.5 and both paramagnetic ✔ — and both are 15-electron species, which is the real reason they match.
 species    e-    config of the top level    Nb   Na   B.O.    unpaired   magnetic
 O2+        15    pi*1                       10    5   2.5        1       para
 O2-        17    pi*3                       10    7   1.5        1       para
 N2+        13    sigma2p1 (bonding lost)     9    4   2.5        1       para
 N2-        15    pi*1                       10    5   2.5        1       para
 N2 2-      16    pi*x1 pi*y1                10    6   2.0        2       para

 (a) 2.5 vs 2.0   different B.O.
 (b) 1.5 vs 2.5   different B.O.
 (c) 2.5 vs 2.5   SAME B.O., both paramagnetic   <-- answer (c)
 (d) 1.5 vs 2.5   different B.O.
⚡ JEE Trick Isoelectronic species have identical bond order and identical magnetism. O2+ and N2− are both 15-electron — just count electrons and the answer falls out in five seconds without drawing a single MO diagram. The trap in (a) is that O2+ (15 e⁻) and N22− (16 e⁻) are both paramagnetic, so a candidate who only checks magnetism picks it; the bond orders 2.5 vs 2.0 are what separates them.
Answer: (c) O2+, N2−
3(2026 Level-5) Given below are two statements: Statement I: The number of species among BF4−, SiF4, XeF4 and SF4, that have unequal E–F bond lengths is two. Here, E is the central atom. Statement II: Among O2−, O22−, F2 and O2+, O2− has the highest bond order. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are false (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (d) Statement I is false but Statement II is trueEquivalent vs inequivalent bonds; bond order rankingBond orders of the whole O₂ family — O₂, O₂⁻, O₂²⁻, O₂⁺, O₂²⁺🟡 SimilaraBoth Statement I and Statement II are false
Concept Bonds around a central atom are equal in length only when they are symmetry-equivalent — that is, when a symmetry operation of the molecule can swap them. Tetrahedral (Td) and square-planar (D4h) geometries make all four bonds equivalent; a trigonal-bipyramidal framework does not, because axial positions (3 bond-pair repulsions at 90°) and equatorial positions (2 at 90°) are physically different environments — axial bonds are always longer. For Statement II, bond order in a homonuclear diatomic depends only on how far the antibonding π* level has been filled.
Key relation All bonds equal ⟺ all terminal atoms symmetry-equivalent; in sp³d, axial bond length > equatorial bond length an axial bond faces three 90° repulsions from the equatorial set, an equatorial bond faces only two from the axial set. And B.O. = ½(Nb − Na), which falls as electrons are added to π*.
Solution
1. BF4−: SN 4, no lone pair ⇒ regular tetrahedron, all four B–F equal.
2. SiF4: SN 4, no lone pair ⇒ regular tetrahedron, all four Si–F equal.
3. XeF4: SN 6 with two trans lone pairs ⇒ square planar; the four F atoms are related by the C4 axis ⇒ all four Xe–F equal. Lone pairs being present does not by itself make bonds unequal — what matters is whether the F atoms remain equivalent.
4. SF4: SN 5, one equatorial lone pair ⇒ see-saw with 2 axial F and 2 equatorial F. Axial S–F ≈ 1.646 Å, equatorial S–F ≈ 1.545 Å ⇒ unequal. ✔
5. So exactly one species has unequal E–F bonds, not two ⇒ Statement I is FALSE.
6. Statement II: O2− (17 e⁻, π*³) ⇒ B.O. 1.5; O22− (18 e⁻, π*⁴) ⇒ 1.0; F2 (18 e⁻) ⇒ 1.0; O2+ (15 e⁻, π*¹) ⇒ 2.5.
7. Highest is clearly O2+ at 2.5, not O2− ⇒ Statement II is FALSE.
8. Both false ⇒ option (a). Options (b), (c), (d) each require at least one statement to survive, and neither does.
 species    SN / shape              terminal atoms equivalent?   E-F lengths
 BF4-       4, tetrahedral          yes (Td)                     all equal
 SiF4       4, tetrahedral          yes (Td)                     all equal
 XeF4       6 (4bp+2lp), sq planar  yes (D4h)                    all equal
 SF4        5 (4bp+1lp), see-saw    NO (2 axial + 2 equatorial)  ax 1.646 > eq 1.545  <-- the only one
                                              count = 1, not 2   <-- Stmt I FALSE

 species    e-    pi* filling   B.O.
 O2+        15    pi*1          2.5   <-- highest
 O2-        17    pi*3          1.5
 O2 2-      18    pi*4          1.0
 F2         18    pi*4          1.0
                       claim "O2- highest" <-- Stmt II FALSE   ⇒ answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Screen for unequal bonds with one question: is the geometry trigonal bipyramidal or derived from it (sp³d)? If yes ⇒ axial ≠ equatorial (PCl5, SF4, ClF3); if it is Td, D4h, Oh or linear ⇒ all equal. The trap here is XeF4 — students see the two lone pairs and assume distortion, but the lone pairs sit trans along the C4 axis and leave the four Xe–F bonds perfectly equivalent. For Statement II, remember the single ladder O2+(2.5) > O2(2) > O2−(1.5) > O22−(1) — adding electrons to O2 can only weaken it.
Answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are false
4(2026 Level-4) Given below are two statements: Statement I: The correct order in terms of bond dissociation enthalpy is Cl2 > Br2 > F2 > I2. Statement II: The correct trend in the covalent character of the metal halides is [SnCl4 > SnCl2], [PbCl4 > PbCl2] and [UF4 > UF6]. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer: (a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are falseHalogen bond enthalpy anomaly; Fajans' rulePredicting covalent character with Fajans' rule — charge, size and polarising power🟡 SimilaraStatement I is true but Statement II is false
Concept Bond dissociation enthalpy in the halogens would fall smoothly down the group on size grounds alone, but F2 breaks the pattern: the two fluorine atoms are so small that their three lone pairs each are crushed together at a bond length of only 1.42 Å, and the resulting lone-pair/lone-pair repulsion cancels much of the bonding — F2 ends up weaker than both Cl2 and Br2. Statement II is pure Fajans' rule: covalent character rises with the polarising power of the cation, which rises with charge and falls with size.
Key relation B.D.E.: Cl2 (242) > Br2 (193) > F2 (158) > I2 (151) kJ mol⁻¹ F₂ is anomalously low because of lone-pair repulsion at a very short bond length and the absence of d orbitals. Polarising power ∝ charge / size ⇒ higher oxidation state ⇒ more covalent this is why the +4 chloride is always more covalent than the +2 chloride of the same metal.
Solution
1. Statement I: the printed order Cl2 > Br2 > F2 > I2 matches the experimental values exactly (242, 193, 158, 151 kJ mol⁻¹) ⇒ Statement I is TRUE.
2. Statement II, part 1: Sn4+ is smaller and more highly charged than Sn2+ ⇒ far greater polarising power ⇒ SnCl4 > SnCl2 in covalent character ✔ (SnCl4 is in fact a fuming liquid, SnCl2 an ionic solid).
3. Part 2: identically, Pb4+ > Pb2+ in polarising power ⇒ PbCl4 > PbCl2 ✔ (PbCl4 is a covalent oily liquid, PbCl2 an ionic white solid).
4. Part 3: the same rule demands UF6 > UF4, since U(VI) polarises far harder than U(IV). The printed claim UF4 > UF6 is the reverse. Physically obvious: UF6 is a volatile molecular solid that sublimes at 56 °C and is used for gaseous isotope separation, while UF4 ("green salt") is a high-melting ionic solid melting near 1036 °C.
5. One wrong sub-claim makes the whole statement wrong ⇒ Statement II is FALSE.
6. True + False ⇒ option (a). Eliminate (b) and (c) because Statement II fails on the uranium pair; eliminate (d) because Statement I is genuinely correct.
 X2      bond length (A)    B.D.E. (kJ/mol)    comment
 F2          1.42                158           anomalously low: lp-lp repulsion
 Cl2         1.99                242           maximum
 Br2         2.28                193
 I2          2.66                151           lowest, poor overlap
             printed order Cl2 > Br2 > F2 > I2       <-- Stmt I TRUE

 pair                  cation charge/size          expected (Fajans)   printed    verdict
 SnCl4 vs SnCl2        Sn4+ > Sn2+                 SnCl4 more coval.   same        ok
 PbCl4 vs PbCl2        Pb4+ > Pb2+                 PbCl4 more coval.   same        ok
 UF4  vs UF6           U6+ > U4+                   UF6  more coval.    reversed    WRONG
                                                          <-- Stmt II FALSE  ⇒ answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Two one-line memory hooks kill this question. (i) "F2 is the weak one" — the halogen B.D.E. order is Cl > Br > F > I, the same anomaly that makes N–N and O–O bonds weaker than P–P and S–S. (ii) Higher oxidation state ⇒ more covalent, always. The examiner buries two correct Fajans pairs first so you stop reading and tick "both true"; the falsehood is planted in the third, least familiar pair. Read every sub-claim in a compound statement.
Answer: (a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false
5(2026 Level-4) Given below are two statements: Statement I: The halogen that makes longest bond with hydrogen in HX, has the smallest covalent radius in its group. Statement II: A group 15 element's hydride EH3 has the lowest boiling point among corresponding hydrides of other group 15 elements. The maximum covalency of that element E is 4. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are true. (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true. (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are false. (d) Statement I is true but Statement II is false.Covalent radius vs H–X bond length; hydride boiling points; covalencyOrder of boiling points of HF, HCl, HBr and HI — and why HF breaks the trend🟡 SimilarcBoth Statement I and Statement II are false.
Concept A covalent bond length is essentially the sum of the two covalent radii, so within the HX series the H is fixed and the bond length simply tracks the halogen's size — bigger halogen, longer bond. Statement II tests two separate facts: which group-15 hydride boils lowest (a hydrogen-bonding question, where NH3 is the anomaly that boils high, leaving PH3 lowest), and what maximum covalency phosphorus can reach (a d-orbital availability question).
Key relation d(H–X) ≈ rH + rX ⇒ longest H–X ⟺ LARGEST halogen. Boiling points of EH3: PH3 (−88 °C) < AsH3 (−62 °C) < NH3 (−33 °C) < SbH3 (−17 °C) NH₃ is lifted out of order by intermolecular hydrogen bonding; from PH₃ onward the trend is a normal van der Waals rise with molar mass.
Solution
1. Statement I: covalent radii in group 17 rise F (72 pm) < Cl (99) < Br (114) < I (133), so H–X lengths rise HF (92 pm) < HCl (127) < HBr (141) < HI (161).
2. The longest H–X bond is therefore HI, and iodine has the largest covalent radius in its group, not the smallest. The halogen with the smallest covalent radius is F, and HF has the shortest bond — the exact opposite of the claim. ⇒ Statement I is FALSE.
3. Statement II, first half: NH3 hydrogen bonds strongly and boils at −33 °C, well above PH3. P–H is essentially non-polar (χP ≈ χH ≈ 2.1), so PH3 has no H-bonding and boils lowest at −88 °C ⇒ the element E is phosphorus. ✔
4. Second half: phosphorus has empty 3d orbitals and routinely expands its octet — PCl5 shows covalency 5 and [PF6]−, [PCl6]− show covalency 6. So the maximum covalency of P is 6, not 4. ⇒ Statement II is FALSE. Covalency 4 is the ceiling for nitrogen (NH₄⁺), which has no d orbitals — the examiner is baiting you into answering for N.
5. Both false ⇒ option (c). Eliminate (a) and (d) since I is wrong; eliminate (b) since the covalency claim in II is wrong.
 HX     r(X) covalent (pm)    d(H-X) (pm)
 HF            72                 92     shortest  (F = smallest radius)
 HCl           99                127
 HBr          114                141
 HI           133                161     LONGEST   (I = LARGEST radius)
        claim: "longest bond <-> smallest radius"        <-- Stmt I FALSE

 EH3      b.p. (deg C)   H-bonding?          max covalency of E
 NH3         -33         yes (anomalous)     4   (no d orbitals)
 PH3         -88   <-- lowest, so E = P      6   (PF6-, PCl6-)
 AsH3        -62          no                 6
 SbH3        -17          no                 6
