        claim: "max covalency of E is 4", but E = P has 6  <-- Stmt II FALSE  ⇒ answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Both halves of this question are built on the second-period anomaly. Statement I hopes you confuse "smallest radius" with "longest bond"; write d ≈ rH + rX once and the confusion disappears. Statement II hopes you answer "N" because NH3 is the famous group-15 hydride — but the question asks for the lowest boiling point, and NH3 is the one lifted out of the trend by H-bonding, so the answer is PH3. The rule to bank: maximum covalency = 4 only for period-2 elements (no d orbitals); from period 3 it becomes 6.
Answer: (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are false
6(2026 Level-4) Two p-block elements X and Y form fluorides of the type EF3. The fluoride compound XF3 is a Lewis acid and YF3 is a Lewis base. The hybridization of the central atoms of XF3 and YF3 respectively are (a) Both sp3 (b) sp2 and sp3 (c) sp3 and sp2 (d) Both sp2Lewis acid vs Lewis base EF₃ — hybridisationBF₃ (planar, sp²) versus NF₃ (pyramidal, sp³) — structure and polarity compared🟡 Similarbsp2 and sp3
Concept Lewis acidity and basicity in an EF3 molecule are decided entirely by whether the central atom carries a lone pair. If the central atom has only three σ pairs and an empty orbital (steric number 3), it must accept an electron pair to complete its octet ⇒ Lewis acid, and sp2 hybridisation with a vacant unhybridised p orbital. If it has three σ pairs plus a lone pair (steric number 4), that lone pair is available to donate ⇒ Lewis base, and sp3 hybridisation with a pyramidal shape.
Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs; SN 3, 0 lp ⇒ sp², trigonal planar, electron deficient ⇒ Lewis acid; SN 4, 1 lp ⇒ sp³, pyramidal, lone pair donor ⇒ Lewis base the empty p orbital perpendicular to the BF₃ plane is precisely the orbital that accepts the incoming pair, converting B to sp³ in the adduct.
Solution
1. Identify X. A p-block EF3 that is a Lewis acid means the central atom stops at six electrons — the group-13 fluorides BF3, AlF3. Take X = B: valence 3, all three electrons used in B–F bonds, no lone pair ⇒ SN = 3.
2. Three σ pairs and nothing else ⇒ sp2, trigonal planar, 120°, with one empty 2p orbital perpendicular to the plane. That vacancy is what makes BF3 a Lewis acid (BF3 + NH3 → F3B←NH3).
3. Identify Y. A p-block EF3 that is a Lewis base means the central atom keeps a lone pair — the group-15 fluorides NF3, PF3. Take Y = N: valence 5, three electrons in N–F bonds, two left over as a lone pair ⇒ SN = 3 + 1 = 4.
4. Four electron domains ⇒ sp3, tetrahedral arrangement, trigonal pyramidal shape (≈102°), lone pair on top and available for donation ⇒ Lewis base. PF₃ is the textbook example — it donates to transition metals exactly as CO does.
5. So XF3 is sp2 and YF3 is sp3 ⇒ option (b).
6. Eliminate: (a) "both sp3" would give both a lone pair, so neither could be a Lewis acid; (d) "both sp2" would leave neither with a lone pair, so neither could be a Lewis base; (c) is the right pair of labels attached to the wrong molecules — the order asked for is XF3 first, then YF3.
                 XF3 (Lewis ACID)              YF3 (Lewis BASE)
 example         BF3                           NF3 / PF3
 group           13                            15
 valence e-      3                             5
 sigma bonds     3                             3
 lone pairs      0                             1
 steric number   3                             4
 hybridisation   sp2                           sp3          <-- answer (b)
 shape           trigonal planar, 120 deg      pyramidal, ~102 deg
 why acid/base   empty p orbital accepts       lone pair donates
⚡ JEE Trick Lewis acid = vacancy = no lone pair = sp2; Lewis base = lone pair = sp3. You never need to name B or N at all — reading "acid" and "base" is enough to write down sp2 and sp3 in that order. The single trap the paper sets is option (c), which contains exactly the right two hybridisations but swapped; candidates who solve the chemistry correctly and then read the order carelessly land straight on it. Always re-check which of X and Y was asked first.
Answer: (b) sp2 and sp3
7(2026 Level-4) Among H2S, H2O, NF3, NH3 and CHCl3, identify the molecule (X) with lowest dipole moment value. The number of lone pairs of electrons present on the central atom of the molecule (X) is : (a) 2 (b) 0 (c) 1 (d) 3Lowest dipole moment; lone pairs on central atomThe NH₃ (1.5 D) versus NF₃ (0.2 D) dipole puzzle solved🟡 Similarc1
Concept The net dipole moment of a molecule is the vector sum of the individual bond moments plus the lone-pair moment. In a pyramidal AB3 molecule the lone pair always points away from the three bonds, so its contribution either reinforces the resultant bond moment (when the central atom is the positive end of each bond) or opposes it (when the central atom is the negative end). NH3 and NF3 are the classic pair where this sign flip happens: N is less electronegative than F but more electronegative than H, so the bond moments reverse direction while the lone pair stays put.
Key relation μnet = μlone pair ± Σ μbond. Measured values: NF3 0.24 D < H2S 0.97 D < CHCl3 1.04 D < NH3 1.47 D < H2O 1.85 D in NH₃ the lone pair and the three N←H moments point the same way and add; in NF₃ the three N→F moments point opposite to the lone pair and largely cancel it.
Solution
1. All five molecules are polar (none is symmetric), so we must rank rather than eliminate.
2. H2O (bent, 104.5°): two strongly polar O–H moments plus two lone pairs all resolve in the same direction ⇒ large, 1.85 D.
3. NH3 (pyramidal): three N–H moments point from H toward N, the same way the lone pair points ⇒ they add ⇒ 1.47 D.
4. CHCl3: three C–Cl moments resolve along the C–H axis and are only partly opposed by the small C–H moment ⇒ 1.04 D.
5. H2S: bent like water, but the S–H bond is barely polar (Δχ ≈ 0.4) and the angle is ≈92° ⇒ 0.97 D.
6. NF3: the three N–F moments point from N toward F, i.e. downward, directly against the upward lone-pair moment ⇒ near-total cancellation ⇒ 0.24 D, the lowest. So X = NF3.
7. Now count lone pairs on the central atom of NF3: N has 5 valence electrons, three are used in N–F σ bonds, leaving one lone pair (SN = 3 + 1 = 4, sp³, pyramidal, ≈102°).
8. Answer (c) 1. Eliminate (b) 0 — that would make NF3 planar and non-polar; eliminate (a) 2 and (d) 3 — those would be the counts for a bent O/S centre and for a terminal halogen respectively.
 molecule   shape        bond moment direction        lone pair adds?   mu (D)
 H2O        bent         O is -ve end, both O-H       yes, 2 lp         1.85
 NH3        pyramidal    N is -ve end (H -> N)        YES, reinforces   1.47
 CHCl3      tetrahedral  C -> Cl, partly opposed      n/a (0 lp)        1.04
 H2S        bent         weakly polar S-H             yes but small     0.97
 NF3        pyramidal    N is +ve end (N -> F)        NO, OPPOSES       0.24  <-- X, lowest

 X = NF3 :  N valence 5 - 3 (used in sigma) = 2 e- left = 1 lone pair   <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick One sentence settles every "lowest dipole" question of this family: if the central atom is the positive end of its bonds and still carries a lone pair, the lone pair fights the bond moments and μ collapses. That is NF3 (0.24 D), and the same logic makes NCl3 ≈ 0.6 D. Distractor (b) "0" is planted for students who over-apply the cancellation and conclude NF3 is non-polar — it is nearly zero, not zero, and the question asks for lone pairs anyway, not μ. Distractor (a) "2" catches anyone who picked H2S as X.
Answer: (c) 1
8(2026 Level-4) Identify the molecule (X) with maximum number of lone pairs of electrons (obtained using Lewis dot structure) among HNO3, H2SO4, NF3 and O3. Choose the correct bond angle made by the central atom of the molecule (X). (a) 120° (b) 107° (c) 102° (d) 116°Counting lone pairs from Lewis structures; bond angle of NF₃Bond angle order in NH₃, NCl₃ and NF₃ — why NF₃ falls to ≈102°🟡 Similarc102°
Concept The question is a two-stage trap. Stage one asks for the total number of lone pairs in the whole Lewis structure — not just on the central atom — so highly electronegative terminal atoms (F with three lone pairs each) dominate the count even though the molecule looks small. Stage two then asks for the bond angle at the central atom, which for a pyramidal sp³ nitrogen is set by how hard the lone pair squeezes the bond pairs, and how much the electronegative substituent pulls bonding density away from nitrogen.
Key relation Lone pairs available on a neutral atom in a molecule: lp = (V − bonds used)/2, so F gives 3, a doubly bonded O gives 2, a singly bonded −O(H) gives 2, an O⁻ gives 3. For bond angles: lp–bp repulsion > bp–bp repulsion and more electronegative substituent ⇒ bond pair pulled away ⇒ smaller angle this is Bent's rule in its simplest form and it is why NH₃ 107° > NCl₃ 107° > NF₃ 102°.
Solution
1. HNO3: structure HO–N(=O)–O⁻ with N⁺. Lone pairs: the –OH oxygen 2, the =O oxygen 2, the O⁻ 3, nitrogen 0 ⇒ 7.
2. H2SO4: (HO)2S(=O)2. Lone pairs: each –OH oxygen 2 (total 4), each =O oxygen 2 (total 4), sulfur 0 ⇒ 8.
3. NF3: each fluorine keeps 3 lone pairs ⇒ 9, plus 1 on nitrogen ⇒ 10. ✔ maximum
4. O3: central O 1, the =O terminal 2, the O⁻ terminal 3 ⇒ 6.
5. Maximum is NF3 with 10 lone pairs ⇒ X = NF3. The three fluorines alone contribute nine — this is why a "small" molecule beats sulfuric acid.
6. Geometry of NF3: SN = 3 σ + 1 lp = 4 ⇒ sp³, trigonal pyramidal. The ideal 109.5° is compressed twice — once by lp–bp repulsion, and again because the highly electronegative F pulls each bond pair far from N, weakening bp–bp repulsion.
7. Observed F–N–F angle = 102° (102.5°) ⇒ option (c).
8. Eliminate: (a) 120° is trigonal planar (BF3, NO3−); (b) 107° is NH3, planted for anyone who assumes all pyramidal nitrogens are alike; (d) 116° is the O–N–O angle of a nitro group and belongs to HNO3, the decoy X.
 molecule    lone pairs, atom by atom                                total
 HNO3        HO-O 2  +  =O 2  +  O(-) 3  +  N 0                        7
 H2SO4       2 x (HO-O 2) = 4  +  2 x (=O 2) = 4  +  S 0               8
 NF3         3 x F 3 = 9  +  N 1                                      10   <-- X, maximum
 O3          central O 1  +  =O 2  +  O(-) 3                           6

 X = NF3 :  SN = 3 sigma + 1 lp = 4  ->  sp3, trigonal pyramidal
            109.5 deg ideal
            - lp/bp repulsion            compresses
            - F pulls bond pairs away    compresses further
            observed F-N-F = 102 deg                                  <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick When a question says "maximum number of lone pairs", count every atom, not just the central one — and remember each halogen contributes 3. A quick shortcut: total lone pairs = (total valence electrons − 2 × number of bonds)/2, which for NF3 gives (26 − 6)/2 = 10 in one line. For the angle, bank the ladder NH3 107° > NCl3 107° > NF3 102°: more electronegative substituent ⇒ smaller angle. The examiner offers 107° precisely because most students remember "pyramidal nitrogen = 107°" without asking which substituent is attached.
Answer: (c) 102°
9(2026 Level-4) Which statements are NOT TRUE about XeO2F2? (A) It has a see-saw shape. (B) Xe has 5 electron pairs in its valence shell in XeO2F2. (C) The O–Xe–O bond angle is close to 180°. (D) The F–Xe–F bond angle is close to 180°. (E) Xe has 16 valence electrons in XeO2F2. Choose the correct answer from the options given below. (a) B, C and E only (b) B and D only (c) A and D only (d) B, D and E onlyStructure of XeO₂F₂ — see-saw geometrysp³d / sp³d² / sp³d³ shapes — PCl₅, SF₄ see-saw, BrF₃, XeF₄, IF₇🟡 SimilaraB, C and E only
Concept XeO2F2 is the xenon analogue of SF4. Xenon supplies eight valence electrons; each F takes one in a σ bond and each O takes two in a Xe=O double bond, leaving one pair on Xe. Counting σ domains gives a steric number of 5 ⇒ trigonal-bipyramidal arrangement, and since a lone pair always occupies an equatorial site (only two 90° repulsions there instead of three), the shape is see-saw. The doubly bonded oxygens, being the fattest domains, also take equatorial positions, which forces the two fluorines into the two axial sites.
Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs = 4 + 1 = 5 ⇒ sp³d; lone pair and π-rich double bonds ⇒ equatorial; σ-only ligands ⇒ axial. Total electron pairs in the valence shell (counting both pairs of each double bond): 2 (Xe–F) + 4 (2 × Xe=O) + 1 (lp) = 7 pairs = 14 electrons steric number counts domains, not pairs — one Xe=O is a single domain but two electron pairs.
Solution
1. Build the structure: Xe centre, equatorial = 2 O (double bonds) + 1 lone pair; axial = 2 F. Ideal angles distort to O–Xe–O ≈ 105°, F–Xe–F ≈ 180°, O–Xe–F ≈ 90°.
2. (A) See-saw shape — with 4 σ domains and 1 equatorial lone pair on a trigonal bipyramid, the shape is exactly see-saw, as in SF4. TRUE, so A is not in the answer.
3. (B) Xe has 5 electron pairs — 5 is the steric number (number of domains), but the number of electron pairs is 2 + 4 + 1 = 7. The statement confuses domains with pairs ⇒ NOT TRUE ✔
4. (C) O–Xe–O ≈ 180° — both oxygens are equatorial, separated by roughly 105° (compressed from 120° by the lone pair between them). Nowhere near 180° ⇒ NOT TRUE ✔
5. (D) F–Xe–F ≈ 180° — the two fluorines occupy the two axial sites, which are collinear through Xe (slightly bent to ≈174° by the equatorial lone pair). TRUE, so D is not in the answer.
6. (E) Xe has 16 valence electrons — 7 pairs = 14 electrons, not 16. 16 would need 8 pairs, i.e. one more bond than the molecule has. ⇒ NOT TRUE ✔
7. NOT TRUE = B, C and E ⇒ option (a). Eliminate (b), (c) and (d) — each of them wrongly includes D (or A), both of which are correct statements.
 XeO2F2 :  Xe valence 8 e-;  2 Xe-F sigma, 2 Xe=O double, 1 lone pair
 arrangement = trigonal bipyramidal (SN 5, sp3d);  shape = SEE-SAW

               O                    equatorial : O, O, lone pair
               |                    axial      : F, F
          F -- Xe -- F  (axial, ~180 deg)
               |
               O            O-Xe-O ~ 105 deg (equatorial)

 statement                                   value in the real molecule    verdict
 (A) see-saw shape                           see-saw                       TRUE
 (B) 5 electron pairs on Xe                  7 pairs (2+4+1)               NOT TRUE  <--
 (C) O-Xe-O close to 180                     ~105 deg (both equatorial)    NOT TRUE  <--
 (D) F-Xe-F close to 180                     ~174-180 deg (both axial)     TRUE
 (E) Xe has 16 valence electrons             14 e- (7 pairs)               NOT TRUE  <--
                                                        B, C, E            <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Draw the see-saw once and label which atoms sit axial and which equatorial — every angle question then answers itself: axial–central–axial ≈ 180°, equatorial–central–equatorial < 120°. The examiner's real weapon here is (B): steric number ≠ number of electron pairs whenever double bonds are present. SN counts domains (5); pairs count electrons (7). Anyone who equates them ticks (c) or (d) and loses the mark. Cross-check with (E): 7 pairs must mean 14 electrons, so B and E stand or fall together.
Answer: (a) B, C and E only
10(2026 Level-4) Given below are statements about some molecules/ions. Identify the CORRECT statements. (A) The dipole moment value of NF3 is higher than that of NH3. (B) The dipole moment value of BeH2 is zero. (C) The bond order of O22− and F2 is same. (D) The formal charge on the central oxygen atom of ozone is −1. (E) In NO2, all the three atoms satisfy the octet rule, hence it is very stable. Choose the correct answer from the options given below : (a) A, B, C, D & E (b) B & C only (c) B, C & D only (d) A, C & D onlyDipole moment, bond order, formal charge, octet ruleFormal charge in chemical bonding — full class-11 lesson with worked examples🟡 SimilarbB & C only
Concept Five unrelated micro-facts are bundled together, so the winning strategy is to test each one against a single reliable rule rather than to reason about the options. The rules needed are: (i) the lone-pair/bond-moment competition that flips μ between NH3 and NF3; (ii) symmetry ⇒ zero dipole for a linear AB2; (iii) B.O. = ½(Nb − Na) for isoelectronic species; (iv) the formal-charge formula; (v) the odd-electron exception to the octet rule.
Key relation F.C. = V − L − ½B where V = valence electrons, L = lone-pair electrons, B = bonding electrons applied to the central O of ozone this gives 6 − 2 − ½(6) = +1, not −1. And isoelectronic ⇒ identical bond order, which settles O22− vs F2 instantly.
Solution
1. (A) μ(NF3) = 0.24 D and μ(NH3) = 1.47 D. In NH3 the lone pair reinforces the bond moments; in NF3 it opposes them. So NF3 is lower, not higher ⇒ FALSE.
2. (B) BeH2 has Be with 2 σ bonds and no lone pair ⇒ SN 2 ⇒ sp, linear H–Be–H. The two equal Be–H moments point in exactly opposite directions and cancel ⇒ μ = 0 ⇒ TRUE ✔
3. (C) O22− has 18 valence electrons: B.O. = ½(10 − 8) = 1. F2 also has 18: B.O. = ½(10 − 8) = 1. They are isoelectronic, so of course they match ⇒ TRUE ✔
4. (D) Ozone is O=O⁺–O⁻. For the central O: V = 6, L = 2 (one lone pair), B = 6 (a double bond and a single bond) ⇒ F.C. = 6 − 2 − 3 = +1. The −1 sits on the singly bonded terminal oxygen ⇒ FALSE.
5. (E) NO2 has 5 + 6 + 6 = 17 valence electrons — an odd number, so complete pairing is impossible and nitrogen is left with 7 electrons. It is an odd-electron species that dimerises to N2O4 precisely because it is not stable ⇒ FALSE on both counts.
6. Correct statements are B and C only ⇒ option (b). Eliminate (a) and (d) since A fails; eliminate (c) since D fails.
 statement                                          check                              verdict
 (A) mu(NF3) > mu(NH3)            0.24 D vs 1.47 D -- reversed                          FALSE
 (B) mu(BeH2) = 0                 sp, linear H-Be-H, moments cancel                     TRUE   <--
 (C) B.O.(O2 2-) = B.O.(F2)       both 18 e-, both 1/2(10-8) = 1                        TRUE   <--
 (D) F.C. on central O of O3      6 - 2 - (6/2) = +1, not -1                            FALSE
 (E) NO2 obeys octet, very stable 17 e- (odd), N has 7; dimerises to N2O4               FALSE
                                                              B and C only              <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn ozone's charge distribution as a picture, not a calculation: central O is +1, the singly bonded terminal O is −1, the doubly bonded terminal O is 0 — and the molecule is neutral overall, so the signs must cancel. Statement (D) simply moves the −1 onto the wrong atom. For (E), the fastest filter for "does it obey the octet" is to add up the valence electrons and look for an odd total: 17 for NO2, 19 for NO, 7 for CH3• — an odd total makes the octet impossible, no structure drawing required.
Answer: (b) B & C only
11(2026 Level-4) Match List-I with List-II according to shape. List-I: (A) XeO3, (B) XeF2, (C) XeO2F2, (D) XeOF4. List-II: (I) BrF3, (II) NH3, (III) [I3]−, (IV) SF4. Choose the correct answer from the options given below : (a) A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV (b) A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-I (c) A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV (d) A-III, B-II, C-IV, D-IShapes of xenon compounds — VSEPR matchingsp³d² and sp³d³ shapes compared — SF₆, XeF₆, XeF₄, IF₇🟡 SimilarbA-II, B-III, C-IV, D-I
Concept Every entry is solved by the same three-step VSEPR routine: count σ bonds, count lone pairs on the central atom, add them to get the steric number, then remove the lone-pair positions from the parent electron geometry to read off the molecular shape. Crucially, a double bond counts as one domain, so Xe=O contributes exactly as much to the steric number as a Xe–F single bond does. That is why XeO3 behaves like NH3 and XeO2F2 behaves like SF4.
Key relation lp on Xe = (8 − electrons Xe donates to bonds)/2 — each F takes 1, each O takes 2. Then SN 4 with 1 lp ⇒ pyramidal; SN 5 with 3 lp ⇒ linear; SN 5 with 1 lp ⇒ see-saw; SN 6 with 1 lp ⇒ square pyramidal lone pairs always take equatorial sites in a trigonal bipyramid and mutually trans sites in an octahedron.
Solution
1. (A) XeO3: three Xe=O take 6 of Xe's 8 electrons ⇒ 1 lone pair; SN = 3 + 1 = 4 ⇒ sp³ ⇒ trigonal pyramidal. The List-II species with the same shape is NH3 (II) — 3 bp + 1 lp, pyramidal. ⇒ A–II
2. (B) XeF2: two Xe–F take 2 electrons ⇒ 3 lone pairs; SN = 2 + 3 = 5 ⇒ sp³d, all three lone pairs equatorial ⇒ linear. In List-II, [I3]− (III) is the textbook isostructural partner — 2 bp + 3 lp, linear. ⇒ B–III
3. (C) XeO2F2: two Xe=O (4 e⁻) + two Xe–F (2 e⁻) = 6 ⇒ 1 lone pair; SN = 4 + 1 = 5 ⇒ sp³d with an equatorial lone pair ⇒ see-saw. That is exactly SF4 (IV). ⇒ C–IV
4. Three matches are now fixed, and only option (b) contains all three (A–II, B–III, C–IV). The remaining pairing D–I follows by elimination.
5. Eliminate the rest: (a) puts B with BrF3 (T-shaped, not linear) and C with [I3]−; (c) puts C with BrF3; (d) puts A with [I3]−, i.e. pyramidal XeO3 matched to a linear ion — all fail on the first check.
⚠ Note Strictly, XeOF4 has 5 σ domains + 1 lone pair (SN 6, sp³d²) and is square pyramidal — the same shape as BrF5, not as BrF3, which is T-shaped (SN 5, 3 bp + 2 lp). List-II offers no square-pyramidal species, so D–I is a leftover pairing rather than a genuine shape match. Our derivation of the first three rows is unambiguous and singles out option (b), which agrees with the printed key, so the answer letter is unaffected.
 List-I       sigma   lp on Xe   SN   hybrid   shape                 List-II partner        shape of partner
 (A) XeO3       3         1       4   sp3      trigonal pyramidal    (II)  NH3              pyramidal      OK
 (B) XeF2       2         3       5   sp3d     linear                (III) [I3]-            linear         OK
 (C) XeO2F2     4         1       5   sp3d     see-saw               (IV)  SF4              see-saw        OK
 (D) XeOF4      5         1       6   sp3d2    square pyramidal      (I)   BrF3             T-shaped       by elimination

 A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-I                                                                     <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick For any Xe compound, get the lone-pair count in one line: lp = (8 − F count − 2 × O count)/2. XeO3 → (8 − 0 − 6)/2 = 1; XeF2 → (8 − 2 − 0)/2 = 3; XeO2F2 → (8 − 2 − 4)/2 = 1; XeOF4 → (8 − 4 − 2)/2 = 1. Then SN = σ + lp and the shape falls out. In a four-row match, you almost never need all four rows — nail the two you are surest of (here B–III linear and C–IV see-saw) and the options collapse to one. That also protects you when, as here, the fourth pairing is imperfect.
Answer: (b) A-II, B-III, C-IV, D-I
12(2026 Level-3) The correct increasing order of C–H(A), C–O(B), C=O(C) and C≡N(D) bonds in terms of covalent bond length is: (a) A < B < C < D (b) A < D < C < B (c) D < C < B < A (d) D < C < A < BBond length ordering — bond order and atomic sizeArranging C–C, C=C and C≡C by bond length and bond energy🟡 SimilarbA < D < C < B
Concept Bond length is governed by two competing factors. First, the sizes of the two atoms — a bond to hydrogen, the smallest atom of all, is always short. Second, the bond order — every extra π bond pulls the nuclei closer, so for the same pair of atoms triple < double < single. Here the four bonds mix both effects: A involves tiny H, while B, C and D all involve a second-period p-block partner (O or N) at different bond orders, so you must rank by bond order among them and slot the C–H bond in by size.
Key relation bond length ∝ 1 / bond order (for a fixed atom pair) and d ≈ r1 + r2. Typical values: C–H ≈ 109 pm, C≡N ≈ 116 pm, C=O ≈ 122 pm, C–O ≈ 143 pm r(H) is only 37 pm, so even a single C–H bond beats a carbon–nitrogen triple bond.
Solution
1. (A) C–H: covalent radii 77 + 37 = 114 pm, observed ≈ 109 pm. Hydrogen has no core electrons at all, so this is the shortest bond in the set.
2. (D) C≡N: bond order 3, and nitrogen is small ⇒ ≈ 116 pm (as in HCN and nitriles). Two π bonds contract it strongly.
3. (C) C=O: bond order 2 ⇒ ≈ 122 pm (carbonyl group, 121 pm in formaldehyde). One π bond only, so it cannot contract as far as the triple bond.
4. (B) C–O: bond order 1 ⇒ ≈ 143 pm (ethers, alcohols). No π contraction at all ⇒ the longest of the four.
5. Increasing order: 109 < 116 < 122 < 143, i.e. A < D < C < B ⇒ option (b).
6. Eliminate: (a) A < B < C < D places the single C–O below the double and triple bonds — it reverses the bond-order rule entirely; (c) and (d) both start from D, which would require the triple bond to be shorter than C–H, ignoring how small hydrogen is. (c) is simply the exact reverse of a decreasing-order answer — a classic reading trap when the question says "increasing".
 label   bond     bond order   partner atom radius (pm)   length (pm)   rank
 (A)     C-H          1          H  = 37                     109         1st (shortest)
 (D)     C(tri)N      3          N  = 75                     116         2nd
 (C)     C=O          2          O  = 73                     122         3rd
 (B)     C-O          1          O  = 73                     143         4th (longest)

 increasing:  109  <  116  <  122  <  143
                A  <   D   <   C   <   B                       <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Two rules, applied in this order, solve every bond-length ranking. (1) Anything bonded to hydrogen is shortest — H has no inner shell, so C–H (109 pm), N–H (101 pm), O–H (96 pm) all beat heavy-atom multiple bonds. (2) Within one atom pair, length falls as bond order rises — single > double > triple. Note that C–O and C=O straddle C≡N precisely because bond order outranks the small radius difference between N and O. Finally, always underline "increasing" or "decreasing" before you look at the options: (c) here is the perfect reverse of the correct sequence and is placed there to catch exactly that slip.
Answer: (b) A < D < C < B
13(2026 Level-3) The formal charges on the atoms marked as (1) to (4) in the Lewis representation of the HNO₃ molecule respectively are:  H—Ö⁽¹⁾—N⁽²⁾=Ö⁽³⁾ with a third oxygen :Ö:⁽⁴⁾ singly bonded below N.  (a) +1, 0, 0, −1  (b) 0, −1, 0, +1  (c) 0, +1, 0, −1  (d) 0, 0, −1, +1Formal chargeFormal charge on an atom — the V, L and S counts explained🟡 Similarc0, +1, 0, −1
Concept Formal charge is a bookkeeping device, not a real charge. You pretend every covalent bond is split perfectly evenly, hand one electron of each shared pair to each partner, and then ask: does this atom now hold more or fewer electrons than a free neutral atom of that element? The whole point is that it tells you which resonance structure is best and where the charge sits in species like HNO₃, where nitrogen is famously forced to carry +1 because a second-period atom cannot expand its octet to make two N=O double bonds.
Formula FC = V − L − ½ B where V = valence electrons of the free atom, L = electrons in lone pairs on that atom, B = total electrons in bonds to that atom. Equivalently FC = V − L − (number of bonds), since each bond contributes 2 to B. A cross-check that never fails: Σ FC over all atoms = overall charge on the species HNO₃ is neutral, so the four formal charges must add to 0 — this alone kills two options.
Solution
1. First fix the connectivity actually drawn: H—O⁽¹⁾—N⁽²⁾, N⁽²⁾=O⁽³⁾, and N⁽²⁾—O⁽⁴⁾ where O⁽⁴⁾ carries three lone pairs.
2. O⁽¹⁾ (the —OH oxygen): V = 6, two σ bonds (to H and to N) so bonds = 2, and 2 lone pairs so L = 4. FC = 6 − 4 − 2 = 0.
3. N⁽²⁾: V = 5, four bonds in all (one to O⁽¹⁾, two in the double bond to O⁽³⁾, one to O⁽⁴⁾), no lone pair so L = 0. FC = 5 − 0 − 4 = +1. Nitrogen is period-2: only four orbitals, so four bonds is its ceiling and it must pay for the fourth with a positive formal charge.
4. O⁽³⁾ (the doubly-bonded oxygen): V = 6, bonds = 2, L = 4. FC = 6 − 4 − 2 = 0.
5. O⁽⁴⁾ (the singly-bonded oxygen with three lone pairs): V = 6, bonds = 1, L = 6. FC = 6 − 6 − 1 = −1.
6. Sequence is 0, +1, 0, −1 ⇒ option (c). Eliminate: (a) puts +1 on the OH oxygen, impossible for a two-bond two-lone-pair oxygen; (b) and (d) both put a negative charge on nitrogen, which contradicts N having four bonds.
 atom   V    lone e (L)   bonds   FC = V − L − bonds
 O(1)   6        4          2        6 − 4 − 2 =  0
 N(2)   5        0          4        5 − 0 − 4 = +1
 O(3)   6        4          2        6 − 4 − 2 =  0
 O(4)   6        6          1        6 − 6 − 1 = −1
 -------------------------------------------------
 sum                                        0  (neutral HNO₃ ✓)
                                  0, +1, 0, −1  <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the two oxygen patterns and you never compute again: 2 bonds + 2 lone pairs → FC 0; 1 bond + 3 lone pairs → FC −1; 3 bonds + 1 lone pair → FC +1. Then note that in every neutral oxyacid/oxyanion of N (HNO₃, NO₃⁻, NO₂⁺) the nitrogen carries +1 the moment it shows four bonds. Trap: students count the N=O double bond as "one bond" and get FC(N) = 5 − 3 = +2, or forget that the octet rule bars N from making five bonds and try to draw a charge-free structure. The sum rule (must total 0) is the fastest single filter here.
Answer: (c) 0, +1, 0, −1.
14(2026 Level-3) Among the following, the CORRECT combinations are: (A) IF₃ → T-shaped (sp³d)  (B) IF₅ → square pyramidal (sp³d²)  (C) IF₇ → pentagonal bipyramidal (sp³d³)  (D) ClO₄⁻ → square planar (sp²d). Choose the correct answer: (a) A, B and C only  (b) A and B only  (c) A, B, C and D  (d) B, C and D onlyInterhalogen shapes & hybridisationsp³d² hybridisation worked out for SF₆, IF₅ and XeF₄🟡 SimilaraA, B and C only
Concept Every one of these four is decided by a single number — the steric number (SN) of the central atom, i.e. σ-bonds + lone pairs. SN fixes the electron-pair geometry and the hybridisation in one step; the lone pairs then carve the molecular shape out of that geometry by occupying the least-crowded positions. The trap the examiner has built in is that three of the four species are iodine interhalogens (where I genuinely expands its octet using 5d orbitals) but the fourth, ClO₄⁻, is an oxyanion where the central atom has no lone pair at all and cannot be square planar.
Key relation SN = (number of σ-bonded atoms) + (number of lone pairs), and SN = 4 → sp³ (tetrahedral); 5 → sp³d (TBP); 6 → sp³d² (octahedral); 7 → sp³d³ (pentagonal bipyramidal). Lone pairs on a TBP always go equatorial; on an octahedron they go trans to each other. Square planar needs SN = 6 with exactly 2 lone pairs — a condition ClO₄⁻ can never meet.
Solution
1. (A) IF₃. I has 7 valence e⁻; 3 are used in I—F bonds, leaving 4 e⁻ = 2 lone pairs. SN = 3 + 2 = 5 ⇒ sp³d, trigonal-bipyramidal electron geometry. Both lone pairs sit equatorial, leaving two axial F and one equatorial F ⇒ T-shaped. CORRECT.
2. (B) IF₅. 7 − 5 = 2 e⁻ = 1 lone pair. SN = 5 + 1 = 6 ⇒ sp³d², octahedral geometry; one vertex taken by the lone pair ⇒ square pyramidal. CORRECT.
3. (C) IF₇. 7 − 7 = 0 lone pairs. SN = 7 + 0 = 7 ⇒ sp³d³ ⇒ pentagonal bipyramidal. CORRECT. IF₇ is the textbook example of the maximum covalency of iodine.
4. (D) ClO₄⁻. Four σ Cl—O bonds and zero lone pairs on Cl ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp³, tetrahedral — exactly like SO₄²⁻ and PO₄³⁻. The claim "square planar (sp²d)" is wrong on both counts. INCORRECT.
5. Therefore A, B, C are right and D is wrong ⇒ option (a). Eliminate (b) — it drops the perfectly correct IF₇ entry; (c) and (d) both keep the false ClO₄⁻ entry.
 species    σ    lp   SN   hybrid   e-geometry        shape                verdict
 IF₃        3     2    5   sp³d     trig. bipyramid   T-shaped              ✓ (A)
 IF₅        5     1    6   sp³d²    octahedral        square pyramidal      ✓ (B)
 IF₇        7     0    7   sp³d³    pent. bipyramid   pent. bipyramidal     ✓ (C)
 ClO₄⁻      4     0    4   sp³      tetrahedral       tetrahedral (NOT sq.pl.) ✗ (D)
 -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
                                                    A, B and C only  <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick For an interhalogen XYn the lone pairs on X are simply (7 − n)/2 — so XY₃ has 2 lp (T-shaped), XY₅ has 1 lp (square pyramidal), XY₇ has 0 lp (pentagonal bipyramidal). Learn that ladder once and A, B, C are answered in five seconds. Trap: "square planar" is a magnet distractor because XeF₄ is square planar — but XeF₄ has SN 6 with two lone pairs, while ClO₄⁻ has SN 4 with none. Also note sp²d is a dsp²-type label that belongs to coordination complexes, never to a simple tetrahedral oxyanion.
Answer: (a) A, B and C only.
15(2026 Level-3) Given below are two statements: Statement I: The increasing order of boiling point of hydrogen halides is HCl < HBr < HI < HF. Statement II: The increasing order of melting point of hydrogen halides is HCl < HBr < HF < HI. Choose the correct option: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are true  (b) Statement I is true but Statement II is false  (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are false  (d) Statement I is false but Statement II is trueH-bonding vs van der Waals in HXHydrogen bonding in HF and its effect on boiling point (NCERT)🟡 SimilaraBoth Statement I and Statement II are true
Concept Two competing intermolecular forces run through the HX series in opposite directions. Going HCl → HBr → HI the molecules get bigger and more polarisable, so London dispersion forces rise steadily and so do the transition temperatures. HF breaks the pattern because fluorine's small size and huge electronegativity switch on strong intermolecular hydrogen bonding (zig-zag F—H⋯F chains), which lifts HF far above HCl and HBr. The subtlety the examiner is testing is that H-bonding lifts the boiling point of HF above even HI, but does not lift its melting point above HI — because melting also depends on how well the molecules pack in the lattice, and heavy HI has the larger dispersion energy in the solid.
Data Boiling points: HCl −85 °C < HBr −67 °C < HI −35 °C < HF +19.5 °C. Melting points: HCl −114 °C < HBr −87 °C < HF −83.6 °C < HI −50.8 °C. Note HF is the top of the bp list but only the third of four on the mp list — that single fact is the whole question.
Solution
1. Statement I. Read off the bp column: HCl (−85) < HBr (−67) < HI (−35) < HF (+19.5). This is exactly the printed order ⇒ Statement I is TRUE.
2. Statement II. Read off the mp column: HCl (−114) < HBr (−87) < HF (−83.6) < HI (−50.8). Again exactly the printed order ⇒ Statement II is TRUE. In the solid, HF's hydrogen-bonded chains are already a low-density zig-zag; HI's much larger dispersion attraction wins, so HI melts highest.
3. Both statements are true ⇒ option (a). Eliminate (b), (c) and (d) — each of them calls at least one true statement false.
 HX     m.p. / °C     b.p. / °C     dominant force
 HF       −83.6         +19.5       H-bonding (F—H···F chains)
 HCl     −114.2         −85.1       weak dipole + small dispersion
 HBr      −86.9         −66.8       larger dispersion
 HI       −50.8         −35.4       largest dispersion
 ------------------------------------------------------------
 b.p. order :  HCl < HBr < HI < HF     -> Statement I  TRUE
 m.p. order :  HCl < HBr < HF < HI     -> Statement II TRUE
                                        both true  <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Store one sentence: "HF tops the boiling points but only reaches third in the melting points." Everything else in both lists is just size order HCl < HBr < HI. Trap: the paper is betting you will assume H-bonding must make HF highest in both lists and mark (b). It also bets you will forget that HCl — not HF — has the lowest mp and bp of all four, because HCl is the one with neither strong H-bonding nor large dispersion.
Answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are true.
16(JEE Main 2025, 22 Jan Shift 2 · Level-5) Arrange the following compounds in increasing order of their dipole moment: HBr, H₂S, NF₃ and CHCl₃.  (a) H₂S < HBr < NF₃ < CHCl₃  (b) NF₃ < HBr < H₂S < CHCl₃  (c) HBr < H₂S < NF₃ < CHCl₃  (d) CHCl₃ < NF₃ < HBr < H₂SDipole moment orderingOrdering molecules by polarity and net dipole moment🟡 SimilarbNF₃ < HBr < H₂S < CHCl₃
Concept The net dipole moment is the vector sum of all bond moments plus the lone-pair moment, so geometry matters at least as much as electronegativity difference. NF₃ is the classic casualty: N—F bonds are strongly polar, but each bond moment points away from N toward F while the nitrogen lone-pair moment points the opposite way, and the two nearly cancel, leaving a famously tiny μ. CHCl₃, by contrast, has three C—Cl moments whose resultant points the same way as the C—H moment, so they reinforce. The diatomic HBr has a single moderate bond moment and nothing to cancel it, and bent H₂S has two small S—H moments plus a lone-pair contribution that survive partial cancellation.
Data μ(NF₃) ≈ 0.23 D < μ(HBr) ≈ 0.79 D < μ(H₂S) ≈ 0.97 D < μ(CHCl₃) ≈ 1.04 D Compare μ(NH₃) ≈ 1.47 D — same shape as NF₃ but the lone pair now adds to the bond moments instead of opposing them.
Solution
1. NF₃ — pyramidal, sp³. Bond moments N→F point down and outward; lone-pair moment points up. Near-cancellation ⇒ μ ≈ 0.23 D, the smallest by a wide margin. This alone fixes NF₃ as first and kills options (a), (c) and (d).
2. HBr — linear diatomic, Δχ(Br−H) ≈ 0.7, so a moderate single moment with no geometric cancellation ⇒ μ ≈ 0.79 D.
3. H₂S — bent (≈92°). Each S—H moment is small (Δχ ≈ 0.4) but the very sharp angle means they cancel little, and the two lone pairs add a further component ⇒ μ ≈ 0.97 D, just above HBr. A small bond angle is a dipole amplifier: the more acute the angle, the larger the resultant of two equal bond moments.
4. CHCl₃ — tetrahedral with three C—Cl bond moments summing along the C—H axis, reinforced rather than cancelled ⇒ μ ≈ 1.04 D, the largest here.
5. Increasing order NF₃ < HBr < H₂S < CHCl₃ ⇒ option (b).
 molecule   shape          μ / D    why
 NF₃        pyramidal      0.23     lone-pair moment OPPOSES the N→F moments
 HBr        linear         0.79     one moderate bond moment, nothing cancels
 H₂S        bent (≈92°)    0.97     small moments but sharp angle + 2 lone pairs
 CHCl₃      tetrahedral    1.04     three C→Cl moments REINFORCE along C—H
 ----------------------------------------------------------------------
 NF₃ < HBr < H₂S < CHCl₃                              <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick When NF₃ appears in any dipole-moment list, put it at the bottom immediately — the lone-pair-versus-bond-moment cancellation makes it the standard "surprisingly non-polar polar molecule". That one placement is usually enough to pick the answer without ranking the rest. Trap: candidates rank by electronegativity difference alone (N—F is the most polar bond here) and put NF₃ at the top, walking straight into option (c) or (d). Remember also that CHCl₃ > CH₂Cl₂ is false but CHCl₃ > CCl₄ = 0 is always true — symmetry, not bond polarity, is the decider.
Answer: (b) NF₃ < HBr < H₂S < CHCl₃.
17(JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 1 · Level-5) Which statements are true with respect to H₂O, NH₃ and CH₄? (A) The central atoms of all the molecules are sp³ hybridized. (B) The H—O—H, H—N—H and H—C—H angles are 104.5°, 107.5° and 109.5° respectively. (C) The increasing order of dipole moment is CH₄ < NH₃ < H₂O. (D) Both H₂O and NH₃ are Lewis acids and CH₄ is a Lewis base. (E) A solution of NH₃ in H₂O is basic; in this solution NH₃ and H₂O act as Lowry–Brønsted acid and base respectively.  (a) A, B and C only  (b) A, D and E only  (c) C, D and E only  (d) A, B, C and E onlysp³ hydrides — angle, dipole, acid–baseWhy CH₄, NH₃ and H₂O are all sp³ yet have different bond angles🟡 SimilaraA, B and C only
Concept CH₄, NH₃ and H₂O form the canonical isoelectronic sp³ series: all three have steric number 4, but with 0, 1 and 2 lone pairs respectively. Each added lone pair squeezes the bonding pairs harder (lp–lp > lp–bp > bp–bp repulsion), so the angle falls in ~2.5° steps, and each added lone pair also gives the molecule a bigger permanent dipole. The last two options move from structure to acid–base behaviour, and both are deliberately inverted: a molecule with a lone pair to donate is a Lewis base, and in aqueous ammonia it is water that hands over a proton, so water is the Brønsted acid.
Key relations Repulsion order: lp–lp > lp–bp > bp–bp ⇒ angle shrinks as lone pairs increase. Lewis base = electron-pair donor; Lewis acid = electron-pair acceptor. NH₃ + H₂O ⇌ NH₄⁺ + OH⁻ H₂O donates the proton, so H₂O is the Brønsted acid and NH₃ the Brønsted base — the exact reverse of what (E) claims.
Solution
1. (A) CH₄: SN = 4 + 0; NH₃: SN = 3 + 1; H₂O: SN = 2 + 2. All SN = 4 ⇒ all sp³. TRUE.
2. (B) Observed angles: H—O—H = 104.5°, H—N—H = 107.5° (often quoted 107°), H—C—H = 109.5°. Exactly as stated ⇒ TRUE. Two lone pairs on O compress hardest, one on N less, none on C leaves the ideal tetrahedral value.
3. (C) μ(CH₄) = 0 D (Td symmetry, all bond moments cancel) < μ(NH₃) = 1.47 D < μ(H₂O) = 1.85 D ⇒ TRUE.
4. (D) H₂O and NH₃ each carry lone pairs and donate them, so they are Lewis bases, not acids; CH₄ has no lone pair and no vacant low-lying orbital, so it is neither. Statement is doubly wrong ⇒ FALSE.
5. (E) The roles are swapped: in NH₃ + H₂O ⇌ NH₄⁺ + OH⁻, NH₃ accepts H⁺ (Brønsted base) and H₂O donates H⁺ (Brønsted acid) ⇒ FALSE, even though the opening clause "the solution is basic" is true. A statement is false if any part of it is false — the true lead-in is bait.
6. True set = {A, B, C} ⇒ option (a). (b) and (c) keep the false D; (d) keeps the false E.
 statement                                  verdict   reason
 A  all sp³                                   TRUE    SN = 4 for all three
 B  104.5°, 107.5°, 109.5°                    TRUE    lone-pair compression ladder
 C  μ: CH₄ < NH₃ < H₂O  (0 < 1.47 < 1.85 D)  TRUE    lone pairs add to μ
 D  H₂O, NH₃ Lewis acids; CH₄ Lewis base     FALSE    they are Lewis BASES
 E  NH₃ acid, H₂O base in aq. ammonia        FALSE    roles are the other way round
 -----------------------------------------------------------------------
                                     A, B and C only  <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick In any multi-statement question mixing structure with acid–base theory, attack the acid–base lines first — they are almost always the planted false ones, and killing one option-set collapses the choice instantly. Here the single word "acids" in (D) and the single word "respectively" in (E) do all the damage. Trap: (E) opens with a true fact (aqueous NH₃ is basic) to make you nod along past the reversed labels.
Answer: (a) A, B and C only.
18(JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 2 · Level-5) Given below are two statements: Statement (I): Experimentally determined oxygen–oxygen bond lengths in O₃ are found to be the same, and the bond length is greater than that of an O=O double bond but less than that of a single O—O bond. Statement (II): The strong lone pair–lone pair repulsion between oxygen atoms is solely responsible for the fact that the bond length in ozone is smaller than that of a double bond (O=O) but more than that of a single bond O—O.  (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are false  (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true  (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false  (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are trueResonance in ozoneResonance structures of NO₃⁻ — how delocalisation makes all bond lengths equal🟡 SimilarcStatement I is true but Statement II is false
Concept Ozone is the standard NCERT demonstration that a single Lewis structure is inadequate. Either canonical form shows one O=O and one O—O, which would predict two different bond lengths (≈121 pm and ≈148 pm). Experiment finds one length, 128 pm, intermediate between the two. The explanation is resonance: the real molecule is a hybrid in which the π electron pair is delocalised over both O—O linkages, giving each a bond order of 1.5. Lone-pair repulsion exists in O₃ and does affect the bond angle (117° rather than 120°), but it is emphatically not what equalises or shortens the bonds — so the word "solely" in Statement II makes it flatly wrong.
Key relation Bond order in O₃ = (1 + 2)/2 = 1.5 for each O—O linkage; higher bond order ⇒ shorter bond. O—O single ≈ 148 pm, O₃ ≈ 128 pm, O=O double ≈ 121 pm — the observed value sits neatly between, exactly as bond order 1.5 demands.
Solution
1. Statement I. Both O—O distances in O₃ are experimentally 127.8 pm — identical, and 121 < 128 < 148. So the bonds are equal, longer than a double bond and shorter than a single bond ⇒ TRUE exactly as written.
2. Statement II. It attributes the intermediate length "solely" to lone pair–lone pair repulsion. Repulsion between lone pairs would tend to lengthen and would never make both bonds identical; the true cause is delocalisation of the π pair (resonance), which raises the single bond to 1.5 and lowers the double bond to 1.5 ⇒ FALSE.
3. Statement I true, Statement II false ⇒ option (c). Eliminate (a) — I is demonstrably true; (b) and (d) both accept the false causal claim in II.
 bond                          length / pm    bond order
 O—O single (H₂O₂)                148            1
 O₃ (both linkages, observed)     128           1.5   <-- equal, intermediate
 O=O double (O₂)                  121            2
 -----------------------------------------------------------------
 cause of the 1.5 order : RESONANCE (π delocalisation), not lp–lp repulsion
 => I true, II false                                   <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick In assertion–reason and two-statement items, treat absolutist words — "solely", "only", "always", "entirely" — as red flags: a reason that claims to be the sole cause is almost always the false half. Here the correct one-word answer is "resonance", and lone-pair repulsion is a real effect pointed at the wrong observable (it explains the 117° angle, not the 128 pm length). Trap: because Statement II names a genuine phenomenon and quotes the correct bond-length inequality, it reads as plausible right up to the word "solely".
Answer: (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false.
19(JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 1 · Level-4) Which of the following linear combination of atomic orbitals will lead to formation of molecular orbitals in homonuclear diatomic molecules (internuclear axis in z-direction)? (A) 2pz and 2px  (B) 2s and 2px  (C) 3dxy and 3dx²−y²  (D) 2s and 2pz  (E) 2pz and 3dx²−y². Choose the correct answer: (a) A and B only  (b) D only  (c) C only  (d) C and D onlyLCAO symmetry condition2s overlapping 2px or 2py with z as the internuclear axis — why nothing happens🟡 SimilarbD only
Concept Two atomic orbitals can combine into molecular orbitals only if three conditions hold together: comparable energy, appreciable overlap, and — the one being tested here — the same symmetry about the internuclear axis. Classify every orbital by how many nodal planes contain the axis: none ⇒ σ-type, one ⇒ π-type, two ⇒ δ-type. Combine two orbitals of different classes and the constructive lobe overlap is exactly matched by an equal destructive lobe overlap, so the overlap integral vanishes identically and no bonding/antibonding pair is produced. Two orbitals of the same class can still fail if their nodal planes are rotated relative to one another, which is precisely the trap in option (C).
Key relation With z as the internuclear axis: σ-type: s, pz, dz²   π-type: px, py, dxz, dyz   δ-type: dxy, dx²−y². Overlap is non-zero only within a class, and for the δ pair only when both have the same orientation. dxy and dx²−y² are both δ about z but are rotated 45° with respect to each other, so their overlap integral is also zero.
Solution
1. (A) 2pz + 2px. pz is σ-type (0 nodal planes through z); px is π-type (the yz plane contains z). σ with π ⇒ net overlap zero. No MO.
2. (B) 2s + 2px. s is spherically symmetric ⇒ σ-type; px is π-type. The positive lobe of px overlaps the s sphere exactly as much as the negative lobe does ⇒ zero. No MO.
3. (C) 3dxy + 3dx²−y². Both are δ-type about z, so the symmetry class matches — but dxy has its lobes between the x and y axes while dx²−y² has them along those axes, a 45° mismatch. Every constructive lobe pairing is cancelled by an equal destructive one ⇒ zero. No MO.
4. (D) 2s + 2pz. Both σ-type about z, energies comparable in period 2 ⇒ genuine head-on overlap ⇒ bonding σ and antibonding σ* are formed. MO forms. This is exactly the s–p mixing that raises σ2pz above π2p in B₂, C₂ and N₂.
5. (E) 2pz + 3dx²−y². σ-type paired with δ-type ⇒ zero. No MO. (Their energies are also far apart.)
6. Only D survives ⇒ option (b). Eliminate (a) — A and B are the two textbook zero-overlap cases; (c) and (d) both keep the 45°-mismatched δ pair C.
 combination (axis = z)        symmetry of each     net overlap   MO formed?
 A  2p_z  + 2p_x                 σ    and  π             0           no
 B  2s    + 2p_x                 σ    and  π             0           no
 C  3d_xy + 3d_x²−y²             δ    and  δ (45° off)   0           no
 D  2s    + 2p_z                 σ    and  σ           large        YES   <-- (D)
 E  2p_z  + 3d_x²−y²             σ    and  δ             0           no
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------
                                                        D only  <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Reduce the whole question to a one-line rule: with z as the axis, "z-flavoured" orbitals (s, pz, dz²) talk only to each other; x/y-flavoured ones talk only to their exact twin. Scan each option for a mixed pair and cross it out — you never have to draw a single lobe. Trap: option (C) is engineered for students who have learnt "d + d can give a δ bond" but not that the two δ orbitals must share the same orientation; a dxy + dxy pair would have worked.
Answer: (b) D only.
20(JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 1 · Level-4) Given below are two statements: Statement (I): The oxidation state of an element in a particular compound is the charge acquired by its atom on the basis of electron gain enthalpy consideration from other atoms in the molecule. Statement (II): pπ–pπ bond formation is more prevalent in second period elements over other periods. Choose the most appropriate answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct  (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect  (c) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct  (d) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrectOxidation state definition · pπ–pπ bondingpπ–pπ and pπ–dπ bonding — why second-period elements form multiple bonds🟡 SimilarcStatement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct
Concept Statement I is a one-word substitution test on the NCERT definition. Oxidation number is assigned by giving every shared pair entirely to the more electronegative partner — electronegativity, a property of the atom in a molecule, is the criterion. Electron gain enthalpy is a property of an isolated gaseous atom and does not even order the elements the same way (Cl has a more negative ΔHeg than F, yet F is the more electronegative and always takes −1). Statement II tests why C, N and O form strong multiple bonds while Si, P and S largely do not: only the compact 2p orbitals of period 2 can approach closely enough for effective sideways overlap.
Key relations Oxidation number ← electronegativity, NOT electron gain enthalpy ΔHeg(Cl) = −349 kJ mol⁻¹ is more negative than ΔHeg(F) = −328, so an ΔHeg-based rule would wrongly make Cl the electron-taker in ClF. pπ–pπ overlap ∝ 1/(bond length) and needs small, compact p orbitals ⇒ effective for 2p (C, N, O), poor for 3p and beyond.
Solution
1. Statement I. The definition given swaps "electronegativity" for "electron gain enthalpy". That is the wrong property — and it is wrong in substance, not just wording, since the two do not rank F and Cl the same way. ⇒ INCORRECT.
2. Statement II. Second-period elements have small atomic radii and compact 2p orbitals, giving short bonds and effective lateral (pπ–pπ) overlap: hence O=O, N≡N, C=C, C=O, C≡N are all common and strong. Third-period analogues have diffuse 3p orbitals and longer bonds, so S prefers eight single bonds (S₈) over S=S and P prefers P₄ over P≡P. ⇒ CORRECT.
3. I incorrect, II correct ⇒ option (c). Eliminate (a) and (d) — both accept the faulty definition; (b) wrongly rejects the sound pπ–pπ statement.
 statement                                          verdict   key reason
 I  oxidn. state from ELECTRON GAIN ENTHALPY        WRONG     must be ELECTRONEGATIVITY
    (test case: ClF → F is −1 although ΔHeg(Cl) is more negative)
 II pπ–pπ more prevalent in 2nd period              RIGHT     compact 2p ⇒ good lateral overlap
    N₂ (N≡N) vs P₄ (only single P—P) ; O₂ (O=O) vs S₈ (only single S—S)
 ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
 I incorrect, II correct                                    <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a "definition" statement names a thermodynamic quantity, check that the quantity is a property of the atom in the bond. Oxidation number, bond polarity and partial charge all key off electronegativity; ionisation enthalpy and electron gain enthalpy describe isolated gaseous atoms and never appear in these definitions. For Statement II, the memory hook is the pair N₂ vs P₄ and O₂ vs S₈: period 2 makes multiple bonds, period 3 makes catenated single bonds instead. Trap: Statement I sounds authoritative and is only one noun away from the NCERT sentence.
Answer: (c) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct.
21(JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 2 · Level-4) The correct increasing order for bond angles among BF₃, PF₃ and ClF₃ is:  (a) BF₃ < PF₃ < ClF₃  (b) ClF₃ < PF₃ < BF₃  (c) PF₃ < BF₃ < ClF₃  (d) BF₃ = PF₃ < ClF₃Bond angle ordering (VSEPR)Working out the correct order of bond angles — a full comparison🟡 SimilarbClF₃ < PF₃ < BF₃
Concept Three EF₃ molecules, three completely different geometries — because the number of lone pairs on the central atom differs. Count valence electrons on E, subtract the three used in E—F bonds, and halve what is left: B keeps 0 lone pairs, P keeps 1, Cl keeps 2. Each lone pair pushes the bonding pairs closer together, so the angle collapses from the trigonal-planar 120° of BF₃, through the pyramidal ~97° of PF₃, down to the T-shaped ~87.5° of ClF₃. This is a pure "count the lone pairs" question dressed up to look like a periodic-trend question.
Key relation lone pairs on E = (valence e⁻ of E − 3)/2 for an EF₃ species; more lone pairs ⇒ smaller F—E—F angle because lp–bp repulsion exceeds bp–bp repulsion. Highly electronegative F also drags the bonding pairs away from E, shrinking the angle further in PF₃ and ClF₃.
Solution
1. BF₃. B has 3 valence e⁻, all three used in bonds ⇒ 0 lone pairs. SN = 3 ⇒ sp², trigonal planar, F—B—F = 120°. B is electron-deficient here; there is nothing left to compress the angle.
2. PF₃. P has 5 valence e⁻, 3 used ⇒ (5−3)/2 = 1 lone pair. SN = 4 ⇒ sp³, trigonal pyramidal, ideal 109.5° compressed by the lone pair (and by the very electronegative F) to ≈97.8°.
3. ClF₃. Cl has 7 valence e⁻, 3 used ⇒ (7−3)/2 = 2 lone pairs. SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d, trigonal-bipyramidal geometry with both lone pairs equatorial ⇒ T-shaped, axial–equatorial F—Cl—F squeezed to ≈87.5°.
4. Increasing angle: 87.5° (ClF₃) < 97.8° (PF₃) < 120° (BF₃) ⇒ option (b). Eliminate (a) — it is the exact reverse; (c) — BF₃ cannot lie between the two lone-pair-bearing molecules; (d) — BF₃ and PF₃ are not equal, one is planar and the other pyramidal.
 molecule   val. e⁻ on E   lone pairs   SN   hybrid   shape             angle
 ClF₃            7              2        5   sp³d     T-shaped          ≈ 87.5°
 PF₃             5              1        4   sp³      trig. pyramidal   ≈ 97.8°
 BF₃             3              0        3   sp²      trig. planar        120°
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------
 increasing:  ClF₃ < PF₃ < BF₃                              <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick For any EF₃ the lone-pair count is (group-valence e⁻ − 3)/2, and the angle ladder follows automatically: 0 lp → 120°, 1 lp → ~97–107°, 2 lp → ~87°. Rank by lone pairs in reverse and you are done — no electronegativity argument needed. Trap: the three central atoms sit in three different groups, which tempts candidates to invoke "size increases down the group" or "electronegativity decreases", both of which are irrelevant here because the lone-pair count changes first and dominates everything.
Answer: (b) ClF₃ < PF₃ < BF₃.
22(JEE Main 2025, 22 Jan Shift 1 · Level-3) (Integer type) The number of molecules/ions that show linear geometry among the following is _____ : SO₂, BeCl₂, CO₂, N₃⁻, NO₂, F₂O, XeF₂, NO₂⁺, I₃⁻, O₃Linear geometry countTrick to find the shape and geometry of a molecule using VSEPR🟡 Similar66
Concept "Linear" refers to the shape (positions of the atoms), not the electron-pair geometry, so a species is linear either when the central atom has SN = 2 with no lone pair (BeCl₂, CO₂, N₃⁻, NO₂⁺) or when it has SN = 5 with exactly three equatorial lone pairs, which leaves the two ligands trans-axial (XeF₂, I₃⁻). Everything else in this list — SO₂, NO₂, F₂O, O₃ — has one or two lone pairs on a three-electron-domain or four-electron-domain centre and is therefore bent. The examiner has deliberately mixed the two distinct routes to linearity in one list.
Key relation SN = σ-bonded atoms + lone pairs; linear ⟺ (SN = 2, lp = 0) or (SN = 5, lp = 3) a π bond never adds to SN, so CO₂ with two double bonds still has SN = 2. Odd-electron NO₂ counts its single unpaired electron as one (weakly repelling) domain, which is why its angle is 134° — wide, but not 180°.
Solution
1. BeCl₂ (gaseous monomer): Be has 2 valence e⁻, both bonded, 0 lp ⇒ SN 2, sp ⇒ linear ✓
2. CO₂: two σ + two π to C, 0 lp ⇒ SN 2, sp ⇒ linear ✓
3. N₃⁻ (azide): central N is sp, isoelectronic with CO₂ ⇒ linear ✓
4. NO₂⁺ (nitronium): also isoelectronic with CO₂, 0 lp on N ⇒ linear ✓ Contrast with NO₂ and NO₂⁻, which do carry a lone electron / lone pair and are bent.
5. XeF₂: Xe has 8 valence e⁻, 2 bonded ⇒ 3 lp; SN 5, sp³d, all three lp equatorial ⇒ linear ✓
6. I₃⁻: central I has 3 lp, SN 5, sp³d ⇒ linear ✓
7. Rejected: SO₂ (1 lp, sp², bent ≈119°), NO₂ (1 unpaired e⁻, bent ≈134°), F₂O (2 lp, sp³, bent ≈103°), O₃ (1 lp, sp², bent ≈117°).
8. Count of linear species = 6.
 species   σ   lp    SN   hybrid   shape            linear?
 SO₂       2    1     3   sp²      bent  ≈119°         no
 BeCl₂     2    0     2   sp       linear  180°       YES
 CO₂       2    0     2   sp       linear  180°       YES
 N₃⁻       2    0     2   sp       linear  180°       YES
 NO₂       2   ½(1e) ~3   sp²      bent  ≈134°         no
 F₂O       2    2     4   sp³      bent  ≈103°         no
 XeF₂      2    3     5   sp³d     linear  180°       YES
 NO₂⁺      2    0     2   sp       linear  180°       YES
 I₃⁻       2    3     5   sp³d     linear  180°       YES
 O₃        2    1     3   sp²      bent  ≈117°         no
 ------------------------------------------------------------
 total linear = 6                                 <-- answer 6
⚡ JEE Trick Split the list mentally into "CO₂-like" (sp, SN 2) and "XeF₂-like" (sp³d, 3 lone pairs) — those are the only two families that are linear, and both give exactly 180°. Anything with 22 valence electrons is CO₂-like: CO₂, N₃⁻, NO₂⁺, NCO⁻, N₂O all qualify instantly. Trap: the list pairs NO₂ with NO₂⁺ and O₃ with CO₂ so that a careless glance at "triatomic" marks far too many as linear; also remember BeCl₂ is linear only as the gaseous monomer (solid BeCl₂ is a polymeric chain with 98° angles).
Answer: 6 (BeCl₂, CO₂, N₃⁻, XeF₂, NO₂⁺, I₃⁻).
23(JEE Main 2025, 8 April Shift 1 · Level-3) (Integer type) Number of molecules from the following which are exceptions to the octet rule is _____ : CO₂, NO₂, H₂SO₄, BF₃, CH₄, SiF₄, ClO₂, PCl₅, BeF₂, C₂H₆, CHCl₃, CBr₄Exceptions to the octet ruleRecognising which molecules break the octet rule🟡 Similar66
Concept There are exactly three ways to break the octet rule, and every exception in any JEE list belongs to one of them: (i) an incomplete octet — a central atom with fewer than 8 electrons, typical of Be and B; (ii) an odd-electron (hypovalent) species — a molecule with an odd total electron count, so one electron must remain unpaired and one atom cannot reach 8; (iii) an expanded octet — a central atom from period 3 or below using vacant d orbitals to hold 10, 12 or more electrons. Anything whose central atom is a period-2 element with four bonds and no odd electron obeys the rule.
Key relation Incomplete: BeX₂ (4 e⁻), BX₃ (6 e⁻)   Odd-electron: NO, NO₂, ClO₂, ClO₃   Expanded: PCl₅ (10 e⁻), SF₆ (12), H₂SO₄ (12 on S), XeF₄ (12). The central atom must be period 3 or lower to expand — this is why SiF₄, with a period-3 centre, still does not expand: it only has four bonds, so its octet is exactly complete.
Solution
1. NO₂ — total valence e⁻ = 5 + 12 = 17, an odd number ⇒ one unpaired electron on N, which ends up with 7 ⇒ exception (odd-electron).
2. ClO₂ — 7 + 12 = 19 e⁻, again odd ⇒ exception (odd-electron).
3. BF₃ — B forms only three σ bonds and has no lone pair ⇒ 6 electrons around B ⇒ exception (incomplete octet).
4. BeF₂ — Be forms two σ bonds, no lone pair ⇒ 4 electrons around Be ⇒ exception (incomplete octet).
5. PCl₅ — five σ bonds on P ⇒ 10 electrons ⇒ exception (expanded octet, sp³d).
6. H₂SO₄ — S carries two S—OH single bonds and two S=O double bonds ⇒ 12 electrons around S ⇒ exception (expanded octet).
7. Obey the rule: CO₂ (C has 4 bonds = 8 e⁻), CH₄, C₂H₆, CHCl₃, CBr₄ (all four-bonded carbon) and SiF₄ (Si has exactly four bonds = 8 e⁻, no expansion). SiF₄ is the planted decoy — a period-3 central atom that nevertheless keeps a plain octet.
8. Exceptions = NO₂, ClO₂, BF₃, BeF₂, PCl₅, H₂SO₄ ⇒ 6.
 molecule    e⁻ around central atom     status
 CO₂                8                   obeys
 NO₂          7 (17 e⁻ total, odd)      EXCEPTION  — odd electron
 H₂SO₄             12                   EXCEPTION  — expanded octet
 BF₃                6                   EXCEPTION  — incomplete octet
 CH₄                8                   obeys
 SiF₄               8                   obeys      (decoy: no expansion)
 ClO₂         7 (19 e⁻ total, odd)      EXCEPTION  — odd electron
 PCl₅              10                   EXCEPTION  — expanded octet
 BeF₂               4                   EXCEPTION  — incomplete octet
 C₂H₆               8                   obeys
 CHCl₃              8                   obeys
 CBr₄               8                   obeys
 -------------------------------------------------------------
 total exceptions = 6                            <-- answer 6
⚡ JEE Trick Scan the list for just three signatures and you never draw a structure: Be or B at the centre (incomplete), an odd total valence-electron count (odd-electron), and more than four σ bonds or a period-3 centre carrying > 8 electrons (expanded). Odd-electron cases are fastest of all — add the valence electrons and check parity. Trap: SiF₄ is included precisely because "Si is period 3, so it must expand"; it does not, since four bonds is exactly an octet. A second trap is H₂SO₄, whose expansion sits on the S=O bonds rather than on any obvious high coordination number.
Answer: 6 (NO₂, ClO₂, BF₃, BeF₂, PCl₅, H₂SO₄).
24(JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 1 · Level-3) (Integer type) The total number of species from the following in which exactly one unpaired electron is present is _____ : N₂, O₂, C₂⁻, O₂⁻, O₂²⁻, H₂⁺, CN⁻, He₂⁺MOT — counting unpaired electronsMOT diagram of O₂ — the half-filled π* orbitals and counting unpaired electrons🟡 Similar44
Concept Fill each species' molecular orbitals in order of energy and apply Hund's rule to the degenerate π and π* pairs; the number of unpaired electrons then falls straight out of the last partly-filled level. The one thing you must get right is the two different orderings: for species with 14 or fewer electrons (up to N₂) s–p mixing pushes σ2pz above the π2p pair, while for O₂ and beyond σ2pz lies below π2p. Get that switch wrong and C₂⁻ in particular is misread.
Key relations ≤14 e⁻: σ1s σ*1s σ2s σ*2s π2px=π2py σ2pz π*2px=π*2py σ*2pz; >14 e⁻: … σ2pz π2px=π2py π*… σ*…. Only a degenerate pair (π or π*) that is half-occupied can give 2 unpaired electrons; a lone electron in a non-degenerate σ level gives exactly 1.
Solution
1. N₂ (14 e⁻): … π2p4 σ2pz2 — every orbital doubly filled ⇒ 0 unpaired, diamagnetic. ✗
2. O₂ (16 e⁻): … σ2pz2 π2p4 π*2px1 π*2py1 ⇒ 2 unpaired (the classic paramagnetism) — two, not one. ✗
3. C₂⁻ (13 e⁻): C₂ is … π2p4 with everything paired; the extra electron enters the next level, σ2pz, giving σ2pz1 ⇒ 1 unpaired. ✓ Here the ≤14-electron ordering is essential — if you wrongly put σ2pz below π2p you would fill π2p only partially and predict a different count.
4. O₂⁻ (17 e⁻): π* holds 3 electrons ⇒ π*x2 π*y1 ⇒ 1 unpaired. ✓
5. O₂²⁻ (18 e⁻): π*4, completely filled ⇒ 0 unpaired, diamagnetic peroxide. ✗
6. H₂⁺ (1 e⁻): σ1s1 ⇒ 1 unpaired. ✓
7. CN⁻ (14 e⁻, isoelectronic with N₂): all paired ⇒ 0. ✗
8. He₂⁺ (3 e⁻): σ1s2 σ*1s1 ⇒ 1 unpaired. ✓
9. Species with exactly one unpaired electron: C₂⁻, O₂⁻, H₂⁺, He₂⁺ ⇒ 4.
 species   e⁻   highest occupied MOs             unpaired   count?
 N₂        14   π2p⁴ σ2p_z²                          0        no
 O₂        16   π2p⁴ π*2p_x¹ π*2p_y¹                 2        no (two, not one)
 C₂⁻       13   π2p⁴ σ2p_z¹                          1       YES
 O₂⁻       17   π*2p_x² π*2p_y¹                      1       YES
 O₂²⁻      18   π*2p⁴                                0        no
 H₂⁺        1   σ1s¹                                 1       YES
 CN⁻       14   π2p⁴ σ2p_z²   (N₂-like)              0        no
 He₂⁺       3   σ1s² σ*1s¹                           1       YES
 ---------------------------------------------------------------
 exactly one unpaired e⁻ : 4                         <-- answer 4
⚡ JEE Trick Use the parity shortcut first: an odd total electron count guarantees at least one unpaired electron, and unless the odd electron lands in an empty degenerate pair it gives exactly one. C₂⁻ (13), O₂⁻ (17), H₂⁺ (1) and He₂⁺ (3) are all odd — that is the whole answer, and only the even-electron O₂ needed a real check (it gives 2, so it is excluded). Trap: O₂ is thrown in because "paramagnetic" is drilled so hard that students count it without noticing the question demands exactly one; O₂²⁻ is thrown in because the peroxide ion looks like it should still be paramagnetic.
Answer: 4 (C₂⁻, O₂⁻, H₂⁺, He₂⁺).
25(JEE Advanced 2025, Paper-1 · Level-3) Regarding the molecular orbital (MO) energy levels for homonuclear diatomic molecules, the INCORRECT statement(s) is(are)  (a) Bond order of Ne₂ is zero.  (b) The highest occupied molecular orbital (HOMO) of F₂ is σ-type.  (c) Bond energy of O₂⁺ is smaller than the bond energy of O₂.  (d) Bond length of Li₂ is larger than the bond length of B₂.MO energy-level order, HOMO, bond order trendsMO diagrams of O₂, F₂ and Ne₂ side by side — where the last electrons go🟡 Similarb, c(b) and (c)
Concept A "which statement is wrong" MO question is really four small questions bolted together, and each one is answered by the same two-step routine: count the total electrons, then fill the correct energy-level ladder. Everything else — bond order, bond length, bond energy, magnetic character, which orbital is the HOMO — falls out of that one filled diagram. The single fact that decides more of these than anything else is that the ladder changes shape at oxygen: for B₂, C₂, N₂ (Z ≤ 7) the two degenerate π2p orbitals lie below σ2pz because 2s–2p mixing pushes σ2pz up, while for O₂, F₂, Ne₂ (Z ≥ 8) the 2s and 2p levels are far enough apart that mixing dies away and σ2pz drops back below π2p. Miss that switch and you will read the HOMO off the wrong rung.
Key relations BO = ½ (Nb − Na) and the two monotonic consequences BO ↑ ⇒ bond length ↓, BO ↑ ⇒ bond dissociation energy ↑. Bond order is a count of net bonding electron pairs, so more of them means a tighter, shorter, stronger bond — this single chain converts any bond-order statement into a bond-length or bond-energy statement. The two ladders you must keep separate:
Z ≤ 7: σ2s < σ*2s < π2px = π2py < σ2pz < π*2p < σ*2p
Z ≥ 8: σ2s < σ*2s < σ2pz < π2px = π2py < π*2p < σ*2p
Solution
1. (a) Ne₂. Ne has 10 electrons, so Ne₂ has 20. Every MO built from 1s, 2s and 2p is filled: Nb = 10, Na = 10, BO = ½(10 − 10) = 0. A bond order of zero is exactly why Ne₂ does not exist as a stable molecule. The statement is correct, so it is not an answer.
2. (b) HOMO of F₂. F₂ has 18 electrons and F is Z = 9, so use the Z ≥ 8 ladder: σ1s² σ*1s² σ2s² σ*2s² σ2pz² π2p4 π*2p4. The last electrons enter π*2p, which is a π-type (antibonding) orbital, not σ-type. σ*2p is still empty — that is the LUMO. Calling the HOMO "σ" is precisely the mistake you make if you forget that π* sits below σ*. The statement is INCORRECT ⇒ an answer.
3. (c) Bond energy of O₂⁺ vs O₂. O₂ has 16 electrons with π*2px¹ π*2py¹, giving BO = ½(10 − 6) = 2. Ionising O₂ removes one electron from a π* antibonding orbital, so O₂⁺ has 15 electrons and BO = ½(10 − 5) = 2.5. Removing an antibonding electron strengthens the bond, so O₂⁺ has the larger bond energy (and the shorter bond, 112 pm vs 121 pm). The statement claims the opposite ⇒ INCORRECT ⇒ an answer.
4. (d) Bond length of Li₂ vs B₂. Li₂ (6 e⁻) has BO 1 built from very diffuse 2s orbitals on large Li atoms; B₂ (10 e⁻) also has BO 1 but from much more compact 2p orbitals on smaller B atoms. Measured values: Li—Li ≈ 267 pm, B—B ≈ 159 pm. Li₂ is indeed longer. Equal bond order does not mean equal bond length — atomic size still rules. Across period 2 the atoms contract sharply, so Li₂ is by far the longest homonuclear bond of the series. The statement is correct.
5. The incorrect statements are therefore (b) and (c).
 statement   species     e⁻   configuration / value                   verdict
 (a)         Ne₂         20   Nb = 10, Na = 10  →  BO = 0             TRUE  (Ne₂ does not exist)
 (b)         F₂          18   … σ2p_z² π2p⁴ π*2p⁴ ; HOMO = π*2p       FALSE (HOMO is π, not σ)
 (c)         O₂⁺ / O₂    15/16  BO 2.5 vs 2.0  →  E(O₂⁺) > E(O₂)      FALSE (larger, not smaller)
 (d)         Li₂ / B₂    6/10   267 pm vs 159 pm                      TRUE
 ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
 INCORRECT statements : (b) and (c)                       <-- answer (b, c)
⚡ JEE Trick Two one-line reflexes clear this whole question. First, "remove an antibonding electron ⇒ bond gets stronger; remove a bonding electron ⇒ bond gets weaker." For the O₂ family the HOMO is π*, so O₂⁺ is always stronger/shorter than O₂ — you never need to recompute. Second, "HOMO of F₂ = π*, HOMO of N₂ = σ2pz" — memorise that contrast and statement (b) dies instantly. The traps: (a) tempts you because "bond order zero" sounds too blunt to be true (it is true); (d) tempts you because both have bond order 1 and you assume equal lengths, forgetting atomic radius; and (c) is the classic reversal, betting that you will pattern-match "cation ⇒ weaker" from ionic chemistry.
Answer: (b) and (c) — the HOMO of F₂ is π*2p (π-type), and O₂⁺ (BO 2.5) has a larger bond energy than O₂ (BO 2).
26(JEE Main 2025, 8 April Shift 2 · Level-2) Number of molecules having bond order 2 from the following molecules is ______  C₂, O₂, Be₂, Li₂, Ne₂, N₂, He₂Bond order from MO configurationsBond order of the C₂ molecule from its MO configuration — the two π bonds🟡 Similar22
Concept This is a pure counting exercise, and the fastest safe route is not to draw seven MO diagrams but to build one master list of homonuclear diatomics of period 1–2 by total electron count and read the bond orders straight off it. The list is worth committing to memory once because JEE recycles it every year in a different disguise ("how many are paramagnetic", "how many do not exist", "how many have bond order 2"). Notice the shape of the sequence: bond order climbs 0, 1, 0, 1, 2, 3 as you move H₂ → He₂ → Li₂ → Be₂ → B₂ → C₂ → N₂, then falls back 3, 2.5… no — it falls 3 → 2 → 1 → 0 across N₂, O₂, F₂, Ne₂ as electrons start filling antibonding orbitals. That rise-and-fall is the whole of MO period-2 chemistry in one picture.
Formula BO = ½ (Nb − Na), counting only valence MOs (the 1s core cancels exactly). Core σ1s² σ*1s² always contributes 2 − 2 = 0, so you may drop it entirely and count valence electrons only — this halves the arithmetic and never changes the answer. Ladder for Z ≤ 7: σ2s, σ*2s, π2p(×2), σ2pz, π*2p(×2), σ*2p. Ladder for Z ≥ 8: σ2s, σ*2s, σ2pz, π2p(×2), π*2p(×2), σ*2p.
Solution
1. C₂ — 12 electrons, 8 valence. Fill (Z ≤ 7 ladder): σ2s² σ*2s² π2px² π2py². Nb = 6, Na = 2 ⇒ BO = ½(6 − 2) = 2. ✓ C₂ is the famous molecule whose double bond is made of two π bonds and no σ bond — a direct consequence of π2p lying below σ2pz.
2. O₂ — 16 electrons. σ2s² σ*2s² σ2pz² π2p4 π*2px¹ π*2py¹. Nb = 8, Na = 4 ⇒ BO = ½(8 − 4) = 2. ✓
3. Be₂ — 8 electrons, 4 valence: σ2s² σ*2s². BO = ½(2 − 2) = 0. ✗ (does not exist)
4. Li₂ — 6 electrons, 2 valence: σ2s². BO = ½(2 − 0) = 1. ✗
5. Ne₂ — 20 electrons; every bonding and antibonding MO filled ⇒ BO = 0. ✗
6. N₂ — 14 electrons: σ2s² σ*2s² π2p4 σ2pz². Nb = 8, Na = 2 ⇒ BO = 3. ✗ (the triple bond)
7. He₂ — 4 electrons: σ1s² σ*1s². BO = 0. ✗
8. Only C₂ and O₂ have bond order exactly 2, so the count is 2.
 molecule   total e⁻   valence configuration                 Nb   Na    BO    BO = 2 ?
 C₂           12       σ2s² σ*2s² π2p_x² π2p_y²              6    2     2       YES
 O₂           16       σ2s² σ*2s² σ2p_z² π2p⁴ π*2p²          8    4     2       YES
 Be₂           8       σ2s² σ*2s²                            2    2     0        no
 Li₂           6       σ2s²                                  2    0     1        no
 Ne₂          20       all bonding + all antibonding full   10   10     0        no
 N₂           14       σ2s² σ*2s² π2p⁴ σ2p_z²                8    2     3        no
 He₂           4       σ1s² σ*1s²                            2    2     0        no
 -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 count with bond order 2 : 2                                        <-- answer 2
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the bond-order strip by heart and this becomes a five-second scan: He₂ 0 · Li₂ 1 · Be₂ 0 · B₂ 1 · C₂ 2 · N₂ 3 · O₂ 2 · F₂ 1 · Ne₂ 0. It is symmetric about N₂, which makes it easy to store. The trap here is the pair of "noble-gas-like" species Be₂ and Ne₂: both have completely filled bonding and antibonding sets, so students who only count bonding electrons award them a bond order and over-count. A second trap is expecting C₂ to be bond order 3 by analogy with acetylene's C≡C — free C₂ has only 12 electrons, not 14, so it stops at 2.
Answer: 2 (only C₂ and O₂ have bond order 2).
27(JEE Main 2025, 28 Jan Shift 1 · Level-1) The molecules having square pyramidal geometry are  (a) BrF₅ & PCl₅  (b) SbF₅ & PCl₅  (c) SbF₅ & XeOF₄  (d) BrF₅ & XeOF₄VSEPR — square pyramidal AX₅E shapesThis exact JEE Main 2025 PYQ — which molecules are square pyramidal🟢 ExactdBrF₅ & XeOF₄
Concept "Square pyramidal" is not a geometry you can reach with bond pairs alone — it is what an octahedron looks like after one of its six positions is handed to a lone pair. So the moment a question says square pyramidal you should immediately write AX₅E, steric number 6, hybridisation sp³d², and start hunting for a central atom that has five bonded atoms plus one lone pair. This is exactly why the distinction between electron-pair geometry (octahedral, counts the lone pair) and molecular shape (square pyramidal, ignores it but feels its repulsion) matters: the lone pair sits at the missing apex and pushes the four basal atoms slightly below the equator, so the Fax—Br—Fbas angle is about 84.8°, not 90°.
Key relation Steric number SN = (σ bonds) + (lone pairs on central atom), and for a neutral molecule lone pairs = ½ [ (valence e⁻ of central atom) − (number of σ bonds, for monovalent partners) ]. A doubly-bonded O, as in XeOF₄, uses up two of the central atom's electrons but still occupies only one VSEPR position — count it as a single domain. SN 5 ⇒ sp³d ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal family; SN 6 ⇒ sp³d² ⇒ octahedral family, and one lone pair in that family gives square pyramidal.
Solution
1. BrF₅. Br has 7 valence electrons; five are used in five Br—F bonds, leaving 2 ⇒ one lone pair. SN = 5 + 1 = 6 ⇒ sp³d², octahedral electron geometry, one vertex occupied by the lone pair ⇒ square pyramidal. ✓
2. XeOF₄. Xe has 8 valence electrons; the four Xe—F bonds use 4 and the Xe=O double bond uses 2, leaving 2 ⇒ one lone pair. Domains = 4 F + 1 O + 1 lone pair = 6 ⇒ sp³d², and with one lone pair the shape is again square pyramidal (four F in the square base, the O at the apex, the lone pair opposite it). ✓
3. PCl₅. P has 5 valence electrons, all five used in P—Cl bonds ⇒ no lone pair. SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal. ✗
4. SbF₅. Sb is the heavier congener of P, also group 15 with 5 valence electrons and five bonds ⇒ SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal as a discrete molecule. ✗ SbF₅ is the standard "looks exotic, behaves like PCl₅" distractor. In the condensed phase it oligomerises through fluorine bridges, but as an isolated molecule it is plainly AX₅ with no lone pair.
5. Only BrF₅ and XeOF₄ carry the AX₅E signature ⇒ option (d). Eliminate (a) and (b) because both contain PCl₅, and (c) because SbF₅ is trigonal bipyramidal.
 molecule   central   valence e⁻   σ domains   lone pairs   SN   hybrid    shape
 BrF₅         Br          7            5           1        6   sp³d²   square pyramidal  ✓
 XeOF₄        Xe          8          4 F + 1 O     1        6   sp³d²   square pyramidal  ✓
 PCl₅          P          5            5           0        5   sp³d    trigonal bipyramidal
 SbF₅         Sb          5            5           0        5   sp³d    trigonal bipyramidal
 --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 square pyramidal pair : BrF₅ and XeOF₄                            <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Square pyramidal has exactly three members in the JEE syllabus — BrF₅, IF₅ and XeOF₄. Memorise those three and you answer every "which is square pyramidal" question by recognition alone. The reasoning shortcut behind it: an odd number of ligands with a lone pair means an octahedral parent minus one vertex. The trap in this question is the double bond in XeOF₄ — students count Xe=O as two domains, get SN 7, and reject the correct option; and SbF₅ is planted to punish anyone who assumes "a heavy pentafluoride must be exotic" rather than doing the two-line electron count.
Answer: (d) BrF₅ & XeOF₄ — both are sp³d², AX₅E, square pyramidal.
28(JEE Main 2025, 8 April Shift 1 · Level-1) Match List-I (Molecule) with List-II (Shape).  List-I: (A) NH₃, (B) BrF₅, (C) PCl₅, (D) CH₄.  List-II: (i) Square pyramid, (ii) Tetrahedral, (iii) Trigonal pyramidal, (iv) Trigonal bipyramidal.  Choose the correct answer:  (a) A-ii, B-iv, C-i, D-iii  (b) A-iii, B-i, C-iv, D-ii  (c) A-iv, B-iii, C-i, D-ii  (d) A-iii, B-iv, C-i, D-iiVSEPR shape matchingAssigning shapes from bond pairs and lone pairs — VSEPR practice set🟡 SimilarbA-iii, B-i, C-iv, D-ii
Concept Matching questions are graded on speed, and the way to be fast is to resist the urge to work through all four rows. Assign the one molecule you are surest about, strike out every option that disagrees, and repeat — usually two rows are enough. The chemistry itself is the standard VSEPR pipeline: steric number fixes the electron-pair geometry, and then each lone pair deletes one vertex to give the molecular shape that the question is asking about. The distinction is the whole trap in row (A): NH₃ has a tetrahedral electron geometry but a trigonal pyramidal shape, and List-II offers both words.
