Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs ⇒ SN 4 = sp³ (tetrahedral parent), SN 5 = sp³d (trigonal bipyramidal parent), SN 6 = sp³d² (octahedral parent). Then: shape = parent geometry with one vertex removed per lone pair. Lone pairs are invisible to structure determination (X-ray sees nuclei, not electrons), which is exactly why "shape" is quoted without them — but their repulsion still squeezes the remaining angles below the ideal value.
Solution
1. (D) CH₄ — the safest anchor. Four bonds, no lone pair, SN = 4 ⇒ sp³ ⇒ tetrahedral = (ii). This alone kills option (a) (which pairs A with ii) and option (c)? No — (c) also has D-ii, so keep going, but (a) is gone.
2. (A) NH₃ — N has 5 valence electrons, three in N—H bonds, leaving one lone pair. SN = 3 + 1 = 4 ⇒ sp³ parent tetrahedron, one vertex is the lone pair ⇒ trigonal pyramidal = (iii), with the angle compressed from 109.5° to 107°. This eliminates (c), which wrongly gives A-iv.
3. (B) BrF₅ — 7 valence electrons on Br, five in bonds, one lone pair. SN = 6 ⇒ sp³d² ⇒ octahedron minus one vertex ⇒ square pyramid = (i). This eliminates (d), which gives B-iv.
4. (C) PCl₅ — 5 valence electrons, five bonds, no lone pair. SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal = (iv), with two long axial bonds (240 pm) and three short equatorial bonds (202 pm).
5. A-iii, B-i, C-iv, D-ii ⇒ option (b), and it is the only survivor.
 List-I   central   bond pairs   lone pairs   SN   hybrid    shape                 List-II
 (A) NH₃     N          3            1         4   sp³     trigonal pyramidal      (iii)
 (B) BrF₅   Br          5            1         6   sp³d²   square pyramid          (i)
 (C) PCl₅    P          5            0         5   sp³d    trigonal bipyramidal    (iv)
 (D) CH₄     C          4            0         4   sp³     tetrahedral             (ii)
 ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 A-iii, B-i, C-iv, D-ii                                        <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick In a four-row match, find the row whose answer appears in the fewest options and use it as a scalpel. Here "square pyramid" (i) is claimed by C in options (a), (c) and (d) but by B only in (b) — and BrF₅ is unmistakably the square pyramid, so option (b) is forced after one line of work. The trap is the NH₃ row: "tetrahedral" is offered because NH₃'s electron geometry genuinely is tetrahedral, and a student who quotes hybridisation instead of shape walks straight into option (a).
Answer: (b) A-iii, B-i, C-iv, D-ii.
29(JEE Main 2025, 8 April Shift 2 · Level-1) When ψA and ψB are the wave functions of atomic orbitals, then σ* is represented by:  (a) ψA + 2ψB  (b) ψA − ψB  (c) ψA + ψB  (d) ψA − 2ψBLCAO — bonding vs antibonding wave functionsBonding and antibonding MOs by LCAO — adding and subtracting the atomic waves🟢 ExactbψA − ψB
Concept Molecular orbitals are built by the LCAO method — Linear Combination of Atomic Orbitals — and the physics behind it is ordinary wave interference. Two atomic orbitals are two waves; bring them together in phase and they interfere constructively, piling electron density up in the internuclear region and screening the two nuclei from each other, which lowers the energy: that is the bonding MO. Bring them together out of phase and they interfere destructively, cancelling exactly at the midpoint to create a nodal plane between the nuclei; the nuclei now repel each other nakedly and the energy rises: that is the antibonding MO, written with a star. So "σ*" translates directly into "the subtraction combination", and the answer is fixed before you look at the options.
Key relations ψ(σ) = N (ψA + ψB) — bonding, no node between the nuclei, energy lowered by Δ.
ψ(σ*) = N* (ψA − ψB) — antibonding, one nodal plane midway, energy raised by slightly more than Δ. The destabilisation of σ* always exceeds the stabilisation of σ, which is why He₂ (σ1s² σ*1s²) is net unstable rather than merely non-bonding. Note that N and N* are only normalisation constants, not chemical weightings.
Solution
1. σ* is an antibonding orbital, so it must come from the out-of-phase (destructive) combination ⇒ a minus sign. That immediately restricts the choice to (b) or (d).
2. Between (b) ψA − ψB and (d) ψA − 2ψB: for a homonuclear combination the two atomic orbitals are identical in energy and must contribute equally, so the coefficients have equal magnitude, |cA| = |cB| = 1. A factor of 2 would make the orbital lopsided towards B, which is a polar (heteronuclear) situation, not what σ* means here. ⇒ (b). Unequal coefficients do occur — in HF the bonding MO is richer in the fluorine orbital — but they are never written as a tidy "2" and the question gives no polarity information.
3. Eliminate (c) ψA + ψB: that is the bonding σ, the constructive combination — the single most popular wrong answer, chosen by anyone who reads past the star.
4. Eliminate (a) ψA + 2ψB: wrong sign and wrong coefficients.
5. Sanity check by counting orbitals: two atomic orbitals in must give two molecular orbitals out, and (ψA + ψB), (ψA − ψB) is exactly that pair — one bonding, one antibonding. Any other combination would break the orbital count.
 option            combination        interference    node between nuclei   identity
 (a) ψA + 2ψB      in phase, uneven   constructive          no             not a valid MO here
 (b) ψA − ψB       out of phase       destructive          YES             σ*  (antibonding)  ✓
 (c) ψA + ψB       in phase           constructive          no             σ   (bonding)
 (d) ψA − 2ψB      out of phase, uneven  destructive       yes             wrong coefficients
 --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 σ* = N(ψA − ψB)                                                <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Tie the notation to the sign once and never rethink it: "star = subtract" (antibonding, node, higher energy) and "no star = add" (bonding, no node, lower energy). The single trap is the asterisk itself — it is small, it is easy to skim past, and option (c) is sitting there waiting. A second, subtler trap is the coefficient 2 in (a) and (d), which tries to make you think a "bigger" contribution means a "stronger" orbital; in a homonuclear diatomic, symmetry forbids unequal contributions outright.
Answer: (b) ψA − ψB — the out-of-phase LCAO combination, with a nodal plane between the nuclei.
30(JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 1 · Level-1) In which one of the following pairs the central atoms exhibit sp² hybridization?  (a) H₂O and NO₂  (b) BF₃ and NO₂⁻  (c) NH₂⁻ and H₂O  (d) NH₂⁻ and BF₃Hybridisation from steric numberLewis structures and hybridisation of NO₃⁻, NO₂⁻, NO₂ and NO₂⁺ compared🟡 SimilarbBF₃ and NO₂⁻
Concept Hybridisation is decided by how many electron domains surround the central atom, and nothing else — not by the shape, not by whether the species is charged, not by how many π bonds there are. A domain is one σ bond or one lone pair; a double or triple bond still counts as a single domain because the π component uses an unhybridised p orbital that sits outside the hybrid set. Three domains ⇒ three hybrid orbitals ⇒ sp², planar, 120°. The reason sp² questions are error-prone is that the three domains can be split 3 + 0 (BF₃), 2 + 1 (NO₂⁻, SO₂), or even 2 + 1 with a single unpaired electron (NO₂), and all three look completely different on paper while being the same hybridisation underneath.
Key relation SN = (number of σ bonds) + (number of lone pairs on the central atom), with lone pairs = ½ [ V + (negative charge) − (positive charge) − (σ bonds, monovalent counting) ] after the σ bonds are drawn. The charge enters the electron count on the central atom: NO₂⁻ carries an extra electron relative to NO₂, which is exactly what converts an odd single electron into a full lone pair and closes the bond angle from 134° to 115°. Then SN 2 ⇒ sp, SN 3 ⇒ sp², SN 4 ⇒ sp³.
Solution
1. BF₃. B has 3 valence electrons, all three used in three B—F σ bonds, no lone pair (boron is electron-deficient). SN = 3 + 0 = 3 ⇒ sp², trigonal planar, 120°. ✓
2. NO₂⁻. Nitrogen is bonded to two oxygens (one N=O, one N—O⁻, and the two are equivalent by resonance) ⇒ 2 σ domains. Electron count: N contributes 5, the ion carries an extra electron ⇒ after the σ and π framework, one lone pair remains on N. SN = 2 + 1 = 3 ⇒ sp², bent with a 115° angle. ✓ Bent shape, sp² hybridisation — shape ≠ hybridisation, and the lone pair is what separates the two.
3. So option (b) has both centres sp². Now confirm the rest fail.
4. H₂O. O has 6 valence electrons: two O—H σ bonds and two lone pairs. SN = 2 + 2 = 4 ⇒ sp³, bent at 104.5°. ✗ — this kills options (a) and (c).
5. NH₂⁻ (amide ion). N has 5 valence electrons plus 1 for the negative charge = 6: two N—H σ bonds and two lone pairs. SN = 2 + 2 = 4 ⇒ sp³, bent at ≈104°, isoelectronic with H₂O. ✗ — this kills options (c) and (d).
6. Note that NO₂ (neutral) in option (a) is sp² (2 σ + one single unpaired electron acting as a domain), but it is paired with H₂O, which is sp³ — so (a) still fails. Only (b) has both centres sp².
 species    central   σ bonds   lone pairs   SN   hybridisation   shape / angle
 BF₃           B         3          0         3       sp²        trigonal planar, 120°   ✓
 NO₂⁻          N         2          1         3       sp²        bent, ≈115°             ✓
 H₂O           O         2          2         4       sp³        bent, 104.5°
 NH₂⁻          N         2          2         4       sp³        bent, ≈104°
 NO₂ (neutral) N         2       1 odd e⁻     3       sp²        bent, ≈134°
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 both centres sp² : BF₃ and NO₂⁻                        <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Run the whole question on one number — the steric number — and never draw a full Lewis structure. Faster still: anything of the form AB₂ with one lone pair, or AB₃ with none, is sp². That covers SO₂, O₃, NO₂⁻, NO₂, BF₃, CO₃²⁻ and SO₃ in one sweep. The traps built into this question are (i) NH₂⁻ looks "like NH₃ minus something" so students guess sp², when adding the negative charge actually gives it two lone pairs and sp³; and (ii) NO₂⁻ is bent, so anyone who equates "bent ⇒ sp³" from the water reflex rejects the correct option. Distinguish shape from hybridisation and both traps disappear.
Answer: (b) BF₃ and NO₂⁻ — both have steric number 3, hence sp².
31(JEE Main 2024, 4 April Shift 2 · Level-5) Number of compounds / species from the following with non-zero dipole moment is ______  BeCl₂, BCl₃, NF₃, XeF₄, CCl₄, H₂O, H₂S, HBr, CO₂, H₂, HClDipole moment and molecular symmetryCounting the zero-dipole molecules among CH₄, BF₃, H₂O, HF, NH₃, CO₂ and SO₂🟡 Similar55
Concept Dipole moment is a vector sum, so a molecule can be built entirely from strongly polar bonds and still have μ = 0 — CCl₄ is the standard proof. What actually decides the answer is symmetry: if the molecule has a centre of symmetry, or if the bond vectors are arranged so that every one is cancelled by an equal partner pointing the opposite way, the resultant vanishes. That reduces the eleven-species list to a single question asked eleven times: is the arrangement symmetric? Two structural features break the symmetry and guarantee a non-zero μ — an unsymmetrical shape forced by a lone pair on the central atom (NF₃, H₂O, H₂S), or a simple heteronuclear diatomic, which cannot cancel anything (HBr, HCl).
Key relation μnet = | Σ μ⃗bond |, and for the common symmetric families the sum is exactly zero: linear AB₂ (180°), trigonal planar AB₃ (120°), tetrahedral AB₄ (109.5°), square planar AB₄ (90°), octahedral AB₆. All of these are centrosymmetric or have the bond vectors distributed evenly around the central atom, so for every vector there is a partner (or a pair of partners) whose components cancel it exactly. Any homonuclear species (H₂, N₂, Cl₂) has zero bond moment to begin with, since Δχ = 0.
Solution
1. BeCl₂ — Be has no lone pair, SN = 2 ⇒ sp, linear Cl—Be—Cl. The two equal Be—Cl moments are exactly opposed ⇒ μ = 0. ✗
2. BCl₃ — SN = 3, no lone pair ⇒ sp², trigonal planar. Three equal vectors at 120° sum to zero ⇒ μ = 0. ✗
3. NF₃ — N has one lone pair, SN = 4 ⇒ trigonal pyramidal, not planar, so the three N—F moments cannot cancel ⇒ μ ≠ 0 (small, 0.24 D, because the lone-pair moment opposes them, but definitely non-zero). ✓
4. XeF₄ — Xe has two lone pairs, SN = 6 ⇒ sp³d², and the two lone pairs go trans to each other leaving a square planar XeF₄ ⇒ μ = 0. ✗ This is the one lone-pair-bearing species on the list that is still symmetric — lone pairs do not automatically create a dipole, their placement does.
5. CCl₄ — tetrahedral, four identical polar bonds pointing at the corners of a tetrahedron ⇒ μ = 0. ✗
6. H₂O — two lone pairs, bent at 104.5°; bond moments plus the lone-pair moment add ⇒ μ = 1.85 D. ✓
7. H₂S — bent like water (≈92°) ⇒ μ = 0.97 D. ✓ Smaller than water because Δχ(S—H) is tiny, but the question asks only whether it is non-zero.
8. HBr — heteronuclear diatomic ⇒ μ = 0.79 D. ✓
9. CO₂ — linear O=C=O, the two C=O moments cancel ⇒ μ = 0. ✗
10. H₂ — homonuclear, Δχ = 0 ⇒ μ = 0. ✗
11. HCl — heteronuclear diatomic ⇒ μ = 1.03 D. ✓
12. Non-zero: NF₃, H₂O, H₂S, HBr, HCl ⇒ 5.
 species    shape                         symmetric ?   μ            non-zero ?
 BeCl₂      linear (sp)                       yes       0                no
 BCl₃       trigonal planar (sp²)             yes       0                no
 NF₃        trigonal pyramidal (sp³, 1 lp)     NO       0.24 D          YES
 XeF₄       square planar (sp³d², 2 lp trans) yes       0                no
 CCl₄       tetrahedral (sp³)                 yes       0                no
 H₂O        bent, 104.5° (sp³, 2 lp)           NO       1.85 D          YES
 H₂S        bent, ≈92°  (2 lp)                 NO       0.97 D          YES
 HBr        heteronuclear diatomic             NO       0.79 D          YES
 CO₂        linear O=C=O                      yes       0                no
 H₂         homonuclear diatomic              yes       0                no
 HCl        heteronuclear diatomic             NO       1.03 D          YES
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------
 count with non-zero dipole moment : 5                     <-- answer 5
⚡ JEE Trick Do not compute anything — count the exceptions. Scan the list for exactly two patterns: (i) a central atom with a stereochemically active lone pair that leaves the shape lopsided (pyramidal or bent), and (ii) a heteronuclear diatomic. Everything else on a JEE list — linear, trigonal planar, tetrahedral, square planar, octahedral — is zero by symmetry. Here that gives NF₃, H₂O, H₂S | HBr, HCl = 5 in about ten seconds. The two traps: XeF₄ has lone pairs yet is symmetric (they sit trans), and NF₃ has a famously tiny dipole moment that students round down to "zero" by confusing it with the comparison against NH₃ — small is not zero.
Answer: 5 (NF₃, H₂O, H₂S, HBr and HCl).
32(JEE Main 2024, 29 Jan Shift 1 · Level-4) The number of species from the following which are paramagnetic and with bond order equal to one is ______  H₂, He₂⁺, O₂⁺, N₂²⁻, O₂²⁻, F₂, Ne₂⁺, B₂MOT — bond order together with magnetic behaviourBuilding the B₂ MO diagram — bond order 1 and why it is paramagnetic🟡 Similar11
Concept This question is deliberately built as an AND gate: a species qualifies only if it clears both hurdles, paramagnetism and bond order exactly 1. Most of the eight species clear one hurdle and fail the other, which is precisely the design. Paramagnetism needs at least one unpaired electron, and in MO language an unpaired electron appears only when the last electrons enter a degenerate pair of orbitals (π2p or π*2p) in numbers that Hund's rule forces to spread out, or when the total electron count is odd. Bond order 1 needs Nb − Na = 2. Only one species in the whole period-2 series manages both at once, and it is the one that is easy to overlook.
Key relations BO = ½ (Nb − Na) and paramagnetic ⇔ at least one unpaired electron. An odd total electron count guarantees paramagnetism, but the converse fails — B₂ and O₂ have even counts and are still paramagnetic because Hund's rule spreads two electrons over the degenerate π (or π*) pair. Ladder for Z ≤ 7: … π2px = π2py < σ2pz …; for Z ≥ 8: … σ2pz < π2px = π2py …
Solution
1. H₂ (2 e⁻): σ1s². BO = 1 ✓ but all electrons paired ⇒ diamagnetic ✗. Fails the magnetism test.
2. He₂⁺ (3 e⁻): σ1s² σ*1s¹. Odd ⇒ paramagnetic ✓, but BO = ½(2 − 1) = 0.5 ✗.
3. O₂⁺ (15 e⁻): … π2p⁴ π*2p¹. Paramagnetic ✓, but BO = ½(10 − 5) = 2.5 ✗.
4. N₂²⁻ (16 e⁻): isoelectronic with O₂, so … π*2px¹ π*2py¹. Paramagnetic ✓, but BO = ½(10 − 6) = 2 ✗.
5. O₂²⁻ (18 e⁻, peroxide): π*2p4 completely filled. BO = ½(10 − 8) = 1 ✓ but diamagnetic ✗.
6. F₂ (18 e⁻): same configuration as O₂²⁻. BO = 1 ✓, diamagnetic ✗. O₂²⁻ and F₂ are the two big decoys — they clear the bond-order hurdle perfectly and fail only on magnetism.
7. Ne₂⁺ (19 e⁻): one electron short of the fully filled Ne₂. Odd ⇒ paramagnetic ✓, but BO = ½(10 − 9) = 0.5 ✗.
8. B₂ (10 e⁻): σ2s² σ*2s² π2px¹ π2py¹ on the Z ≤ 7 ladder. BO = ½(6 − 4) = 1 ✓ and Hund's rule leaves two unpaired electrons ⇒ paramagnetic ✓. Both conditions met. ✓✓
9. Exactly one species qualifies ⇒ answer 1.
 species    e⁻   highest occupied MOs              BO     unpaired e⁻   para ?   BO = 1 ?   both ?
 H₂          2   σ1s²                              1.0        0          no       yes        no
 He₂⁺        3   σ1s² σ*1s¹                        0.5        1         YES        no        no
 O₂⁺        15   π2p⁴ π*2p¹                        2.5        1         YES        no        no
 N₂²⁻       16   π2p⁴ π*2p_x¹ π*2p_y¹              2.0        2         YES        no        no
 O₂²⁻       18   π*2p⁴                             1.0        0          no       yes        no
 F₂         18   π*2p⁴                             1.0        0          no       yes        no
 Ne₂⁺       19   π*2p⁴ σ*2p¹                       0.5        1         YES        no        no
 B₂         10   π2p_x¹ π2p_y¹                     1.0        2         YES       yes       YES
 ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 paramagnetic AND bond order 1 : B₂ only  →  1                        <-- answer 1
⚡ JEE Trick Filter on the rarer condition first. Bond order exactly 1 is much rarer than paramagnetism here, so scan for it: H₂, O₂²⁻, F₂ and B₂ — four candidates — then ask which of those four is paramagnetic. Three are closed-shell; only B₂ survives, because it is the one molecule of the series whose last two electrons land in the degenerate π2p pair. Remember B₂ as the standard exam answer for "bond order 1 and paramagnetic". The trap is the ordering rule: if you (wrongly) put σ2pz below π2p for boron, you get σ2pz² — paired, diamagnetic — and conclude the answer is 0. The Z ≤ 7 ladder is the entire question.
Answer: 1 (only B₂ is paramagnetic with bond order 1).
33(JEE Main 2024, 31 Jan Shift 2 · Level-4) Which of the following is least ionic?  (a) BaCl₂  (b) AgCl  (c) KCl  (d) CoCl₂Fajans' rule — the 18-electron cationFajans' rule — how covalent character creeps into an ionic compound🟡 SimilarbAgCl
Concept "Least ionic" means "most covalent", and covalent character in a nominally ionic salt is measured by how badly the cation distorts the anion's electron cloud — polarisation. Fajans' rules list three drivers: a small, highly charged cation, a large, soft anion, and — the one that decides this question — the cation's electronic configuration. A cation with a noble-gas shell (K⁺ = 2, 8, 8) has its nuclear charge well screened by the compact s and p shells. A cation with an 18-electron pseudo-noble-gas shell (Ag⁺ = 2, 8, 18, 4d¹⁰) is screened by diffuse d electrons, which shield very poorly; the effective nuclear charge felt from outside is therefore much higher for the same size, the polarising power shoots up, and the salt slides towards covalency. That is why AgCl is water-insoluble while KCl dissolves freely, and why AgCl is photosensitive.
Key relation polarising power ∝ (charge on cation) / (radius of cation)², i.e. charge density — but this must be corrected for shell type via 18 e⁻ (d¹⁰) shell > 18 + 2 shell > 8 e⁻ noble-gas shell in polarising ability at equal size and charge. All four compounds share the same anion, Cl⁻, so the anion term drops out completely and the ranking is decided by the cation alone — that is what makes this question a one-variable comparison.
Solution
1. Same anion throughout (Cl⁻), so compare the cations K⁺, Ba²⁺, Co²⁺ and Ag⁺ only.
2. K⁺ — 8-electron noble-gas shell (Ar core), charge +1, large (r ≈ 138 pm). Lowest charge density of the set and excellent screening ⇒ weakest polariser ⇒ KCl is the most ionic.
3. Ba²⁺ — noble-gas (Xe) shell, charge +2 but very large (r ≈ 135 pm). The bigger charge is offset by the size, and the shell type is still the well-screening 8-electron type ⇒ still highly ionic.
4. Co²⁺ — charge +2 and small (r ≈ 74 pm), with a 3d⁷ shell. High charge density plus an incomplete d shell makes it a decent polariser, so CoCl₂ has appreciable covalent character (it is soluble in organic solvents and famously colour-changing).
5. Ag⁺ — charge only +1, but the 4d¹⁰ 18-electron outer shell shields the nucleus badly, so its effective polarising power exceeds even that of the +2 ions here ⇒ maximum polarisation of Cl⁻ ⇒ most covalent = least ionic. ⇒ option (b).
6. Cross-check with real behaviour: KCl and BaCl₂ are freely soluble in water and are typical salts; CoCl₂ is soluble but shows covalent character; AgCl is insoluble in water, has a low melting point relative to the alkali halides, and darkens in light — all hallmarks of covalent character. The chemistry agrees with Fajans.
 compound   cation   charge   ionic radius   outer shell type      polarising power   ionic character
 KCl         K⁺        +1        138 pm      8 e⁻  (noble gas)         lowest             HIGHEST
 BaCl₂       Ba²⁺      +2        135 pm      8 e⁻  (noble gas)          low                high
 CoCl₂       Co²⁺      +2         74 pm      3d⁷  (incomplete d)       high                lower
 AgCl        Ag⁺       +1        115 pm      18 e⁻ (4d¹⁰ pseudo)      HIGHEST             LOWEST   ✓
 ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 ionic character : KCl > BaCl₂ > CoCl₂ > AgCl              →  least ionic = AgCl   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a Fajans list contains a d-block or post-transition cation — Ag⁺, Cu⁺, Zn²⁺, Cd²⁺, Hg²⁺, Sn²⁺, Pb²⁺ — that compound is the covalent one, full stop. You do not need radii or charges: the 18-electron shell beats charge and size arguments in every JEE question of this type. The trap is exactly the reverse reasoning — students see Ag⁺ carrying only +1 against Co²⁺ and Ba²⁺ at +2 and pick a divalent option, because "higher charge ⇒ more polarising" is the rule they memorised without the shell-type correction.
Answer: (b) AgCl — the 4d¹⁰ 18-electron Ag⁺ shell shields poorly, making it the strongest polariser and AgCl the least ionic (most covalent).
34(JEE Main 2024, 1 Feb Shift 1 · Level-4) Arrange the bonds in order of increasing ionic character in the molecules: LiF, K₂O, N₂, SO₂ and ClF₃.  (a) ClF₃ < N₂ < SO₂ < K₂O < LiF  (b) LiF < K₂O < ClF₃ < SO₂ < N₂  (c) N₂ < SO₂ < ClF₃ < K₂O < LiF  (d) N₂ < ClF₃ < SO₂ < K₂O < LiFIonic character from electronegativity differenceQuick trick for ranking compounds by ionic character🟡 SimilarcN₂ < SO₂ < ClF₃ < K₂O < LiF
Concept Every bond between unlike atoms is a blend of covalent and ionic, and the mixing parameter is the electronegativity difference Δχ between the bonded partners. When Δχ = 0 the shared pair sits exactly midway and the bond is 100 % covalent; as Δχ grows the pair is dragged towards the more electronegative atom, partial charges δ⁺/δ⁻ appear, and the percentage ionic character rises. Pauling's empirical relation shows this is not linear — the ionic character climbs slowly at first and then steeply, crossing 50 % at about Δχ = 1.7. The practical consequence for a ranking question is that you never need the formula, only the ordering of Δχ values.
Formula % ionic character = 100 [ 1 − e−0.25 (Δχ)² ] (Pauling), monotonic in Δχ, so larger Δχ ⇒ larger ionic character. Pauling electronegativities used here: K 0.8 · Li 1.0 · S 2.5 · N 3.0 · Cl 3.0 · O 3.5 · F 4.0. For a homonuclear bond such as N≡N the two partners are identical, so Δχ = 0 exactly and the bond is purely covalent — it must occupy the bottom of any increasing-ionic-character list.
Solution
1. N₂ — N≡N is homonuclear: Δχ = 3.0 − 3.0 = 0. Purely covalent ⇒ lowest, first in an increasing list. This single observation eliminates options (a) and (b).
2. LiF — Δχ = 4.0 − 1.0 = 3.0, the largest difference available in the whole periodic table for a simple binary compound ⇒ highest ionic character, last in the list. Both surviving options (c) and (d) agree.
3. K₂O — Δχ = 3.5 − 0.8 = 2.7. An alkali metal with oxygen: strongly ionic, second only to LiF here.
4. SO₂ — Δχ(S—O) = 3.5 − 2.5 = 1.0. Polar covalent.
5. ClF₃ — Δχ(Cl—F) = 4.0 − 3.0 = 1.0. Also polar covalent, and on the rounded Pauling scale numerically tied with SO₂.
6. The tie is broken in favour of ClF₃ > SO₂: the Cl—F bonds are pure single σ bonds in which fluorine — the most electronegative element there is — pulls without opposition, whereas in SO₂ the S=O linkages carry substantial π character, and π back-donation from oxygen returns electron density to sulfur, reducing the net charge separation. So SO₂ ends up slightly the more covalent of the two.
7. Increasing order: N₂ (0) < SO₂ (≈1.0) < ClF₃ (≈1.0) < K₂O (2.7) < LiF (3.0) ⇒ option (c). Option (d) reverses only the SO₂/ClF₃ pair.
 molecule   bond    χ (more EN)   χ (less EN)   Δχ     character            rank (increasing)
 N₂         N≡N        3.0           3.0        0.0    100 % covalent             1
 SO₂        S=O        3.5           2.5        1.0    polar covalent, π-rich     2
 ClF₃       Cl—F       4.0           3.0        1.0    polar covalent, pure σ     3
 K₂O        K—O        3.5           0.8        2.7    strongly ionic             4
 LiF        Li—F       4.0           1.0        3.0    most ionic                 5
 -----------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 N₂ < SO₂ < ClF₃ < K₂O < LiF                                <-- answer (c)
⚠ Note The SO₂ vs ClF₃ step is genuinely close. On the rounded Pauling scale used above the two Δχ values are identical (1.0 each), and on the refined Pauling scale (S 2.58, O 3.44, Cl 3.16, F 3.98) they invert slightly to Δχ(S—O) = 0.86 and Δχ(Cl—F) = 0.82. Δχ alone therefore cannot settle this pair, which is exactly why options (c) and (d) differ only here; the accepted key is (c), justified by the π-bonding argument in step 6. The other three placements — N₂ lowest, then K₂O, then LiF highest — are unambiguous and are the same in both options.
⚡ JEE Trick Rank by how far apart the two elements sit in the periodic table rather than by looking up numbers: a metal from group 1 bonded to a group 16/17 non-metal is at the top; two non-metals from adjacent groups are in the middle; a homonuclear bond is at the bottom. Then attack the options from the ends — fix the first entry (N₂, the only homonuclear molecule) and the last (LiF, the largest possible Δχ), and in this question two of the four options die immediately. The trap is option (b), which is simply the correct sequence printed backwards, ready to catch anyone who reads "increasing" as "decreasing".
Answer: (c) N₂ < SO₂ < ClF₃ < K₂O < LiF.
35(JEE Main 2024, 4 April Shift 2 · Level-4) The correct statement/s about Hydrogen bonding is/are  A. Hydrogen bonding exists when H is covalently bonded to the highly electronegative atom.  B. Intermolecular H bonding is present in o-nitrophenol.  C. Intramolecular H bonding is present in HF.  D. The magnitude of H bonding depends on the physical state of the compound.  E. H-bonding has a powerful effect on the structure and properties of compounds.  Choose the correct answer:  (a) A, B, D only  (b) A, D, E only  (c) A only  (d) A, B, C onlyHydrogen bonding — intra vs intero-Nitrophenol vs p-nitrophenol — intramolecular against intermolecular H-bonding🟡 SimilarbA, D, E only
Concept A hydrogen bond forms when a hydrogen atom already covalently bonded to a small, strongly electronegative atom (F, O or N) is left so electron-poor that it can be attracted to a lone pair on another such atom. Two geometric varieties exist and the exam almost always tests the difference. Intermolecular H-bonding joins separate molecules into associated chains or rings, so it raises melting point, boiling point and water solubility. Intramolecular H-bonding closes a ring within one molecule — possible only when the donor and acceptor are held close, as in an ortho-disubstituted benzene — and because that molecule is now self-satisfied it no longer associates with its neighbours, so its boiling point and water solubility fall while its volatility rises.
Statement X—H ⋯ Y where X, Y ∈ {F, O, N}. Two rules settle nearly every question: ortho + suitable groups ⇒ intramolecular (chelation) and meta / para ⇒ intermolecular. The ortho positions place the phenolic —OH and the —NO₂ oxygen roughly 2.6 Å apart, closing a stable six-membered ring; at meta or para no such ring is geometrically possible, so the molecules bond to each other instead.
Solution
1. A — TRUE. This is the definition: H must be covalently bonded to a highly electronegative atom (F, O, N) for the δ⁺ hydrogen to appear. Without that polarisation there is no hydrogen bond — which is why HCl, despite chlorine's electronegativity, does not H-bond appreciably (Cl is too large and too diffuse).
2. B — FALSE. o-Nitrophenol shows intramolecular H-bonding, not intermolecular: the —OH and the ortho —NO₂ close a six-membered chelate ring. The experimental proof is decisive — o-nitrophenol boils at 214 °C and is steam-volatile and only sparingly soluble in water, while p-nitrophenol (which must H-bond intermolecularly) melts at 114 °C, is far less volatile and much more water-soluble. Statement B is the intended trap and it eliminates options (a) and (d).
3. C — FALSE. HF is a diatomic molecule — there is only one hydrogen and one fluorine, so there is nothing inside the molecule for the H to bond to. HF's hydrogen bonding is emphatically intermolecular, producing zig-zag (HF)n chains, and it is what makes HF a liquid at 19.5 °C while HCl boils at −85 °C, and what allows the bifluoride ion [F—H⋯F]⁻ to exist in KHF₂. This eliminates option (d) again.
4. D — TRUE. The magnitude of hydrogen bonding does depend on physical state: it is maximal in the solid, where molecules are locked in an ordered network (ice has every water molecule tetrahedrally H-bonded to four neighbours), reduced in the liquid as thermal motion breaks and reforms bonds, and essentially absent in the dilute vapour. NCERT states this explicitly.
5. E — TRUE. Hydrogen bonding governs the anomalously high boiling point of water, the lower density of ice than liquid water, the double-helical pairing of DNA, the α-helix of proteins, and the solubility of alcohols and sugars. "Powerful effect on structure and properties" is an understatement.
6. Correct set = A, D, E ⇒ option (b). Option (c) "A only" is eliminated because D and E are also true.
 statement                                                   verdict   reason
 A  H covalently bonded to highly electronegative atom        TRUE     the defining condition (F, O, N)
 B  Intermolecular H bonding in o-nitrophenol                 FALSE    ortho ⇒ INTRAmolecular chelate ring
 C  Intramolecular H bonding in HF                            FALSE    diatomic ⇒ can only be INTERmolecular
 D  Magnitude depends on physical state                       TRUE     solid > liquid >> gas
 E  Powerful effect on structure and properties               TRUE     water, ice, DNA, proteins
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 correct statements : A, D, E                                       <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Statements B and C are a matched pair of swapped labels — the examiner has taken the two textbook examples of each type and exchanged them. Fix the two anchors permanently: o-nitrophenol = intramolecular (and therefore the more volatile, less soluble isomer) and HF = intermolecular (zig-zag chains, abnormally high boiling point). With both statements dead, only options (b) and (c) survive, and E is too obviously true for (c) to stand. The deeper trap is that "intramolecular" and "intermolecular" differ by three letters and are easy to misread under time pressure — circle the prefix before you judge the chemistry.
Answer: (b) A, D and E only — B and C have their prefixes swapped.
36(JEE Main 2024, 5 April Shift 2 · Level-4) Assertion (A): NH₃ and NF₃ molecules have pyramidal shape with a lone pair of electrons on the nitrogen atom. The resultant dipole moment of NH₃ is greater than that of NF₃.  Reason (R): In NH₃, the orbital dipole due to the lone pair is in the same direction as the resultant dipole moment of the N—H bonds. F is the most electronegative element.  (a) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)  (b) (A) is false but (R) is true  (c) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is NOT the correct explanation of (A)  (d) (A) is true but (R) is falseNH₃ vs NF₃ dipole moment — lone-pair vectorWhy NH₃ has a much higher dipole moment than NF₃ — the vector argument🟢 ExactaBoth (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
Concept NH₃ and NF₃ are the textbook demonstration that a dipole moment is a vector sum, and the lone pair is one of the vectors. Both molecules are trigonal pyramidal (sp³ nitrogen, one lone pair), so their geometries are essentially identical — yet their measured dipole moments differ by a factor of six. The whole difference is a matter of direction. In NH₃ nitrogen is more electronegative than hydrogen, so each N—H bond moment points from H towards N, i.e. upward towards the lone pair; the lone-pair orbital moment also points up and away from the nucleus, so the two contributions reinforce. In NF₃ fluorine is more electronegative than nitrogen, so each N—F moment points from N towards F, i.e. downward — directly against the lone-pair moment, which still points up. The bond moments and the lone-pair moment now partially cancel.
Key relation μnet = μlone pair ± Σ μbond — plus when the two point the same way, minus when they oppose. Bond moments always point from the less electronegative atom towards the more electronegative one. Electronegativities: H 2.1 < N 3.0 < F 4.0. Nitrogen is the electron sink in NH₃ but the electron source in NF₃, and that single reversal flips the bond-moment vectors through 180°. Measured values: μ(NH₃) = 1.46 D, μ(NF₃) = 0.24 D.
Solution
1. Test the Assertion. Both molecules are AX₃E with SN = 4 ⇒ sp³ ⇒ trigonal pyramidal with one lone pair on N — true as stated. And μ(NH₃) = 1.46 D really is greater than μ(NF₃) = 0.24 D. (A) is TRUE.
2. Test the Reason. In NH₃ the resultant of the three N—H bond moments points along the C₃ axis from the H₃ base towards nitrogen, and the lone-pair orbital dipole points along that same axis outward from nitrogen. They are collinear and additive — exactly as (R) states. And F is indeed the most electronegative element. (R) is TRUE.
3. Test the explanation link. Does (R) explain (A)? Yes, and completely. The first half of (R) establishes that in NH₃ the lone pair adds to the bond moments, giving a large μ. The second half — F is the most electronegative element — forces the N—F bond moments in NF₃ to point away from nitrogen, i.e. opposite to the lone-pair moment, so they subtract and give a small μ. Together the two halves give the observed inequality. ⇒ option (a).
4. Eliminate: (b) fails because (A) is verifiably true from the measured dipole moments. (d) fails because (R) contains no false statement. (c) fails because (R) is not some unrelated true fact — it supplies precisely the vector argument that produces (A).
 molecule   shape                 χ order      bond moment direction   lone-pair moment   combination   μ
 NH₃    trigonal pyramidal (sp³)  N (3.0) > H (2.1)   H → N  (upward)      upward          ADD        1.46 D
 NF₃    trigonal pyramidal (sp³)  F (4.0) > N (3.0)   N → F  (downward)    upward       PARTIAL CANCEL 0.24 D
 ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 (A) true · (R) true · (R) explains (A)                                    <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Draw one arrow and you are done: put the lone pair at the top of the pyramid with its arrow pointing up, then ask which way the bond arrows point. If the outer atoms are less electronegative than the centre (H in NH₃) the arrows also point up ⇒ add ⇒ big μ; if they are more electronegative (F in NF₃) the arrows point down ⇒ oppose ⇒ small μ. The same one-arrow test instantly explains why NH₃ > NF₃, why H₂O > OF₂, and why PH₃ > PF₃. The trap in assertion–reason form is option (c): a student who accepts both statements but does not see that "F is the most electronegative element" is doing the second half of the vector argument will judge the link incidental and choose (c) instead of (a).
Answer: (a) — both (A) and (R) are true, and (R) is the correct explanation of (A).
37(JEE Main 2024, 6 Apr Shift-2 · Level-4) Total number of species from the following with central atom utilising sp2 hybrid orbitals for bonding is ______  NH₃, SO₂, SiO₂, BeCl₂, C₂H₂, C₂H₄, BCl₃, HCHO, C₆H₆, BF₃, C₂H₄Cl₂Counting sp² centres by steric numberFast hybridisation trick — counting σ bonds plus lone pairs to get sp, sp² or sp³🟡 Similar66
Concept Hybridisation is decided by the steric number of the central atom — the number of σ bonds it makes plus the number of lone pairs it carries. π bonds are built from unhybridised p orbitals and are therefore invisible to this count; that is the single fact that makes multiple-bonded species like SO₂, HCHO and C₆H₆ come out sp² rather than sp³. Steric number 2 → sp, 3 → sp², 4 → sp³. For a molecule with more than one candidate "central" atom (the carbons of C₂H₄, C₆H₆, C₂H₄Cl₂) every such atom has the same environment, so the molecule is classified once.
Key relation SN = (number of σ bonds) + (number of lone pairs), and SN = 3 ⇒ sp², trigonal planar σ-framework a double bond contributes ONE σ (and one π that is ignored), so C=O, S=O and C=C never raise the steric number.
Solution
1. NH₃ — N makes 3 σ to H and keeps 1 lone pair ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp³. Not counted.
2. SO₂ — S makes 2 σ to the two O and keeps 1 lone pair ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp² ✔ (bent, ∠O–S–O ≈ 119°).
3. SiO₂ — silica is a giant covalent network in which every Si is tetrahedrally bonded to four O ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp³. The commonest trap in this question: students write SiO₂ "like CO₂" and call it sp. Silicon is too large for effective pπ–pπ overlap, so it polymerises instead of forming discrete O=Si=O. Not counted.
4. BeCl₂ (vapour) — Be makes 2 σ, no lone pair ⇒ SN = 2 ⇒ sp, linear. Not counted.
5. C₂H₂ — each C makes 2 σ (one C–H, one C–C) ⇒ SN = 2 ⇒ sp. Not counted.
6. C₂H₄ — each C makes 3 σ (2 C–H + 1 C–C) ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp² ✔.
7. BCl₃ — B makes 3 σ, no lone pair (electron deficient) ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp² ✔.
8. HCHO — the carbonyl C makes 3 σ (2 C–H + 1 C–O) ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp² ✔.
9. C₆H₆ — every ring carbon makes 3 σ (2 C–C + 1 C–H) ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp² ✔; the leftover p orbitals build the delocalised π cloud.
10. BF₃ — same as BCl₃ ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp² ✔.
11. C₂H₄Cl₂ — a saturated dihaloethane; each C makes 4 σ ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp³. Not counted.
12. Tally of the ticks: SO₂, C₂H₄, BCl₃, HCHO, C₆H₆, BF₃ ⇒ 6.
 species      sigma bonds   lone pairs   SN   hybridisation   sp2?
 NH3               3            1         4        sp3          -
 SO2               2            1         3        sp2         YES
 SiO2 (network)    4            0         4        sp3          -
 BeCl2             2            0         2        sp           -
 C2H2 (each C)     2            0         2        sp           -
 C2H4 (each C)     3            0         3        sp2         YES
 BCl3              3            0         3        sp2         YES
 HCHO (C)          3            0         3        sp2         YES
 C6H6 (each C)     3            0         3        sp2         YES
 BF3               3            0         3        sp2         YES
 C2H4Cl2 (each C)  4            0         4        sp3          -
                                              total sp2 = 6   <-- answer (6)
⚡ JEE Trick Run the whole list with one question per species — "how many things are attached, counting a lone pair as a thing, and counting a double bond as one thing?" You never need a structure diagram. The two traps planted here are SiO₂ (network sp³, not linear sp like CO₂) and C₂H₄Cl₂ (saturated sp³, deliberately written to look like C₂H₄). Miss either one and you land on the distractor values 7 or 5.
Answer: 6 — SO₂, C₂H₄, BCl₃, HCHO, C₆H₆ and BF₃.
38(JEE Advanced 2024, Paper-1 · Level-4) Based on VSEPR model, match the xenon compounds in List-I with the corresponding geometries and the number of lone pairs on xenon in List-II.  List-I: (P) XeF₂  (Q) XeF₄  (R) XeO₃  (S) XeO₃F₂.  List-II: (1) Trigonal bipyramidal and two lone pairs  (2) Tetrahedral and one lone pair  (3) Octahedral and two lone pairs  (4) Trigonal bipyramidal and no lone pair  (5) Trigonal bipyramidal and three lone pairs.  (a) P−5, Q−2, R−3, S−1  (b) P−5, Q−3, R−2, S−4  (c) P−4, Q−3, R−2, S−1  (d) P−4, Q−2, R−5, S−3VSEPR — electron-pair geometry and lone pairs on XeXeF₂ by VSEPR — why it is trigonal bipyramidal with three equatorial lone pairs🟡 SimilarbP−5, Q−3, R−2, S−4
Concept Read the wording carefully: List-II names the electron-pair geometry (the arrangement of all pairs, bonding + lone), not the molecular shape. So XeF₂ must be described as "trigonal bipyramidal with three lone pairs", not as "linear". The steric number of Xe fixes that geometry: 5 → trigonal bipyramidal, 6 → octahedral, 4 → tetrahedral. The lone pairs on Xe come from its 8 valence electrons minus those used in σ bonding, with every Xe=O double bond treated as one σ plus one π that eats a whole lone pair.
Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs; lone pairs on Xe = (8 − 1×(single-bonded atoms) − 2×(doubly-bonded atoms))/2 each Xe=O consumes two of Xe's electrons for σ+π, so XeO₃ uses 6 of the 8, leaving exactly one lone pair.
Solution
1. (P) XeF₂ — Xe has 8 valence e⁻, two go into the two Xe–F σ bonds, leaving 6 e⁻ = 3 lone pairs. SN = 2 + 3 = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal with three lone pairs ⇒ (5). (The lone pairs take the three equatorial sites, so the shape is linear — but that is not what is being asked.)
2. (Q) XeF₄ — four σ bonds use 4 e⁻, leaving 4 e⁻ = 2 lone pairs. SN = 4 + 2 = 6 ⇒ octahedral with two lone pairs ⇒ (3) (shape: square planar).
3. (R) XeO₃ — three Xe=O bonds consume 3×2 = 6 e⁻, leaving 2 e⁻ = 1 lone pair. SN = 3 σ + 1 lp = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral with one lone pair ⇒ (2) (shape: trigonal pyramidal).
4. (S) XeO₃F₂ — three Xe=O use 6 e⁻ and two Xe–F use 2 e⁻; all 8 valence electrons are spent, so no lone pair. SN = 5 σ + 0 = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal with no lone pair ⇒ (4) (the two F sit axial, the three O equatorial).
5. P−5, Q−3, R−2, S−4 ⇒ option (b). Eliminate the rest instantly: every one of (a), (c), (d) assigns S to a lone-pair-bearing entry, but XeO₃F₂ has exhausted all eight electrons of Xe — it cannot keep a lone pair.
 species    sigma   lone pairs on Xe   SN   electron-pair geometry   List-II
 XeF2         2            3            5   trigonal bipyramidal      (5)
 XeF4         4            2            6   octahedral                (3)
 XeO3         3            1            4   tetrahedral               (2)
 XeO3F2       5            0            5   trigonal bipyramidal      (4)
                                             P-5, Q-3, R-2, S-4  <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick For any xenon compound, lone pairs on Xe = 4 − (½ × number of electrons Xe donates to bonds). Faster still: XeF₂ → 3 lp, XeF₄ → 2 lp, XeF₆ → 1 lp, XeO₃ → 1 lp, XeO₄ → 0 lp, XeO₃F₂ → 0 lp — memorise this six-item table and every Xe question in the paper collapses to a lookup. The trap is that four of the five List-II entries say "trigonal bipyramidal", so you must discriminate purely on the lone-pair count.
Answer: (b) P−5, Q−3, R−2, S−4.
39(JEE Main 2024, 27 Jan Shift 1 · Level-3) Sum of bond order of CO and NO⁺ is ______MOT bond order of 14-electron diatomicsMO treatment of CO, NO, NO⁺ and CN⁻ — bond order and magnetic behaviour🟢 Exact66
Concept CO and NO⁺ are both 14-electron species, which makes them isoelectronic with N₂ — and isoelectronic diatomics fill the very same MO ladder, so they must share N₂'s bond order of 3. Because the total electron count is below 14+ (i.e. ≤ N₂), the s–p mixing regime applies and the ordering is σ2s < σ*2s < π2px = π2py < σ2pz < π*2p < σ*2p. The heteronuclear character of CO and NO⁺ skews the orbital energies but does not change how many electrons are bonding versus antibonding, so the bond order is unaffected.
Key relation Bond order = ½ (Nb − Na); CO: 6 + 8 = 14 e⁻  ·  NO⁺: 7 + 8 − 1 = 14 e⁻ both are isoelectronic with N₂ (14 e⁻), whose configuration gives Nb = 10, Na = 4.
Solution
1. Count electrons in CO: C contributes 6, O contributes 8 ⇒ 14 electrons.
2. Fill the N₂-type ladder: σ2s² σ*2s² π2px² π2py² σ2pz². Bonding electrons Nb = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8 from the valence set (10 counting σ1s-type core pairs in the full scheme); antibonding Na = 2. Using the standard valence count, BO = ½(8 − 2) = 3.
3. Count electrons in NO⁺: N gives 7, O gives 8, minus 1 for the positive charge ⇒ 14 electrons — identical to CO and to N₂.
4. Same configuration ⇒ BO(NO⁺) = 3. This is exactly why NO⁺ (BO 3, diamagnetic) is a stronger, shorter bond than neutral NO (15 e⁻, BO 2.5, paramagnetic) — removing an electron takes it out of the antibonding π* level.
5. Required sum = 3 + 3 = 6.
 species   total e-   isoelectronic with   Nb   Na   bond order
 CO           14              N2           10    4       3.0
 NO+          14              N2           10    4       3.0
                                       sum of bond orders = 6   <-- answer (6)
 (for contrast)
 NO           15              -           10    5       2.5
 NO-          16              O2          10    6       2.0
⚡ JEE Trick Never build an MO diagram for these — just count electrons and map to the nearest homonuclear twin: 14 e⁻ → N₂ → BO 3; 15 e⁻ → BO 2.5; 16 e⁻ → O₂ → BO 2. CO, NO⁺, CN⁻ and N₂ all sit at 14, so any question that mixes them is really a single question asked four ways. The trap here is charge sign: a student who adds an electron for the "+" gets 16 and answers 5 instead of 6.
Answer: 6 — BO(CO) = 3 and BO(NO⁺) = 3.
40(JEE Main 2024, 29 Jan Shift 1 · Level-3) Number of compounds with one lone pair of electrons on central atom amongst following is ______  O₃, H₂O, SF₄, ClF₃, NH₃, BrF₅, XeF₄Lone pairs on the central atom (VSEPR count)Shortcut for finding hybridisation and the lone-pair count on a central atom🟡 Similar44
Concept The lone-pair count on a central atom A in AXn follows from a two-line bookkeeping: take A's valence electrons, subtract one for each σ bond it forms to a monovalent atom (and two for each double bond), then halve what remains. Every species here is a textbook VSEPR case, so the arithmetic is quick; the only one that needs thought is ozone, where the central oxygen — not the terminal ones — is what the question asks about.
Key relation lone pairs on A = ½ [ VA − (electrons A commits to bonds) ], equivalently lp = SN − (number of σ bonds) SN itself comes from the familiar ½(V + monovalent atoms − charge) formula.
Solution
1. O₃ — the central O forms one single and one double bond (2 σ) and, of its 6 valence electrons, 3 are committed to bonding; the remaining pair is 1 lone pair. SN = 3 ⇒ sp², bent. ✔ counted.
2. H₂O — O forms 2 σ, keeps 4 e⁻ ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 4, bent. ✘
3. SF₄ — S has 6 valence e⁻, 4 go to the four S–F bonds, leaving 2 ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 5 ⇒ see-saw. ✔ counted.
4. ClF₃ — Cl has 7 valence e⁻, 3 to bonding, leaving 4 ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 5 ⇒ T-shaped. ✘
5. NH₃ — N has 5 valence e⁻, 3 to bonding, leaving 2 ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 4 ⇒ pyramidal. ✔ counted.
6. BrF₅ — Br has 7 valence e⁻, 5 to bonding, leaving 2 ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 6 ⇒ square pyramidal. ✔ counted.
7. XeF₄ — Xe has 8 valence e⁻, 4 to bonding, leaving 4 ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 6 ⇒ square planar. ✘
8. Ticks: O₃, SF₄, NH₃, BrF₅ ⇒ 4.
 species   V(central)   e- used in bonds   e- left   lone pairs   shape
 O3            6              4 (1 sig+1 dbl)   2         1        bent      YES
 H2O           6              2                 4         2        bent       -
 SF4           6              4                 2         1        see-saw   YES
 ClF3          7              3                 4         2        T-shaped   -
 NH3           5              3                 2         1        pyramidal YES
 BrF5          7              5                 2         1        sq.pyr.   YES
 XeF4          8              4                 4         2        sq.planar  -
                                                        count = 4   <-- answer (4)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the interhalogen/noble-gas family by lone-pair count instead of by name: 1 lone pair → SF₄, BrF₅, ClF₅, NH₃, XeO₃, O₃(central); 2 lone pairs → ClF₃, XeF₄, H₂O, ICl₂⁺; 3 lone pairs → XeF₂, I₃⁻. The trap in this list is O₃ — many candidates picture it as symmetric like CO₂ (0 lone pairs on the centre) and answer 3.
Answer: 4 — O₃, SF₄, NH₃ and BrF₅.
41(JEE Main 2024, 29 Jan Shift 2 · Level-3) The total number of anti bonding molecular orbitals, formed from 2s and 2p atomic orbitals in a diatomic molecule is ______LCAO — conservation of orbital number2s and 2p atomic orbitals combine to give how many molecular orbitals?🟢 Exact44
Concept The LCAO method conserves orbitals: n atomic orbitals always produce exactly n molecular orbitals, never more and never fewer. Each pair of equivalent AOs, one from each atom, combines in-phase to give one bonding MO and out-of-phase to give one antibonding MO — so bonding and antibonding MOs are produced in a strict 1 : 1 ratio. The question is therefore pure counting, with no electron configuration involved at all: it does not matter which diatomic molecule you pick.
Key relation n AOs → n MOs = n/2 bonding + n/2 antibonding; per atom: 2s (1 orbital) + 2p (3 orbitals) = 4 AOs the 2p subshell holds three degenerate orbitals — 2px, 2py, 2pz — a point candidates routinely forget, counting "2p" as one orbital.
Solution
1. Count the atomic orbitals contributed by one atom: 2s = 1 orbital, 2p = 3 orbitals ⇒ 4 AOs.
2. A diatomic has two atoms ⇒ total AOs entering the combination = 2 × 4 = 8.
3. Orbital conservation ⇒ 8 molecular orbitals are produced.
4. They come in bonding/antibonding pairs ⇒ 4 bonding and 4 antibonding.
5. Name them to be sure: bonding σ2s, σ2pz, π2px, π2py; antibonding σ*2s, σ*2pz, π*2px, π*2py ⇒ exactly 4 starred orbitals. Note that π*2px and π*2py are degenerate but are still two distinct orbitals — counting the degenerate π* pair as one is the classic route to the wrong answer 3.
 atomic orbitals per atom :  2s, 2px, 2py, 2pz            = 4
 for two atoms            :  4 x 2                        = 8 AOs
 LCAO conservation        :  8 AOs -> 8 MOs
   bonding    : sigma2s, sigma2pz, pi2px, pi2py           = 4
   antibonding: sigma*2s, sigma*2pz, pi*2px, pi*2py       = 4   <-- answer (4)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a question asks "how many MOs / how many bonding / how many antibonding", just count AOs and halve — the answer never depends on the molecule. Including 1s as well would give 10 MOs and 5 antibonding, so read whether the question restricts itself to the valence shell; here it explicitly says "from 2s and 2p", which fixes the answer at 4. The two traps are (i) treating 2p as a single orbital (gives 2) and (ii) forgetting the second atom (gives 2 again).
Answer: 4 — σ*2s, σ*2pz, π*2px and π*2py.
42(JEE Main 2024, 31 Jan Shift-1 · Level-3) The number of species from the following in which the central atom uses sp3 hybrid orbitals in its bonding is ______  NH₃, SO₂, SiO₂, BeCl₂, CO₂, H₂O, CH₄, BF₃Counting sp³ centres by steric numberAssigning sp, sp² and sp³ hybridisation from the electron-domain count🟡 Similar44
Concept Same machinery as Q37, now filtered for steric number 4. An sp³ centre needs four electron domains around it, where a domain is a σ bond or a lone pair. Lone pairs count fully, which is why H₂O (2 bonds + 2 lone pairs) is sp³ despite being bent and NH₃ (3 bonds + 1 lone pair) is sp³ despite being pyramidal. Conversely, π bonds are built from leftover unhybridised p orbitals, so multiple bonding lowers the hybridisation index rather than raising it.
Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs = 4 ⇒ sp³; for a neutral AXn species SN = ½ (VA + nmonovalent) this shortcut ignores multiply-bonded terminal atoms exactly as required, because each of them contributes zero to the formula.
Solution
1. NH₃ — SN = ½(5 + 3) = 4 ⇒ sp³ ✔ (3 bonds + 1 lone pair, trigonal pyramidal).
2. SO₂ — 2 σ + 1 lone pair ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp². ✘
3. SiO₂ — the silica network puts four O around each Si ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp³ ✔. Si–O pπ–pπ overlap is poor because of the large Si radius, so silicon cannot form discrete O=Si=O; it builds an infinite tetrahedral lattice instead. This single fact is why SiO₂ is a hard high-melting solid while CO₂ is a gas.
4. BeCl₂ — 2 σ + 0 lone pairs ⇒ SN = 2 ⇒ sp, linear. ✘
5. CO₂ — 2 σ (each C=O counts once) + 0 lone pairs on C ⇒ SN = 2 ⇒ sp, linear. ✘
6. H₂O — SN = ½(6 + 2) = 4 ⇒ sp³ ✔ (2 bonds + 2 lone pairs, bent).
7. CH₄ — SN = ½(4 + 4) = 4 ⇒ sp³ ✔ (perfect tetrahedron).
8. BF₃ — SN = ½(3 + 3) = 3 ⇒ sp², trigonal planar. ✘
9. Ticks: NH₃, SiO₂, H₂O, CH₄ ⇒ 4.
 species   sigma   lone pairs   SN   hybridisation   shape            sp3?
 NH3         3          1        4        sp3        pyramidal        YES
 SO2         2          1        3        sp2        bent              -
 SiO2        4          0        4        sp3        network tetra.   YES
 BeCl2       2          0        2        sp         linear            -
 CO2         2          0        2        sp         linear            -
 H2O         2          2        4        sp3        bent             YES
 CH4         4          0        4        sp3        tetrahedral      YES
 BF3         3          0        3        sp2        trig. planar      -
                                                     count = 4   <-- answer (4)
⚡ JEE Trick Pair this question with Q37 — the examiners recycle the same eleven-species list and only change "sp²" to "sp³". Build the table once: sp → BeCl₂, CO₂, C₂H₂; sp² → SO₂, BF₃, BCl₃, C₂H₄, C₆H₆, HCHO; sp³ → NH₃, H₂O, CH₄, SiO₂, C₂H₄Cl₂. The single deliberate trap is SiO₂ vs CO₂: they look like twins on paper and hybridise differently. Treating SiO₂ as sp gives the popular wrong answer 3.
Answer: 4 — NH₃, SiO₂, H₂O and CH₄.
43(JEE Main 2024, 01 Feb Shift 1 · Level-3) The number of molecules/ion/s having trigonal bipyramidal shape is ______  PF₅, BrF₅, PCl₅, PtCl₄²⁻, BF₃, Fe(CO)₅VSEPR shape — trigonal bipyramidal geometryHybridisation and geometry of PCl₅ (sp³d, trigonal bipyramidal) and SF₆🟡 Similar33
Concept The question asks for the shape (the arrangement of atoms), not the electron-pair geometry — so a species is trigonal bipyramidal only if it has five bond pairs and zero lone pairs on the central atom. The moment a lone pair appears the five-coordinate skeleton distorts to see-saw (1 lp) or T-shaped (2 lp), and a six-domain centre with a lone pair becomes square pyramidal. A TBP is also the one common geometry whose bonds are not all equivalent: three equatorial at 120° and two axial at 90°, the axial pair being longer and weaker.
Key relation TBP shape ⇔ SN = 5 AND lone pairs = 0 ⇒ sp³d for transition-metal carbonyls the same count applies to the ligand number: 5 ligands with no stereochemically active lone pair also gives TBP, though the hybridisation is described as dsp³.
Solution
1. PF₅ — P has 5 valence e⁻, all five used in P–F bonds ⇒ 5 bp, 0 lp ⇒ sp³d ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal ✔.
2. BrF₅ — Br has 7 valence e⁻, 5 bonded, 2 left ⇒ 5 bp + 1 lp ⇒ SN = 6 ⇒ octahedral electron geometry, square pyramidal shape ✘.
3. PCl₅ — identical count to PF₅ ⇒ 5 bp, 0 lp ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal ✔ (axial P–Cl 219 pm > equatorial 204 pm, which is why it ionises to PCl₄⁺PCl₆⁻ in the solid).
4. PtCl₄²⁻ — a d⁸ Pt(II) complex; four ligands, dsp² hybridisation ⇒ square planar ✘. d⁸ metals of the 4d/5d series are almost always square planar because of the very large Δ splitting — never assume tetrahedral just because there are four ligands.
5. BF₃ — 3 bp, 0 lp ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ trigonal planar ✘ (the deliberate near-miss: "trigonal planar" is not "trigonal bipyramidal").
6. Fe(CO)₅ — five CO ligands, Fe(0), no stereochemically active lone pair ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal ✔.
7. Ticks: PF₅, PCl₅, Fe(CO)₅ ⇒ 3.
 species     domains   bond pairs   lone pairs   hybridisation   shape           TBP?
 PF5            5           5           0            sp3d        trig. bipyr.    YES
 BrF5           6           5           1            sp3d2       square pyramid   -
 PCl5           5           5           0            sp3d        trig. bipyr.    YES
 PtCl4^2-       4           4           0 (d8)       dsp2        square planar    -
 BF3            3           3           0            sp2         trig. planar     -
 Fe(CO)5        5           5           0            dsp3        trig. bipyr.    YES
                                                              count = 3   <-- answer (3)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the five-coordinate family as a ladder driven by lone pairs: 0 lp → TBP (PF₅, PCl₅, Fe(CO)₅, SOF₄), 1 lp → see-saw (SF₄, XeO₂F₂), 2 lp → T-shaped (ClF₃, BrF₃), 3 lp → linear (XeF₂, I₃⁻). Then scan the list once. Two traps are planted: BF₃ reads as "trigonal…" and BrF₅ has the five that your eye is hunting for — but it carries a lone pair, so it is square pyramidal.
Answer: 3 — PF₅, PCl₅ and Fe(CO)₅.
44(JEE Main 2024, 4 Apr Shift-1 · Level-3) Which one of the following molecules has maximum dipole moment?  (a) NF₃  (b) CH₄  (c) PF₅  (d) NH₃Dipole moment — bond moments vs lone-pair momentWhy NH₃ has a much larger dipole moment than NF₃🟡 SimilardNH₃
Concept A molecular dipole is the vector sum of every bond moment plus the moment of each lone pair. Symmetric molecules cancel to zero however polar their individual bonds are, which disposes of CH₄ (Td) and PF₅ (D3h) at a glance. That leaves the classic NH₃ / NF₃ comparison, where both are pyramidal with one lone pair on nitrogen but the relative direction of the lone-pair moment and the bond moments is opposite — reinforcing in NH₃, opposing in NF₃.
Key relation μnet = μlone pair ± Σ μbond; measured values μ(NH₃) = 1.47 D, μ(NF₃) = 0.24 D, μ(CH₄) = μ(PF₅) = 0 the lone-pair moment always points away from the nucleus, out of the apex of the pyramid.
Solution
1. (b) CH₄ — regular tetrahedron; the four C–H bond moments are equal and point to the vertices of a Td figure, so they sum to zero. Rejected.
2. (c) PF₅ — trigonal bipyramidal; the three equatorial P–F moments cancel at 120° and the two axial ones oppose each other exactly ⇒ zero. Rejected. This is why a highly polar-bonded molecule can still be non-polar overall: symmetry, not electronegativity, decides.
3. (a) NF₃ — pyramidal. N is less electronegative than F, so each N→F bond moment points from N towards F, i.e. downwards away from the apex. The lone-pair moment on N points upwards, opposite to the resultant of the three bond moments ⇒ they largely cancel ⇒ small μ = 0.24 D.
4. (d) NH₃ — also pyramidal. Now N is more electronegative than H, so each N–H bond moment points from H towards N, i.e. upwards towards the apex — the same direction as the lone-pair moment. The two contributions add ⇒ large μ = 1.47 D.
5. Ranking: NH₃ (1.47 D) > NF₃ (0.24 D) > CH₄ = PF₅ (0) ⇒ answer (d).
 molecule   shape            symmetry   bond moment direction   lone pair   mu (D)
 CH4        tetrahedral        Td       cancels                    none      0.00
 PF5        trig. bipyramidal  D3h      cancels                    none      0.00
 NF3        pyramidal          C3v      N -> F (downwards)          up       0.24
 NH3        pyramidal          C3v      H -> N (upwards)            up       1.47  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Two-step filter: first kill every molecule whose central atom has no lone pair and all identical substituents (μ = 0 by symmetry) — that removed two options here without any thought. Then, among the survivors, ask whether the lone pair adds to or fights the bond moments. The mnemonic: if the central atom is the more electronegative partner, the lone pair and the bonds pull together and μ is big. The trap is assuming "more electronegative F ⇒ bigger dipole", which sells you option (a).
Answer: (d) NH₃ — bond moments and the lone-pair moment reinforce, giving μ = 1.47 D.
45(JEE Main 2024, 4 Apr Shift-1 · Level-3) Number of molecules/ions from the following in which the central atom is involved in sp3 hybridization is  NO₃⁻, BCl₃, ClO₂⁻, ClO₃⁻  (a) 4  (b) 3  (c) 2  (d) 1Hybridisation of oxyanionsWorking out the hybridisation of nitrogen in NO₂⁺, NO₃⁻ and NH₄⁺🟡 Similarc2
Concept For an ion the steric-number shortcut must account for the charge: an anion has extra electrons on the central atom, which show up as additional lone pairs and therefore push the hybridisation up the sp → sp² → sp³ ladder. The four species here are a deliberately balanced set — two are planar π-delocalised (sp²) and two are pyramidal/bent with lone pairs on chlorine (sp³) — so the answer hinges entirely on counting lone pairs correctly.
Key relation SN = ½ [ VA + (monovalent atoms) − (charge if +) + (charge if −) ], ignoring doubly-bonded O; equivalently SN = σ bonds + lone pairs for oxyanions it is fastest to draw the σ skeleton, then dump all remaining central-atom electrons into lone pairs.
Solution
1. NO₃⁻ — N forms 3 σ to three O and has no lone pair (its fourth electron pair is delocalised into the π system over all three O) ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp², trigonal planar ✘.
2. BCl₃ — B forms 3 σ, is electron deficient with no lone pair ⇒ SN = 3 ⇒ sp², trigonal planar ✘.
3. ClO₂⁻ — Cl has 7 valence e⁻ plus 1 for the negative charge = 8; two are used in the two Cl–O σ bonds, leaving 6 e⁻ = 2 lone pairs, plus the π bonding is handled by the remaining electrons ⇒ σ bonds 2 + lone pairs 2 ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp³, bent ✔.
4. ClO₃⁻ — 3 Cl–O σ bonds and one lone pair left on Cl ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp³, trigonal pyramidal ✔. Contrast this directly with NO₃⁻: both are XO₃⁻, but nitrogen (period 2, no d orbitals, strong pπ–pπ) goes planar sp² while chlorine keeps a stereochemically active lone pair and goes pyramidal sp³.
5. Count of sp³ centres = ClO₂⁻ and ClO₃⁻ = 2 ⇒ option (c). Eliminate (a) 4 — it would require NO₃⁻ and BCl₃ to be pyramidal, which they demonstrably are not; (b) 3 and (d) 1 both mis-sort exactly one chlorine species.
 species    sigma bonds   lone pairs on centre   SN   hybridisation   shape          sp3?
 NO3^-           3                 0              3        sp2       trig. planar     -
 BCl3            3                 0              3        sp2       trig. planar     -
 ClO2^-          2                 2              4        sp3       bent            YES
 ClO3^-          3                 1              4        sp3       trig. pyramidal YES
                                                                     count = 2  <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick The NO₃⁻ / ClO₃⁻ pair is a standing JEE favourite precisely because the formulas look identical. Rule of thumb: period-2 central atoms (B, C, N, O) rarely keep a lone pair in their oxyanions and end up sp²; period-3 and beyond (Cl, S, P, I) keep theirs and end up sp³. Counting the pairs on the halogen is the whole question.
Answer: (c) 2 — ClO₂⁻ and ClO₃⁻.
46(JEE Main 2024, 4 Apr Shift-1 · Level-3) Number of molecules/species from the following having one unpaired electron is ______  O₂, O₂⁻¹, NO, CN⁻¹, O₂²⁻MOT — unpaired electrons in π* orbitalsMO configuration and bond order of the superoxide ion O₂⁻🟡 Similar22
Concept For diatomics with more than 14 electrons the frontier orbitals are the two degenerate antibonding π*2p levels, and it is how these are filled that decides the number of unpaired electrons. Hund's rule forces the electrons to occupy π*x and π*y singly before pairing, so π*¹ ⇒ 1 unpaired, π*² ⇒ 2 unpaired, π*³ ⇒ 1 unpaired, π*⁴ ⇒ 0 unpaired. Every species in this list is simply O₂ with electrons added or removed, except CN⁻ which drops back to the 14-electron closed shell.
Key relation π*2px and π*2py are degenerate ⇒ fill singly first (Hund); unpaired e⁻ = 0 ⇒ diamagnetic, ≥ 1 ⇒ paramagnetic adding electrons to π* both lowers the bond order and changes the magnetism, which is why O₂ → O₂⁻ → O₂²⁻ runs 2 → 1 → 0 unpaired and BO 2 → 1.5 → 1.
Solution
1. O₂ — 16 electrons; valence configuration … σ2pz² π2p⁴ π*2px¹ π*2py¹ ⇒ 2 unpaired ✘ (paramagnetic, BO 2).
2. O₂⁻ (superoxide) — 17 electrons; the extra electron pairs up in one π* ⇒ π*x² π*y¹ ⇒ 1 unpaired ✔ (BO 1.5).
3. NO — 7 + 8 = 15 electrons; one electron sits alone in π* ⇒ 1 unpaired ✔ (BO 2.5, and the reason NO dimerises at low temperature and is easily oxidised to NO⁺).
4. CN⁻ — 6 + 7 + 1 = 14 electrons, isoelectronic with N₂; all levels up to σ2pz are doubly filled and π* is empty ⇒ 0 unpaired ✘ (diamagnetic, BO 3).
5. O₂²⁻ (peroxide) — 18 electrons; π*x² π*y² completely full ⇒ 0 unpaired ✘ (diamagnetic, BO 1).
6. Species with exactly one unpaired electron: O₂⁻ and NO ⇒ 2.
 species   total e-   pi* occupancy      unpaired e-   bond order   magnetism
 O2           16       pi*x1 pi*y1            2           2.0       para
 O2^-         17       pi*x2 pi*y1            1           1.5       para   YES
 NO           15       pi*x1                  1           2.5       para   YES
 CN^-         14       pi* empty              0           3.0       dia
 O2^2-        18       pi*x2 pi*y2            0           1.0       dia
                                       count with exactly 1 unpaired = 2  <-- answer (2)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not draw five MO diagrams — count electrons and read off the π* filling: 14 e⁻ → 0 unpaired, 15 → 1, 16 → 2, 17 → 1, 18 → 0. The whole question becomes an electron tally. The trap is O₂ itself: it is the most famous paramagnetic molecule, so candidates tick it reflexively — but it has two unpaired electrons, not one, and the question said "one".
Answer: 2 — O₂⁻ and NO.
47(JEE Main 2024, 5 Apr Shift-2 · Level-3) Number of compounds from the following with zero dipole moment is ______  HF, H₂, H₂S, CO₂, NH₃, BF₃, CH₄, CHCl₃, SiF₄, H₂O, BeF₂Dipole moment and molecular symmetryCounting how many compounds in a list have zero dipole moment🟡 Similar66
Concept Zero dipole moment is a symmetry statement, not a bonding-polarity statement. If the molecule has a symmetric arrangement of identical substituents around a central atom carrying no lone pair, every bond moment is matched by an equal and opposite partner and the vector sum vanishes — even when each individual bond is strongly polar (SiF₄ is the extreme case here). Conversely, any lone pair on the central atom, or any substitution that breaks the symmetry, leaves a residual moment. Homonuclear diatomics are trivially non-polar because the bond itself has no charge separation.
Key relation μ = 0 ⇔ the molecule has a centre / axis of symmetry that maps every bond moment onto an equal opposite one; practically μ = 0 ⇔ no lone pair on the central atom AND all peripheral atoms identical the "no lone pair" clause is what separates CH₄ (μ = 0) from NH₃ and H₂O (μ ≠ 0), even though all three are AX-type with identical peripheral atoms.
Solution
1. HF — heteronuclear diatomic, ΔEN large ⇒ μ ≈ 1.91 D ⇒ polar ✘.
2. H₂ — homonuclear, identical atoms share the pair equally ⇒ μ = 0 ✔.
3. H₂S — bent (2 lone pairs on S) ⇒ moments do not cancel ⇒ polar ✘ (μ ≈ 0.95 D).
4. CO₂ — linear O=C=O, the two C=O moments are collinear and opposite ⇒ μ = 0 ✔.
5. NH₃ — pyramidal, lone pair on N ⇒ polar ✘ (μ = 1.47 D).
6. BF₃ — trigonal planar, three moments at 120° ⇒ resultant zero ⇒ μ = 0 ✔.
7. CH₄ — regular tetrahedron ⇒ μ = 0 ✔.
8. CHCl₃ — tetrahedral but with one H replacing a Cl, so the symmetry is broken ⇒ polar ✘ (μ ≈ 1.04 D).
9. SiF₄ — regular tetrahedron of very polar Si–F bonds; symmetry still cancels them all ⇒ μ = 0 ✔. The best illustration of the principle: the most polar bonds in the list produce the least polar molecule.
10. H₂O — bent, two lone pairs ⇒ polar ✘ (μ = 1.85 D).
11. BeF₂ — linear F–Be–F in the vapour phase, no lone pair on Be ⇒ μ = 0 ✔.
12. Ticks: H₂, CO₂, BF₃, CH₄, SiF₄, BeF₂ ⇒ 6.
 compound   geometry            lone pairs on centre   moments cancel?   mu
 HF         diatomic (hetero)          -                    no          1.91
 H2         diatomic (homo)            -                   yes (no bond moment)  0   YES
 H2S        bent                       2                    no          0.95
 CO2        linear                     0                   yes           0        YES
 NH3        pyramidal                  1                    no          1.47
 BF3        trigonal planar            0                   yes           0        YES
 CH4        tetrahedral                0                   yes           0        YES
 CHCl3      distorted tetrahedral      0 (unlike atoms)     no          1.04
 SiF4       tetrahedral                0                   yes           0        YES
 H2O        bent                       2                    no          1.85
 BeF2       linear                     0                   yes           0        YES
                                                              count = 6   <-- answer (6)
⚡ JEE Trick Sweep the list with one yes/no test — "lone pair on the central atom, or mixed substituents?" If either is true the molecule is polar; otherwise μ = 0. Homonuclear diatomics are automatic zeros. The three traps planted here are SiF₄ (very polar bonds, zero molecule), CHCl₃ (looks tetrahedral, isn't symmetric) and BeF₂ (looks ionic, but as a vapour it is a linear covalent AX₂ with no lone pair). Miscount any one and you land on 5 or 7.
Answer: 6 — H₂, CO₂, BF₃, CH₄, SiF₄ and BeF₂.
48(JEE Main 2024, 27 Jan Shift 1 · Level-2) Choose the polar molecule from the following:  (a) CCl₄  (b) CO₂  (c) CH₂=CH₂  (d) CHCl₃Polarity from symmetry breakingWhy the dipole moment order runs CH₃Cl > CH₂Cl₂ > CHCl₃ > CCl₄🟡 SimilardCHCl₃
Concept Three of these four molecules belong to high-symmetry point groups in which every bond moment has an exact opposite partner, so the resultant is rigorously zero. Only CHCl₃ breaks that symmetry: replacing one chlorine of CCl₄ by a hydrogen removes the cancelling partner of the remaining three C–Cl moments, leaving a resultant along the C–H axis. Note that polarity here is decided entirely by geometry — CCl₄ and CHCl₃ contain the very same kind of polar C–Cl bond.
Key relation μ = |Σ μbond|; for a tetrahedral CX₄, Σ μ = 0 by Td symmetry, whereas for CHX₃ μ ≠ 0 along the C–H axis in CHCl₃ the three C→Cl moments have a resultant equal in size to one C–Cl moment pointing away from H, and the small C→H moment adds to rather than cancels it.
Solution
1. (a) CCl₄ — regular tetrahedron, four identical polar C–Cl bonds pointing to the Td vertices ⇒ vector sum zero ⇒ non-polar. Rejected.
2. (b) CO₂ — linear, the two C=O moments are equal, collinear and opposed ⇒ zero ⇒ non-polar. Rejected.
3. (c) CH₂=CH₂ — planar and centrosymmetric; every C–H bond has an equivalent partner related by the inversion centre ⇒ zero ⇒ non-polar. Rejected. Contrast with cis-1,2-dichloroethene, which has no inversion centre and therefore is polar, while the trans isomer is not — the standard follow-up question.
4. (d) CHCl₃ — one substituent differs, so the molecule has only C3v symmetry. The three C–Cl moments give a resultant along the C–H axis that is not cancelled ⇒ μ ≈ 1.04 D ⇒ polar. Answer (d).
 molecule   geometry                 symmetry   equal-and-opposite partners?   mu (D)
 CCl4       tetrahedral                Td       yes, all four                   0.00
 CO2        linear                     D(inf)h  yes, both                       0.00
 CH2=CH2    planar, centrosymmetric    D2h      yes, all C-H                    0.00
 CHCl3      distorted tetrahedral      C3v      NO - H breaks the set           1.04  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Look for a centre of symmetry or a full set of identical substituents; if the molecule has one, μ = 0 and you can strike it out without any calculation. Only one option in a "choose the polar molecule" MCQ ever survives that filter. The trap is the intuition that "more chlorines ⇒ more polar", which points you at CCl₄; in fact the dipole order is CH₃Cl (1.87) > CH₂Cl₂ (1.60) > CHCl₃ (1.04) > CCl₄ (0), decreasing as symmetry is restored.
Answer: (d) CHCl₃ — the only molecule in the set whose bond moments do not cancel.
49(JEE Main 2024, 29 Jan Shift 2 · Level-2) The total number of molecules with zero dipole moment among CH₄, BF₃, H₂O, HF, NH₃, CO₂ and SO₂ is ______Zero dipole moment from molecular symmetryCounting the zero-dipole molecules in a mixed list — NH₃, BF₃, NF₃, CCl₄🟡 Similar33
Concept Dipole moment is a vector quantity: the molecular moment is the vector sum of every individual bond moment plus the moment contributed by any lone pair on the central atom. A molecule can therefore be built entirely from polar bonds and still be completely non-polar, provided each bond moment finds an exactly equal and opposite partner. That cancellation happens only when two conditions hold together — (i) every terminal atom attached to the central atom is identical, and (ii) the geometry is a regular ABn arrangement, which in practice means the central atom carries no stereochemically active lone pair. One lone pair is enough to bend the molecule and destroy the cancellation, which is exactly why CO₂ has μ = 0 while its look-alike SO₂ does not.
Key relation μnet = |Σ μbond + μlone pair| and μnet = 0 ⟺ regular geometry + all terminal atoms identical + zero lone pairs on the central atom a centre of symmetry (or a proper axis with n ≥ 2 that maps each bond onto an opposite one) guarantees the vector sum vanishes, so you never have to add anything numerically.
Solution
1. CH₄ — steric number 4, zero lone pairs, regular tetrahedron with four identical C–H bonds. The four bond moments point to the corners of a tetrahedron and sum to zero ⇒ μ = 0. ✔
2. BF₃ — boron has only 3 bond pairs and no lone pair (incomplete octet), so the geometry is a regular trigonal plane. Three identical B–F moments at 120° cancel ⇒ μ = 0. ✔Two of the moments resolve to a resultant of magnitude μ along the third bond's reverse direction; adding the third gives exactly zero.
3. H₂O — oxygen has 2 bond pairs + 2 lone pairs, so the molecule is bent at 104.5°. The two O–H moments add to a non-zero resultant that the lone-pair moment reinforces ⇒ μ = 1.85 D. ✘
4. HF — a heteronuclear diatomic has a single bond moment with nothing to cancel it ⇒ μ = 1.91 D. ✘Any A–B diatomic with A ≠ B is polar; only homonuclear diatomics (H₂, N₂, Cl₂) are non-polar.
5. NH₃ — 3 bond pairs + 1 lone pair ⇒ trigonal pyramidal. The resultant of the three N–H moments points from N towards the H₃ base and the lone-pair moment points the opposite way along the same axis, but the two are unequal ⇒ μ = 1.47 D. ✘
6. CO₂ — carbon is sp hybridised with no lone pair, giving a linear symmetric O=C=O. The two C=O moments are equal and antiparallel ⇒ μ = 0. ✔
7. SO₂ — sulphur is sp² with one lone pair, so the molecule is bent at ≈119°. The two S=O moments no longer oppose each other ⇒ μ = 1.63 D. ✘
8. Zero-dipole molecules = CH₄, BF₃, CO₂ ⇒ count = 3.
 molecule   central atom   b.p. / l.p.   shape              cancellation?     mu (D)
 CH4        C               4 / 0        tetrahedral        yes (Td)          0.00   *
 BF3        B               3 / 0        trigonal planar    yes (D3h)         0.00   *
 H2O        O               2 / 2        bent 104.5         no                1.85
 HF         -               1 / 3        linear diatomic    nothing to cancel 1.91
 NH3        N               3 / 1        pyramidal          no                1.47
 CO2        C               2 / 0        linear             yes (D(inf)h)     0.00   *
 SO2        S               2 / 1        bent ~119          no                1.63
                                                    zero-dipole count = 3   <-- answer (3)
⚡ JEE Trick Run a two-second filter: write the steric number, and if the central atom has even one lone pair, the molecule is polar (the only exceptions are the symmetric lone-pair cases XeF₂, XeF₄, I₃⁻ and the trans-type AB₄E₂ / AB₃E₂ shapes, none of which appear here). Everything left over with identical terminal atoms is non-polar. The trap this question sets is the CO₂/SO₂ pair and the BF₃/NH₃ pair — both look like "same formula type, same answer", but in each pair one central atom has a lone pair and the other does not, so they land on opposite sides. A second trap is HF: students strike it out as a "simple" molecule when it is in fact the most polar species in the list.
Answer: 3 — CH₄, BF₃ and CO₂.
50(JEE Main 2024, 30 Jan Shift 1 · Level-2) The total number of molecular orbitals formed from 2s and 2p atomic orbitals of a diatomic molecule is ______LCAO — conservation of orbital countTotal number of molecular orbitals formed from a given set of atomic orbitals🟢 Exact88
Concept Molecular orbitals are built by the Linear Combination of Atomic Orbitals, and LCAO is nothing more than a change of basis: every in-phase (additive) combination that produces a bonding MO is inevitably accompanied by an out-of-phase (subtractive) combination that produces the matching antibonding MO. Because the mathematics is a one-to-one transformation, the number of molecular orbitals produced is always exactly equal to the number of atomic orbitals fed in — orbitals are neither created nor destroyed, only relabelled and re-energised. The question therefore reduces to counting atomic orbitals, not electrons, and not shells.
Key relation no. of MOs = no. of AOs combined, and for two atoms NMO = 2 × (orbitals per atom) = Nbonding + Nantibonding, with Nbonding = Nantibonding the 2s subshell holds 1 orbital and the 2p subshell holds 3 (px, py, pz), so each atom brings 4.
Solution
1. Count the atomic orbitals on one atom: 2s → 1 orbital; 2p → 3 orbitals (2px, 2py, 2pz). Total = 4.
2. A diatomic molecule has two such atoms ⇒ total atomic orbitals entering the combination = 4 × 2 = 8.
3. Apply orbital conservation: 8 AOs in ⇒ 8 MOs out.
4. Check the split: they come out as 4 bonding (σ2s, σ2pz, π2px, π2py) and 4 antibonding (σ*2s, σ*2pz, π*2px, π*2py), which is the standard second-row MO diagram.Head-on overlap along the internuclear axis gives σ/σ*; the two perpendicular p orbitals overlap sideways and give the degenerate π/π* pairs.
5. Eliminate the traps: 4 is the count for one atom only; 4 is also the number of bonding MOs alone; 16 comes from confusing orbitals with the electrons they can hold. The answer is 8.
 atomic orbitals in                molecular orbitals out
 atom A : 2s, 2px, 2py, 2pz  = 4   bonding    : sigma-2s, sigma-2pz, pi-2px, pi-2py   = 4
 atom B : 2s, 2px, 2py, 2pz  = 4   antibonding: sigma*2s, sigma*2pz, pi*2px, pi*2py   = 4
