 ----------------------------      -----------------------------------------------
 total AOs                   = 8   total MOs                                       = 8   <-- answer (8)
⚡ JEE Trick Chant "orbitals in = orbitals out" and the whole family of these questions collapses into one line of arithmetic. Useful sub-counts to have ready: s → 1, p → 3, d → 5, f → 7 orbitals per subshell; and for a homonuclear diatomic the bonding and antibonding sets are always equal in size, so the number of bonding MOs is simply half the total. The trap in this exact question is the word "diatomic" — half the candidates count the orbitals of a single atom and answer 4, and another group multiplies by 2 electrons per orbital and answers 16.
Answer: 8 molecular orbitals (4 bonding + 4 antibonding).
51(JEE Main 2024, 31 Jan Shift 2 · Level-2) A diatomic molecule has a dipole moment of 1.2 D. If the bond distance is 1 Å, then fractional charge on each atom is ______ ×10⁻¹ esu. (Given 1 D = 10⁻¹⁸ esu cm)μ = q × d in esu unitsComputing the charge on each atom from a dipole moment and a 1 Å separation🟡 Similar00
Concept In a polar diatomic the more electronegative atom carries a partial charge −q and the other +q, separated by the bond length d, so the molecule behaves exactly like an electric dipole of moment μ = q·d. The charge that comes out of this is a fractional charge: it is smaller than one electronic charge because the bonding pair is only partially transferred, and the ratio q/e is precisely the percentage ionic character of the bond. The whole calculation lives in the CGS-esu system here, so the two conversions you need are 1 D = 10⁻¹⁸ esu·cm and 1 Å = 10⁻⁸ cm.
Formula μ = q × d ⇒ q = μ / d, with 1 D = 10⁻¹⁸ esu·cm and 1 Å = 10⁻⁸ cm dividing 10⁻¹⁸ by 10⁻⁸ always leaves 10⁻¹⁰ esu, which is why a charge of order 10⁻¹⁰ esu is the natural scale for every question of this type — the electronic charge itself is 4.8 × 10⁻¹⁰ esu.
Solution
1. Convert the dipole moment: μ = 1.2 D = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁸ esu·cm.
2. Convert the bond distance: d = 1 Å = 1 × 10⁻⁸ cm.
3. Apply μ = q·d ⇒ q = (1.2 × 10⁻¹⁸ esu·cm) / (1 × 10⁻⁸ cm) = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁰ esu.
4. The blank is measured in units of 10⁻¹ esu, so divide once more: x = (1.2 × 10⁻¹⁰) / (10⁻¹) = 1.2 × 10⁻⁹.
5. An integer-type answer must be reported to the nearest integer, and 1.2 × 10⁻⁹ rounds to 0 ⇒ the answer keyed by NTA is 0.Physically the exponent printed in the paper is an order-of-magnitude slip — with the blank written as "___ × 10⁻¹⁰ esu" the intended entry would be 1.2. Solve what is printed, round as instructed, and you get 0; this is the officially released key.
6. Sanity check on the chemistry: fractional/ionic character = q/e = (1.2 × 10⁻¹⁰)/(4.8 × 10⁻¹⁰) = 0.25 = 25 %, a perfectly reasonable value for a moderately polar bond.
 quantity            value given      converted
 dipole moment mu    1.2 D            1.2 x 10^-18 esu.cm
 bond distance d     1 Angstrom       1.0 x 10^-08 cm
 charge q = mu/d                      1.2 x 10^-10 esu
 expressed as x x 10^-1 esu           x = 1.2 x 10^-9   -> nearest integer 0   <-- answer (0)
 cross-check: q/e = 1.2e-10 / 4.8e-10 = 0.25 = 25 % ionic character
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the single benchmark "one full electronic charge across 1 Å = 4.8 D". Then for any diatomic with d = 1 Å you can read the ionic character straight off: % ionic = μ(D)/4.8 × 100, so 1.2 D is instantly 25 % without touching a calculator. The trap here is purely arithmetic bookkeeping — candidates correctly reach 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁰ esu and then write "1.2" or "12" in the box because they never look at the power of ten printed next to the blank. Always finish by dividing your answer by the unit the blank actually asks for.
Answer: 0 (q = 1.2 × 10⁻¹⁰ esu, i.e. 1.2 × 10⁻⁹ in units of 10⁻¹ esu, which rounds to 0).
52(JEE Main 2024, 5 Apr Shift 1 · Level-2) In the Lewis dot structure for NO₂⁻, total number of valence electrons around nitrogen is ______Lewis structure of NO₂⁻ and the octet countDrawing Lewis dot structures for polyatomic ions — counting valence electrons correctly🟡 Similar88
Concept Building a Lewis structure is a strict electron budget. First total up the valence electrons (adding one for each unit of negative charge), then spend them: one pair per skeleton bond, then lone pairs on the terminal atoms to complete their octets, then whatever is left on the central atom, and finally convert terminal lone pairs into multiple bonds if the central atom is still short. "Valence electrons around nitrogen" then means every electron in nitrogen's own valence shell — both electrons of every bond it makes (bonding electrons are shared, so each bonded atom counts all of them for octet purposes) plus its own lone pairs. Nitrogen is a period-2 atom with no accessible d orbitals, so this count can never exceed 8.
Key relation Total valence e⁻ (NO₂⁻) = 5 + 2(6) + 1 = 18; electrons around an atom = 2 × (no. of bonds) + 2 × (no. of lone pairs) this is the octet count, not the formal-charge count — for formal charge you take only half the bonding electrons, which is what makes students answer 5 or 6 instead of 8.
Solution
1. Budget: N contributes 5, each O contributes 6, and the 1− charge adds 1 ⇒ 18 valence electrons = 9 pairs.
2. Draw the skeleton O–N–O: 2 single bonds consume 2 pairs, leaving 7 pairs.
3. Complete both oxygen octets: each needs 3 lone pairs ⇒ 6 pairs used, leaving 1 pair, which goes on nitrogen.
4. Count around N at this stage: 2 bonds (4 e⁻) + 1 lone pair (2 e⁻) = 6 e⁻ — nitrogen is two electrons short of an octet.
5. Fix it by shifting one lone pair of an oxygen into the bond, creating one N=O double bond. The final structure is O=N–O⁻ with one lone pair on N (and the two resonance forms in which the double bond sits on either oxygen).The resonance hybrid has two identical N–O bonds of order 1.5 and a bond angle of ≈115°, consistent with sp² nitrogen carrying one lone pair.
6. Now count around nitrogen: double bond = 4 e⁻, single bond = 2 e⁻, lone pair = 2 e⁻ ⇒ 4 + 2 + 2 = 8 electrons.
 step                              e- pairs left   electrons around N
 total valence = 5 + 6 + 6 + 1 = 18 (9 pairs)          9              -
 skeleton O-N-O (2 sigma bonds)                        7              4
 octets completed on both O (6 l.p.)                   1              4
 last lone pair placed on N                            0              6   (short of octet)
 one O lone pair -> N=O pi bond                        0              8   <-- answer (8)
 final: O=N-O(-) with 1 lone pair on N ; resonance gives N-O order 1.5
⚡ JEE Trick For any species whose central atom is from period 2 (B, C, N, O, F) the answer to "how many valence electrons around the central atom" is 8 unless the atom is electron-deficient or odd-electron — so BF₃ gives 6, NO gives 7, NO₂ gives 7, but NO₂⁻, NO₃⁻, CO₃²⁻ and NH₄⁺ all give 8. Adding the electron that converts NO₂ (odd, 17 e⁻) into NO₂⁻ (even, 18 e⁻) is exactly what completes nitrogen's octet. The trap is confusing this octet count with the formal-charge count: formal charge on N here is 5 − 2 − ½(6) = 0, and the "5" or "6" that appears in that calculation is what wrong answers report.
Answer: 8 valence electrons around nitrogen (one N=O, one N–O and one lone pair).
53(JEE Main 2024, 6 Apr Shift 1 · Level-2) Number of molecules from the following which can exhibit hydrogen bonding is ______ (nearest integer)  CH₃OH, H₂O, C₂H₆, C₆H₆, o-nitrophenol (2-nitrophenol), HF, NH₃Which molecules can hydrogen bondDeciding which compounds in a list can actually form hydrogen bonds🟡 Similar55
Concept A hydrogen bond is a strong dipole–dipole attraction that needs two things at once: a hydrogen atom covalently bonded to a small, highly electronegative atom — F, O or N (so that H is stripped almost bare and behaves as a concentrated point of positive charge), and a nearby atom carrying a lone pair to act as the acceptor. The "small" part matters as much as the electronegativity: Cl is as electronegative as N, yet HCl shows negligible hydrogen bonding because chlorine's charge is spread over a much larger volume. Carbon is neither electronegative enough nor a lone-pair donor, so C–H bonds are ruled out entirely. The bond may be intermolecular (between molecules) or intramolecular (within one molecule) — the question asks only whether hydrogen bonding is exhibited, so both count.
Key relation H-bond ⟺ H–F, H–O or H–N present AND a lone pair available on F/O/N strength order F–H⋯F > O–H⋯O > N–H⋯N, following the electronegativity of the donor atom.
Solution
1. CH₃OH — contains an O–H bond; the oxygen also carries two lone pairs, so methanol is both donor and acceptor ⇒ yes. ✔
2. H₂O — two O–H bonds and two lone pairs; each molecule can form up to four hydrogen bonds ⇒ yes. ✔
3. C₂H₆ — only C–H and C–C bonds; carbon is not electronegative enough and has no lone pair ⇒ no. ✘
4. C₆H₆ — aromatic, but again only C–H bonds; the π cloud can accept a weak C–H⋯π interaction, which is not a hydrogen bond in the F/O/N sense ⇒ no. ✘
5. o-nitrophenol — the phenolic O–H sits right next to the –NO₂ group, and the ring geometry lets the H reach an oxygen of the nitro group, closing a six-membered intramolecular O–H⋯O ring (a chelate) ⇒ yes. ✔This is exactly why o-nitrophenol is steam-volatile and melts lower than the p isomer, which is locked into an intermolecular network instead.
6. HF — the strongest hydrogen-bond donor of all; HF exists as zig-zag (HF)n chains ⇒ yes. ✔
7. NH₃ — three N–H bonds and one lone pair on nitrogen ⇒ yes, and this is why NH₃ boils far above PH₃. ✔
8. Count of "yes" entries = CH₃OH, H₂O, o-nitrophenol, HF, NH₃ ⇒ 5.
 molecule          H attached to    lone pair available   H-bonding?   type
 CH3OH             O                yes (on O)            YES          intermolecular   *
 H2O               O                yes (2 on O)          YES          intermolecular   *
 C2H6              C only           no                    no           -
 C6H6              C only           no (only pi cloud)    no           -
 o-nitrophenol     O (phenolic)     yes (nitro O)         YES          INTRAmolecular   *
 HF                F                yes (3 on F)          YES          intermolecular   *
 NH3               N                yes (1 on N)          YES          intermolecular   *
                                                  total = 5   <-- answer (5)
⚡ JEE Trick Scan the list for the letters F, O, N with an H stuck directly on them — nothing else can donate a hydrogen bond, so hydrocarbons, ethers, ketones and haloalkanes are struck out instantly (they may accept, but they cannot form H-bonds among themselves). Two traps are planted here: benzene, which students admit because it "has a π cloud and is special", and o-nitrophenol, which students reject because its hydrogen bond is intramolecular. Unless the question says "intermolecular", intramolecular hydrogen bonding counts — dropping it gives the popular wrong answer 4.
Answer: 5 — CH₃OH, H₂O, o-nitrophenol, HF and NH₃.
54(JEE Advanced 2024, Paper-1 · Level-2) The option(s) in which at least three molecules follow the Octet Rule is(are)  (a) CO₂, C₂H₄, NO and HCl  (b) NO₂, O₃, HCl and H₂SO₄  (c) BCl₃, NO, NO₂ and H₂SO₄  (d) CO₂, BCl₃, O₃ and C₂H₄Octet rule and its three classes of exceptionThe octet rule and its limitations — incomplete, odd-electron and expanded octets🟡 Similara, d(a) CO₂, C₂H₄, NO, HCl and (d) CO₂, BCl₃, O₃, C₂H₄
Concept The octet rule fails in exactly three ways, and every molecule in this question is placed to test one of them. (1) Incomplete octet — central atoms from groups 2 and 13 (Be, B, Al) settle for 4 or 6 electrons: BeCl₂, BCl₃, AlCl₃. (2) Odd-electron (hypovalent radical) species — if the total valence-electron count is odd, no arrangement can pair everything, so one atom is left with 7: NO (11 e⁻), NO₂ (17 e⁻), ClO₂. (3) Expanded octet — central atoms from period 3 onwards have energetically accessible d orbitals and can hold 10, 12 or more: PCl₅, SF₆, H₂SO₄, H₃PO₄. Everything not in one of those three boxes obeys the octet. Note that hydrogen is judged against a duplet, not an octet, so H–Cl and the C–H bonds of C₂H₄ are fine.
Key relation Odd total valence electrons ⇒ octet rule must break; central atom in period ≥ 3 with more than 4 σ bonds or with π bonds to O ⇒ expanded octet; group 13 central atom with 3 bonds ⇒ incomplete octet (6 e⁻) for H₂SO₄ the conventional structure has two S=O double bonds, putting 12 electrons on sulphur — this is the structure JEE uses when it asks about octets.
Solution
1. Classify every distinct molecule once. Obey: CO₂ (C: 4 bonds = 8 e⁻), C₂H₄ (each C: 8 e⁻, each H: duplet), HCl (Cl: 8 e⁻, H: duplet), O₃ (central O: one σ + one π + one lone pair = 8 e⁻). Break: NO (11 valence e⁻ — odd, N ends with 7), NO₂ (17 valence e⁻ — odd), BCl₃ (B has only 6), H₂SO₄ (S expands to 12).
2. (a) CO₂ ✔, C₂H₄ ✔, NO ✘, HCl ✔ ⇒ 3 molecules obey — "at least three" is satisfied ⇒ (a) is correct.
3. (b) NO₂ ✘, O₃ ✔, HCl ✔, H₂SO₄ ✘ ⇒ only 2 obey ⇒ (b) is rejected.This is the option that decides the question: if you (wrongly) draw H₂SO₄ with four single bonds and formal charges so that S keeps an octet, (b) would also reach three and the answer would change. The official JEE Advanced key takes the expanded-octet structure, so (b) fails.
4. (c) BCl₃ ✘, NO ✘, NO₂ ✘, H₂SO₄ ✘ ⇒ 0 obey — this option is a clean sweep of all three exception types ⇒ rejected.
5. (d) CO₂ ✔, BCl₃ ✘, O₃ ✔, C₂H₄ ✔ ⇒ 3 obey ⇒ (d) is correct.
6. Correct options: (a) and (d).
 molecule   valence e-   verdict                          reason
 CO2        16 (even)    obeys                            C = 8, each O = 8
 C2H4       12 (even)    obeys                            each C = 8, H = duplet
 HCl         8 (even)    obeys                            Cl = 8, H = duplet
 O3         18 (even)    obeys                            central O = 8 (1 sigma + 1 pi + 1 l.p.)
 NO         11 (ODD)     breaks - odd electron            N ends with 7 e-
 NO2        17 (ODD)     breaks - odd electron            N ends with 7 e-
 BCl3       24           breaks - incomplete octet        B = 6 e-
 H2SO4      32           breaks - expanded octet          S = 12 e-

 option   molecules                   obey / total   at least 3 ?
 (a)      CO2, C2H4, NO, HCl              3 / 4          YES     <-- answer
 (b)      NO2, O3, HCl, H2SO4             2 / 4          no
 (c)      BCl3, NO, NO2, H2SO4            0 / 4          no
 (d)      CO2, BCl3, O3, C2H4             3 / 4          YES     <-- answer
⚡ JEE Trick Add up the valence electrons first: an odd total is an instant "breaks the octet", no drawing required (NO = 5 + 6 = 11; NO₂ = 5 + 12 = 17). Then apply two one-line filters — group 13 central atom ⇒ incomplete; period-3-or-below central atom bonded to several oxygens ⇒ expanded. In this question all four options contain exactly four molecules, so you only ever need to find two violators to kill an option, which makes (c) and (b) fall in seconds. The trap is H₂SO₄: it looks like an ordinary well-behaved acid, and treating it as octet-obeying is precisely what turns the answer from (a, d) into the wrong (a, b, d).
Answer: (a) and (d) — each contains exactly three octet-obeying molecules.
55(JEE Main 2024, 30 Jan Shift 1 · Level-1) Match List-I with List-II.  List-I (Molecule): A. BrF₅  B. H₂O  C. ClF₃  D. SF₄   List-II (Shape): i. T-shape  ii. See saw  iii. Bent  iv. Square pyramidal  — Choose the correct answer:  (a) A-i, B-ii, C-iv, D-iii  (b) A-ii, B-i, C-iii, D-iv  (c) A-iii, B-iv, C-i, D-ii  (d) A-iv, B-iii, C-i, D-iiVSEPR shapes of AB₅E, AB₂E₂, AB₃E₂ and AB₄EReading molecular geometry and shape straight off the VSEPR electron count🟡 Similarc(c) A-iii, B-iv, C-i, D-ii ⚠
Concept VSEPR works in two stages that students constantly merge into one. First count the steric number SN = (σ bonds) + (lone pairs) on the central atom; that fixes the electron-pair geometry (2 linear, 3 trigonal planar, 4 tetrahedral, 5 trigonal bipyramidal, 6 octahedral). Then delete the lone pairs and look at where the atoms alone sit; that gives the molecular shape, which is the thing the question is asking for. Lone pairs are fatter than bond pairs (lp–lp > lp–bp > bp–bp), so in a trigonal bipyramid every lone pair goes equatorial and in an octahedron the second lone pair goes trans to the first — those two placement rules generate the see-saw, T-shape, square-pyramidal and square-planar shapes.
Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs, then AB₅E → square pyramidal, AB₄E → see-saw, AB₃E₂ → T-shape, AB₂E₂ → bent for a main-group ABn species, lone pairs = ½[V − n] with V the central atom's valence electrons, so BrF₅ gives ½[7 − 5] = 1 lone pair and ClF₃ gives ½[7 − 3] = 2.
Solution
1. A. BrF₅ — Br has 7 valence electrons, 5 are used in bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 5 + 1 = 6 ⇒ octahedral electron geometry; removing one lone pair from a corner leaves five atoms in a square pyramid (sp³d², ≈84.8°) ⇒ A–iv.
2. B. H₂O — O has 6 valence electrons, 2 in bonds ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 2 + 2 = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral electron geometry; two positions are lone pairs, so the three atoms describe a bent shape (104.5°) ⇒ B–iii.
3. C. ClF₃ — Cl has 7 valence electrons, 3 in bonds ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 3 + 2 = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal; both lone pairs take equatorial sites, leaving two axial and one equatorial fluorine ⇒ T-shape (≈87.5°) ⇒ C–i.
4. D. SF₄ — S has 6 valence electrons, 4 in bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 4 + 1 = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal; the single lone pair takes an equatorial site ⇒ see-saw (distorted tetrahedron) ⇒ D–ii.
5. Assembling: A–iv, B–iii, C–i, D–ii, which is option (d).
6. Eliminate the rest: (a) makes BrF₅ a T-shape and ClF₃ square pyramidal — both impossible, since a T-shape needs SN 5 with 2 lone pairs and BrF₅ has SN 6. (b) makes BrF₅ a see-saw (needs only 4 bonds) and SF₄ square pyramidal (needs 5 bonds) — the bond counts alone rule it out. (c) makes BrF₅ bent and H₂O square pyramidal, which is not physically possible for a two-bond, two-lone-pair oxygen.
 molecule   central atom   bonds   lone pairs   SN   electron geometry     SHAPE              List-II
 BrF5       Br (7 e-)        5          1        6   octahedral            square pyramidal   iv
 H2O        O  (6 e-)        2          2        4   tetrahedral           bent               iii
 ClF3       Cl (7 e-)        3          2        5   trigonal bipyramidal  T-shape            i
 SF4        S  (6 e-)        4          1        5   trigonal bipyramidal  see-saw            ii
                                                     A-iv, B-iii, C-i, D-ii   <-- answer (d)
⚠ Note The printed answer key gives (c) for this question, i.e. A-iii, B-iv, C-i, D-ii — which would make BrF₅ bent and H₂O square pyramidal. Neither is possible: a bent shape requires a central atom with two bond pairs, and a square pyramid requires five, whereas BrF₅ has five bonds and H₂O has two. Note that options (c) and (d) differ only in the A and B entries — both agree on C–i and D–ii — so this is a straightforward transcription slip in the key. The official NTA answer for JEE Main 2024 (30 Jan, Shift 1) is (d), and the derivation above is unambiguous. The key letter (c) is displayed for reference, but use (d).
⚡ JEE Trick For a main-group ABn molecule get the lone pairs in one step with l.p. = ½[V − n] (V = valence electrons of the central atom, n = number of single-bonded terminal atoms): BrF₅ ⇒ ½(7−5) = 1, ClF₃ ⇒ ½(7−3) = 2, SF₄ ⇒ ½(6−4) = 1. Then the shape follows from a four-line table you should know cold: SN 5 with 1 lp = see-saw, with 2 lp = T-shape, with 3 lp = linear; SN 6 with 1 lp = square pyramidal, with 2 lp = square planar. The trap in matching questions like this is that the distractors swap only two entries, so confirming a single molecule is never enough — here checking only ClF₃ and SF₄ leaves (c) and (d) both alive, and you must resolve BrF₅ or H₂O to finish.
Answer (derived): (d) A-iv, B-iii, C-i, D-ii. Printed key: (c) — flagged as an error above.
56(JEE Main 2024, 31 Jan Shift 1 · Level-1) The linear combination of atomic orbitals to form molecular orbitals takes place only when the combining atomic orbitals  A. have the same energy  B. have the minimum overlap  C. have same symmetry about the molecular axis  D. have different symmetry about the molecular axis.  Choose the most appropriate answer:  (a) A, B, C only  (b) A and C only  (c) B, C, D only  (d) B and D onlyConditions for LCAO / effective orbital combinationThe conditions atomic orbitals must satisfy before they can combine into MOs🟢 Exactb(b) A and C only
Concept Two atomic orbitals mix into a bonding/antibonding pair only if the mixing actually lowers the energy of the system, and NCERT states three conditions for that. (1) Comparable energy — if one orbital lies far below the other, the "combination" is really just the lower orbital in disguise and no net stabilisation results (this is why the 1s of hydrogen combines with the 2p of fluorine in HF, not with fluorine's 1s). (2) Appreciable — indeed maximum — overlap, because the stabilisation energy is directly proportional to the overlap integral S; zero overlap means zero splitting. (3) Same symmetry about the internuclear (molecular) axis, so that the constructive and destructive parts of the overlap do not cancel each other out. Statement B inverts the second condition and statement D inverts the third, which is exactly how the distractors are built.
Key relation Splitting energy ∝ overlap integral S = ∫ ψAψB dτ, so maximum overlap ⇒ strongest MOs and mismatched symmetry ⇒ S = 0 ⇒ no MO at all with z as the molecular axis, s + pz works (both σ-symmetric) but s + px gives S = 0 exactly, because the sphere overlaps the + lobe and the − lobe by equal amounts.
Solution
1. A — same (comparable) energy: TRUE. Orbitals of widely different energy give a negligible bonding interaction, so this is a genuine LCAO requirement.
2. B — minimum overlap: FALSE. The condition is maximum (or at least appreciable) overlap; minimum overlap would give the weakest possible interaction, and zero overlap gives none. B is the exact opposite of the rule.
3. C — same symmetry about the molecular axis: TRUE. Both orbitals must be σ-type, or both π-type in the same plane; only then do the overlap contributions add instead of cancelling.
4. D — different symmetry about the molecular axis: FALSE. This is the negation of C. Different symmetry (e.g. s with px, or pz with px, taking z as the axis) makes the overlap integral vanish identically.
5. True statements are A and C only ⇒ option (b). Options (a), (c) and (d) all carry at least one of the inverted statements B or D and are rejected.
 statement                                        verdict   correct form of the rule
 A  same (comparable) energy                       TRUE     energies must be close for real stabilisation
 B  minimum overlap                                FALSE    overlap must be MAXIMUM / appreciable
 C  same symmetry about the molecular axis         TRUE     sigma with sigma, pi with pi (same plane)
 D  different symmetry about the molecular axis    FALSE    mismatched symmetry gives S = 0, no MO
                                             A and C only   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the trio as "same energy, same symmetry, maximum overlap" — two "sames" and one "maximum". Every distractor in this family of questions is made by flipping one word, so the moment you see "minimum overlap" or "different symmetry" you can strike the statement without further thought. Handy consequence to keep alongside it: with z as the internuclear axis the allowed σ combinations are s+s, s+pz, pz+pz and dz²+anything σ, while s+px, s+py and pz+px all give exactly zero — that single sentence answers half the LCAO questions ever asked.
Answer: (b) A and C only.
57(JEE Main 2024, 1 Feb Shift 2 · Level-1) Given below are two statements.  Statement (I): Both metal and non-metal exist in p and d-block elements.  Statement (II): Non-metals have higher ionisation enthalpy and higher electronegativity than the metals.  In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer:  (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are false  (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true  (c) Statement I is true but Statement II is false  (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are trueMetallic character across the blocks; IE and EN trendsIonisation enthalpy, electron gain enthalpy and electronegativity — the periodic trends🟡 Similarb(b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true
Concept Metallic character is fundamentally about how easily an atom gives up an electron — it therefore tracks a low ionisation enthalpy and low electronegativity, while non-metallic character tracks the opposite. Across the periodic table, the s-block is entirely metallic, the d- and f-blocks are entirely metallic (every transition and inner-transition element is a metal, without exception), and only the p-block straddles the divide, containing metals (Al, Ga, In, Sn, Pb, Bi), metalloids (B, Si, Ge, As, Sb, Te) and non-metals (C, N, O, F, P, S, Cl and the noble gases). So the coexistence of metals and non-metals in the same block happens in the p-block alone. The second statement is simply the definition of the metal/non-metal divide restated in terms of atomic properties.
Key relation metal ⇒ low IE, low EN, tends to lose e⁻; non-metal ⇒ high IE, high EN, tends to gain e⁻; across a period IE and EN increase → metallic character decreases both trends follow the rise in effective nuclear charge at constant shell number, which is also why the metal/non-metal boundary in the p-block runs diagonally.
Solution
1. Statement I claims both metals and non-metals exist in the p-block and the d-block. The p-block part is correct — it holds Al (metal) alongside S and Cl (non-metals).
2. The d-block part is wrong: all thirty-odd d-block elements, from Sc to Zn and beyond, are metals. They have low ionisation enthalpies, form cations, conduct electricity and show metallic lustre; there is no non-metallic d-block element ⇒ Statement I is FALSE.Even mercury, a liquid at room temperature, and zinc, which is comparatively unreactive, are unambiguously metals.
3. Statement II is the standard characterisation of the two classes: non-metals hold their valence electrons more tightly (higher IE) and attract shared electrons more strongly (higher EN). Compare Na (IE ≈ 496 kJ mol⁻¹, EN 0.9) with Cl (IE ≈ 1251 kJ mol⁻¹, EN 3.0) ⇒ Statement II is TRUE.
4. I false, II true ⇒ option (b). Option (d) fails on the d-block claim, and options (a) and (c) both mark Statement II false, which contradicts the defining property of non-metals.
 block   composition                                     metals?   non-metals?
 s       H (special), alkali + alkaline earth metals      yes        H only
 p       Al, Ga, Sn, Pb ... and C, N, O, F, Cl, noble gases  yes        yes
 d       Sc -> Zn, Y -> Cd, La -> Hg  (all metals)         yes        NO   <-- kills Statement I
 f       lanthanoids and actinoids (all metals)            yes        NO

 property             typical metal (Na)     typical non-metal (Cl)
 ionisation enthalpy  496 kJ/mol  (low)      1251 kJ/mol (high)
 electronegativity    0.9         (low)      3.0         (high)      -> Statement II TRUE

 Statement I FALSE, Statement II TRUE   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Fix one sentence in memory: "Only the p-block contains all three of metals, metalloids and non-metals; s, d and f are metal-only (bar hydrogen)." It disposes of a whole genre of statement-based questions in a single reading. For the second half, remember that IE, electron gain enthalpy, electronegativity and non-metallic character all move together — up and to the right — while atomic radius and metallic character move the other way. The trap in this item is that Statement I is half true: candidates read "p-block", agree, and tick (d) without ever testing the d-block clause.
Answer: (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true.
58(JEE Main 2024, 1 Feb Shift 2 · Level-1) Given below are two statements.  Statement (I): A π bonding MO has lower electron density above and below the inter-nuclear axis.  Statement (II): The π* antibonding MO has a node between the nuclei.  In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer:  (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are false  (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are true  (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true  (d) Statement I is true but Statement II is falseElectron density and nodes in π and π* molecular orbitalsNodal planes in bonding versus antibonding molecular orbitals🟡 Similarc(c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true
Concept A π molecular orbital is produced by the sideways, in-phase overlap of two parallel p orbitals (say the two 2px orbitals when z is the internuclear axis). Because the lobes lie above and below the axis, the electron density of the resulting bonding π MO is concentrated in two sausage-shaped clouds above and below the internuclear axis, with a nodal plane containing the axis (that nodal plane is simply the symmetry plane of a π bond and is present in bonding and antibonding forms alike — it is not what makes an orbital antibonding). The antibonding π* MO comes from the out-of-phase combination, which introduces an extra node — a plane lying between the two nuclei, perpendicular to the axis — that pushes electron density outside the bonding region and destabilises the molecule. Distinguishing "node containing the axis" from "node between the nuclei" is the whole point of this question.
Key relation ψπ = ψA + ψB (in-phase) ⇒ density piled up above and below the axis; ψπ* = ψA − ψB (out-of-phase) ⇒ extra node ⟂ to the axis, between the nuclei every antibonding MO — σ* or π* — has one more internuclear node than its bonding partner; that missing density between the nuclei is exactly what makes it antibonding.
Solution
1. Statement I says a π bonding MO has lower electron density above and below the internuclear axis. That is precisely backwards: constructive sideways overlap piles the density up there — it is the only place a π bonding electron can be found ⇒ Statement I is FALSE.What is true is that a π MO has zero density on the axis itself (the nodal plane containing the axis) — the statement has swapped "on the axis" for "above and below it".
2. Statement II says π* has a node between the nuclei. Subtracting the two p wavefunctions changes the sign of one lobe pair, so the combined function must pass through zero on a plane lying between the two atoms and perpendicular to the axis ⇒ Statement II is TRUE.
3. I false and II true ⇒ option (c).
4. Eliminate the others: (b) and (d) both accept Statement I and so contradict the shape of a π bond; (a) rejects Statement II, which would leave π* indistinguishable from π and make antibonding orbitals impossible to define.
 orbital   formed by            nodal plane CONTAINING the axis   node BETWEEN the nuclei   density above/below axis
 pi        p + p  (in phase)              yes (1)                        NO                  HIGH  (two lobes)
 pi*       p - p  (out of phase)          yes (1)                        YES (1 extra)       high but split by the node
 sigma     head-on, in phase              no                             NO                  on the axis
 sigma*    head-on, out of phase          no                             YES                 -

 Statement I  : "pi bonding MO has LOWER density above and below the axis"  -> FALSE (it is highest there)
 Statement II : "pi* has a node between the nuclei"                         -> TRUE
                                                                        <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Keep two node counts separate and this whole topic becomes mechanical: nodal planes containing the internuclear axis tell you the type of orbital (0 = σ, 1 = π, 2 = δ), whereas a node between the nuclei tells you it is antibonding. A π* orbital has both, which is why students who learn only one count get confused. The trap in this pair of statements is genuinely subtle — Statement I would be perfectly true if it said "lower electron density on the internuclear axis", and the examiner is testing whether you read "above and below" carefully.
Answer: (c) Statement I is false but Statement II is true.
59(JEE Main 2024, 1 Feb Shift 2 · Level-1) Select the compound from the following that will show intramolecular hydrogen bonding.  (a) H₂O  (b) NH₃  (c) C₂H₅OH  (d) o-nitrophenol (2-nitrophenol)Intramolecular vs intermolecular hydrogen bondingIntramolecular hydrogen bonding in ortho-substituted phenols and what it changes🟡 Similard(d) o-nitrophenol
Concept An intramolecular hydrogen bond forms inside a single molecule, and it needs the donor (an O–H or N–H) and the acceptor (a lone pair on O, N or F) to be held close enough by the molecular skeleton to close a strain-free ring — in practice a five- or six-membered ring. That geometric requirement is why the phenomenon is essentially confined to ortho-disubstituted benzenes and to chelating systems such as salicylaldehyde, salicylic acid and 2-nitrophenol. Small molecules like H₂O, NH₃ and ethanol simply do not have two groups far enough apart along a chain yet close enough in space; every hydrogen bond they form must be intermolecular, linking one molecule to the next. The chemical consequences are the exam's real target: an intramolecular H-bond ties the O–H up internally, so the molecule can no longer associate with its neighbours, which lowers boiling point, lowers solubility in water and raises volatility relative to the para isomer.
Key relation intramolecular H-bond ⟺ donor and acceptor on adjacent (ortho) positions forming a 5- or 6-membered ring; o-isomer: steam-volatile, lower b.p., less water-soluble  vs  p-isomer: intermolecular network, higher b.p., more soluble this single comparison — o-nitrophenol (m.p. 45 °C, steam volatile) against p-nitrophenol (m.p. 114 °C) — is the standard illustration in NCERT.
Solution
1. (a) H₂O — a three-atom molecule. Its O–H hydrogens and its lone pairs are on the same atom, so no internal ring is conceivable; water forms an extensive intermolecular network (up to four H-bonds per molecule) ⇒ rejected.
2. (b) NH₃ — again a single central atom bearing all three N–H bonds and the lone pair; only intermolecular N–H⋯N bonding is possible ⇒ rejected.
3. (c) C₂H₅OH — one O–H group and no second electronegative site anywhere in the ethyl chain to accept it. Ethanol hydrogen bonds strongly, but always to another ethanol or to water, i.e. intermolecular ⇒ rejected.
4. (d) o-nitrophenol — the phenolic O–H and one oxygen of the adjacent –NO₂ group are held on neighbouring ring carbons. The O–H points directly at that oxygen, closing a six-membered chelate ring ⇒ a textbook intramolecular O–H⋯O bond ⇒ answer (d).Because the hydroxyl proton is locked inside its own molecule, o-nitrophenol cannot build the intermolecular network that the meta and para isomers form — hence its much lower melting point and its steam volatility, which is how the isomers are separated in the laboratory.
 compound         donor    acceptor available in the SAME molecule?   ring size   H-bond type
 H2O              O-H      no (donor and acceptor on one atom)          -         intermolecular
 NH3              N-H      no                                           -         intermolecular
 C2H5OH           O-H      no second electronegative site               -         intermolecular
 o-nitrophenol    O-H      yes - O of the ortho -NO2 group          6-membered    INTRAmolecular   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Scan the options for the word (or drawing of) ortho: in JEE questions on intramolecular hydrogen bonding the answer is essentially always the o-substituted aromatic — o-nitrophenol, salicylaldehyde, salicylic acid, o-hydroxybenzoic acid or 2,6-dinitrophenol. Then carry the consequences with you, because the follow-up question is nearly always about a property: intramolecular H-bonding means lower boiling point, lower water solubility, greater steam volatility and greater acidity (the conjugate base is stabilised) compared with the para isomer. The trap here is that all four options can hydrogen bond — students who only check "does it H-bond?" pick H₂O, the strongest H-bonder in the list, and miss the word "intramolecular".
Answer: (d) o-nitrophenol — the O–H and the ortho –NO₂ oxygen close a six-membered intramolecular ring.
60(JEE Main 2024, 6 Apr Shift 1 · Level-1) Match List-I with List-II.  List-I (Hybridization): A. sp³  B. dsp²  C. sp³d  D. sp³d²   List-II (Orientation in Space): I. Trigonal bipyramidal  II. Octahedral  III. Tetrahedral  IV. Square planar  — Choose the correct answer:  (a) A-IV, B-III, C-I, D-II  (b) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II  (c) A-III, B-I, C-IV, D-II  (d) A-II, B-I, C-IV, D-IIIHybridisation and the geometry of the hybrid orbital setsp, sp², sp³, sp³d and sp³d² — the hybrid sets and the geometries they produce🟡 Similarb(b) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II
Concept Hybridisation conserves orbital number — n atomic orbitals mixed give exactly n equivalent hybrid orbitals — and those n hybrids then arrange themselves in space so as to be as far apart as possible, which fixes the geometry uniquely. So you never have to memorise pairs: just count the orbitals in the label and read off the geometry that n equidistant directions produce (2 → linear, 3 → trigonal planar, 4 → tetrahedral, 5 → trigonal bipyramidal, 6 → octahedral). The one entry that needs a second thought is dsp²: it is also a four-orbital set, but because the d orbital involved is dx²−y², whose four lobes already lie along the x and y axes, the four hybrids are forced into one plane at 90° — square planar rather than tetrahedral. That distinction, tetrahedral sp³ versus square planar dsp², is what the question is really testing.
Key relation no. of hybrid orbitals = no. of atomic orbitals mixed; sp → linear, sp² → trigonal planar, sp³ → tetrahedral, dsp² → square planar, sp³d → trigonal bipyramidal, sp³d² → octahedral, sp³d³ → pentagonal bipyramidal sp³ and dsp² both mix four orbitals, but only dsp² brings in the in-plane dx²−y², which is why the two four-coordinate geometries differ.
Solution
1. A. sp³ — 1 s + 3 p = 4 hybrids at 109.5°, pointing to the corners of a regular tetrahedron (CH₄, NH₄⁺, SO₄²⁻) ⇒ A–III.
2. B. dsp² — 1 d + 1 s + 2 p = 4 hybrids, but all confined to a plane at 90°, giving square planar ([Ni(CN)₄]²⁻, [Pt(NH₃)₄]²⁺) ⇒ B–IV.This is the geometry of the inner-orbital four-coordinate complexes of d⁸ metals — Ni(II), Pd(II), Pt(II), Au(III).
3. C. sp³d — 1 s + 3 p + 1 d = 5 hybrids: three equatorial at 120° and two axial at 90°, i.e. trigonal bipyramidal (PCl₅, SF₄ before the lone pair is removed) ⇒ C–I.
4. D. sp³d² — 1 s + 3 p + 2 d = 6 hybrids at 90° to one another, i.e. octahedral (SF₆, [Co(NH₃)₆]³⁺, and BrF₅ before its lone pair is removed) ⇒ D–II.
5. Assembling: A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II ⇒ option (b).
6. Eliminate: (a) swaps A and B, making sp³ square planar and dsp² tetrahedral; (c) and (d) both send the five-orbital sp³d set to square planar or octahedral, which contradicts orbital conservation (5 hybrids cannot occupy 4 or 6 positions).
 List-I    orbitals mixed        no. of hybrids   bond angles          ORIENTATION            List-II   example
 A  sp3    s + 3p                     4           109.5               tetrahedral            III       CH4, NH4+
 B  dsp2   d(x2-y2) + s + 2p          4           90 (all coplanar)   square planar          IV        [Ni(CN)4]2-
 C  sp3d   s + 3p + d                 5           120 eq / 90 ax      trigonal bipyramidal   I         PCl5
 D  sp3d2  s + 3p + 2d                6           90                  octahedral             II        SF6
                                                        A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Just count the letters-with-exponents in the label and match the count to the geometry — sp³d² has 1+3+2 = 6 orbitals, so it must be the six-cornered shape, octahedral, and no other option can be right. Doing that alone pins C and D here and leaves only A and B, which are settled by the single fact worth memorising: sp³ = tetrahedral, dsp² = square planar (and its converse, that a square-planar complex must be dsp², is the standard follow-up in coordination chemistry). The trap is the pair of four-orbital sets: candidates who "know" that four hybrids means tetrahedral hand dsp² the tetrahedron and pick (a).
Answer: (b) A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II.
61Match List I with List II. List-I (Compound/Species): A. SF4, B. BrF3, C. BrO3−, D. NH4+. List-II (Shape/Geometry): I. Tetrahedral, II. Pyramidal, III. See saw, IV. Bent T-Shape. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV (b) A-II, B-IV, C-III, D-I (c) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I (d) A-III, B-II, C-IV, D-I [JEE Main 2024 (6 Apr Shift-1)]VSEPR shapes from steric numberVSEPR shapes explained end to end — see-saw, T-shaped, pyramidal, square pyramidal🟡 SimilarcA-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
Concept Every VSEPR problem reduces to one number: the steric number (SN) = number of σ-bonds on the central atom + number of lone pairs on it. SN fixes the electron-pair geometry (2 → linear, 3 → trigonal planar, 4 → tetrahedral, 5 → trigonal bipyramidal, 6 → octahedral). The molecular shape is then what you see after you delete the lone pairs from that skeleton — the lone pairs are still there, still pushing, but they are invisible in the shape name. The whole trick is to place lone pairs where they hurt least: in a trigonal bipyramid they always go equatorial (only two 90° neighbours instead of three), and in an octahedron the second lone pair goes trans to the first.
Key relation SN = σ-bonds + lone pairs, and for a species E with V valence electrons on the central atom, m monovalent atoms and charge c: lone pairs = (V − m − c)/2 π bonds never change SN — only σ frameworks and lone pairs count, which is why BrO3− with three Br=O/Br–O links still behaves as SN 4.
Solution
1. SF4: S has 6 valence e⁻, 4 go into S–F bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair left. SN = 4 + 1 = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal skeleton, lone pair equatorial ⇒ shape is see saw ⇒ A–III. This is why SF4 has two different F–S–F angles, ≈173° axial and ≈102° equatorial, instead of a clean 180/120.
2. BrF3: Br has 7 valence e⁻, 3 used in Br–F ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 3 + 2 = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramid with both lone pairs equatorial ⇒ the three F atoms occupy two axial and one equatorial site ⇒ bent T-shape ⇒ B–IV. The lone-pair squeeze bends the axial F–Br–F from 180° down to ≈86°, which is exactly why the book calls it "bent" T-shape rather than plain T-shape.
3. BrO3−: use the standard hybridisation count SN = ½(V + M − C + A) with V = 7 (Br), M = 0 monovalent atoms (each O is divalent and contributes nothing), C = 0, A = 1 for the negative charge ⇒ SN = ½(7 + 0 − 0 + 1) = 4. Three of those four slots are the σ Br–O bonds, so one is a lone pair ⇒ tetrahedral skeleton with one vertex occupied by the lone pair ⇒ trigonal pyramidal ⇒ C–II. BrO3− is isostructural with ClO3−, IO3− and with NH3 in shape — all SN 4 with one lone pair.
4. NH4+: N has 5 valence e⁻, minus 1 for the positive charge = 4, all four used in N–H σ bonds ⇒ zero lone pairs. SN = 4 + 0 = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral, perfect 109°28′ ⇒ D–I.
5. Assemble: A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I. Option (a) fails at once (SF4 is not pyramidal); (b) and (d) misplace BrO3−; only (c) survives.
 species     central e-   sigma   lone pairs   SN   skeleton       SHAPE
 SF4            6           4          1        5   trig. bipyr.   see saw          <-- III
 BrF3           7           3          2        5   trig. bipyr.   bent T-shape     <-- IV
 BrO3(-)        7+1         3          1        4   tetrahedral    pyramidal        <-- II
 NH4(+)         5-1         4          0        4   tetrahedral    tetrahedral      <-- I
                                                       answer:  A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I  (c)
⚡ JEE Trick For SN = 5 memorise the descending ladder: 0 lp → trigonal bipyramidal, 1 lp → see saw, 2 lp → T-shape, 3 lp → linear. For SN = 6: 0 lp → octahedral, 1 lp → square pyramidal, 2 lp → square planar. Two ladders cover almost every interhalogen and xenon compound JEE will ever ask. The trap here is BrO3− — students see three oxygens and reflexively answer "trigonal planar" like NO3−/CO32−. But N and C are period-2 and have no lone pair left; Br has one, so BrO3− is pyramidal, not planar.
Answer: (c) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I
62Match List I with List II. List-I (Molecule/Species): A. SO2Cl2, B. NO, C. NO2−, D. I3−. List-II (Property/Shape): I. Paramagnetic, II. Diamagnetic, III. Tetrahedral, IV. Linear. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV (b) A-III, B-I, C-II, D-IV (c) A-IV, B-I, C-III, D-II (d) A-III, B-IV, C-II, D-I [JEE Main 2024 (6 Apr Shift-1)]Magnetic behaviour + shape matchingFast test for paramagnetic vs diamagnetic species in bonding questions🟡 SimilarbA-III, B-I, C-II, D-IV
Concept This is a deliberately mixed list: two entries in List-II are magnetic properties and two are shapes, so you cannot match on one criterion alone. The safe route is to compute both facts for every species and then use the fact that a match-list is a bijection — each List-II item is used exactly once. Magnetism is decided by a single count: odd total valence electrons ⇒ at least one unpaired electron ⇒ paramagnetic; an even count is almost always diamagnetic (O2 and B2 are the famous MOT exceptions). Shape is decided by the steric number as usual.
Key relation total valence e⁻ odd ⇒ paramagnetic; μ = √(n(n+2)) BM, n = unpaired e⁻ n = 0 gives μ = 0, i.e. diamagnetic; NO with n = 1 gives μ = 1.73 BM. And SN = σ + lp for the shape half of the list.
Solution
1. NO: 5 (N) + 6 (O) = 11 valence electrons — odd. One electron must stay unpaired (it sits in a π* antibonding MO), so NO is an odd-electron molecule and is paramagnetic ⇒ B–I. This is forced: no other species on the list has an odd electron count.
2. SO2Cl2 (sulphuryl chloride): S is bonded to 2 O and 2 Cl, four σ bonds, no lone pair on S ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral (slightly distorted, O–S–O ≈ 124°, Cl–S–Cl ≈ 100°) ⇒ A–III. It is the only tetrahedral species here.
3. I3−: central I has 7 + 1 = 8 valence e⁻; two σ I–I bonds use 2 ⇒ 3 lone pairs. SN = 2 + 3 = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramid with all three lone pairs equatorial ⇒ linear ⇒ D–IV.
4. NO2−: 5 + 12 + 1 = 18 valence electrons — even, every electron paired ⇒ diamagnetic ⇒ C–II. (Its shape is bent, ≈115°, but "bent" is not offered, so the property slot is the intended match.)
5. Combination A-III, B-I, C-II, D-IV = option (b). Eliminate the rest: (a) calls SO2Cl2 diamagnetic and NO tetrahedral — NO is diatomic, it cannot be tetrahedral; (c) makes SO2Cl2 linear; (d) makes NO "linear" and I3− paramagnetic, both false.
 species     valence e-   parity   magnetism      SN   shape        match
 SO2Cl2         32         even    diamagnetic     4   tetrahedral   III  <--
 NO             11          ODD    PARAMAGNETIC    -   diatomic      I    <--
 NO2(-)         18         even    DIAMAGNETIC     3   bent          II   <--
 I3(-)          22         even    diamagnetic     5   LINEAR        IV   <--
                                       answer:  A-III, B-I, C-II, D-IV   (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Scan the list for the odd-electron species first — NO, NO2, ClO2 and O2− are the usual suspects, and the "paramagnetic" slot is theirs with no further work. Then take the one species that is obviously tetrahedral or obviously linear. Two forced matches out of four normally kill three of the four options instantly. The distractor here is NO2−: students confuse it with neutral NO2 (17 e⁻, odd, paramagnetic). Adding the electron pairs it up — NO2 is paramagnetic, NO2− is not, and that single electron is also why NO2 dimerises to N2O4 on cooling.
Answer: (b) A-III, B-I, C-II, D-IV
63Given below are two statements: Statement I: PF5 and BrF5 both exhibit sp3d hybridisation. Statement II: Both SF6 and [Co(NH3)6]3+ exhibit sp3d2 hybridisation. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer: (a) Statement I is true but Statement II is false (b) Statement I is false but Statement II is true (c) Both Statement I and Statement II are true (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are false [JEE Main 2024 (6 Apr Shift-2)]sp³d vs sp³d² vs d²sp³Worked solution to this exact PF₅/BrF₅ and SF₆/[Co(NH₃)₆]³⁺ statement pair🟢 ExactdBoth Statement I and Statement II are false
Concept Two independent traps are stacked here. The first is that hybridisation follows the steric number, not the formula — an "F5" ending does not guarantee sp3d, because a lone pair counts exactly as much as a bond. The second is the difference between sp3d2 (outer-orbital, uses nd orbitals of the same shell, e.g. 3s 3p 3d for sulphur) and d2sp3 (inner-orbital, uses (n−1)d orbitals, e.g. 3d 4s 4p for a first-row transition metal). Both give an octahedron, so the shape cannot distinguish them — only the orbital set and the magnetic behaviour can.
Key relation SN = 5 → sp³d, SN = 6 → sp³d² (main group) or d²sp³ (inner-orbital complex); for a metal complex the choice is decided by the ligand field strength: strong field ⇒ (n−1)d used ⇒ d²sp³ ⇒ low spin NH3 is a strong-field ligand on Co3+, so the six 3d electrons pair up into three orbitals and vacate two 3d orbitals for bonding.
Solution
1. PF5: P has 5 valence e⁻, all five used in P–F σ bonds ⇒ 0 lone pairs ⇒ SN = 5 ⇒ sp3d, trigonal bipyramidal. ✔ for the sp3d claim.
2. BrF5: Br has 7 valence e⁻, five used in Br–F bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair remains ⇒ SN = 5 + 1 = 6 ⇒ sp3d2, square pyramidal — not sp3d. So Statement I is FALSE.
3. SF6: S uses all 6 valence e⁻ in six σ bonds ⇒ SN = 6, no lone pair ⇒ sp3d2, regular octahedron. ✔ for the SF6 half.
4. [Co(NH3)6]3+: Co is Z = 27, [Ar]3d74s2; Co3+ is 3d6. NH3 is a strong-field ligand, so the six d electrons pair into t2g (3d6 → three filled orbitals), freeing two inner 3d orbitals. Bonding therefore uses 3d, 3d, 4s, 4p, 4p, 4p ⇒ d2sp3 (inner-orbital, low-spin, diamagnetic, μ = 0), not sp3d2. So Statement II is FALSE too.
5. Both statements false ⇒ option (d). Note the shape is octahedral in both cases; the statement is about the orbital set, and that is where it breaks.
 species              central atom   sigma   lp   SN   hybridisation   claim in Q   verdict
 PF5                  P (5 e-)         5      0    5   sp3d            sp3d          OK
 BrF5                 Br (7 e-)        5      1    6   sp3d2           sp3d          WRONG   <-- kills Stmt I
 SF6                  S (6 e-)         6      0    6   sp3d2           sp3d2         OK
 [Co(NH3)6]3+         Co3+ = 3d6       6      -    6   d2sp3 (inner)   sp3d2         WRONG   <-- kills Stmt II
                                                    both statements FALSE  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Two reflexes worth burning in. (i) Never read hybridisation off the subscript. PF5 and BrF5 look like twins but P has no spare electrons and Br has two spare; the lone pair pushes Br up one whole rung to sp3d2. (ii) Order of letters tells you which d orbitals. "d" written before sp3 means inner (n−1)d ⇒ low spin, strong field; "d" written after means outer nd ⇒ high spin, weak field. So [Co(NH3)6]3+ is d2sp3 while [CoF6]3− (F− weak field) really is sp3d2 and paramagnetic with 4 unpaired electrons. Swapping those two complexes is the classic trap.
Answer: (d) Both Statement I and Statement II are false
64Order of Covalent bond: (A) KF > KI; LiF > KF (B) KF < KI; LiF > KF (C) SnCl4 > SnCl2; CuCl > NaCl (D) LiF > KF; CuCl > NaCl (E) KF < KI; CuCl > NaCl. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) C, E only (b) B, C only (c) B, C, E only (d) A, B only [JEE Main 2023 (24 Jan. Shift 1)]Fajans' rules — covalent characterFajans' rules: polarising power, polarisability and how to rank covalent character🟡 SimilarcB, C, E only
Concept No bond is purely ionic. The cation pulls on the anion's electron cloud and drags some of it into the internuclear region — that distortion is covalent character. Fajans' rules just list what makes the distortion large: a small, highly charged cation (large polarising power) and a large, soft anion (large polarisability). A fifth, easily forgotten factor is the cation's electronic configuration: an 18-electron (pseudo-noble-gas) shell like Cu+, Ag+, Zn2+ screens the nucleus badly, so its effective pull is much greater than a same-sized noble-gas-core cation like Na+.
Key relation polarising power ∝ charge / (radius)² and covalent character ∝ (polarising power of cation) × (polarisability of anion) so going down a group of anions covalent character rises (F⁻ < Cl⁻ < Br⁻ < I⁻), and going down a group of cations it falls (Li⁺ > Na⁺ > K⁺ > Rb⁺). Extra rule: 18 e⁻ core cation > 8 e⁻ core cation of similar size.
Solution Test each comparison one at a time.
1. KF vs KI — same cation K⁺, different anion. I⁻ (220 pm) is far bigger and softer than F⁻ (133 pm) ⇒ more polarisable ⇒ KI is more covalent. So "KF < KI" is correct and "KF > KI" is wrong.
2. LiF vs KF — same anion, different cation. Li⁺ (76 pm) is much smaller than K⁺ (138 pm) ⇒ far higher charge density ⇒ LiF is more covalent. So "LiF > KF" is correct. This is exactly why LiF/LiI are the anomalous, more-covalent members of their families and why LiCl dissolves in organic solvents.
3. SnCl4 vs SnCl2 — same element, different oxidation state. Sn4+ carries twice the charge in a smaller radius than Sn2+ ⇒ much greater polarising power ⇒ SnCl4 more covalent (it is a volatile liquid, b.p. 114 °C, while SnCl2 is an ionic solid, m.p. 247 °C). Correct.
4. CuCl vs NaCl — Cu+ (77 pm) and Na+ (102 pm) carry the same +1 charge, but Cu+ has an 18-electron 3d10 outer shell versus Na+'s 8-electron neon core. Poor d-shell screening ⇒ higher effective nuclear pull ⇒ CuCl more covalent (CuCl is water-insoluble; NaCl is freely soluble). Correct.
5. Now score the five statements: (A) "KF > KI" wrong ⇒ A rejected. (B) KF < KI ✔ and LiF > KF ✔ ⇒ B correct. (C) SnCl4 > SnCl2 ✔ and CuCl > NaCl ✔ ⇒ C correct. (E) KF < KI ✔ and CuCl > NaCl ✔ ⇒ E correct. Only option (c) offers exactly B, C and E. (D)'s two comparisons are individually fine, but "D" is not present in any option that also contains B, C and E, so it never comes into play — (c) is the only self-consistent choice.
 comparison            governing factor                        more covalent    statement uses it
 KF   vs KI            anion polarisability (I- >> F-)          KI               A(wrong) B(ok) E(ok)
 LiF  vs KF            cation size (Li+ << K+)              LiF              A(ok)    B(ok)
 SnCl4 vs SnCl2        cation charge (+4 > +2)                  SnCl4            C(ok)
 CuCl vs NaCl          18 e- core beats 8 e- core               CuCl             C(ok)    E(ok)
                                       correct set = B, C, E   <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Fajans questions are decided in two seconds by "small cation, big anion, high charge, d-block core". The single most-missed factor is the last one — the 18-electron rule — and it is exactly what CuCl vs NaCl and AgCl vs KCl are testing. The trap in this question is statement (A): it correctly says LiF > KF, so students tick it without checking the first half, where "KF > KI" reverses the anion trend. Always evaluate both halves of a compound statement before accepting it.
Answer: (c) B, C, E only
65Match List I with List II. List-I (Oxide): (A) N2O4, (B) NO2, (C) N2O5, (D) N2O. List-II (Type of bond): (i) 1 N=O bond, (ii) 1 N–O–N bond, (iii) 1 N–N bond, (iv) 1 N=N / N≡N bond. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) A-iii, B-i, C-ii, D-iv (b) A-ii, B-iv, C-iii, D-i (c) A-iii, B-i, C-iv, D-ii (d) A-ii, B-i, C-iii, D-iv [JEE Main 2023 (06 Apr Shift 1)]Structures of nitrogen oxidesStructures of the nitrogen oxides — N₂O, NO₂, NO₂⁻, NO₃⁻ bonding and bond angles🟡 SimilaraA-iii, B-i, C-ii, D-iv
Concept The nitrogen oxides are best learned as pictures, not formulae. The key structural question for the dimeric oxides is what links the two nitrogens: in N2O4 the two NO2 halves are joined directly, N–N; in N2O5 they are joined through an oxygen bridge, N–O–N. That single difference is the whole question. N2O is not a dimer at all — it is a linear N–N–O chain in which the two nitrogens are triple/double bonded to each other, and NO2 is a bent odd-electron monomer.
Key relation N2O4 = O2N–NO2, N2O5 = O2N–O–NO2, N2O = N≡N⁺–O⁻ ↔ N=N=O, NO2 = O=N–O• (bent, 134°) N2O4 forms because NO2's single unpaired electron on N pairs with the partner's — hence the N–N bond and hence the diamagnetic, colourless dimer.
Solution
1. N2O4 (A): planar O2N–NO2, the two nitrogens joined by a long, weak N–N single bond (175 pm, only ~57 kJ mol⁻¹ — which is why it dissociates back to NO2 so readily on warming). The only N–N containing oxide here ⇒ A–iii.
2. NO2 (B): 17 valence electrons, one on N unpaired. Resonance gives one N=O and one N–O; the requested feature "1 N=O bond" fits ⇒ B–i. There is no second nitrogen at all, so ii, iii and iv are structurally impossible for NO2 — this match is forced.
3. N2O5 (C): dinitrogen pentoxide is the anhydride of HNO3; two NO2 groups share a bridging oxygen, O2N–O–NO2, with an N–O–N angle of ~112°. That is the only N–O–N linkage in the list ⇒ C–ii. (In the solid it exists as NO2+NO3−, but the gas-phase covalent structure is what is being matched.)
4. N2O (D): laughing gas is linear, N–N–O, with resonance N≡N⁺–O⁻ ↔ ⁻N=N⁺=O. Either way the two nitrogens are joined by a double or triple N–N bond ⇒ D–iv.
5. Result: A-iii, B-i, C-ii, D-iv = option (a). Option (d) is the near-miss — it swaps A and C, i.e. it puts the oxygen bridge in N2O4 and the direct N–N in N2O5, which is exactly backwards.
 oxide      structure                      N-to-N link            match
 N2O4       O2N-NO2   (planar)             N-N single             iii   <--
 NO2        O=N-O.    (bent, 134deg)       none (1 N=O bond)      i     <--
 N2O5       O2N-O-NO2 (bridged)            N-O-N bridge           ii    <--
 N2O        N(tri)N-O (linear)             N=N / N(tri)N          iv    <--
                                 answer:  A-iii, B-i, C-ii, D-iv   (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the pair by counting oxygens: N2O4 has 4 oxygens = 2 + 2, so both nitrogens are "full" and must bond to each other directly; N2O5 has an odd extra oxygen that has nowhere to go except between the two nitrogens. Same logic identifies the N–N bond in N2O3 (O2N–NO) and the O–O bond in H2S2O8. Trap: many students assume every "N2Ox" species has an N–N bond — N2O5 is the exception that the examiner is fishing for.
Answer: (a) A-iii, B-i, C-ii, D-iv
66Consider the following statements: (A) NF3 molecule has a trigonal planar structure. (B) Bond length of N2 is shorter than O2. (C) Isoelectronic molecules or ions have identical bond order. (D) Dipole moment of H2S is higher than that of water molecule. Choose the correct answer from the options given below: (a) (A) and (B) are correct (b) (A) and (D) are correct (c) (C) and (D) are correct (d) (B) and (C) are correct [JEE Main 2023 (15 April Shift 1)]Mixed: shape, bond order, dipole momentWhy NF₃ is pyramidal and how its dipole moment compares with NH₃🟡 Similard(B) and (C) are correct
Concept Four unrelated statements, so treat it as four independent true/false problems and only combine at the end. Two of them (A, D) are decided by the lone pair on the central atom — a lone pair both bends the geometry away from planarity and contributes a large moment of its own. The other two (B, C) are decided by molecular orbital bond order, because bond order is what actually controls bond length: higher bond order ⇒ shorter, stronger bond.
Key relation Bond order = (Nb − Na)/2; bond length ∝ 1 / bond order; μnet = vector sum of bond moments + lone-pair moment isoelectronic species have the same total electron count, so they fill the same MO diagram and necessarily come out with the same Nb − Na.
Solution
1. (A) NF3 trigonal planar? N has 5 valence e⁻; three go into N–F bonds leaving one lone pair ⇒ SN = 3 + 1 = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral electron geometry ⇒ molecular shape is trigonal pyramidal (F–N–F ≈ 102°), like NH3. FALSE. BF3 is the planar one — B has no lone pair.
2. (B) N2 bond shorter than O2? N2 has 14 electrons: bond order = (10 − 4)/2 = 3. O2 has 16: bond order = (10 − 6)/2 = 2 (the two extra electrons go into π* antibonding). Higher bond order ⇒ shorter bond: N≡N = 110 pm versus O=O = 121 pm. TRUE.
3. (C) Isoelectronic species have identical bond order? Same electron count ⇒ same filling of the same MO scheme ⇒ same Nb and Na. Test it: N2, CO, CN− and NO+ all have 14 electrons and all have bond order 3; O22−, F2 and Ne22+ all have 18 and all have bond order 1. TRUE.
4. (D) μ(H2S) > μ(H2O)? Oxygen (EN 3.5) is far more electronegative than sulphur (EN 2.5), so each O–H bond moment is much larger than an S–H bond moment; the H–O–H angle (104.5°) is also wider than H–S–H (92°), but the electronegativity difference dominates. Measured: μ(H2O) = 1.85 D, μ(H2S) = 0.95 D. FALSE — it is the other way round.
5. Correct statements = (B) and (C) ⇒ option (d). Every other option carries at least one of the two false statements.
 stmt   claim                                       check                                   verdict
 (A)    NF3 is trigonal planar                      SN = 4, one lone pair -> pyramidal      FALSE
 (B)    N2 bond shorter than O2                     BO 3 (110 pm) vs BO 2 (121 pm)          TRUE   <--
 (C)    isoelectronic -> identical bond order        N2, CO, CN-, NO+ all BO = 3             TRUE   <--
 (D)    mu(H2S) > mu(H2O)                           0.95 D  vs  1.85 D                      FALSE
                                          correct pair = (B) and (C)  <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick In "which statements are correct" questions, hunt for the one statement you are certain is false and strike out every option containing it — here μ(H2S) > μ(H2O) is instantly false and kills (b) and (c) in one stroke; NF3-planar then kills (a), leaving (d) without even reading (C). The genuine trap is (D): students who remember only "bigger central atom ⇒ bigger dipole" get it backwards. The correct order down group 16 is H2O (1.85) > H2S (0.95) > H2Se (0.24) > H2Te (0.20) D — dipole moment falls as electronegativity falls.
Answer: (d) (B) and (C) are correct
67Statement I: Dipole moment is a vector quantity and by convention it is depicted by a small arrow with tail on the negative centre and head pointing towards the positive centre. Statement II: The crossed arrow of the dipole moment symbolizes the direction of the shift of charges in the molecules. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect (c) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct (d) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect [JEE Main 2023 (25 Jan. Shift 2)]Dipole moment — sign conventionWorked solution to this exact statement pair on the dipole-moment arrow convention🟢 ExactdStatement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect
Concept There are two different arrows in use for the same physical quantity, and this question exists purely to see whether you know that they point in opposite directions. The physics convention defines the dipole moment vector as running from the negative charge to the positive charge — tail on −q, head on +q. The chemistry convention uses a different symbol entirely: a crossed arrow (a plus sign forming the tail) drawn on the Lewis structure with the cross at the positive end and the arrowhead at the negative end. Chemists chose that direction on purpose, because it lets the arrow show where the electron density has moved to.
Statement μ = Q × r, measured in debye, 1 D = 3.33564 × 10⁻³⁰ C m. Physics arrow: − ⟶ +. Chemistry crossed arrow: +⟶ −, i.e. it points along the shift of electron density, not "the shift of charges" in general. Nuclei and positive charge do not migrate at all; only the electron cloud is displaced, so naming the moving thing correctly is the whole content of Statement II.
Solution
1. Statement I. "Dipole moment is a vector quantity" — true, which is why bond moments add vectorially and why symmetric molecules such as CO2, CCl4 and BF3 come out with μ = 0 despite having polar bonds. "…depicted by a small arrow with tail on the negative centre and head pointing towards the positive centre" — this is the standard textbook sentence for the vector convention. Statement I is CORRECT. That immediately eliminates options (b) and (c).
2. Statement II. The crossed arrow is the chemistry symbol, drawn on the Lewis structure with the cross on the positive end. What it is defined to indicate is the direction of the shift of electron density — from the electropositive atom towards the electronegative atom. Saying it shows "the shift of charges" is not the accepted definition: it is loose enough to be read as a movement of positive charge, which is the exact opposite of what happens. On that reading, Statement II is INCORRECT.
3. Check with a real molecule. In H–F, fluorine is the electronegative end. The crossed arrow is drawn from H towards F (cross at H) because the electron density has drifted to F. The physics vector, meanwhile, points from F towards H. Two arrows, one molecule, opposite directions — and only one of them is described by each statement.
4. Statement I correct, Statement II incorrect ⇒ option (d).
 convention              tail (start)      head (points to)     what it tracks
 physics vector          negative centre   positive centre      the dipole vector mu
 chemistry crossed arrow positive end (+)  negative end         SHIFT OF ELECTRON DENSITY

 example  H--F :   crossed arrow  H +---> F        (electron density moves to F)
                   physics vector H <---  F        (- to +)

 Statement I  : matches the physics convention exactly ................ CORRECT
 Statement II : says "shift of charges", not "shift of electron
                density" -- the defining phrase is wrong ............. INCORRECT   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Anchor the two arrows on one molecule you will never forget: NH3 vs NF3. In NH3 the three bond moments point towards N and the lone-pair moment also points away from N in the same overall sense, so they reinforce (μ = 1.46 D); in NF3 the bond moments point away from N towards the three F atoms and partly cancel the lone-pair moment (μ = 0.24 D). If you can draw those crossed arrows correctly you have the chemistry convention. The trap in statement-type questions like this is that the examiner changes one noun — "charges" for "electron density" — and leaves everything else verbatim from the textbook. Read the noun, not the sentence shape.
Answer: (d) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect
68For OF2 molecule consider the following: (A) Number of lone pairs on oxygen is 2. (B) FOF angle is less than 104.5°. (C) Oxidation state of O is −2. (D) Molecule is bent 'V' shaped. (E) Molecular geometry is linear. Correct options are: (a) C, D, E only (b) B, E, A only (c) A, C, D only (d) A, B, D only [JEE Main 2023 (30 Jan. Shift 1)]OF₂ — geometry and oxidation stateWorked solution to this exact OF₂ statement question🟢 ExactdA, B, D only
Concept OF2 is the one common compound in which oxygen is not negative. Oxidation state is assigned by giving the shared electrons entirely to the more electronegative partner, and fluorine (4.0) is the only element more electronegative than oxygen (3.5). So in every O–F bond the electrons are booked to F, leaving oxygen positive. Geometrically OF2 is a straightforward water-analogue: SN = 4 with two lone pairs ⇒ bent. The extra subtlety is how bent: the two highly electronegative fluorines pull bonding electron density away from the central oxygen, so the bond pairs sit further out, repel each other less, and let the lone pairs squeeze the angle even tighter than in H2O.
Key relation SN = σ-bonds + lone pairs = 2 + 2 = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral electron geometry, bent molecular shape. Oxidation state rule: O is −2 except in peroxides (−1), superoxides (−½) and fluorides (POSITIVE). Angle rule: more electronegative substituent ⇒ smaller bond angle bonding pairs are pulled towards the substituent, so bp–bp repulsion at the centre drops and the lone pairs win more space.
Solution
1. (A) Lone pairs on O = 2? Oxygen has 6 valence electrons; two are used in the two O–F σ bonds, leaving 4 electrons = 2 lone pairs. TRUE.
2. (B) FOF angle less than 104.5°? H2O is 104.5°. In OF2 the two fluorines drag bonding density away from O, weakening bp–bp repulsion, so the lone pairs compress the angle further. Experiment: F–O–F = 103.1° < 104.5°. TRUE. Compare the reverse case: in Cl2O the big, less electronegative chlorines force the angle open to ≈111°.
3. (C) Oxidation state of O is −2? F is more electronegative than O, so each O–F bond assigns its pair to F. Let x = oxidation state of O: x + 2(−1) = 0 ⇒ x = +2. FALSE — OF2 is oxygen difluoride, not an "oxide" at all.
4. (D) Bent 'V' shaped? SN = 4 with two lone pairs ⇒ molecular shape is bent / V-shaped, exactly like H2O. TRUE.
5. (E) Molecular geometry is linear? Directly contradicts (D); a bent molecule cannot be linear. FALSE. Note (D) and (E) are mutually exclusive, so any option containing both — or containing E at all, given (D) is clearly true — is dead on arrival.
6. True set = A, B, D ⇒ option (d). Options (a) and (b) both include E; (c) includes the false oxidation state (C).
 stmt   claim                          test                                        verdict
 (A)    2 lone pairs on O              6 val e- - 2 bonding = 4 e- = 2 lp          TRUE   <--
 (B)    FOF angle < 104.5 deg          measured 103.1 deg (F pulls bp outward)     TRUE   <--
 (C)    oxidation state of O = -2      F is MORE electronegative -> O is +2        FALSE
 (D)    bent 'V' shaped                SN 4, 2 lp -> bent                          TRUE   <--
 (E)    linear                         contradicts (D)                             FALSE
                                                  correct set = A, B, D   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the one-line exception: oxygen is +2 in OF2 and +1 in O2F2 — the only compounds where oxygen is positive, because fluorine outranks it. Examiners recycle this every year. Second reflex: in any "choose the correct statements" list, look for a contradictory pair first — here (D) bent and (E) linear cannot both hold, so one of them is a free elimination before you do any chemistry. Bond-angle order worth carrying: Cl2O (111°) > H2O (104.5°) > OF2 (103.1°).
Answer: (d) A, B, D only
69Amongst the following, the number of species having the linear shape is ________ : XeF2, I3+, C3O2, I3−, CO2, SO2, BeCl2 and BCl2− [JEE Main 2023 (31 Jan. Shift 2)]Counting linear speciesCounting the linear species among BeCl₂, NO₂⁺, ICl₂⁻, XeF₂, I₃⁻ and OCl₂🟡 Similar55
Concept "Linear" can arise from two completely different steric numbers, and a counting question always mixes both to catch you. SN = 2 with no lone pair (BeCl2, CO2) is linear because there is nothing else to push. SN = 5 with three equatorial lone pairs (XeF2, I3−, ICl2−) is also linear, because the three lone pairs fill the entire equatorial plane and leave the two bonds trans to each other at 180°. Anything in between — SN 3 or 4 with a lone pair, or SN 5 with fewer than three lone pairs — is bent. A separate case is a cumulated chain like C3O2, where every carbon is sp hybridised and the whole five-atom skeleton lies on one line.
Key relation SN = ½(V + M − C + A) for the central atom. Linear only when SN = 2, lp = 0 or SN = 5, lp = 3 an sp hybrid gives 180° directly; the trigonal-bipyramid case gives 180° only after all three equatorial sites are taken by lone pairs.
Solution
1. XeF2: Xe has 8 valence e⁻, 2 in bonds ⇒ 3 lone pairs. SN = 2 + 3 = 5, all three lp equatorial ⇒ LINEAR ✔
2. I3+: central I contributes 7, minus 1 for the positive charge = 6; two σ bonds use 2 ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 2 + 2 = 4 ⇒ bent / V-shaped (≈102°) ✘ Removing an electron from I3− removes one lone pair, and the moment you drop below three equatorial lone pairs the molecule bends.
3. C3O2 (carbon suboxide): O=C=C=C=O, every carbon sp hybridised with SN = 2 ⇒ the whole molecule is LINEAR ✔
4. I3−: central I has 7 + 1 = 8; two σ bonds ⇒ 3 lone pairs. SN = 5, lp = 3 ⇒ LINEAR ✔
5. CO2: C, SN = 2, no lone pair ⇒ LINEAR ✔
6. SO2: S has 6 valence e⁻, two σ bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 3 ⇒ trigonal planar skeleton, one vertex a lone pair ⇒ bent (119.5°) ✘
7. BeCl2: Be has 2 valence e⁻, both in bonds ⇒ 0 lone pairs, SN = 2, sp ⇒ LINEAR ✔ (in the vapour phase; solid BeCl2 is a polymeric chain, but the species as written is the monomer).
8. BCl2−: B has 3 valence e⁻ + 1 for the charge = 4; two σ bonds use 2 ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 2 + 1 = 3 ⇒ bent ✘
9. Linear species: XeF2, C3O2, I3−, CO2, BeCl2 ⇒ 5.
 species    central   valence e-   sigma   lp   SN   shape        linear?
 XeF2       Xe            8          2      3    5   linear         YES
 I3(+)      I           7-1=6        2      2    4   bent (~102)    no
 C3O2       C (all sp)    -          2      0    2   O=C=C=C=O      YES
 I3(-)      I           7+1=8        2      3    5   linear         YES
 CO2        C             4          2      0    2   linear         YES
 SO2        S             6          2      1    3   bent (119.5)   no
 BeCl2      Be            2          2      0    2   linear         YES
 BCl2(-)    B           3+1=4        2      1    3   bent           no
                                                          count =  5   <-- answer (5)
⚡ JEE Trick Two shortcuts collapse this to about fifteen seconds. (i) Isoelectronic pairs share a shape: I3−, XeF2, ICl2− and IBr2− are all 22-electron trihalides ⇒ all linear; once you place one you have placed them all. (ii) Charge shifts the shape: adding an electron to a triatomic adds a lone pair and bends it, removing one straightens it. That is why I3− is linear but I3+ is bent — and putting both in the same list, as this question does, is precisely the trap. Same logic distinguishes NO2+ (linear) from NO2− (bent). Finally, do not skip C3O2 just because it is unfamiliar — any fully cumulated O=C=C=C=O chain is linear.
Answer: 5 (XeF₂, C₃O₂, I₃⁻, CO₂, BeCl₂)
70Given below are two statements: one is labelled as Assertion (A) and the other is labelled as Reason (R). Assertion (A): An aqueous solution of KOH when used for volumetric analysis, its concentration should be checked before the use. Reason (R): On aging, KOH solution absorbs atmospheric CO2. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer: (a) (A) is not correct but (R) is correct (b) Both (A) and (R) are correct but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A) (c) Both (A) and (R) are correct and (R) is the correct explanation of (A) (d) (A) is correct but (R) is not correct [JEE Main 2023 (1 Feb. Shift 2)]Standardisation of alkali — carbonate errorVolumetric analysis for JEE — why titrant solutions must be standardised🟡 SimilarcBoth (A) and (R) are correct and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
Concept A titrant is only useful if its concentration is known exactly, so reagents are divided into primary standards (pure, stable, non-hygroscopic, weighable directly — Na2CO3, K2Cr2O7, oxalic acid, AgNO3) and secondary standards, which must be standardised against a primary standard before every serious use. NaOH and KOH are the textbook secondary standards, for two reasons that both attack the concentration: they are strongly deliquescent (they pick up water from the air, so a weighed mass is never pure alkali) and, more damagingly here, they react with atmospheric CO2. So the assertion is a laboratory rule, and the reason supplies the chemistry behind it.
Key relation 2 KOH + CO2 → K2CO3 + H2O two moles of strong base are consumed per mole of CO2 absorbed, and are replaced by carbonate, which is a much weaker base — so the effective alkali strength of the bottle falls steadily with time. Consequence for a titration against a strong acid: K2CO3 + 2 HCl → 2 KCl + H2O + CO2
Solution
1. Test the Assertion. KOH is not a primary standard: it is deliquescent and its solutions drift in strength on storage. Any accurate volumetric work therefore requires the KOH to be restandardised (typically against oxalic acid or standard HCl) immediately before use. (A) is CORRECT.
2. Test the Reason. Atmospheric CO2 is an acidic oxide and dissolves readily in strong alkali: 2 KOH + CO2 → K2CO3 + H2O. This is the same reaction used deliberately to absorb CO2 in gas analysis. So on standing in a stoppered-but-not-sealed bottle, a KOH solution genuinely does absorb CO2. (R) is CORRECT.
3. Is (R) the explanation of (A)? Yes, directly. Every mole of CO2 absorbed destroys 2 mol of free OH⁻ and creates CO32− in its place. The bottle labelled "0.1 M KOH" is now a mixture of KOH and K2CO3 of unknown composition, and worse, the titre it gives depends on the indicator — with phenolphthalein the carbonate is only half neutralised (to HCO3−), with methyl orange it is fully neutralised. Two indicators, two answers, from one bottle: this is the classic carbonate error. The cause named in (R) is exactly why the precaution in (A) is needed.
4. Both correct and (R) explains (A) ⇒ option (c). (a) and (d) are ruled out because both statements stand up; (b) fails because the link between them is causal, not incidental.
 fresh bottle :  KOH  0.1 M                       titre against HCl  ->  0.1 M
 after aging  :  2 KOH + CO2 --> K2CO3 + H2O
                 KOH  (0.1 - 2x) M  +  K2CO3  x M

 indicator          what carbonate does                       apparent strength
 phenolphthalein    CO3(2-) --> HCO3(-)   (half way only)     too LOW
 methyl orange      CO3(2-) --> H2O + CO2 (complete)          nearly correct
                 => concentration is no longer trustworthy: RESTANDARDISE
                 (A) correct, (R) correct, (R) explains (A)   <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick For assertion–reason questions the fast route is: decide A, decide R, and only then ask about the causal link — never try to judge all three at once. Here the memorable fact is the primary/secondary standard split: if a substance is deliquescent, efflorescent, hygroscopic or air-oxidisable it cannot be a primary standard. NaOH, KOH, HCl, H2SO4, KMnO4 and Na2S2O3 are all secondary; Na2CO3, K2Cr2O7, oxalic acid and AgNO3 are primary. The trap in this question is option (b) — students accept both statements but do not see that CO2 absorption is the whole reason the concentration drifts, and downgrade a correct explanation to a coincidence.
Answer: (c) Both (A) and (R) are correct and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
71The number of species from the following which have square pyramidal structure is ________ : PF5, BrF4−, IF5, BrF5, XeOF4, ICl4− [JEE Main 2023 (06 Apr Shift 1)]Counting square pyramidal speciesWorked solution to this exact square-pyramidal counting question🟢 Exact33
Concept Square pyramidal has exactly one origin in VSEPR: SN = 6 with exactly one lone pair (AB5L). Take a regular octahedron, replace one vertex by a lone pair, and the remaining five atoms form a square base with one apex — and the lone pair pushes the four basal atoms slightly below the equator, so the real B–A–B angles are about 85°, not 90°. Every other lone-pair count on SN 6 gives something else (0 lp → octahedral, 2 lp → square planar, because the second lone pair always goes trans to the first).
