So the entire question is: for each species, compute SN and the lone-pair count, and keep only the (6, 1) cases.
Key relation SN = ½(V + M − C + A), counting only σ-bonds and lone pairs. SN 6: 0 lp → octahedral, 1 lp → SQUARE PYRAMIDAL, 2 lp → square planar a doubly bonded oxygen, as in XeOF4, occupies one σ position and contributes nothing extra — the π bond is invisible to VSEPR.
Solution
1. PF5: P has 5 valence e⁻, all five in P–F bonds ⇒ 0 lp, SN = 5 ⇒ sp3d, trigonal bipyramidal ✘ SN 5 can never give square pyramidal — the count is wrong from the start.
2. BrF4−: Br has 7 + 1 = 8 valence e⁻; four σ bonds use 4 ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 4 + 2 = 6, lp = 2 ⇒ square planar ✘
3. IF5: I has 7 valence e⁻; five σ bonds use 5 ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 5 + 1 = 6, lp = 1 ⇒ SQUARE PYRAMIDAL ✔
4. BrF5: identical count to IF5 — Br has 7, five bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair, SN = 6 ⇒ SQUARE PYRAMIDAL ✔ (F–Br–F basal angle ≈ 84.8°, measurably squashed by the lone pair).
5. XeOF4: Xe has 8 valence e⁻. Four go into the four Xe–F bonds and two into the Xe=O bond ⇒ 8 − 4 − 2 = 2 electrons left = 1 lone pair. σ-framework = 4 F + 1 O = 5 ⇒ SN = 5 + 1 = 6, lp = 1 ⇒ SQUARE PYRAMIDAL ✔ (four F in the square base, the O at the apex, the lone pair trans to O).
6. ICl4−: I has 7 + 1 = 8; four σ bonds ⇒ 2 lone pairs, SN = 6 ⇒ square planar ✘ (isostructural with BrF4− and XeF4).
7. Square pyramidal: IF5, BrF5, XeOF4 ⇒ 3.
 species     central   valence e-   sigma   lp   SN   shape               counts?
 PF5         P             5          5      0    5   trig. bipyramidal     no
 BrF4(-)     Br          7+1=8        4      2    6   square planar         no
 IF5         I             7          5      1    6   SQUARE PYRAMIDAL      YES
 BrF5        Br            7          5      1    6   SQUARE PYRAMIDAL      YES
 XeOF4       Xe            8        4F+1O    1    6   SQUARE PYRAMIDAL      YES
 ICl4(-)     I           7+1=8        4      2    6   square planar         no
                                                             count =  3   <-- answer (3)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the SN = 6 family as a three-step staircase and you never have to think again: AB6 octahedral → AB5L square pyramidal → AB4L2 square planar (SF6 → BrF5/IF5/XeOF4 → XeF4/BrF4−/ICl4−). Two traps are planted here. First, PF5 is put at the head of the list so that the "5" tempts you into the square-pyramidal box — but SN 5 gives a trigonal bipyramid, never a square pyramid. Second, XeOF4 looks like an SN 5 species until you remember the doubly bonded O still uses a σ position; count σ-bonds plus lone pairs and it lands squarely on 6.
Answer: 3 (IF₅, BrF₅, XeOF₄)
72The number of following factors which affect the percent covalent character of the ionic bond is ________ : (a) Polarising power of cation (b) Extent of distortion of anion (c) Polarisability of the anion (d) Polarising power of anion [JEE Main 2023 (08 April Shift 1)]Fajans' rules — which factors matterWhat Fajans' rules actually say about covalent character and polarisation🟡 Similar33 — printed key shows "c" ⚠
Concept Fajans' rules describe a one-way process: the cation polarises, the anion is polarised. A cation is a small, electron-poor centre with an exposed nucleus, so it pulls; an anion is a large, electron-rich, loosely held cloud, so it yields. The extent to which the anion's cloud is dragged into the internuclear region is the covalent character of the "ionic" bond. Because the process has a fixed direction, only three quantities can matter: how hard the cation pulls, how easily the anion yields, and the resulting distortion itself. The property that does not exist in this picture is any polarising ability of the anion — a diffuse negative cloud has essentially no power to distort a compact cation.
Key relation covalent character ∝ (polarising power of cation) × (polarisability of anion), with polarising power ∝ charge / radius² and polarisability ∝ size and charge of the anion "extent of distortion of the anion" is simply the observable outcome of those two acting together — it is the polarisation itself, so it is a legitimate factor and not a duplicate.
Solution Judge each listed factor against Fajans' rules.
1. (a) Polarising power of cation — the central Fajans variable. Small size and high charge raise it: Li⁺ > Na⁺ > K⁺, Al3+ > Mg2+ > Na⁺. This is why AlCl3 is covalent and NaCl is ionic. COUNTS ✔
2. (b) Extent of distortion of anion — the distortion of the anionic electron cloud is exactly what "partial covalent character" means physically; a larger distortion means more shared electron density between the nuclei. COUNTS ✔
3. (c) Polarisability of the anion — the other half of Fajans. A big, soft anion yields easily: I⁻ > Br⁻ > Cl⁻ > F⁻, so AgI is far more covalent than AgF. COUNTS ✔
4. (d) Polarising power of anion — not a Fajans factor. Polarising power is a cation property; it scales as charge/radius², and an anion is large with a diffuse, well-shielded cloud, so its charge density is far too low to distort the small cation appreciably. Anions are polarised, not polarising. DOES NOT COUNT ✘
5. Factors that affect the percent covalent character: (a), (b), (c) ⇒ 3.
 factor                              is it a Fajans variable?                       counts
 (a) polarising power of CATION      yes - charge/radius^2 of the cation             YES
 (b) extent of distortion of anion   yes - this IS the polarisation / covalency      YES
 (c) polarisability of the ANION     yes - size and softness of the anion            YES
 (d) polarising power of ANION       NO  - anions are polarised, not polarising       no
                                                                    count =  3   <-- answer (3)
                                                     printed key "c"  -> flagged, see Note
⚠ Note The printed answer key on page 8 gives "72. c". That cannot be the answer to this item: the question is of numerical-value type — "the number of following factors … is ______" — so (a)–(d) are the factors being counted, not answer options, and the response must be an integer. The independently derived and officially reported value is 3. The most likely cause is a transcription slip in which the digit 3 was set as the letter c (the two glyphs are easily confused in the key's typeface, and the neighbouring numerical entries 71 → 3, 73 → 4 and 75 → 5 are printed as digits). Use 3.
⚡ JEE Trick Fix the direction of the arrow once: cation → polarises → anion. Any option that reverses it ("polarisability of the cation", "polarising power of the anion") is a distractor by construction, and NTA plants exactly one of them in every Fajans question. The one genuinely subtle entry is (b): students discard "extent of distortion of anion" as a restatement rather than a factor. It is not a duplicate — it is the measured outcome, the quantity the other two control, and the examiner counts it. Keep the working list at four real factors overall: cation size, cation charge, anion size/polarisability, and the cation's electronic configuration (18 e⁻ cores like Cu⁺ and Ag⁺ polarise far more strongly than 8 e⁻ cores of the same radius).
Answer: 3 — factors (a), (b) and (c) ⚠
73The number of species from the following carrying a single lone pair on central atom Xenon is ____ : XeF5+, XeO3, XeO2F2, XeF5−, XeO3F2, XeOF4, XeF4 (JEE Main 2023, 08 April Shift 2 — Level-4)Lone pairs on Xe in xenon oxyfluoridesCounting how many compounds carry exactly one lone pair on the central atom🟡 Similar44
Concept Xenon brings 8 valence electrons to the table. Every terminal F forms one single bond and therefore consumes one of Xe's electrons; every terminal O forms a Xe=O double bond and therefore consumes two. Whatever electrons are left over on xenon stay behind as lone pairs, always in pairs. So you never need a Lewis diagram for these — one arithmetic line per species settles it. The charge is folded in at the start: a positive charge removes one electron from the central atom's pool, a negative charge adds one.
Key relation For a xenon species XeOxFy of charge q: LP = (8 − q − 2x − y) / 2 8 = valence electrons of Xe; subtract q for the charge (q = +1 removes one, q = −1 adds one, i.e. −(−1) = +1); each O eats 2 electrons, each F eats 1; halve because leftovers pair up. The steric number then follows as SN = (number of σ bonds) + LP, which fixes the shape.
Solution
1. XeF5+: LP = (8 − 1 − 0 − 5)/2 = 1. SN = 5 + 1 = 6 ⇒ sp3d2, square pyramidal. Counts.
2. XeO3: LP = (8 − 0 − 6 − 0)/2 = 1. SN = 3 + 1 = 4 ⇒ sp3, trigonal pyramidal. Counts.
3. XeO2F2: LP = (8 − 0 − 4 − 2)/2 = 1. SN = 4 + 1 = 5 ⇒ sp3d, see-saw (lone pair in an equatorial site). Counts.
4. XeF5−: LP = (8 + 1 − 0 − 5)/2 = 2. SN = 5 + 2 = 7 ⇒ pentagonal planar. Two lone pairs, so it does not count. This is the one anion in the list — the extra electron is exactly what pushes it from one lone pair to two.
5. XeO3F2: LP = (8 − 0 − 6 − 2)/2 = 0. SN = 5 + 0 = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal, no lone pair at all. Does not count.
6. XeOF4: LP = (8 − 0 − 2 − 4)/2 = 1. SN = 5 + 1 = 6 ⇒ square pyramidal. Counts.
7. XeF4: LP = (8 − 0 − 0 − 4)/2 = 2. SN = 4 + 2 = 6 ⇒ square planar. Does not count.
8. Species with exactly one lone pair: XeF5+, XeO3, XeO2F2, XeOF4 ⇒ 4.
 species        8 − q − 2x − y        LP    σ    SN   shape                 counts?
 XeF5+          8 − 1 − 0 − 5 = 2      1    5     6   square pyramidal        YES
 XeO3           8 − 0 − 6 − 0 = 2      1    3     4   trigonal pyramidal      YES
 XeO2F2         8 − 0 − 4 − 2 = 2      1    4     5   see-saw                 YES
 XeF5−          8 + 1 − 0 − 5 = 4      2    5     7   pentagonal planar        no
 XeO3F2         8 − 0 − 6 − 2 = 0      0    5     5   trigonal bipyramidal     no
 XeOF4          8 − 0 − 2 − 4 = 2      1    5     6   square pyramidal        YES
 XeF4           8 − 0 − 0 − 4 = 4      2    4     6   square planar            no
                                                       total with 1 LP = 4   <-- answer (4)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the three "one-lone-pair" xenon families and you can answer on sight: XeO3 (pyramidal), XeO2F2 (see-saw) and XeOF4/XeF5+ (square pyramidal). The traps set here are the two two-lone-pair species that look innocent — XeF4 (everyone remembers it as square planar but forgets that "square planar" means 2 LP) and XeF5−, where the negative sign is easy to read as a positive. The other trap is XeO3F2: three oxygens plus two fluorines exhaust all eight electrons, so it is the rare xenon compound with zero lone pairs.
Answer: 4
74Given below are two statements: Statement I: SO2 and H2O both possess V-shaped structure. Statement II: The bond angle of SO2 is less than that of H2O. In the light of the above statements, choose the most appropriate answer: (a) Both Statement I and Statement II are incorrect (b) Both Statement I and Statement II are correct (c) Statement I is incorrect but Statement II is correct (d) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect (JEE Main 2023, 13 April Shift 2 — Level-4)V-shape and bond angle: SO2 vs H2OWorked solution of this exact statement pair on SO₂ and H₂O🟢 ExactdStatement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect
Concept Two molecules can share the same name for their shape and still have very different bond angles, because the angle is decided by how many lone pairs squeeze the bond pairs, not by the shape label. Both SO2 and H2O are AB2E-type bent (V-shaped) molecules, but SO2 carries one lone pair on sulfur while H2O carries two on oxygen. Repulsion strength runs lp–lp > lp–bp > bp–bp, so each extra lone pair closes the angle further.
Key relation SO2: SN = 2 σ + 1 lp = 3 ⇒ sp2, ideal 120°, observed ≈ 119.5° only one lone pair, so the ideal trigonal-planar angle barely shrinks. H2O: SN = 2 σ + 2 lp = 4 ⇒ sp3, ideal 109.5°, observed ≈ 104.5° two lone pairs close it by a full 5° from an already smaller ideal.
Solution
1. Statement I. SO2: S has 6 valence electrons, two go into the two σ bonds to O and the remaining pair stays as a lone pair ⇒ SN = 3, one position occupied by a lone pair ⇒ bent / V-shaped. H2O: O has 6 valence electrons, two σ bonds and two lone pairs ⇒ SN = 4, two positions occupied by lone pairs ⇒ bent / V-shaped. Both are V-shaped ⇒ Statement I is correct.
2. Statement II. SO2 ≈ 119.5°, H2O ≈ 104.5°, so the SO2 angle is greater, not less. Statement II is incorrect. Two independent effects both push the same way: SO2 is sp2 (higher s-character ⇒ wider ideal angle) and it has one fewer lone pair than water.
3. Correct + incorrect ⇒ option (d). Eliminate (b) — it would need SO2 < H2O, which is false. Eliminate (c) and (a) — both call Statement I wrong, but the V-shape of both molecules is textbook.
 molecule   σ bonds   lone pairs   SN   hybridisation   shape       bond angle
 SO2           2          1         3       sp2         V-shaped     ~119.5°
 H2O           2          2         4       sp3         V-shaped     ~104.5°
 Statement I  : both V-shaped                                 -> CORRECT
 Statement II : angle(SO2) < angle(H2O)?  119.5 > 104.5 -> INCORRECT   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick For any two bent molecules, rank the angle by lone-pair count first, then s-character. One lone pair (SO2, O3, NO2−) sits near 115–120°; two lone pairs (H2O, OF2, SCl2) sit near 100–105°. The distractor here is entirely psychological: the paper puts a true statement first, and once you have ticked "correct" you tend to nod along to the second one. Read Statement II as a standalone inequality and check it against the two numbers — that alone kills (b).
Answer: (d) Statement I is correct but Statement II is incorrect
75Among I3+, SiO44−, SO2Cl2, XeF2, SF4, ClF3, Ni(CO)4, XeO2F2, PtCl42−, XeF4, and SOCl2, the total number of species having sp3 hybridised central atom is ____ . (JEE Adv. 2023, Paper-2 — Level-4)Counting sp3 centresFull solution of this JEE Advanced 2023 Paper-2 counting problem (answer 5)🟢 Exact55
Concept Hybridisation is read off the steric number = (σ bonds) + (lone pairs on the central atom). π bonds never enter the count, because they use unhybridised p (or d) orbitals. SN 4 ⇒ sp3, SN 5 ⇒ sp3d, SN 6 ⇒ sp3d2. Two special cases must be handled separately from VSEPR arithmetic: a metal carbonyl like Ni(CO)4, where Ni(0) is 3d10 with no lone pair on the valence s/p set and pairs into a tetrahedral sp3 set, and a d8 square-planar complex like PtCl42−, which is dsp2 — not sp3 — even though it has four ligands.
Key relation LP = (V ± charge − nσ) / 2 , SN = nσ + LP V is the central atom's valence-electron count; add 1 for each unit of negative charge on the species and subtract 1 for each unit of positive charge; a doubly-bonded O consumes 2 of the central atom's electrons (1 σ + 1 π) so it still contributes only 1 to nσ.
Solution
1. I3+: central I, V = 7, charge +1 ⇒ 6; nσ = 2 ⇒ LP = (6 − 2)/2 = 2; SN = 4 ⇒ sp3 (bent). ✔
2. SiO44−: four σ bonds, no lone pair ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp3 (tetrahedral). ✔
3. SO2Cl2: S makes 2 σ to O (plus π) and 2 σ to Cl; V = 6 is fully used ⇒ LP = 0; SN = 4 ⇒ sp3. ✔
4. XeF2: LP = (8 − 2)/2 = 3, SN = 5 ⇒ sp3d. ✘   SF4: LP = (6 − 4)/2 = 1, SN = 5 ⇒ sp3d. ✘   ClF3: LP = (7 − 3)/2 = 2, SN = 5 ⇒ sp3d. ✘
5. Ni(CO)4: CO is a strong-field ligand, Ni(0) 3d84s2 rearranges to 3d104s0, and the four CO lone pairs enter one 4s + three 4p orbitals ⇒ sp3, tetrahedral, diamagnetic. ✔
6. XeO2F2: LP = (8 − 4 − 2)/2 = 1, nσ = 4, SN = 5 ⇒ sp3d (see-saw). ✘
7. PtCl42−: Pt2+ is 5d8; with four ligands it pairs up its d electrons and uses dsp2 ⇒ square planar, not sp3. ✘ this is the single most missed entry in the list — four ligands does not automatically mean tetrahedral.
8. XeF4: LP = (8 − 4)/2 = 2, SN = 6 ⇒ sp3d2. ✘   SOCl2: S makes 1 σ to O and 2 σ to Cl, LP = 1 ⇒ SN = 4 ⇒ sp3 (pyramidal). ✔
9. sp3 centres: I3+, SiO44−, SO2Cl2, Ni(CO)4, SOCl2 ⇒ 5.
 species      nσ   LP   SN   hybridisation   sp3 ?
 I3+           2    2    4       sp3           YES
 SiO4 4−       4    0    4       sp3           YES
 SO2Cl2        4    0    4       sp3           YES
 XeF2          2    3    5       sp3d           no
 SF4           4    1    5       sp3d           no
 ClF3          3    2    5       sp3d           no
 Ni(CO)4       4    0    4       sp3           YES
 XeO2F2        4    1    5       sp3d           no
 PtCl4 2−      4    -    4       dsp2           no   (square planar, d8)
 XeF4          4    2    6       sp3d2          no
 SOCl2         3    1    4       sp3           YES
                                        total = 5   <-- answer (5)
⚡ JEE Trick Scan the list for anything with 5 or 6 around the centre and strike it out first — XeF2, SF4, ClF3, XeO2F2, XeF4 all die in one pass. Then, among what is left, the only genuine decision is PtCl42− vs Ni(CO)4: d8 ⇒ square planar dsp2, d10 ⇒ tetrahedral sp3. The paper deliberately puts both in the same list so that a student who "knows" one of them applies the same answer to both and lands on 4 or 6.
Answer: 5
76According to MO theory the bond orders for O22−, CO and NO+ respectively, are (a) 1, 3 and 3 (b) 1, 3 and 2 (c) 1, 2 and 3 (d) 2, 3 and 3 (JEE Main 2023, 29 Jan. Shift 2 — Level-3)Bond order from MOT; isoelectronic speciesMO diagrams and bond-order comparison for the CO, O₂ and N₂ families🟡 Similara1, 3 and 3
Concept Bond order in MO theory is fixed entirely by how many valence electrons the species has, provided you use the right ordering of levels. For light diatomics/heterodiatomics with total electron count ≤ 14 (B2 through N2, CO, CN−, NO+), s–p mixing pushes σ2pz above the π2p pair; for O2, F2 and their ions (> 14 electrons) σ2pz lies below π2p. Either way the filling order below the π* level is identical, so for these three species the ordering question never changes the count.
Key relation BO = (Nb − Na) / 2 Nb = electrons in bonding MOs, Na = electrons in antibonding MOs; core 1s electrons cancel in pairs, so count valence electrons only. Valence configuration used: σ2s² σ*2s² (σ2pz² π2px² π2py²) π*2px π*2py σ*2pz
Solution
1. O22− (peroxide ion): valence electrons = 6 + 6 + 2 = 14. Fill: σ2s² σ*2s² σ2pz² π2p⁴ π*2p⁴. Nb = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8, Na = 2 + 4 = 6 ⇒ BO = (8 − 6)/2 = 1. both π* orbitals are completely full, so it is also diamagnetic — exactly the single O–O bond you draw in H2O2.
2. CO: valence electrons = 4 + 6 = 10. Fill: σ2s² σ*2s² π2p⁴ σ2pz². Nb = 2 + 4 + 2 = 8, Na = 2 ⇒ BO = (8 − 2)/2 = 3.
3. NO+: valence electrons = 5 + 6 − 1 = 10 — isoelectronic with CO, CN− and N2 ⇒ same filling ⇒ BO = 3.
4. So the triple is (1, 3, 3) ⇒ option (a). Eliminate (b): it needs NO+ = 2, which would be the neutral NO (11 valence electrons, BO 2.5) misremembered. Eliminate (c): it needs CO = 2, contradicting the C≡O triple bond. Eliminate (d): it needs O22− = 2, which is neutral O2, not the peroxide ion.
 species    valence e-   Nb   Na   BO = (Nb−Na)/2   magnetism      isoelectronic with
 O2 2−         14         8    6         1          diamagnetic    F2
 CO            10         8    2         3          diamagnetic    N2, CN−, NO+
 NO+           10         8    2         3          diamagnetic    N2, CN−, CO
                                    (1, 3, 3)  <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the O2 ladder once — O2+ 2.5, O2 2.0, O2− 1.5, O22− 1.0 — every added electron goes into π* and drops the bond order by exactly 0.5. And memorise the 10-valence-electron club: N2, CO, CN−, NO+, C22− all have BO = 3 and are all diamagnetic. Between them those two facts answer most MOT numerical questions in under ten seconds. The distractors here are built from near-miss species (neutral O2 for O22−, neutral NO for NO+), so always recount the charge before you count electrons.
Answer: (a) 1, 3 and 3
77Match List I with List II — List I (molecules/ions): (A) IF7, (B) ICl4−, (C) XeF6, (D) XeF2; List II (No. of lone pairs of e− on central atom): (i) Three, (ii) One, (iii) Two, (iv) Zero. Choose the correct answer: (a) A-ii, B-iii, C-iv, D-i (b) A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i (c) A-ii, B-i, C-iv, D-iii (d) A-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii (JEE Main 2023, 30 Jan. Shift 1 — Level-3)Lone-pair count on interhalogen / noble-gas centresWorking out the lone pairs on the central atom of XeF₂, XeF₄ and XeF₆🟡 SimilarbA-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i
Concept Every one of these four is a halogen or noble-gas centre surrounded only by single-bonded halogens, so the arithmetic is as simple as it gets: take the central atom's valence electrons, adjust for the ionic charge, spend one electron per bond, and pair up whatever survives. Notice how the answer marches down in steps of one as you strip fluorines off xenon — XeF6 1, XeF4 2, XeF2 3 — which is the pattern the examiner expects you to recall instantly.
Key relation LP = (V + (negative charge) − (number of σ bonds)) / 2 V = 7 for the halogens I and Cl, V = 8 for Xe; the anionic charge adds electrons to the central atom's pool.
Solution
1. (A) IF7: V = 7, seven σ bonds ⇒ LP = (7 − 7)/2 = 0 ⇒ (iv) Zero. SN = 7 ⇒ sp3d3, pentagonal bipyramidal.
2. (B) ICl4−: V = 7, +1 for the charge ⇒ 8; four σ bonds ⇒ LP = (8 − 4)/2 = 2 ⇒ (iii) Two. SN = 6 ⇒ sp3d2, square planar (isostructural with XeF4).
3. (C) XeF6: V = 8, six σ bonds ⇒ LP = (8 − 6)/2 = 1 ⇒ (ii) One. SN = 7 ⇒ distorted octahedral / capped octahedral, the classic "non-ideal" geometry caused by that stereochemically active single lone pair.
4. (D) XeF2: V = 8, two σ bonds ⇒ LP = (8 − 2)/2 = 3 ⇒ (i) Three. SN = 5 ⇒ sp3d; all three lone pairs occupy the equatorial plane, leaving the molecule linear.
5. A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i ⇒ option (b). (a) fails at once because IF7 cannot have one lone pair with seven bonds; (c) and (d) both place ICl4− at "Three", which would demand SN = 7 for a square-planar ion.
 species     V   charge   nσ   LP = (V+q−nσ)/2   SN   shape                    List II
 IF7         7     0       7        0             7   pentagonal bipyramidal   (iv) Zero
 ICl4−       7    +1       4        2             6   square planar            (iii) Two
 XeF6        8     0       6        1             7   distorted octahedral     (ii) One
 XeF2        8     0       2        3             5   linear                   (i) Three
                                    A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i   <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Match-the-list questions are decided by the most extreme entry. Here IF7 is the only species that can possibly have zero lone pairs (seven bonds exhaust all seven of iodine's electrons), and only options (b) and (d) put A at (iv). One more check — XeF2 has three lone pairs — and (d) dies too. Two decisions, no full matching required. The trap is reading ICl4− as neutral ICl4, which would give a fractional lone-pair count and signal immediately that you dropped the charge.
Answer: (b) A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i
78What is the number of unpaired electron(s) in the highest occupied molecular orbital of the following species: N2, N2+, O2, O2+ ? (a) 0, 1, 2, 1 (b) 2, 1, 2, 1 (c) 0, 1, 0, 1 (d) 2, 1, 0, 1 (JEE Main 2023, 24 Jan. Shift 2 — Level-2)HOMO occupancy and unpaired electronsWorked solution: unpaired electrons in the HOMO of N₂, N₂⁺, O₂ and O₂⁺🟢 Exactc0, 1, 0, 1 — printed key shows "c" ⚠
Concept The HOMO is simply the last molecular orbital that receives electrons. Which orbital that is depends on the electron count and on whether s–p mixing is operating. For species with 14 or fewer total electrons (N2, N2+) the mixing is strong and σ2pz sits above π2p, so σ2pz is the HOMO. For O2 and O2+ (more than 14 electrons) the mixing collapses, σ2pz drops below π2p, and the last electrons go into the degenerate π*2p pair — which is why Hund's rule matters and why O2 is paramagnetic.
Key relation ≤ 14 e⁻: σ1s σ*1s σ2s σ*2s π2px = π2py σ2pz π*2p σ*2p σ2pz above π2p because of 2s–2p mixing. > 14 e⁻: σ1s σ*1s σ2s σ*2s σ2pz π2px = π2py π*2px = π*2py σ*2pz Hund's rule applies within the degenerate π* pair — two electrons there stay unpaired.
Solution
1. N2 (14 e⁻): …π2px² π2py² σ2pz². HOMO = σ2pz, holding 2 paired electrons ⇒ 0 unpaired. Diamagnetic, BO 3.
2. N2+ (13 e⁻): the electron is removed from the HOMO, leaving σ2pz1 ⇒ 1 unpaired. Paramagnetic, BO 2.5.
3. O2 (16 e⁻): …σ2pz² π2p⁴ π*2px1 π*2py1. HOMO = the degenerate π*2p set with two electrons, and by Hund's rule they occupy the two orbitals singly with parallel spins ⇒ 2 unpaired. This is the textbook triumph of MOT: it explains the paramagnetism of O2 that Lewis structures cannot. BO 2.
4. O2+ (15 e⁻): one π* electron is gone ⇒ π*2px1 ⇒ 1 unpaired. Paramagnetic, BO 2.5.
5. The sequence is therefore 0, 1, 2, 1, i.e. option (a). (b) and (d) both claim 2 unpaired electrons in N2, which would make nitrogen paramagnetic — it is not. (c) claims 0 unpaired for O2, which would make dioxygen diamagnetic — it is not.
 species   total e−   HOMO           occupancy of HOMO      unpaired   magnetism
 N2          14       σ2pz           2 (paired)                 0      diamagnetic
 N2+         13       σ2pz           1                          1      paramagnetic
 O2          16       π*2p (x,y)     2 (one in each, Hund)      2      paramagnetic
 O2+         15       π*2p (x)       1                          1      paramagnetic
                                              0, 1, 2, 1   <-- derived answer (a)
                                              printed key : (c) 0, 1, 0, 1
⚠ Note Answer-key mismatch. The printed key on page 8 of the answer booklet reads 78. c — i.e. 0, 1, 0, 1. That requires O2 to have zero unpaired electrons in its HOMO, which directly contradicts the single most famous result in molecular orbital theory: O2 is paramagnetic precisely because its two highest electrons sit unpaired in the degenerate π*2px and π*2py orbitals. Option (c) also differs from (a) only in that one position, so the slip is a one-character misprint of "a" as "c". The chemically correct choice is (a) 0, 1, 2, 1. Note that the very next question in this Strong Box's own Level sections and the standard NCERT treatment both quote O2 as having two unpaired electrons.
⚡ JEE Trick Answer this without any diagram by using magnetism as the shortcut: even electron count + no degenerate half-filled level ⇒ 0 unpaired; odd electron count ⇒ exactly 1 unpaired; O2 itself is the one even-electron exception with 2. So the pattern for (N2, N2+, O2, O2+) is (even ⇒ 0, odd ⇒ 1, the O2 exception ⇒ 2, odd ⇒ 1). The distractors are built by breaking exactly one of those rules at a time — (b)/(d) break the N2 entry, (c) breaks the O2 entry.
Answer: derived (a) 0, 1, 2, 1; printed key shows (c) 0, 1, 0, 1 — flagged as a key error
79The number of P−O−P bonds in H4P2O7, (HPO3)3, and P4O10 are respectively (a) 0, 3, 6 (b) 0, 3, 4 (c) 1, 2, 4 (d) 1, 3, 6 (JEE Main 2023, 15 April Shift 1 — Level-2)Bridging oxygens in phosphorus oxyacids and oxidesCounting the P−O−P bridges in H₄P₂O₇, (HPO₃)₃ and P₄O₁₀🟢 Exactd1, 3, 6
Concept Every phosphorus in these species sits at the centre of a PO4 tetrahedron. A P−O−P linkage is simply an oxygen shared between two tetrahedra — a "bridging" oxygen. So the question is really "how many corners do the tetrahedra share?" Counting corners is far more reliable than trying to draw the whole structure: an open chain of n tetrahedra shares n − 1 corners, a closed ring of n tetrahedra shares n corners, and the cage P4O10 shares one corner along each of the six edges of a P4 tetrahedron.
Key relation bridging O = total O − (terminal O per P) × (number of P) in an oxyacid each P keeps one P=O and its own P−OH oxygens; whatever oxygen is left over must be shared. Geometric shortcuts: open chain of n tetrahedra ⇒ n − 1 bridges; ring of n ⇒ n bridges; P4 cage ⇒ 6 bridges (one per edge)
Solution
1. H4P2O7 (pyrophosphoric acid) is two PO4 tetrahedra joined at one corner: (HO)2(O)P−O−P(O)(OH)2. Check by counting: 2 P × (1 P=O + 2 P−OH) = 6 oxygens accounted for, and there are 7, so exactly 1 oxygen bridges. Chain of n = 2 ⇒ n − 1 = 1 P−O−P.
2. (HPO3)3 (cyclotrimetaphosphoric acid) is a six-membered −P−O−P−O−P−O− ring of three tetrahedra. Each P keeps one P=O and one P−OH (2 terminal oxygens × 3 P = 6), and the formula supplies 9 oxygens, leaving 9 − 6 = 3 bridging oxygens. Ring of n = 3 ⇒ 3 P−O−P. every P in a metaphosphate is doubly bridged, which is exactly what makes the ring close.
3. P4O10: the four P atoms sit at the corners of a tetrahedron; the six edges each carry one bridging oxygen, and the four remaining oxygens are terminal P=O groups pointing outwards (6 + 4 = 10 ✓). Hence 6 P−O−P.
4. The triple is (1, 3, 6) ⇒ option (d). Options (a) and (b) put 0 for pyrophosphoric acid, confusing it with orthophosphoric acid H3PO4 (a single isolated tetrahedron, no bridge). Option (c) puts 4 for P4O10, which is the number of terminal oxygens, not the bridges.
 species        P atoms   total O   terminal O   bridging O = P−O−P   structure
 H4P2O7            2         7      2×3 = 6            1             2 tetrahedra, corner-shared
 (HPO3)3           3         9      3×2 = 6            3             6-membered P–O–P ring
 P4O10             4        10      4×1 = 4            6             P4 cage, 1 O per edge
                                                   (1, 3, 6)   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Keep three anchor numbers in memory and you never have to draw anything: P4O6 ⇒ 6 bridges and 0 terminal O; P4O10 ⇒ 6 bridges and 4 terminal O (the four extra oxygens in P4O10 are exactly the four P=O caps), and any metaphosphate ring (HPO3)n ⇒ n bridges. The classic trap is P4O10: students who remember "10 oxygens, 4 phosphorus" often answer 4, which is why option (c) exists.
Answer: (d) 1, 3, 6
80The total number of lone pairs of electrons on oxygen atoms of ozone is ____ . (JEE Main 2023, 25 Jan. Shift 1 — Level-1)Lewis structure and lone-pair count of O3Counting every lone pair on the three oxygens of ozone🟢 Exact66
Concept The safest way to count lone pairs in a small molecule is electron bookkeeping, not drawing. Add up all valence electrons, subtract the ones locked into bonds, and everything left is lone pairs. The subtlety in ozone is that the question says "on oxygen atoms" — that is all three oxygens, central and terminal, not just the central one. Ozone is also a resonance hybrid, but resonance moves the π bond around without ever changing the total lone-pair count, so any one canonical form gives the right number.
Key relation total valence e⁻ = 3 × 6 = 18 each O contributes 6. electrons in bonds = 2 σ (O–O) + 1 π = 3 bonds × 2 e⁻ = 6 lone-pair electrons = 18 − 6 = 12 ⇒ lone pairs = 12 / 2 = 6
Solution
1. Ozone is bent (SN = 3 on the central O: two σ bonds + one lone pair ⇒ sp2, angle ≈ 117°). One canonical structure is Oa=Ocentre+−Ob−.
2. Central O (formal charge +1): it uses four of its six electrons in the σ + π framework, leaving 1 lone pair. a formal +1 on oxygen always means three bonds and one lone pair, exactly as in H3O+.
3. Doubly-bonded terminal Oa (formal charge 0): two bonds used, 2 lone pairs left.
4. Singly-bonded terminal Ob (formal charge −1): one bond used and one extra electron gained, 3 lone pairs.
5. Total = 1 + 2 + 3 = 6, which agrees exactly with the bookkeeping check (18 − 6)/2 = 6. Switching to the other resonance form just swaps Oa and Ob, so the total is unchanged.
 atom              bonds made        formal charge   lone pairs
 terminal Oa        1 σ + 1 π  (=O)        0              2
 central  O         2 σ + 1 π             +1              1
 terminal Ob        1 σ        (−O−)      −1              3
                                        total lone pairs = 6   <-- answer (6)
 cross-check: total valence 18 e−, bonds use 6 e−, (18−6)/2 = 6 lone pairs
⚡ JEE Trick For any species, lone pairs = (total valence electrons − 2 × number of bonds) / 2, where "number of bonds" counts a double bond as 2 and a triple as 3. One line, no drawing, no resonance worries. Two traps are set here: reading "on oxygen atoms" as "on the central oxygen" (which would give 1) and forgetting that the terminal oxygens are inequivalent in any single canonical form (2 and 3, not 2 and 2 — which would give the wrong total of 5).
Answer: 6
81The number of given orbitals which have electron density along the axis is ____ : px, py, pz, dxy, dyz, dxz, dz², dx²−y² (JEE Main 2023, 25 Jan. Shift 2 — Level-1)Orientation of p and d orbitalsWhich orbitals point along the axes and which point between them🟡 Similar55
Concept An orbital's name encodes its orientation. A subscript with a single axis letter (x, y, z) means the lobes lie along that axis; a subscript with a product of two letters (xy, yz, xz) means the lobes lie in that plane but bisecting the two axes, i.e. between them, at 45°. The two odd-looking d orbitals are the axial ones: dx²−y² is a difference of squared coordinates, so its four lobes sit exactly on the ±x and ±y axes, and dz² has its principal lobes on ±z. This distinction is the entire basis of crystal field theory — the axial set (eg) is raised in energy in an octahedral field precisely because it points straight at the ligands.
Key relation single-letter subscript (px, py, pz, dz²) ⇒ lobes ON the axis two-letter product subscript (dxy, dyz, dxz) ⇒ lobes BETWEEN the axes (t2g set) dx²−y² ⇒ lobes ON the x and y axes (eg set, with dz²) eg = axial pair, t2g = non-axial trio; that is the whole octahedral splitting in one line.
Solution
1. px, py, pz — a p orbital is two lobes on opposite sides of the nucleus lying directly on its named axis. All three are axial ⇒ 3.
2. dxy, dyz, dxz — four lobes each, lying in the named plane but rotated 45° off the axes, so the electron density on the axes themselves is zero (the axes are the nodal planes' intersections). These are the t2g set ⇒ contribute 0.
3. dz² — two large lobes on ±z plus a doughnut in the xy plane; the dominant density is along z ⇒ axial ⇒ 1.
4. dx²−y² — four lobes lying exactly on ±x and ±y ⇒ axial ⇒ 1.
5. Total axial orbitals = 3 + 1 + 1 = 5.
 orbital        subscript type        lobes point        axial ?
 px              single letter        along x              YES
 py              single letter        along y              YES
 pz              single letter        along z              YES
 dxy             product xy           between x and y       no   (t2g)
 dyz             product yz           between y and z       no   (t2g)
 dxz             product xz           between x and z       no   (t2g)
 dz2             single letter z      along z              YES   (eg)
 dx2−y2          squares of x, y      along x and y        YES   (eg)
                                                total = 5   <-- answer (5)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember it as "products are between, squares and singles are along". Then in one glance: all p orbitals are axial (3), the eg pair dz² and dx²−y² is axial (2), and the t2g trio is not (0) ⇒ 5. The trap is dz²: its full name is d2z²−x²−y², so some students file it with the "two-letter" group and answer 4. The second trap is forgetting the p orbitals entirely and answering 2 (just the eg pair).
Answer: 5
82Match List I with List II — List I: (A) XeF4, (B) SF4, (C) NH4+, (D) BrF3; List II: (i) See-saw, (ii) Square planar, (iii) Bent T-shaped, (iv) Tetrahedral. Choose the correct answer: (a) A-iv, B-iii, C-ii, D-i (b) A-ii, B-i, C-iii, D-iv (c) A-iv, B-i, C-ii, D-iii (d) A-ii, B-i, C-iv, D-iii (JEE Main 2023, 31 Jan. Shift 1 — Level-1)VSEPR shapes: square planar, see-saw, T-shapeMatching molecules to their VSEPR geometries in a list-matching question🟡 SimilardA-ii, B-i, C-iv, D-iii
Concept VSEPR gives the shape in two moves. First find the steric number SN = σ bonds + lone pairs; that fixes the electron-pair geometry. Then delete the lone-pair vertices; what remains is the molecular shape. The three "exotic" names in this list all come from the same parent geometries: an octahedron minus two trans lone pairs is square planar, a trigonal bipyramid minus one equatorial lone pair is a see-saw, and a trigonal bipyramid minus two equatorial lone pairs is a bent T-shape. Lone pairs always take equatorial sites in a trigonal bipyramid because an equatorial site has only two 90° neighbours instead of three.
Key relation LP = (V + charge(−) − charge(+) − nσ) / 2 , SN = nσ + LP SN 4: AX4 tetrahedral · SN 5: AX4E see-saw, AX3E2 T-shape · SN 6: AX4E2 square planar
Solution
1. (A) XeF4: V = 8, nσ = 4 ⇒ LP = (8 − 4)/2 = 2; SN = 6 ⇒ octahedral electron geometry with the two lone pairs trans ⇒ square planar ⇒ (ii).
2. (B) SF4: V = 6, nσ = 4 ⇒ LP = (6 − 4)/2 = 1; SN = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal with one equatorial lone pair ⇒ see-saw ⇒ (i). the lone pair also squeezes the axial F−S−F angle below 180° (≈173°) — the signature of a see-saw.
3. (C) NH4+: V = 5, minus 1 for the positive charge ⇒ 4; nσ = 4 ⇒ LP = 0; SN = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral, perfect 109°28′ ⇒ (iv).
4. (D) BrF3: V = 7, nσ = 3 ⇒ LP = (7 − 3)/2 = 2; SN = 5 ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal with both lone pairs equatorial ⇒ the three F atoms form a slightly bent T ⇒ bent T-shaped ⇒ (iii).
5. A-ii, B-i, C-iv, D-iii ⇒ option (d). (a) and (c) put XeF4 at tetrahedral, ignoring its two lone pairs; (b) puts NH4+ at bent T-shaped, which is impossible for a species with no lone pair at all.
 species    V   q    nσ   LP   SN   electron geometry        molecular shape     List II
 XeF4       8   0     4    2    6   octahedral               square planar       (ii)
 SF4        6   0     4    1    5   trigonal bipyramidal     see-saw             (i)
 NH4+       5  +1     4    0    4   tetrahedral              tetrahedral         (iv)
 BrF3       7   0     3    2    5   trigonal bipyramidal     bent T-shaped       (iii)
                                    A-ii, B-i, C-iv, D-iii   <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the AXnEm ladder for SN 5 and 6 and every one of these questions collapses: AX5 trigonal bipyramidal → AX4E see-saw → AX3E2 T-shape → AX2E3 linear; AX6 octahedral → AX5E square pyramidal → AX4E2 square planar. Here the fastest route is C: NH4+ is obviously tetrahedral, and only option (d) puts C at (iv) — one look ends the question. The distractors are all built by swapping XeF4's square planar with NH4+'s tetrahedral, betting that you forget XeF4 carries two lone pairs.
Answer: (d) A-ii, B-i, C-iv, D-iii
83The number of species having a square planar shape from the following is ____ : XeF4, SF4, SiF4, BF4−, BrF4−, [Cu(NH3)4]2+, FeCl42−, PtCl42− (JEE Main 2023, 06 April Shift 2 — Level-1)Counting square planar species (VSEPR + CFT)Solution to this exact square-planar counting question🟢 Exact44
Concept This list mixes two different families and you must switch tools halfway. For the main-group species use VSEPR: four ligands are square planar only when the central atom also carries two lone pairs (AX4E2, SN = 6). For the transition-metal complexes lone pairs are irrelevant; what decides the geometry is the metal's d-electron count: d8 (and d9 for copper(II)) favours dsp2 square planar, while d5, d6 with weak-field ligands and d10 favour sp3 tetrahedral. Reading four ligands as "tetrahedral" everywhere is exactly the mistake the question is built to punish.
Key relation Main group: square planar ⟺ nσ = 4 and LP = 2 ⟺ SN = 6 Complexes: d8/d9 ⇒ dsp2 square planar; d5, d6(weak field), d10 ⇒ sp3 tetrahedral a d8 metal can empty one d orbital by pairing, freeing it for a dsp2 set; 4d and 5d metals (Pd, Pt) do this almost without exception because their splitting is large.
Solution
1. XeF4: LP = (8 − 4)/2 = 2, SN = 6 ⇒ square planar. ✔
2. SF4: LP = (6 − 4)/2 = 1, SN = 5 ⇒ see-saw. ✘
3. SiF4: LP = (4 − 4)/2 = 0, SN = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral. ✘   BF4−: LP = (3 + 1 − 4)/2 = 0, SN = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral. ✘
4. BrF4−: LP = (7 + 1 − 4)/2 = 2, SN = 6 ⇒ square planar (isostructural with XeF4 and ICl4−). ✔
5. [Cu(NH3)4]2+: Cu2+ is 3d9; with four NH3 it adopts dsp2 square planar (the tetragonally distorted, Jahn–Teller-flattened arrangement) ⇒ square planar. ✔
6. FeCl42−: Fe2+ is 3d6 and Cl− is a weak-field ligand, so no pairing occurs and the ion is sp3 tetrahedral. ✘
7. PtCl42−: Pt2+ is 5d8 — the archetypal dsp2 square planar ion. ✔
8. Square planar: XeF4, BrF4−, [Cu(NH3)4]2+, PtCl42− ⇒ 4.
 species              basis          key number          geometry          square planar?
 XeF4                 VSEPR          LP = 2, SN = 6      square planar        YES
 SF4                  VSEPR          LP = 1, SN = 5      see-saw               no
 SiF4                 VSEPR          LP = 0, SN = 4      tetrahedral           no
 BF4−                 VSEPR          LP = 0, SN = 4      tetrahedral           no
 BrF4−                VSEPR          LP = 2, SN = 6      square planar        YES
 [Cu(NH3)4]2+         CFT/VBT        Cu2+ = d9, dsp2     square planar        YES
 FeCl4 2−             CFT/VBT        Fe2+ = d6 weak, sp3 tetrahedral           no
 PtCl4 2−             CFT/VBT        Pt2+ = d8, dsp2     square planar        YES
                                                              total = 4   <-- answer (4)
⚡ JEE Trick Split the list in two before you start. Main group: square planar needs the central atom to have exactly two lone pairs — the XeF4 / ICl4− / BrF4− family, all with SN 6. Complexes: chant "d8 and Cu(II) go flat, everything else with four ligands goes tetrahedral", which instantly gives Pt2+ and Cu2+ yes, Fe2+(weak field) no. The two planted traps are SF4 (four fluorines but only one lone pair) and FeCl42− (a transition metal that is nevertheless tetrahedral).
Answer: 4
84Match List-I with List-II — List-I (Species): (A) H3O+, (B) Acetylide anion, (C) NH4+, (D) ClO2−; List-II (Geometry/Shape): (i) Tetrahedral, (ii) Linear, (iii) Pyramidal, (iv) Bent. Choose the correct answer: (a) A-(iii), B-(iv), C-(i), D-(ii) (b) A-(iii), B-(i), C-(ii), D-(iv) (c) A-(iii), B-(ii), C-(i), D-(iv) (d) A-(iii), B-(iv), C-(ii), D-(i) (JEE Main 2023, 11 April Shift 1 — Level-1)Shapes of common cations and oxyanionsShape and hybridisation of BF₃, BF₄⁻, NH₃, NH₄⁺, H₂O and H₃O⁺🟡 SimilarcA-(iii), B-(ii), C-(i), D-(iv)
Concept Three of these four are decided by ordinary VSEPR on a single central atom, and the fourth — the acetylide anion C22− — has no single central atom, so VSEPR is replaced by molecular orbital reasoning. C22− carries 10 valence electrons, making it isoelectronic with N2, CO, CN− and NO+; like all of them it has a triple bond (BO 3), sp hybridisation at each carbon and a strictly linear [C≡C]2− skeleton. Note also that a positive charge removes an electron from the central atom's pool while a negative charge adds one — that single adjustment is what separates pyramidal H3O+ from tetrahedral NH4+.
Key relation LP = (V + q(−) − q(+) − nσ) / 2 , SN = nσ + LP SN 4 with 0 LP ⇒ tetrahedral · 1 LP ⇒ pyramidal · 2 LP ⇒ bent 10-valence-electron diatomic unit ⇒ triple bond ⇒ linear
Solution
1. (A) H3O+: O has V = 6, minus 1 for the charge ⇒ 5; nσ = 3 ⇒ LP = (5 − 3)/2 = 1; SN = 4 ⇒ sp3, one vertex is a lone pair ⇒ trigonal pyramidal ⇒ (iii). Angle ≈ 107°, slightly wider than water's 104.5° because one lone pair has been removed.
2. (B) Acetylide anion, C22−: valence electrons = 4 + 4 + 2 = 10 ⇒ isoelectronic with N2 ⇒ C≡C triple bond, each carbon sp-hybridised ⇒ linear ⇒ (ii). this is the anion in CaC2; sp carbons and a triple bond leave no possible bent arrangement.
3. (C) NH4+: N has V = 5, minus 1 for the charge ⇒ 4; nσ = 4 ⇒ LP = 0; SN = 4 ⇒ tetrahedral ⇒ (i).
4. (D) ClO2−: total valence = 7 + 12 + 1 = 20 e⁻. Two Cl−O σ bonds use 4; the two terminal oxygens take 3 lone pairs each (12 e⁻); the remaining 4 e⁻ sit on chlorine as 2 lone pairs. SN = 2 + 2 = 4 ⇒ sp3 with two lone pairs ⇒ bent ⇒ (iv), angle ≈ 111°.
5. A-iii, B-ii, C-i, D-iv ⇒ option (c). Options (a) and (d) call the acetylide anion bent or place NH4+ at linear — both impossible; (b) makes NH4+ linear, which no four-ligand species can be.
 species        V   charge   nσ   LP   SN   hybridisation   shape                List-II
 H3O+           6    +1       3    1    4        sp3        trigonal pyramidal   (iii)
 C2 2−          -     -       -    -    -        sp         linear (C≡C)         (ii)
 NH4+           5    +1       4    0    4        sp3        tetrahedral          (i)
 ClO2−          7    −1       2    2    4        sp3        bent                 (iv)
                                     A-iii, B-ii, C-i, D-iv   <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick All four options here start with A-(iii), so H3O+ gives you nothing — go straight to C. NH4+ is unmistakably tetrahedral (i), and only option (c) says so. That is a one-step solution. More generally, in match-the-list questions always attack the entry you are most certain of and check which options survive; entries common to every option are decoration. The real chemistry trap is B: "acetylide" sounds unfamiliar, but C22− is just N2 in disguise — 10 valence electrons, triple bond, linear, diamagnetic.
Answer: (c) A-(iii), B-(ii), C-(i), D-(iv)
85The maximum number of lone pairs of electron on the central atom from the following species is ____ : ClO3−, XeF4, SF4 and I3− (JEE Main 2023, 11 April Shift 2 — Level-1)Maximum lone-pair count on a central atomFinding which of ClO₃⁻, XeF₄, SF₄ and I₃⁻ has the most lone pairs🟢 Exact33
Concept Read the question carefully: it asks for the maximum value among the four, not the sum. So you compute the lone-pair count on each central atom and report the largest. The general rule is that lone pairs pile up when a central atom with many valence electrons makes few bonds — which is why the halide-rich anion I3− (eight electrons on iodine, only two bonds) wins, while an oxyanion like ClO3− spends nearly everything on its three oxygens.
Key relation LP = (V + q(−) − nσ) / 2 V is the central atom's valence electron count; a negative charge adds one electron; each single bond removes one. For oxyanions confirm with a full electron count, since terminal O may be double-bonded.
Solution
1. ClO3−: total valence = 7 + 3×6 + 1 = 26 e⁻. Three Cl−O σ bonds use 6; three terminal O take 3 lone pairs each = 18; the remaining 2 e⁻ sit on Cl ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 3 + 1 = 4 ⇒ sp3, trigonal pyramidal.
2. XeF4: LP = (8 − 4)/2 = 2. SN = 6 ⇒ square planar.
3. SF4: LP = (6 − 4)/2 = 1. SN = 5 ⇒ see-saw.
4. I3−: the central iodine has V = 7 and gains 1 from the charge ⇒ 8; it makes only two σ bonds to the terminal iodines ⇒ LP = (8 − 2)/2 = 3. SN = 5 ⇒ sp3d with all three lone pairs equatorial ⇒ linear ion.
5. The largest value is 3, from I3−.
 species    central atom   V   q    nσ   LP = (V+q−nσ)/2   SN   shape
 ClO3−           Cl        7   −1     3          1          4   trigonal pyramidal
 XeF4            Xe        8    0     4          2          6   square planar
 SF4             S         6    0     4          1          5   see-saw
 I3−             I         7   −1     2          3          5   linear
                                       maximum = 3 (I3−)   <-- answer (3)
⚡ JEE Trick Scan for the species with the fewest bonds on the heaviest-electron centre — that is always where the lone pairs collect. Among triatomic-type anions, memorise the family I3−, ICl2−, XeF2, BrF2−: three lone pairs, sp3d, linear — it recurs constantly. Two traps are set here: answering the sum 1 + 2 + 1 + 3 = 7 (a different question that JEE Advanced also asks, so read the stem), and answering 2 by stopping at XeF4, whose square planar shape is the most memorable in the list.
Answer: 3
86The bond order and magnetic property of acetylide ion are same as that of (a) O2+ (b) N2+ (c) NO+ (d) O2− (JEE Main 2023, 12 April Shift 1 — Level-1)Isoelectronic species: bond order and magnetismWorked solution: which ion matches acetylide in bond order and magnetism🟢 ExactcNO+
Concept Two diatomic species with the same number of valence electrons fill the same molecular orbitals in the same order, so they must share the same bond order and the same magnetic behaviour. Hence the whole question reduces to counting valence electrons on the acetylide ion and then finding the option that matches. Acetylide, C22−, is the anion in calcium carbide; it has 4 + 4 + 2 = 10 valence electrons, putting it in the famous ten-electron club with N2, CO, CN− and NO+ — all triple-bonded and all diamagnetic.
Key relation BO = (Nb − Na) / 2 · For ≤ 14 total electrons: σ2s² σ*2s² π2p⁴ σ2pz² ⇒ Nb = 8, Na = 2, BO = 3, all electrons paired ⇒ diamagnetic. any unpaired electron makes a species paramagnetic, so an odd valence-electron count guarantees paramagnetism.
Solution
1. Acetylide, C22−: 10 valence e⁻ ⇒ σ2s² σ*2s² π2px² π2py² σ2pz² ⇒ BO = (8 − 2)/2 = 3, all paired ⇒ diamagnetic. We are looking for BO 3 + diamagnetic.
2. (a) O2+: 11 valence e⁻ ⇒ one electron in π*2p ⇒ BO = 2.5, paramagnetic. ✘ (odd count, so it cannot possibly be diamagnetic).
3. (b) N2+: 9 valence e⁻ ⇒ σ2pz1 ⇒ BO = 2.5, paramagnetic. ✘
4. (c) NO+: 5 + 6 − 1 = 10 valence e⁻ ⇒ identical filling to C22− ⇒ BO = 3, diamagnetic. ✔ Both properties match.
5. (d) O2−: 13 valence e⁻ ⇒ π*2p³ ⇒ BO = 1.5, paramagnetic. ✘
 species     valence e−    configuration highlight     BO     magnetism
 C2 2−           10        σ2pz2, π2p filled           3.0    diamagnetic   (target)
 O2+             11        π*2p 1                      2.5    paramagnetic
 N2+              9        σ2pz 1                      2.5    paramagnetic
 NO+             10        σ2pz2, π2p filled           3.0    diamagnetic   <-- answer (c)
 O2−             13        π*2p 3                      1.5    paramagnetic
⚡ JEE Trick Count valence electrons first and match the number — never build four MO diagrams. Here the target is 10, and among the options only NO+ has 10. Even faster: an odd valence-electron count is always paramagnetic, and C22− (even) is diamagnetic, so O2+ (11), N2+ (9) and O2− (13) are all eliminated on parity alone, leaving (c) without any bond-order calculation. The trap is the unfamiliar name "acetylide" — students who do not connect it to C22− in CaC2 guess an O2 species.
Answer: (c) NO+
87In which of the following processes, the bond order increases and the paramagnetic character changes to diamagnetic one? (a) O2 → O2+ (b) O2 → O22− (c) NO → NO+ (d) N2 → N2+ (JEE Main 2023, 13 April Shift 1 — Level-1)Bond order and magnetism on ionisationMO diagrams of NO, NO⁺ and NO⁻ — bond order and magnetic behaviour🟡 SimilarcNO → NO+
Concept Two independent things are being asked at once, and only one process can satisfy both. Removing an electron raises the bond order only if that electron came out of an antibonding MO; pulling it out of a bonding MO lowers the bond order. Independently, a species is diamagnetic only when every MO is doubly filled. So you must find a species whose HOMO is an antibonding orbital holding exactly one electron — take that single electron away and the bond order goes up and the last unpaired spin disappears in the same stroke. That is precisely the situation in nitric oxide, the classic odd-electron molecule.
Key relation B.O. = ½ (Nb − Na) Nb, Na = electrons in bonding and antibonding MOs. Removing one antibonding electron: ΔB.O. = +½; removing one bonding electron: ΔB.O. = −½. Magnetism: unpaired e⁻ = 0 → diamagnetic, ≥1 → paramagnetic.
Solution
1. NO (15 e⁻): σ1s² σ*1s² σ2s² σ*2s² π2px² π2py² σ2pz² π*2px¹. B.O. = ½(10 − 5) = 2.5, one unpaired electron ⇒ paramagnetic.
2. NO+ (14 e⁻): the lone π* electron is the one removed ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 4) = 3.0 and every MO is now doubly occupied ⇒ diamagnetic. Bond order up, para → dia. NO⁺ is isoelectronic with N₂ and CO — hence its very short 1.06 Å bond and its 2150 cm⁻¹ stretch. ⇒ (c).
3. (a) O2 → O2+: O₂ has π*2px¹ π*2py¹ (B.O. 2, two unpaired). Removing one antibonding electron does raise B.O. to 2.5, but O₂⁺ still keeps one unpaired electron ⇒ still paramagnetic. Half-right, therefore wrong.
4. (b) O2 → O22−: two electrons are added to π*, filling it (π*⁴) ⇒ diamagnetic ✓, but B.O. falls 2 → 1 ✗.
5. (d) N2 → N2+: the electron leaves the bonding σ2pz (the HOMO of N₂) ⇒ B.O. falls 3 → 2.5 ✗ and N₂⁺ becomes paramagnetic ✗ — the exact reverse of what is asked.
 process              e- removed/added   B.O. change     unpaired e-      verdict
 O2 -> O2+            antibonding pi*    2   -> 2.5      2 -> 1  (para)   B.O. up, still para
 O2 -> O2 2-          +2 into pi*        2   -> 1        2 -> 0  (dia)    B.O. DOWN
 NO -> NO+            antibonding pi*    2.5 -> 3.0      1 -> 0  (dia)    both satisfied  <-- answer (c)
 N2 -> N2+            bonding sigma2p    3   -> 2.5      0 -> 1  (para)   both reversed
⚡ JEE Trick Scan for the odd-electron species first — NO, NO₂, ClO₂, O₂⁻. Only an odd-electron molecule can go from paramagnetic to diamagnetic by losing one electron, and if that odd electron sits in an antibonding MO the bond order rises at the same time. The trap is option (a): candidates who check only "bond order increases" stop at O₂ → O₂⁺ and never test the magnetism, while candidates who check only "becomes diamagnetic" fall for (b).
Answer: (c) NO → NO⁺ — bond order 2.5 → 3.0 and paramagnetic → diamagnetic.
88The correct molecular orbital diagram for the F2 molecule in the ground state is [four MO energy-level diagrams (a)–(d) are given; they differ in whether the 2σ (σ2p) level lies below or above the 1π (π2p) level, and in the filling of the 1π*, 2σ* levels] (JEE Advanced 2023, Paper-2 — Level-1)MO energy ordering and filling in F₂JEE Advanced 2023 Paper-2 worked solution: the correct MO diagram for F₂🟢 Exactcthe diagram with 2σ below 1π, 1π* filled and 2σ* empty
Concept There are only two things to check, and both are decided before you count a single electron. (i) The ordering. For the light second-row diatomics Li₂ → N₂ the 2s and 2pz orbitals are close enough in energy to mix (s–p mixing); that mixing pushes σ2p above π2p. From O₂ onwards the 2s–2p gap has widened so much (Zeff is large) that mixing is negligible and the "natural" order returns: σ2p below π2p. F₂ is well past the crossover, so its diagram must show 2σ (σ2p) below 1π (π2p). (ii) The filling. Just feed in the electrons by Aufbau. Any option that puts electrons in 2σ* or leaves 1π* half-empty is wrong.
Key relation Crossover rule: Li₂ … N₂ : σ2p > π2p  |  O₂, F₂, Ne₂ : σ2p < π2p the 2s–2p energy separation exceeds ≈ 12 eV after nitrogen, so s–p mixing dies out. F₂ configuration: KK σ2s² σ*2s² σ2pz² π2px² π2py² π*2px² π*2py², B.O. = ½(8 − 6) = 1.
Solution
1. F (Z = 9) ⇒ F₂ has 18 electrons; the valence set (after the two 1s pairs shown as 1σ, 1σ*) is 14 electrons distributed over 2s- and 2p-derived MOs.
2. Fill in the F₂/O₂ order: 1σ² 1σ*² (from 2s) then 2σ² (σ2p), then 1π⁴, then 1π*⁴, leaving 2σ* empty.
3. Diagrams (a) and (b) both draw the bonding 1π below 2σ — that is the N₂-type (s–p mixed) ladder. Correct for B₂, C₂, N₂; wrong for F₂. Diagram (b) additionally places 2σ* below 1π*, which no second-row diatomic shows.
4. Diagram (d) has the right skeleton but the wrong occupancy: it shows the top antibonding σ* level populated and the 1π* level only half filled (two parallel arrows). That is not a ground state — Aufbau forbids occupying 2σ* while 1π* still has vacancies.
5. Diagram (c) shows 1σ², 1σ*², 2σ², 1π⁴, 1π*⁴, 2σ⁰ with 2σ lying below 1π — exactly the F₂ ground state. All electrons paired ⇒ F₂ is diamagnetic, B.O. = 1, consistent with the single F–F σ bond of the Lewis picture. ⇒ (c).
 level      diagram (a)/(b)      diagram (c)          diagram (d)
 2sigma*        empty                empty            OCCUPIED   (wrong)
 1pi*           4 e-                 4 e-             2 e- only  (wrong)
 2sigma         above 1pi (wrong)    2 e-, below 1pi  2 e-
 1pi            below 2sigma (wrong) 4 e-             4 e-
 1sigma*        2 e-                 2 e-             2 e-
 1sigma         2 e-                 2 e-             2 e-
                                     B.O. = 1, diamagnetic          <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise one line: "Be–N mix, O–Ne don't." Everything up to N₂ has σ2p on top of π2p; O₂, F₂ and Ne₂ have σ2p underneath. Then, in any "pick the diagram" question, eliminate on ordering first (kills half the options in one glance) and on occupancy second. The distractors here are built exactly that way — two with N₂ ordering, one with a ground state that violates Aufbau.
Answer: (c) — σ2p (2σ) below π2p (1π), with 2σ² 1π⁴ 1π*⁴ and 2σ* vacant; B.O. = 1, diamagnetic.
89The number of inter-halogens from the following having a square pyramidal structure is ____ : ClF3, IF7, BrF5, BrF3, I2Cl6, IF5, ClF, ClF5 (JEE Main 2022, 28 July Shift-1 — Level-6)Counting square pyramidal interhalogensWhich species are square pyramidal — VSEPR counting problem worked out🟡 Similar3BrF5, IF5 and ClF5
Concept Interhalogens come in exactly four stoichiometries — XX′ (AB), XX′3 (AB₃), XX′5 (AB₅) and XX′7 (AB₇) — and each stoichiometry forces one shape, because the central halogen always brings seven valence electrons. Square pyramidal is the shape of AX5E: five bond pairs plus one lone pair on an octahedral (sp³d²) electron geometry, the lone pair taking one apex and pushing the four basal atoms slightly below the plane. So the whole question reduces to "how many XF5 type interhalogens are in the list?" — provided each species really is an interhalogen.
Key relation Steric number SN = σ-bond pairs + lone pairs, and for a halogen centre lp = (7 − n)/2 for XFn. AB₅ ⇒ SN = 6, sp³d², one lone pair ⇒ SQUARE PYRAMIDAL AB₃ gives SN = 5 with two lone pairs ⇒ T-shaped; AB₇ gives SN = 7 ⇒ pentagonal bipyramidal; AB gives linear.
Solution
1. ClF3, BrF3: 3 bp + 2 lp, SN = 5, sp³d ⇒ T-shaped, not square pyramidal.
2. IF7: 7 bp + 0 lp, SN = 7, sp³d³ ⇒ pentagonal bipyramidal.
3. ClF: a simple diatomic ⇒ linear.
4. I2Cl6: this is the dimer of ICl₃ — a planar chlorine-bridged molecule with two square-planar iodine centres (each I is AX₄E₂). Planar, not square pyramidal. the classic trap: candidates read "ICl₃" and answer T-shaped, or read "6 Cl" and answer octahedral; the real species is a flat bridged dimer.
5. BrF5, IF5, ClF5: each is 5 bp + 1 lp, SN = 6, sp³d² with the lone pair at one octahedral vertex ⇒ square pyramidal (bond angle slightly under 90°, ≈ 84.8° in BrF₅). That is 3 species.
 species     bp   lp   SN   hybrid    shape                     square pyramidal?
 ClF3         3    2    5   sp3d      T-shaped                        no
 IF7          7    0    7   sp3d3     pentagonal bipyramidal          no
 BrF5         5    1    6   sp3d2     SQUARE PYRAMIDAL                yes
 BrF3         3    2    5   sp3d      T-shaped                        no
 I2Cl6        -    -    -   dimer     planar bridged (2 sq. planar I) no
 IF5          5    1    6   sp3d2     SQUARE PYRAMIDAL                yes
 ClF          1    3    4   sp3       linear (diatomic)               no
 ClF5         5    1    6   sp3d2     SQUARE PYRAMIDAL                yes
                                                        count = 3     <-- answer (3)
⚡ JEE Trick For interhalogens the subscript alone gives the shape: 1 → linear, 3 → T-shaped, 5 → square pyramidal, 7 → pentagonal bipyramidal. Just count the "F₅" entries. The two traps planted here are I₂Cl₆ (a planar dimer, deliberately written so that it looks like an AB₆ octahedron) and IF₇ (many students call any 5-or-more-bond halogen "square pyramidal").
Answer: 3 — BrF₅, IF₅ and ClF₅.
90Thermal decomposition of AgNO3 produces two paramagnetic gases. The total number of electrons present in the antibonding molecular orbitals of the gas that has the higher number of unpaired electrons is ____ (JEE Advanced 2022, Paper-2 — Level-5)Decomposition products + antibonding electron countJEE Advanced 2022 worked solution: AgNO₃ decomposition and antibonding electrons🟢 Exact6six antibonding electrons in O2
Concept This is a two-stage question: an inorganic stage (what actually comes off when silver nitrate is heated) followed by an MOT stage (count antibonding electrons). Silver is a noble metal, so its nitrate decomposes all the way to the metal — not to the oxide as with Ca(NO₃)₂ or Pb(NO₃)₂. The two gases are NO₂ and O₂, and both happen to be paramagnetic: NO₂ is an odd-electron molecule (one unpaired electron on N) while O₂ has two unpaired electrons in its degenerate π* pair. So the target gas is O₂, and the question then wants every electron sitting in a starred MO of dioxygen.
Key relation 2AgNO₃ Δ→ 2Ag + 2NO₂ + O₂ metals below Cu in the reactivity series give the free metal; Mg→Cu give the oxide; Na/K give the nitrite. Full MO configuration of O₂ (16 e⁻): σ1s² σ*1s² σ2s² σ*2s² σ2pz² π2px² π2py² π*2px¹ π*2py¹.
Solution
1. Identify the gases. 2AgNO₃ → 2Ag + 2NO₂ + O₂. Brown NO₂ and colourless O₂ are both paramagnetic.
2. Pick the right one. NO₂ has 23 valence electrons ⇒ 1 unpaired electron. O₂ has 2 unpaired electrons (one in each degenerate π*2p, by Hund's rule). Higher number of unpaired electrons ⇒ O₂.
3. Count antibonding electrons in O₂, using the complete 16-electron configuration (the question says "total", so the core σ*1s counts too): σ*1s² + σ*2s² + π*2p² = 2 + 2 + 2 = 6. the bonding set is σ1s² σ2s² σ2p² π2p⁴ = 10, so B.O. = ½(10 − 6) = 2 ✓ — the arithmetic self-checks against the known double bond of O₂.
4. Why not 4? If you drop the KK core you get only σ*2s² + π*2p² = 4. But then the bonding count also drops to 8 and B.O. = ½(8 − 4) = 2 — the bond order is unaffected, which is exactly why the question says "total number of electrons in the antibonding molecular orbitals": the σ*1s pair is a genuine antibonding MO and must be included. Official answer 6.
 O2 (16 electrons)          bonding      antibonding
 sigma1s / sigma*1s            2              2
 sigma2s / sigma*2s            2              2
 sigma2pz                      2              -
 pi2px, pi2py                  4              -
 pi*2px, pi*2py                -              2   (1 + 1, Hund -> paramagnetic)
 ----------------------------------------------
 totals                       10              6        B.O. = (10-6)/2 = 2
                                              ^
                                              <-- answer (6)
⚡ JEE Trick Two hooks decide this problem: (i) AgNO₃ → Ag + NO₂ + O₂ (silver is a noble metal — no oxide is formed), and (ii) only O₂ has two unpaired electrons among common gases, which is also why liquid oxygen sticks to a magnet. The trap is answering 4 by silently ignoring the σ*1s pair — JEE Advanced asked for the total, not the valence-shell count, and the official key is 6.
Answer: 6 — the antibonding MOs of O₂ (σ*1s², σ*2s², π*2p²) hold six electrons in all.
91Assertion: The amphoteric behaviour of water is explained by Lewis acid–base theory. Reason: Water acts as an acid with NH3 and as a base with H2S. (a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A (b) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A (c) A is true but R is false (d) A is false but R is true (JEE Main 2022, 25 June Shift 2 — Level-4)Brønsted vs Lewis definitions; amphoterismAssertion–Reason worked example on the amphoteric behaviour of water🟡 SimilardA is false but R is true
Concept Amphoterism means one substance behaves as an acid towards a stronger base and as a base towards a stronger acid. Which theory explains it depends entirely on what is being transferred. In the two reactions quoted, water is donating a proton to NH₃ and accepting a proton from H₂S — proton transfer is the defining act of the Brønsted–Lowry theory, not the Lewis theory. Lewis acidity/basicity is about electron-pair donation and acceptance; on that definition water is only ever a Lewis base (it has two lone pairs to donate and no empty low-lying orbital to accept a pair into), so Lewis theory cannot account for its amphoteric character at all.
Statement H₂O + NH₃ ⇌ OH⁻ + NH₄⁺ water gives up H⁺ ⇒ Brønsted ACID. H₂O + H₂S ⇌ H₃O⁺ + HS⁻ water takes H⁺ ⇒ Brønsted BASE. Lewis: acid = electron-pair acceptor, base = electron-pair donor — H₂O is a lone-pair donor only.
Solution
1. Test the Reason first (it is the factual half). With the weak base NH₃, water is the stronger proton donor: H₂O + NH₃ → NH₄⁺ + OH⁻, so water acts as an acid. With the weak acid H₂S, water accepts the proton: H₂S + H₂O → HS⁻ + H₃O⁺, so water acts as a base. R is TRUE.
2. Test the Assertion. Both of those are proton-transfer equilibria ⇒ they are explained by Brønsted–Lowry theory. Under the Lewis definition water is a base (donor of an O lone pair, e.g. H₂O → BF₃, or hydration of Cu²⁺) but it is never a Lewis acid, so Lewis theory cannot produce the "acid" half of the amphoteric behaviour. A is FALSE.
3. A false + R true ⇒ (d).
4. Eliminating the rest: (a) and (b) both require A to be true, which it is not; (c) requires R to be false, but the two equations quoted in R are textbook-correct. notice R is a true statement that actually contradicts A — a very common JEE assertion–reason design.
 theory              acid definition         base definition       does H2O do BOTH?
 Arrhenius           gives H+ in water       gives OH- in water    yes (self-ionisation)
 Bronsted-Lowry      proton DONOR            proton ACCEPTOR       YES  <- the reactions in R
 Lewis               e- pair ACCEPTOR        e- pair DONOR         no - H2O is only a donor
                                                                        so A is false
                                            A false, R true        <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever an assertion names a theory and the reason quotes a reaction, match the reaction to the theory before anything else: if an H⁺ moves, it is Brønsted; if a lone pair moves, it is Lewis. The trap here is that the Reason is perfectly true and looks like a supporting example, so the eye slides straight to (a). Remember the one-line fact: water is a Lewis base but never a Lewis acid.
Answer: (d) A is false but R is true — the amphoterism of water is a Brønsted–Lowry (proton-transfer) phenomenon.
92Consider the ions/molecule O2+, O2, O2−, O22−. For increasing bond order the correct option is (a) O2− < O22− < O2 < O2+ (b) O2− < O22− < O2+ < O2 (c) O2− < O2+ < O22− < O2 (d) O22− < O2− < O2 < O2+ (JEE Main 2022, 26 June Shift 1 — Level-4)Bond order series of the dioxygen familyMO diagrams of O₂, O₂⁺, O₂²⁺, O₂⁻ and O₂²⁻ with bond orders🟡 SimilardO22− < O2− < O2 < O2+
Concept All four species share the same MO ladder; they differ only in how many electrons sit in the antibonding π*2p pair, because that pair is the frontier orbital set for the whole dioxygen family. Every electron you add goes into π* and costs half a bond; every electron you remove comes out of π* and buys half a bond. So the bond order is a simple linear function of the charge and you never need to redraw the diagram — just count π* occupancy: O₂⁺ has 1, O₂ has 2, O₂⁻ has 3 and O₂²⁻ has 4 electrons in π*.
Key relation B.O. = ½ (Nb − Na); for O₂n with Nb fixed at 10, B.O. = ½(10 − Na) Na = 4 core/σ* antibonding electrons + the π* electrons. Equivalently B.O. = 3 − ½(π* occupancy).
Solution
1. O2+ (15 e⁻): π*¹ ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 5) = 2.5; 1 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic. Shortest bond, 1.12 Å.
2. O2 (16 e⁻): π*² (one in each degenerate orbital) ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 6) = 2.0; 2 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic. 1.21 Å.
3. O2− (17 e⁻, superoxide): π*³ ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 7) = 1.5; 1 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic. 1.28 Å.
4. O22− (18 e⁻, peroxide): π*⁴ ⇒ B.O. = ½(10 − 8) = 1.0; 0 unpaired ⇒ diamagnetic. Longest bond, 1.49 Å.
5. Increasing order of bond order: O₂²⁻ (1) < O₂⁻ (1.5) < O₂ (2) < O₂⁺ (2.5) ⇒ (d). Options (a), (b) and (c) all place O₂⁻ below O₂²⁻ or scramble the middle two — impossible, since removing charge can only raise the bond order monotonically here.
 species    total e-   pi* occupancy   N_b   N_a   B.O.   unpaired   bond length
 O2 2-         18            4          10     8    1.0      0        1.49 A   (weakest)
 O2 -          17            3          10     7    1.5      1        1.28 A
 O2            16            2          10     6    2.0      2        1.21 A
 O2 +          15            1          10     5    2.5      1        1.12 A   (strongest)
 increasing B.O. :  O2 2-  <  O2 -  <  O2  <  O2 +                    <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick For the O₂ family the bond order is just 2.5, 2.0, 1.5, 1.0 as you walk +1 → 0 → −1 → −2 — half a bond per electron, because every extra electron lands in π*. Bond length runs the other way. The distractors are built by shuffling O₂⁻ and O₂²⁻: read the question's direction word ("increasing") carefully, because the same four values reversed also appear as a printed option in many versions of this problem.
Answer: (d) O₂²⁻ < O₂⁻ < O₂ < O₂⁺ (bond orders 1, 1.5, 2, 2.5).
93The correct order of nucleophilicity is (a) F− > OH− (b) H2O > OH− (c) ROH > RO− (d) NH2− > NH3 (JEE Main 2022, 26 June Shift 2 — Level-4)Nucleophilicity: charge and electronegativityJEE Main 2022 (26 June, Shift 2) solution: the correct order of nucleophilicity🟢 ExactdNH2− > NH3
Concept A nucleophile attacks by donating a lone pair, so anything that makes that lone pair more available makes the species more nucleophilic. Two rules settle every option here. (1) Charge rule: for the same donor atom, the conjugate base is always the better nucleophile than its neutral acid — the negative charge raises the energy of the HOMO and loosens the pair (OH⁻ ≫ H₂O, RO⁻ ≫ ROH, NH₂⁻ ≫ NH₃). (2) Electronegativity rule: across a period, the less electronegative atom holds its lone pair less tightly and is the better nucleophile, so in a protic solvent the order runs NH₂⁻ > OH⁻ > F⁻ — the reverse of basicity? No: it runs parallel to basicity here, and it is fluoride's tight grip plus its heavy solvation shell that makes F⁻ the poorest of the three.
Key relation Same donor atom: A⁻ > AH anion has the higher-energy, more diffuse lone pair. Same period, similar charge: nucleophilicity ∝ 1/electronegativity ⇒ CH₃⁻ > NH₂⁻ > OH⁻ > F⁻.
Solution
1. (a) F− > OH− — false. O is less electronegative than F, so hydroxide releases its pair far more readily; in protic solvents F⁻ is additionally locked up by strong hydrogen bonding. Correct order is OH⁻ > F⁻.
2. (b) H2O > OH− — false. Violates the charge rule outright: OH⁻ is roughly 10⁴–10⁵ times the nucleophile that neutral water is.
3. (c) ROH > RO− — false. Same violation; alkoxide is the reagent of choice in Williamson synthesis precisely because the alcohol itself is too weak a nucleophile.
4. (d) NH2− > NH3 — true. Amide ion carries a full negative charge on the same nitrogen donor ⇒ far more nucleophilic (and far more basic) than ammonia. ⇒ (d).
 option   pair compared        rule invoked                    verdict
 (a)      F- vs OH-           electronegativity: F > O        FALSE  (OH- > F-)
 (b)      H2O vs OH-          charge rule                     FALSE  (OH- > H2O)
 (c)      ROH vs RO-          charge rule                     FALSE  (RO- > ROH)
 (d)      NH2- vs NH3         charge rule, same N donor       TRUE            <-- answer (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Three of the four options are written "neutral > anion" or "more electronegative > less electronegative" — both are automatic falses. Scan for the single option that reads anion > its own neutral molecule and tick it without further work. The trap is (a): candidates who half-remember "in polar aprotic solvents F⁻ becomes a strong nucleophile" over-apply it — even there the comparison with OH⁻ still favours hydroxide.
Answer: (d) NH₂⁻ > NH₃ — the conjugate base of a given donor atom is always the stronger nucleophile.
94Identify the incorrect statement for PCl5 from the following. (a) In this molecule, orbitals of phosphorus are assumed to undergo sp3d hybridization. (b) The geometry of PCl5 is trigonal bipyramidal. (c) PCl5 has two axial bonds stronger than three equatorial bonds. (d) The three equatorial bonds of PCl5 lie in a plane. (JEE Main 2022, 27 June Shift 2 — Level-4)Axial vs equatorial bonds in a trigonal bipyramidWhy the axial bonds of PCl₅ are longer and weaker than the equatorial bonds🟡 Similarcthe axial bonds are in fact weaker, not stronger
Concept A trigonal bipyramid is the one common geometry in which the bonds are not all equivalent. Count the 90° repulsions each bond suffers: an axial P–Cl bond faces three equatorial neighbours at 90°, whereas an equatorial bond faces only two axial neighbours at 90° (its other two equatorial partners sit at a comfortable 120°). More close-range bond-pair/bond-pair repulsion means the axial bonds are pushed out — they are longer (2.19 Å) and therefore weaker than the equatorial bonds (2.04 Å). This inequality is exactly why PCl₅ ionises in the solid as [PCl₄]⁺[PCl₆]⁻ and why it loses the two axial chlorines most readily.
Key relation Repulsion at 90°: axial bond → 3 neighbours  |  equatorial bond → 2 neighbours ⇒ d(P–Cl)axial > d(P–Cl)equatorial ⇒ axial WEAKER bond strength falls as bond length rises for the same atom pair. Hybridisation: SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d.
Solution
1. (a) is correct. P has 5 bond pairs and no lone pair ⇒ steric number 5 ⇒ one s, three p and one d orbital ⇒ sp³d.
2. (b) is correct. Five bond pairs with zero lone pairs ⇒ the VSEPR arrangement of least repulsion is the trigonal bipyramid (angles 120° in the equator, 90° between axial and equatorial).
3. (d) is correct. By construction the three equatorial chlorines are coplanar with P, mutually 120° apart, and the two axial bonds stand perpendicular to that plane.
4. (c) is INCORRECT. It reverses the truth: the axial bonds carry more 90° repulsion, are longer (2.19 Å vs 2.04 Å) and hence weaker, not stronger. ⇒ the answer is (c). this is also why a lone pair in an AX₄E species such as SF₄ always chooses the equatorial site — see Q95.
 bond type    number   90-degree neighbours   length (A)   relative strength
 axial          2              3                2.19            weaker
 equatorial     3              2                2.04            stronger
 statement (c) claims  axial STRONGER than equatorial  -> contradicts the data
                                                                     <-- answer (c) is the incorrect one
⚡ JEE Trick One sentence covers every trigonal-bipyramidal question: "Axial = 3 repulsions at 90° = longer = weaker; equatorial = 2 repulsions = shorter = stronger; lone pairs therefore go equatorial." Note the wording trap — the question asks for the incorrect statement, so three true statements are the distractors and a single false one is the answer; candidates who read "identify the correct statement" tick (b) and lose the mark.
Answer: (c) — the two axial P–Cl bonds are longer and weaker than the three equatorial bonds, so the statement as printed is incorrect.
95In the structure of SF4, the lone pair of electrons on S is in (a) equatorial position and there are two lone pair–bond pair repulsions at 90° (b) equatorial position and there are three lone pair–bond pair repulsions at 90° (c) axial position and there are three lone pair–bond pair repulsions at 90° (d) axial position and there are two lone pair–bond pair repulsions at 90° (JEE Main 2022, 28 June Shift 2 — Level-4)Lone-pair siting in a trigonal bipyramidWhy SF₄ puts its lone pair in the equatorial site rather than the axial site🟡 Similaraequatorial, with two lp–bp repulsions at 90°
Concept SF₄ is AX4E: steric number 5, sp³d, trigonal-bipyramidal electron geometry. Because a lone pair is bulkier and closer to the nucleus than a bond pair, VSEPR ranks repulsions lp–lp > lp–bp > bp–bp, and only the 90° contacts matter (interactions at 120° or 180° are too weak to decide anything). The lone pair therefore takes whichever site gives it the fewest 90° neighbours. An axial site has three neighbours at 90°; an equatorial site has only two. So the lone pair goes equatorial, giving the familiar see-saw shape, with the axial F–S–F angle squeezed from 180° to ≈ 173° and the equatorial angle from 120° to ≈ 102°.
Key relation 90° contacts in a TBP: axial site → 3 equatorial neighbours  |  equatorial site → 2 axial neighbours. Repulsion hierarchy: lp–lp > lp–bp > bp–bp a lone pair is held by one nucleus only, so its charge cloud spreads wider. Minimise the number of 90° lp–bp contacts ⇒ lone pair equatorial.
Solution
1. Count electrons on S: 6 valence + 4 σ bonds ⇒ 4 bp + 1 lp, SN = 5, sp³d, TBP electron geometry.
2. If the lp were axial: it would meet the three equatorial S–F bonds at 90° ⇒ 3 lp–bp repulsions at 90° — the higher-energy arrangement.
3. Lp equatorial (actual): it meets only the two axial S–F bonds at 90° ⇒ 2 lp–bp repulsions at 90°; the other two equatorial bonds are a harmless 120° away. Fewer 90° contacts ⇒ this is the ground-state structure ⇒ (a).
4. Eliminate: (b) pairs the right position with the wrong count (3 belongs to the axial case); (c) and (d) both put the lone pair axial, which is the discarded higher-energy option — and (d) additionally miscounts. the same argument explains ClF₃ (both lone pairs equatorial ⇒ T-shape) and XeF₂ (all three equatorial ⇒ linear).
 lone-pair site   lp-bp contacts at 90 deg   lp-bp at 120 deg   resulting shape   energy
 axial                     3                        -           trigonal pyr.?    HIGHER (rejected)
 equatorial                2                        2           SEE-SAW           LOWER  (adopted)
 SF4 : sp3d, 4 bp + 1 lp, lp EQUATORIAL, TWO 90-degree lp-bp repulsions   <-- answer (a)
 observed angles : F(ax)-S-F(ax) = 173 deg,  F(eq)-S-F(eq) = 102 deg
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the pair of numbers "axial 3, equatorial 2" — it decides lone-pair placement in every sp³d species (SF₄, ClF₃, XeF₂, BrF₃, I₃⁻) in one second. The distractors here are built by crossing the right position with the wrong repulsion count, so you must state both facts before choosing; ticking "equatorial" alone still leaves you a 50-50 guess between (a) and (b).
Answer: (a) equatorial position, with two lone pair–bond pair repulsions at 90°.
96Among the following species N2, N2+, N2−, N22−, O2, O2+, O2−, O22−, the number of species showing diamagnetism is ____ (JEE Main 2022, 25 July Shift-1 — Level-4)Counting diamagnetic diatomic speciesJEE Advanced style counting problem: how many species are diamagnetic (MOT)🟡 Similar2only N2 and O22−
Concept Diamagnetism requires zero unpaired electrons, and in a homonuclear diatomic the only place an unpaired electron can hide is a degenerate level — the π2p pair or the π*2p pair — because Hund's rule forces single occupancy before pairing. Two shortcuts follow. (i) Any species with an odd total electron count is automatically paramagnetic, which instantly disposes of N₂⁺, N₂⁻, O₂⁺ and O₂⁻. (ii) For the even-electron species you only need to ask whether the last two electrons land in a degenerate pair (⇒ one each, paramagnetic) or complete a level (⇒ paired, diamagnetic).
Key relation odd total e⁻ ⇒ paramagnetic (always). For even counts, check the frontier level: 14 e⁻ (N₂ type) ⇒ σ2p² closed ⇒ diamagnetic, 16 e⁻ ⇒ π*² split over two degenerate orbitals ⇒ 2 unpaired, 18 e⁻ ⇒ π*⁴ full ⇒ diamagnetic.
Solution
1. Odd-electron species — paramagnetic by inspection: N₂⁺ (13), N₂⁻ (15), O₂⁺ (15), O₂⁻ (17). Four species eliminated at once.
2. N2 (14 e⁻): σ2s² σ*2s² π2p⁴ σ2p² — every orbital doubly filled, HOMO is the non-degenerate σ2p ⇒ 0 unpaired ⇒ diamagnetic ✓.
3. N22− (16 e⁻): the two extra electrons enter the degenerate π*2p pair, one in each by Hund's rule ⇒ 2 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic ✗. it is isoelectronic with O₂ — same 16-electron count, same two unpaired spins; a very common slip is to call it diamagnetic just because the charge is even.
4. O2 (16 e⁻): π*2px¹ π*2py¹ ⇒ 2 unpaired ⇒ paramagnetic ✗ (the textbook triumph of MOT).
5. O22− (18 e⁻, peroxide): π*2p⁴ — the degenerate pair is now completely filled ⇒ 0 unpaired ⇒ diamagnetic ✓.
6. Diamagnetic species = N₂ and O₂²⁻ ⇒ count = 2.
 species     total e-   frontier occupancy      unpaired e-    magnetism
 N2            14       sigma2p^2                    0         DIAMAGNETIC   *
 N2 +          13       sigma2p^1                    1         paramagnetic
 N2 -          15       pi*^1                        1         paramagnetic
