 N2 2-         16       pi*^1 pi*^1  (Hund)          2         paramagnetic
 O2            16       pi*^1 pi*^1  (Hund)          2         paramagnetic
 O2 +          15       pi*^1                        1         paramagnetic
 O2 -          17       pi*^2 pi*^1                  1         paramagnetic
 O2 2-         18       pi*^2 pi*^2  (full)          0         DIAMAGNETIC   *
                                          diamagnetic count = 2   <-- answer (2)
⚡ JEE Trick Strike out every odd-electron species first — that is half the list gone in three seconds. Then remember the three magic even counts: 14 → diamagnetic, 16 → paramagnetic (2 unpaired), 18 → diamagnetic. The planted trap is N₂²⁻: it wears a nitrogen label but has 16 electrons, so it behaves exactly like O₂ and is paramagnetic.
Answer: 2 — N₂ and O₂²⁻ are the only diamagnetic species.
97Match List-I with List-II. List-I: (A) XeO3 (B) XeF2 (C) XeOF4 (D) XeF6. List-II: (I) sp3d ; linear (II) sp3 ; pyramidal (III) sp3d3 ; distorted octahedral (IV) sp3d2 ; square pyramidal. Choose the correct answer: (a) A–II, B–IV, C–III, D–I (b) A–IV, B–II, C–III, D–I (c) A–II, B–I, C–IV, D–III (d) A–IV, B–II, C–I, D–III (JEE Main 2022, 25 July Shift-2 — Level-4)Hybridisation and shape of xenon compoundsStructures of XeF₂, XeF₄, XeF₆, XeO₃, XeOF₄ and XeO₂F₂ — hybridisation and shape🟡 SimilarcA–II, B–I, C–IV, D–III
Concept Every xenon compound falls out of one count. Xenon brings 8 valence electrons; each F uses one of them, each doubly-bonded O uses two (and therefore does not add to the steric number). Whatever is left over is lone pairs. The steric number then names the hybridisation, and subtracting the lone pairs from the electron geometry names the shape. Because a Xe=O double bond consumes two electrons but occupies only one σ position, oxygen-containing xenon compounds systematically have fewer lone pairs than their fluoride analogues — which is exactly what separates XeOF₄ (square pyramidal) from XeF₆ (distorted octahedral).
Key relation lp on Xe = (8 − nF − 2nO)/2, SN = σ-bonds + lp, SN = 4 → sp³, 5 → sp³d, 6 → sp³d², 7 → sp³d³ shape = electron geometry with the lone-pair vertices deleted.
Solution
1. (A) XeO3: lp = (8 − 6)/2 = 1; SN = 3 σ + 1 lp = 4 ⇒ sp³, tetrahedral electron geometry minus one lone pair ⇒ pyramidal ⇒ II.
2. (B) XeF2: lp = (8 − 2)/2 = 3; SN = 2 + 3 = 5 ⇒ sp³d, TBP with all three lone pairs equatorial ⇒ linear ⇒ I.
3. (C) XeOF4: lp = (8 − 4 − 2)/2 = 1; SN = 5 σ + 1 lp = 6 ⇒ sp³d², octahedral minus one lone pair ⇒ square pyramidal ⇒ IV.
4. (D) XeF6: lp = (8 − 6)/2 = 1; SN = 6 + 1 = 7 ⇒ sp³d³, pentagonal-bipyramidal electron geometry with a stereochemically active lone pair ⇒ distorted octahedral ⇒ III.
5. A–II, B–I, C–IV, D–III ⇒ (c). Options (a), (b) and (d) all mis-assign XeF₂ or XeF₆; note that (b) and (d) even hand XeO₃ the square-pyramidal entry, which needs six σ bonds it does not have.
 species    F   O   lone pairs   sigma   SN   hybrid    shape                    List-II
 XeO3       0   3       1          3      4   sp3       pyramidal                  II
 XeF2       2   0       3          2      5   sp3d      linear                     I
 XeOF4      4   1       1          5      6   sp3d2     square pyramidal           IV
 XeF6       6   0       1          6      7   sp3d3     distorted octahedral       III
                                        A-II, B-I, C-IV, D-III       <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Use the one-line formula lp = (8 − F − 2O)/2 for any xenon species, then read hybridisation straight off SN. The single fact worth memorising outright is that XeF₆ is sp³d³ / distorted octahedral, not regular octahedral — its lone pair is stereochemically active, which is why XeF₆ is fluxional and hydrolyses so readily. Matching questions are graded all-or-nothing, so verify at least two rows independently before committing.
Answer: (c) A–II, B–I, C–IV, D–III.
98Arrange the following in increasing order of their covalent character: (A) CaF2 (B) CaCl2 (C) CaBr2 (D) CaI2. Choose the correct answer: (a) B < A < C < D (b) A < B < C < D (c) A < B < D < C (d) A < C < B < D (JEE Main 2022, 26 July Shift-2 — Level-4)Fajans' rules — anion polarisabilityFajans' rules: polarisation, polarisability and covalent character explained🟡 SimilarbCaF2 < CaCl2 < CaBr2 < CaI2
Concept No ionic bond is 100% ionic: the cation's electric field drags the anion's electron cloud towards itself, and the shared density that results is covalent character. Fajans' rules say this distortion grows with the polarising power of the cation (small, highly charged, or with a pseudo-noble-gas 18-electron shell) and with the polarisability of the anion (large and highly charged, since its outer electrons are far from the nucleus and loosely held). Here the cation is Ca²⁺ throughout, so the cation term is frozen and the whole comparison rests on the halide: the bigger the halide, the softer its electron cloud, the more covalent the salt.
Key relation covalent character ∝ (polarising power of cation) × (polarisability of anion). Ionic radii: F⁻ (133 pm) < Cl⁻ (181) < Br⁻ (196) < I⁻ (220 pm) ⇒ polarisability F⁻ < Cl⁻ < Br⁻ < I⁻ a larger, softer anion has its outermost electrons poorly shielded from distortion.
Solution
1. All four salts share the same cation Ca²⁺ ⇒ polarising power is a constant and cancels out of the comparison.
2. Anion size — and therefore polarisability — increases down group 17: F⁻ < Cl⁻ < Br⁻ < I⁻.
3. Hence covalent character increases in the same sequence: CaF₂ < CaCl₂ < CaBr₂ < CaI₂, i.e. A < B < C < D ⇒ (b).
4. The physical evidence agrees: melting points fall (CaF₂ 1418 °C → CaI₂ 779 °C), solubility in organic solvents rises, and colour appears in the iodide — all standard symptoms of growing covalent character. option (a) inverts CaF₂ and CaCl₂; (c) swaps Br and I; (d) swaps Cl and Br — each is a single-transposition trap.
 salt     anion   ionic radius (pm)   polarisability   covalent character   m.p. (deg C)
 CaF2      F-           133               lowest             LEAST             1418
 CaCl2     Cl-          181                 |                  |                772
 CaBr2     Br-          196                 v                  v                730
 CaI2      I-           220               highest            MOST               779*
 increasing covalent character :  A  <  B  <  C  <  D                <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick When the cation is common to every option, ignore charge and size arguments about the metal entirely — just line the anions up by size. Down a group, bigger anion ⇒ more polarisable ⇒ more covalent ⇒ lower melting point and greater solubility in benzene. All three wrong options here are the correct answer with exactly one adjacent pair swapped, so write the sequence yourself before looking at the options.
Answer: (b) A < B < C < D, i.e. CaF₂ < CaCl₂ < CaBr₂ < CaI₂.
99According to MO theory, the number of species/ions from the following having identical bond order is ____ : CN−, NO+, O2, O2+, O22+ (JEE Main 2022, 27 July Shift-1 — Level-4)Isoelectronic species and bond orderWhich species share the same bond order — MOT comparison worked out🟡 Similar3CN−, NO+ and O22+ (all B.O. = 3)
Concept In molecular orbital theory the bond order of a second-row diatomic depends only on the total electron count, not on which atoms carry them — species with the same number of electrons are isoelectronic and fill the identical ladder to the identical height. So the fastest route is: count electrons for each species, group the equal counts, and only then convert one representative of each group into a bond order. Anything at 14 electrons is an N₂-analogue with a triple bond.
Key relation B.O. = ½ (Nb − Na); for the 14-electron set σ2s² σ*2s² π2p⁴ σ2p² ⇒ B.O. = ½(8 − 2) = 3 valence-electron bookkeeping; adding the KK core changes both counts by 2 and leaves B.O. unchanged.
Solution
1. CN−: 6 + 7 + 1 = 14 e⁻ ⇒ isoelectronic with N₂ ⇒ B.O. = 3, diamagnetic.
2. NO+: 7 + 8 − 1 = 14 e⁻ ⇒ B.O. = 3, diamagnetic.
3. O22+: 16 − 2 = 14 e⁻ ⇒ both π* electrons of O₂ have been stripped away ⇒ B.O. = 3, diamagnetic. this is the species students most often forget; O₂²⁺ is a genuine 14-electron N₂-analogue.
4. O2: 16 e⁻ ⇒ π*² ⇒ B.O. = 2. O2+: 15 e⁻ ⇒ π*¹ ⇒ B.O. = 2.5. Neither matches 3, and they do not match each other.
5. The species sharing one common bond order therefore number 3 — CN⁻, NO⁺ and O₂²⁺.
 species     total e-    configuration of frontier levels     B.O.    group
 CN -           14       pi2p^4  sigma2p^2,  pi* empty          3      *
 NO +           14       pi2p^4  sigma2p^2,  pi* empty          3      *
 O2 2+          14       sigma2p^2 pi2p^4,   pi* empty          3      *
 O2 +           15       pi*^1                                 2.5
 O2             16       pi*^1 pi*^1                           2.0
                      three species share B.O. = 3      <-- answer (3)
⚡ JEE Trick Count electrons, not atoms. 14 electrons ⇒ bond order 3 and diamagnetic — the club contains N₂, CO, CN⁻, NO⁺, C₂²⁻ and O₂²⁺. The question asks for the number of species, not the bond order itself, so the answer is 3 rather than the tempting "3.0"; and the trap is dismissing O₂²⁺ as exotic when it is simply O₂ with the two π* electrons removed.
Answer: 3 — CN⁻, NO⁺ and O₂²⁺ all have bond order 3.
100The number of molecule(s) or ion(s) from the following having a non-planar structure is ____ : NO3−, H2O2, BF3, PCl3, XeF4, SF4, XeO3, PH4+, SO3, Al(OH)4− (JEE Main 2022, 27 July Shift-2 — Level-4)Planar vs non-planar structuresPlanar and non-planar molecules in chemical bonding — IIT-JEE lecture🟡 Similar6H2O2, PCl3, SF4, XeO3, PH4+, Al(OH)4−
Concept "Planar" means every atom of the species can be placed in one plane. For a single-centre species that happens only for three geometries — trigonal planar (AX₃), square planar (AX₄E₂) and bent/linear — so the practical test is: work out the shape from VSEPR and ask whether it is one of those. Anything pyramidal, tetrahedral, see-saw or T-shaped-plus-out-of-plane is non-planar. One species in the list, H₂O₂, has two heavy centres and must be judged differently: its two O–H bonds lie in two different planes (open-book, dihedral ≈ 111° in the gas phase), so it is the classic non-planar molecule.
Key relation AX₃ (sp², 0 lp) → trigonal planar, AX₄ (sp³) → tetrahedral = NON-planar, AX₃E (sp³) → pyramidal = NON-planar, AX₄E₂ (sp³d²) → square planar, AX₄E (sp³d) → see-saw = NON-planar a lone pair on an sp³ centre lifts the central atom out of the plane of its substituents.
Solution
1. Planar four. NO₃⁻ and SO₃ and BF₃: all AX₃ with no lone pair on the central atom, sp², 120° ⇒ trigonal planar. XeF₄: 4 bp + 2 lp, sp³d², the two lone pairs take the axial positions ⇒ square planar.
2. H2O2: H–O–O–H with a dihedral twist of ≈ 111° (gas) / 90.2° (solid) ⇒ non-planar (open-book) ✓.
3. PCl3: 3 bp + 1 lp, sp³ ⇒ pyramidal ✓. XeO3: 3 σ + 1 lp, sp³ ⇒ pyramidal ✓.
4. SF4: 4 bp + 1 lp, sp³d ⇒ see-saw ✓ (two axial F's stick out of the equatorial plane).
5. PH4+ and Al(OH)4−: 4 bp + 0 lp, sp³ ⇒ tetrahedral ✓✓.
6. Non-planar tally: H₂O₂, PCl₃, SF₄, XeO₃, PH₄⁺, Al(OH)₄⁻ ⇒ 6.
 species        bp   lp   hybrid    shape                planar?
 NO3 -           3    0   sp2       trigonal planar      PLANAR
 H2O2            -    -   sp3 (O)   open book, twisted   non-planar   *
 BF3             3    0   sp2       trigonal planar      PLANAR
 PCl3            3    1   sp3       pyramidal            non-planar   *
 XeF4            4    2   sp3d2     square planar        PLANAR
 SF4             4    1   sp3d      see-saw              non-planar   *
 XeO3            3    1   sp3       pyramidal            non-planar   *
 PH4 +           4    0   sp3       tetrahedral          non-planar   *
 SO3             3    0   sp2       trigonal planar      PLANAR
 Al(OH)4 -       4    0   sp3       tetrahedral          non-planar   *
                                          non-planar count = 6   <-- answer (6)
⚡ JEE Trick Sort by steric number in one pass: SN 3 with no lone pair ⇒ planar; SN 4 ⇒ non-planar always (pyramidal or tetrahedral); SN 6 with two lone pairs ⇒ square planar. Two traps are set here — XeF₄ is planar despite having two lone pairs (they cancel along the axis), and H₂O₂ is non-planar even though its Lewis structure looks flat on paper. Miss either and you report 5 or 7.
Answer: 6 — H₂O₂, PCl₃, SF₄, XeO₃, PH₄⁺ and Al(OH)₄⁻.
101Given below are two statements: Assertion A: Zero orbital overlap is an out-of-phase overlap. Reason R: It results due to different orientation/direction of approach of orbitals. In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer: (a) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A (b) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A (c) A is true but R is false (d) A is false but R is true (JEE Main 2022, 28 July Shift-2 — Level-4)Positive, negative and zero orbital overlapOverlapping of atomic orbitals — positive, negative and zero overlap🟡 Similaraprinted key shows "a" ⚠
Concept When two atomic orbitals are brought together, the quantity that matters is the overlap integral S = ∫ψAψB dτ, and it can take three qualitatively different values. If the lobes that meet carry the same sign, S > 0 — positive (in-phase) overlap, electron density builds up between the nuclei and a bonding MO results. If the lobes that meet carry opposite signs, S < 0 — negative (out-of-phase) overlap, giving a node between the nuclei and an antibonding MO. If the geometry is such that the constructive part and the destructive part are exactly equal and cancel, S = 0 — zero overlap, and no bond of any kind (bonding or antibonding) can form. Zero overlap is thus a symmetry result driven by the relative orientation of the two orbitals, not a phase result.
Key relation S > 0 → positive, in-phase, bonding  |  S < 0 → negative, out-of-phase, antibonding  |  S = 0 → zero overlap, non-bonding the classic example: an s orbital approaching a p orbital along a direction perpendicular to the p axis — it overlaps the + lobe and the − lobe equally, and the two contributions annihilate.
Solution
1. Examine R. Zero overlap arises when the two orbitals approach in a mutually perpendicular orientation — s with pz sideways, px with pz, px with dz², and so on. Change the direction of approach and the same pair of orbitals gives good overlap. So the cause really is the orientation / direction of approach. R is TRUE.
2. Examine A. "Out-of-phase overlap" is the standard name for the case S < 0, which produces an antibonding MO (σ*, π*) — a real, non-zero interaction. Zero overlap is a separate, third case in which the in-phase and out-of-phase contributions cancel. Equating the two collapses a three-way classification into two.
3. Therefore A is false, R is true ⇒ derivation gives (d), while the printed key gives (a).
4. Eliminating on the derived reading: (b) and (c) both fail immediately because R is true and is precisely the reason zero overlap happens; the only live question is whether A stands, and on the NCERT three-category treatment it does not.
⚠ Note Answer-key discrepancy. The printed key on page 8 reads "101. a" (both true, R the correct explanation). That reading requires "out-of-phase" to be used loosely for "not in phase", so that a zero-overlap encounter counts as out-of-phase and R then explains it. On the strict NCERT/MOT convention used everywhere else in this chapter, positive, negative and zero overlap are three distinct categories: negative overlap is the out-of-phase one, and zero overlap is the orientation-driven cancellation. On that reading Assertion A is false and Reason R is true, i.e. (d). The key letter is displayed as printed; treat this item as ambiguous and answer according to the convention your teacher follows.
 overlap type   sign of S   phase relationship        MO produced     caused by
 positive        S > 0      same sign lobes meet      bonding         head-on / lateral, correct orientation
 negative        S < 0      opposite sign lobes       antibonding     out-of-phase combination
 zero            S = 0      + and - parts CANCEL      none            perpendicular orientation of approach
 Assertion A equates row 3 with row 2  -> false on the strict reading
 Reason R describes the cause of row 3 -> true
                              derived (d)   |   printed key (a)      <-- flagged
⚡ JEE Trick Keep the three overlap types on separate shelves: in-phase → bonding, out-of-phase → antibonding, zero → non-bonding. The quickest physical test for zero overlap is symmetry — if rotating one orbital by 180° about the internuclear axis maps a + lobe onto a − lobe of the partner, the integral cancels. The trap in every assertion–reason item of this shape is a Reason that is factually true, which pulls the eye straight to option (a) without the assertion ever being tested.
Answer: (a) as printed in the key ⚠ — independent derivation gives (d) (A false, R true); see the Note above.
102For diatomic molecules, the correct statement(s) about the molecular orbitals formed by the overlap of two 2pz orbitals is(are) (a) σ orbital has a total of two nodal planes (b) σ* orbital has one node in the xz-plane containing the molecular axis (c) π orbital has one node in the plane which is perpendicular to the molecular axis and goes through the centre of the molecule (d) π* orbital has one node in the xy-plane containing the molecular axis (JEE Advanced 2022, Paper-1 — Level-4)Nodal structure of σ, σ*, π and π* MOsJEE Advanced 2022 solution: nodal planes of the MOs formed from two 2pz orbitals🟢 Exacta, dmultiple-correct: (a) and (d)
Concept A node of a molecular orbital is a surface on which the wavefunction changes sign. Two families of nodes must be kept apart. Nodes that contain the internuclear axis are inherited from the atomic p orbitals and define the symmetry label: σ has none, π has exactly one, δ has two. Nodes perpendicular to the axis come from the bonding/antibonding combination and from the p orbitals' own nodal planes; an antibonding MO always gains one extra perpendicular node between the nuclei. When two 2pz orbitals meet head-on (axis = z) they give σ and σ*; when they meet side-on (axis along x or y) they give π and π*. The four options deliberately mix the two cases, so treat each statement on its own terms.
Key relation Nodes containing the axis: σ, σ* → 0  |  π, π* → 1. Nodes perpendicular to the axis: σ(pz+pz) → 2 (one outside each nucleus), σ* → 3, π → 0, π* → 1 (through the centre) each atomic pz already carries its own nodal plane, and the antibonding combination adds the central one.
Solution
1. (a) σ has two nodal planes — TRUE. Take the axis along z and write ψσ = pz(A) − pz(B). Along the axis the sign runs (−) (+ big lobe between the nuclei) (−), so the function changes sign twice, at two surfaces perpendicular to the axis lying outside the two nuclei. Two nodes, and none of them contains the axis (σ symmetry). ✓
2. (b) σ* has a node in the xz-plane containing the axis — FALSE. σ* is cylindrically symmetric about the internuclear axis; every plane containing the axis is a symmetry plane, not a node. Its three nodes are all perpendicular to the axis (one between the nuclei, one beyond each nucleus). ✗
3. (c) π has a node perpendicular to the axis through the centre — FALSE. The π bonding MO piles charge up on both sides of the axis with no sign change between the nuclei; its single node is the plane containing the axis. A perpendicular central node is the signature of π*, not π. ✗
4. (d) π* has a node in the xy-plane containing the axis — TRUE. For a lateral pz–pz overlap the molecular axis lies in the xy-plane (say along x). Each pz has the xy-plane as its own nodal plane, so the π* combination certainly has a node there — and that plane does contain the molecular axis. (π* has one further node, the central perpendicular one, but the statement only claims the xy one, which is correct.) ✓
5. Correct statements: (a) and (d) — matching the printed key "102. ad".
 MO      formed by            nodes containing axis   nodes perpendicular to axis   total
 sigma   pz + pz head-on              0                        2                     2   <-- (a) TRUE
 sigma*  pz - pz head-on              0                        3                     3   -- (b) FALSE
 pi      pz + pz side-on              1  (the xy-plane)        0                     1   -- (c) FALSE
 pi*     pz - pz side-on              1  (the xy-plane)        1  (central)          2   <-- (d) TRUE
 correct statements : (a) and (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Two rules crack every nodal-plane question: (i) the Greek letter counts the nodes that contain the axis — σ 0, π 1, δ 2; (ii) a star adds exactly one node perpendicular to the axis. The traps here are (b) and (c), each of which takes a real node and moves it to the wrong orientation: σ* nodes are never parallel to the axis, and a central perpendicular node belongs to π*, never to π. Since this is a multiple-correct item, verify all four statements — stopping at the first true one costs the whole mark.
Answer: (a) and (d).
103The correct order of bond orders of C22−, N22− and O22− is (a) C22− > N22− > O22− (b) O22− > N22− > O22− (printed as such in the book — the last term is a typographical slip for C22−) (c) N22− > O22− > C22− (d) C22− > O22− > N22− (JEE Main 2022, 24 Jun Shift 2 — Level-3)Bond order from MOTWorking out the bond orders of C2, C22− and N2 from their MO diagrams🟡 SimilaraC22− > N22− > O22−
Concept Never build three separate MO diagrams for a question like this. Every second-period homonuclear diatomic species is completely fixed by one number — its total count of valence electrons. Two species with the same valence-electron count are isoelectronic: they fill the identical MO ladder in the identical order and therefore have the identical bond order. So convert each dianion into the neutral molecule it imitates, read the bond order off the five values you already know (B2 1, C2 2, N2 3, O2 2, F2 1) and you are finished in ten seconds. Adding two electrons to C2 pushes it up the ladder into the N2 configuration, whereas adding two to N2 or O2 starts loading the antibonding π* level and pulls the bond order down.
Key relation B.O. = ½ (Nb − Na) Nb and Na = electrons in bonding and in antibonding MOs. Isoelectronic shortcut: C22− (14 e⁻) ≡ N2, N22− (16 e⁻) ≡ O2, O22− (18 e⁻) ≡ F2 counting total electrons is enough here because all three centres are second-period atoms with the same 1s core.
Solution
1. C22−: 6 + 6 + 2 = 14 e⁻, i.e. 10 valence electrons — exactly the N2 count. Configuration σ1s² σ*1s² σ2s² σ*2s² π2px² π2py² σ2pz². B.O. = ½(10 − 4) = 3. The two extra electrons went into the bonding σ2pz, so the acetylide ion is stronger than neutral C2.
2. N22−: 7 + 7 + 2 = 16 e⁻ — the O2 count. The two extra electrons must go into π*2p, one in each of π*2px and π*2py (Hund). B.O. = ½(10 − 6) = 2.Same reasoning tells you N22− is paramagnetic with two unpaired electrons, exactly like O2 — a favourite follow-up.
3. O22− (peroxide): 8 + 8 + 2 = 18 e⁻ — the F2 count. Both π* orbitals are now completely filled. B.O. = ½(10 − 8) = 1, and every MO is doubly occupied ⇒ diamagnetic.
4. Ranking 3 > 2 > 1 gives C22− > N22− > O22− ⇒ option (a). Option (c) inverts the whole trend, option (d) swaps N and O, and option (b) is printed defectively in the book (it repeats O22− where C22− should stand) — even in its intended form O22− > N22− > C22− it is the exact reverse of the right answer.
 species     total e-   isoelectronic with   Nb   Na   B.O. = (Nb-Na)/2
 C2 2-          14            N2             10    4        3.0    <-- largest
 N2 2-          16            O2             10    6        2.0
 O2 2-          18            F2             10    8        1.0    <-- smallest
 order : C2 2-  >  N2 2-  >  O2 2-                          <-- answer (a)
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise the electron-count ladder once — 10 e⁻ → B.O. 1, 12 → 2, 14 → 3, 16 → 2, 18 → 1 (a symmetric tent peaking at 14). Then any second-period diatomic ion is solved by counting electrons and reading off the tent; no MO diagram is ever needed. The trap here is assuming that "adding electrons always weakens a bond": going from C2 (12 e⁻) to C22− (14 e⁻) climbs the left-hand slope of the tent and strengthens it from 2 to 3.
Answer: (a) C22− > N22− > O22−.
104Bonding in which of the following diatomic molecule(s) become(s) stronger, on the basis of MO Theory, by removal of an electron? (A) NO  (B) N2  (C) O2  (D) C2  (E) B2. Choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below: (a) A, B, C only (b) B, C, E only (c) A, C only (d) D only (JEE Main 2022, 25 Jun Shift 1 — Level-3)Ionisation and bond orderComparing bond order, stability and bond enthalpy across B2, C2, N2, O2 and F2🟡 Similar3printed key shows "3" ⚠ — derived answer is (c) A, C only
Concept Ionisation always removes the electron from the HOMO — the highest occupied molecular orbital. Whether the bond gets stronger or weaker therefore depends on one thing only: is the HOMO bonding or antibonding? Take an electron out of an antibonding MO and you delete a destabilising contribution, so Na falls by one and the bond order rises by ½. Take one out of a bonding MO and Nb falls by one, so the bond order drops by ½. The whole question reduces to identifying, for each of the five molecules, whether its HOMO carries a star. Only species that already have electrons sitting in π*2p — the ones to the right of N2 in the second period, plus the odd-electron molecule NO — can be improved by ionisation.
Key relation B.O. = ½ (Nb − Na); remove an antibonding e⁻ ⇒ ΔB.O. = +½ (stronger), remove a bonding e⁻ ⇒ ΔB.O. = −½ (weaker) MO filling order for ≤14 valence electrons is σ2s < σ*2s < π2px = π2py < σ2pz < π*2p; for O2, F2 and NO the σ2pz drops below the π2p pair, which does not affect this argument.
Solution
1. (A) NO — 15 e⁻. Configuration ends …σ2pz² π*2px¹. The HOMO is antibonding. B.O. 2.5 → NO⁺ 3.0 ⇒ stronger. ✓This is why NO⁺ (nitrosonium) is a genuinely stable, isolable cation while most molecules resist ionisation — you are removing the single electron that was spoiling the triple bond.
2. (B) N2 — 14 e⁻. HOMO is σ2pz, a bonding orbital. B.O. 3.0 → N2+ 2.5 ⇒ weaker. ✗
3. (C) O2 — 16 e⁻. HOMO is the half-filled π*2p pair, antibonding. B.O. 2.0 → O2+ 2.5 ⇒ stronger. ✓Confirmed experimentally: the O–O bond length shortens from 121 pm in O2 to 112 pm in O2+ (as in dioxygenyl salts such as O2+[PtF6]−).
4. (D) C2 — 12 e⁻. HOMO is π2p, bonding. B.O. 2.0 → C2+ 1.5 ⇒ weaker. ✗
5. (E) B2 — 10 e⁻. HOMO is the half-filled π2p pair, bonding. B.O. 1.0 → B2+ 0.5 ⇒ weaker. ✗
6. Only A and C qualify ⇒ option (c).
 molecule  valence e-   HOMO        bonding?   B.O. before -> after   effect
 NO            11       pi*2p       ANTI        2.5  ->  3.0          STRONGER  <-- (A)
 N2            10       sigma2pz    bonding     3.0  ->  2.5          weaker
 O2            12       pi*2p       ANTI        2.0  ->  2.5          STRONGER  <-- (C)
 C2             8       pi2p        bonding     2.0  ->  1.5          weaker
 B2             6       pi2p        bonding     1.0  ->  0.5          weaker
 stronger on ionisation : A and C only                                <-- answer (c)
⚠ Note The printed answer key on page 8 reads "104. 3". Q104 is a four-option MCQ with choices (a)–(d), so a bare digit cannot be a valid answer — this is a letter-for-digit slip, the mirror image of the Q72 error elsewhere in this Strong Box (where a numerical answer was printed as a letter). The digit 3 is the ordinal position of option (c), which is almost certainly what was intended and which matches the derivation above and the official NTA key for JEE Main 2022 (25 June, Shift 1). The key value is displayed as printed; treat the correct answer as (c) A, C only.
⚡ JEE Trick One sentence covers every "does ionisation strengthen the bond?" question: only species that already hold electrons in π*2p — i.e. O2, F2, NO, and their relatives with more than 14 valence electrons — are strengthened by removing an electron. Everything at or below N2 is weakened. The trap is (B) N2: students reflexively think "N2+ sounds exotic, so it must be special", but N2 sits exactly at the peak of the bond-order tent and can only go downhill in either direction.
Answer: (c) A, C only — i.e. NO and O2.
105Number of electron deficient molecules among the following PH3, B2H6, CCl4, NH3, LiH and BCl3 is (a) 0  (b) 1  (c) 2  (d) 3 (JEE Main 2022, 25 Jun Shift 1 — Level-3)Electron-deficient moleculesFull worked solution to this exact counting question on electron-deficient molecules🟢 Exactc2 — B2H6 and BCl3
Concept A molecule is electron deficient when its central atom, after all bonds are drawn, is left with fewer than eight electrons in its valence shell and no lone pair to make up the shortfall — it has an empty valence orbital begging for electron density. In practice this is the signature of Group-13 (boron and aluminium) covalent compounds. Two structural styles exist: the incomplete octet type (BCl3, BF3, AlCl3) where boron has only six electrons, and the bridged multicentre type (B2H6, Al2(CH3)6) where there are literally not enough valence electrons to supply a normal 2-centre-2-electron bond to every link, forcing 3-centre-2-electron "banana" bonds. Molecules with a complete octet plus a lone pair (PH3, NH3), or a full octet with no spare orbital (CCl4), are electron precise; predominantly ionic hydrides such as LiH are not covalent molecules at all.
Key relation electron deficient ⇔ valence electrons on the central atom < 8 and no lone pair; B2H6: 12 valence e⁻ for 8 links ⇒ two 3c–2e bridges boron contributes 3 e⁻ each and hydrogen 1 e⁻ each: 2(3) + 6(1) = 12, but eight ordinary bonds would need 16.
Solution
1. PH3 — P: 3 bond pairs + 1 lone pair = 8 e⁻ ⇒ octet complete ⇒ not deficient.
2. B2H6 — diborane; only 12 valence electrons available, so the two bridging B–H–B links are 3-centre-2-electron bonds; each boron still sees only 8 electrons by sharing, and the molecule is the textbook electron-deficient hydride ⇒ YES.
3. CCl4 — C: 4 bond pairs = 8 e⁻, no vacant valence orbital ⇒ electron precise ⇒ not deficient.
4. NH3 — N: 3 bond pairs + 1 lone pair = 8 e⁻ ⇒ not deficient (it is in fact an electron donor, the Lewis base that quenches BCl3).
5. LiH — the Li–H electronegativity gap (≈1.2) makes this an essentially ionic hydride, Li⁺H⁻; the hydride ion has a complete 1s² duplet, so there is no covalent electron shortage to speak of ⇒ not counted.This is the deliberate distractor: candidates who argue "lithium only has 2 electrons around it, so LiH must be deficient" arrive at 3 and pick option (d). Electron deficiency is a property of covalent molecules with a vacant orbital on a shared centre, and NTA's key counts only 2.
6. BCl3 — B: 3 bond pairs = 6 e⁻ only, with a vacant 2pz orbital perpendicular to the trigonal plane ⇒ classic incomplete octet ⇒ YES.
7. Count = 2 (B2H6 and BCl3) ⇒ option (c).
 molecule   central atom   valence e- around it   lone pair?   electron deficient?
 PH3            P                  8                yes              no
 B2H6           B (x2)   3c-2e bridges, 12 e- total  no             YES   <--
 CCl4           C                  8                 no              no
 NH3            N                  8                yes              no
 LiH            ionic Li+ H-       H- duplet full    -               no
 BCl3           B                  6                 no             YES   <--
 count = 2                                                    <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Scan the list for B or Al bonded covalently and stop there — that single filter catches every electron-deficient molecule NTA has ever asked about (BF3, BCl3, BH3, B2H6, AlCl3, Al2Cl6, Al2(CH3)6). The two traps planted in this list are LiH (ionic, not deficient) and CCl4 (complete octet, no vacant orbital) — both are there to tempt you into answering 3.
Answer: (c) 2 — B2H6 and BCl3.
106How many of the following molecules are with non-zero net dipole moment? NH3, HCl, H2O, BeF2, BH3, CCl4 (numerical value) (JEE Main 2022, 25 Jun Shift 2 — Level-3)Dipole moment and symmetryCounting how many of CH4, BF3, H2O, HF, NH3, CO2, SO2 have zero dipole moment🟡 Similar3NH3, HCl and H2O
Concept The molecular dipole moment is the vector sum of the individual bond moments. Polar bonds are necessary but nowhere near sufficient: if the molecule is symmetric enough that the bond vectors cancel, the net moment is exactly zero however polar the individual bonds are. The single fastest test is geometric — a molecule whose central atom carries no lone pair and whose peripheral atoms are all identical and arranged in a regular (linear, trigonal planar, tetrahedral, trigonal bipyramidal, octahedral) pattern has μ = 0. The moment survives only when either (i) a lone pair breaks the symmetry, or (ii) the molecule is a heteronuclear diatomic, where there is nothing to cancel against.
Key relation μ⃗net = Σ μ⃗bond, magnitude μ = Q × r (1 D = 3.336 × 10⁻³⁰ C m). Symmetric cancellation: linear AX2, trigonal planar AX3, tetrahedral AX4 ⇒ μ = 0 the lone pair, if present, contributes its own moment pointing away from the nuclei and always reinforces rather than cancels.
Solution
1. NH3 — N has 3 bond pairs + 1 lone pair ⇒ trigonal pyramidal. Bond moments do not cancel and the lone-pair moment adds to them ⇒ μ ≈ 1.47 D ⇒ non-zero. ✓
2. HCl — heteronuclear diatomic; a single polar bond with nothing to oppose it ⇒ μ ≈ 1.03 D ⇒ non-zero. ✓
3. H2O — O has 2 bond pairs + 2 lone pairs ⇒ bent (≈104.5°) ⇒ the two O–H moments add to a resultant along the bisector ⇒ μ ≈ 1.85 D ⇒ non-zero. ✓
4. BeF2 — sp hybridised, no lone pair on Be ⇒ linear F–Be–F; the two equal Be–F moments point in exactly opposite directions ⇒ μ = 0. ✗
5. BH3 — sp², no lone pair ⇒ trigonal planar; three equal moments at 120° sum to zero ⇒ μ = 0. ✗Even the reversed polarity (here H is more electronegative than B) is irrelevant — perfect symmetry kills the resultant either way.
6. CCl4 — sp³, no lone pair, four identical C–Cl bonds in a regular tetrahedron ⇒ the resultant of any three exactly opposes the fourth ⇒ μ = 0. ✗
7. Non-zero count = 3.
 molecule   shape                 lone pair on centre   vectors cancel?   mu
 NH3        trigonal pyramidal          yes (1)              no          1.47 D   <-- counts
 HCl        diatomic                     -                   no          1.03 D   <-- counts
 H2O        bent, 104.5 deg             yes (2)              no          1.85 D   <-- counts
 BeF2       linear                       no                  YES           0
 BH3        trigonal planar              no                  YES           0
 CCl4       tetrahedral                  no                  YES           0
 number with non-zero dipole moment = 3                              <-- answer 3
⚡ JEE Trick Do not compute anything — just ask "does the central atom have a lone pair, or is this a heteronuclear diatomic?" If yes to either, μ ≠ 0; if no, and all outer atoms are the same, μ = 0. That single question sorts all six molecules here in under fifteen seconds. The classic trap is CCl4: the C–Cl bond is strongly polar, so students count it, forgetting that tetrahedral symmetry annihilates the resultant (whereas CHCl3, where one Cl is replaced, is very much polar).
Answer: 3 — NH3, HCl and H2O.
107Arrange the following in the decreasing order of their covalent character: (A) LiCl  (B) NaCl  (C) KCl  (D) CsCl. Choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below: (a) (A) > (C) > (B) > (D) (b) (B) > (A) > (C) > (D) (c) (A) > (B) > (C) > (D) (d) (A) > (B) > (D) > (C) (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jun Shift 1 — Level-3)Fajans' rulesFajans' rules — how cation size and charge control polarisation and covalent character🟡 SimilarcLiCl > NaCl > KCl > CsCl
Concept No bond is purely ionic. In every ion pair the cation's positive field pulls on the anion's electron cloud and distorts it towards itself; the electron density dragged into the internuclear region is the covalent character. Fajans' rules say this distortion grows with the cation's polarising power (charge ÷ size, so small and highly charged cations polarise best) and with the anion's polarisability (large, soft, highly charged anions deform most). Here the anion is Cl⁻ throughout, so the whole question is decided by the cation: as you descend Group 1 the cation radius grows, the charge density collapses, polarising power falls, and covalent character falls with it.
Key relation polarising power ∝ charge / (radius)², so covalent character ∝ 1 / rcation for a fixed charge and a fixed anion. Ionic radii: Li⁺ 76 pm < Na⁺ 102 pm < K⁺ 138 pm < Cs⁺ 167 pm all are +1, so size alone orders them — and the order of covalent character is simply the reverse of the size order.
Solution
1. All four are M⁺Cl⁻ with the same anion and the same +1 charge, so only cation size matters.
2. Smallest cation ⇒ highest charge density ⇒ strongest polarisation of the chloride cloud ⇒ most covalent. Li⁺ is by far the smallest and is additionally the only one whose valence shell is the compact 1s² core, so LiCl is the most covalent alkali chloride.The physical evidence is unmistakable: LiCl is appreciably soluble in ethanol, acetone and pyridine and sublimes at a much lower temperature than the rest — all covalent behaviour — whereas CsCl is the most typically ionic of the four.
3. Going down the group, r increases Li⁺ < Na⁺ < K⁺ < Cs⁺, so covalent character decreases in exactly that order: LiCl > NaCl > KCl > CsCl, i.e. (A) > (B) > (C) > (D).
4. Eliminate: (a) puts KCl above NaCl, (b) puts NaCl above LiCl, and (d) puts CsCl above KCl — each inverts the size trend somewhere. Only (c) is monotonic in the right direction.
 salt    cation    ionic radius   charge/size   polarising power   covalent character
 LiCl     Li+          76 pm         highest        strongest            HIGHEST   <-- A
 NaCl     Na+         102 pm            |               |                   |      <-- B
 KCl      K+          138 pm            |               |                   |      <-- C
 CsCl     Cs+         167 pm         lowest         weakest              LOWEST    <-- D
 order : A > B > C > D                                             <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick For a series with the same anion, covalent character just tracks 1/cation size — read straight down the group and reverse it. For a series with the same cation, it tracks anion size instead (AgF < AgCl < AgBr < AgI). And when charges differ, charge beats size (AlCl3 is more covalent than MgCl2, which beats NaCl). The trap in this question is a half-remembered "melting point increases down the group" rule — melting point rises because ionic character rises, which is the same trend read backwards, not a contradiction.
Answer: (c) LiCl > NaCl > KCl > CsCl.
108Consider the species CH4, NH4+ and BH4−. Choose the correct option with respect to these three species: (a) They are isoelectronic and only two have tetrahedral structures (b) They are isoelectronic and all have tetrahedral structures (c) Only two are isoelectronic and all have tetrahedral structures (d) Only two are isoelectronic and only two have tetrahedral structures (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jun Shift 2 — Level-3)Isoelectronic species & shapeFull worked solution to this exact CH4 / NH4+ / BH4− question🟢 Exactbisoelectronic and all tetrahedral
Concept Two separate checks are demanded and both must be done properly. Isoelectronic means the same total number of electrons — and for ions you must remember to subtract for positive charge and add for negative charge. Shape comes from VSEPR: count the steric number (σ bonds + lone pairs) on the central atom. Species that are isoelectronic and have the same heavy-atom skeleton are also isostructural, because the same number of electrons distributed over the same number of centres must adopt the same arrangement. Here the three species form a beautiful isoelectronic triad walking across the periodic table — B, C, N — with the charge tuned each time to keep the electron count fixed at ten.
Key relation total e⁻ = Σ Z − (charge); steric number = σ bonds + lone pairs, SN = 4 ⇒ sp³ ⇒ tetrahedral (109°28′) when all four are bond pairs no lone pair on any of these central atoms, so electron geometry and molecular shape coincide exactly.
Solution
1. Electron count. CH4: 6 + 4(1) = 10. NH4+: 7 + 4(1) − 1 = 10. BH4−: 5 + 4(1) + 1 = 10. All three have 10 electrons ⇒ all three are isoelectronic (they also share 8 valence electrons and 5 nuclei).
2. Shape. In each case the central atom forms four σ bonds and retains no lone pair, so SN = 4 + 0 = 4 ⇒ sp³ ⇒ regular tetrahedron, bond angle 109°28′.In NH4+ one N–H bond is formally dative and in BH4− one B–H bond is formally dative, but once formed a coordinate bond is indistinguishable from a normal covalent one — all four bonds in each ion are identical in length and strength, so the tetrahedron stays regular.
3. Therefore "isoelectronic" applies to all three and "tetrahedral" applies to all three ⇒ option (b).
4. Eliminate: (a) and (d) claim only two are tetrahedral — false, none of them has a lone pair to distort the shape; (c) and (d) claim only two are isoelectronic — false, the arithmetic in step 1 gives 10 for every one of them.
 species    Z(central)   H atoms   charge   total e-   sigma bonds   lone pairs   SN   shape
 CH4            6           4         0        10          4             0        4   tetrahedral
 NH4+           7           4        +1        10          4             0        4   tetrahedral
 BH4-           5           4        -1        10          4             0        4   tetrahedral
 all isoelectronic (10 e-)  AND  all tetrahedral                          <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Learn this triad as a single unit — BH4−, CH4, NH4+ (10 e⁻, all sp³ tetrahedral) — alongside its siblings N2, CO, CN−, NO+ (14 e⁻) and CO2, N2O, NO2+, N3− (22 e⁻). Whole questions collapse to a one-line recall. The trap is forgetting the sign convention when counting electrons of an ion: adding an electron for a cation (or removing one for an anion) breaks the tie and pushes you straight to option (c) or (d).
Answer: (b) They are isoelectronic and all have tetrahedral structures.
109Match List-I with List-II. List-I: (A) ΨMO = ΨA − ΨB  (B) μ = Q × r  (C) (Nb − Na)/2  (D) ΨMO = ΨA + ΨB. List-II: (I) Dipole moment  (II) Bonding molecular orbital  (III) Anti-bonding molecular orbital  (IV) Bond order. (a) A−II, B−I, C−IV, D−III (b) A−III, B−IV, C−I, D−II (c) A−III, B−I, C−IV, D−II (d) A−III, B−IV, C−II, D−I (JEE Main 2022, 27 Jul Shift 2 — Level-3)MOT formulae matchingBonding vs antibonding combinations and the bond-order formula in molecular orbital theory🟡 SimilarcA−III, B−I, C−IV, D−II
Concept Matching questions are pure definition recall, but the sign in the LCAO combination is where marks are actually lost. When two atomic wavefunctions are added they are in phase: the amplitudes reinforce between the nuclei, electron density builds up in the internuclear region, the nuclei are screened from each other and the energy drops — that is the bonding MO. When they are subtracted they are out of phase: the amplitudes cancel, a nodal plane appears exactly between the nuclei, electron density is expelled from the very region that would have glued the atoms together, and the energy rises — the antibonding MO. The other two entries are standalone definitions: μ = Q × r defines the dipole moment, and ½(Nb − Na) defines the bond order.
Key relation Ψbonding = ΨA + ΨB (in phase, constructive, lower energy); Ψantibonding = ΨA − ΨB (out of phase, destructive, node between nuclei, higher energy); μ = Q × r; B.O. = ½ (Nb − Na) the antibonding MO is destabilised slightly MORE than the bonding one is stabilised, which is why He2 with σ1s² σ*1s² does not exist.
Solution
1. (A) ΨMO = ΨA − ΨB — the difference, i.e. destructive interference ⇒ anti-bonding molecular orbital ⇒ III.
2. (B) μ = Q × r — magnitude of charge times distance of separation ⇒ dipole moment ⇒ I. (Expressed in debye; 1 D = 10⁻¹⁸ esu·cm = 3.336 × 10⁻³⁰ C m.)
3. (C) (Nb − Na)/2 — half the excess of bonding over antibonding electrons ⇒ bond order ⇒ IV.
4. (D) ΨMO = ΨA + ΨB — the sum, constructive interference ⇒ bonding molecular orbital ⇒ II.
5. Pattern A−III, B−I, C−IV, D−II ⇒ option (c). Option (a) swaps the plus and minus combinations (A−II, D−III), which is the single most common error; (b) and (d) additionally misplace μ and the bond-order formula.
 List-I entry                  meaning                                 List-II
 (A) psi_A - psi_B      out of phase, node between nuclei, E rises   III  anti-bonding MO
 (B) mu = Q x r         charge x separation                            I   dipole moment
 (C) (Nb - Na)/2        half the net bonding electrons                IV   bond order
 (D) psi_A + psi_B      in phase, charge builds up, E falls           II   bonding MO
 A-III, B-I, C-IV, D-II                                        <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Anchor the sign with one image: "plus builds, minus kills" — the plus combination piles electron density up between the nuclei (bonding), the minus combination digs a node there (antibonding). Once A and D are pinned, only one option can survive, so you never need to check B and C at all. The trap in match-list items is exactly this reversal; NTA reliably offers a distractor (here option (a)) that is correct in every pairing except the ± swap.
Answer: (c) A−III, B−I, C−IV, D−II.
110Consider PF5, BrF5, PCl3, SF6, ICl4−, ClF3 and IF5. Amongst the above molecule(s)/ion(s), the number of molecule(s)/ion(s) having sp3d2 hybridisation is ____ (numerical value) (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jul Shift 2 — Level-3)Hybridisation countingsp³d, sp³d² and sp³d³ hybridisation — PF5, SF6 and IF7 built step by step🟡 Similar4BrF5, SF6, ICl4− and IF5
Concept Hybridisation is fixed by the steric number — the number of σ bonds plus the number of lone pairs on the central atom — and by nothing else. The label sp³d² means six hybrid orbitals, so you are hunting for every species with SN = 6, whether those six are all bond pairs (SF6, octahedral), five bonds plus one lone pair (BrF5, IF5, square pyramidal) or four bonds plus two lone pairs (ICl4−, square planar). Crucially, lone pairs count towards the hybridisation even though they do not show up in the shape — that is the whole trap of this question, because a square-planar ion looks like an sp³ or dsp² case until you count its two lone pairs.
Key relation SN = (number of σ bonds) + (number of lone pairs); for a main-group centre lone pairs = ½ [V − (bonds) ± charge] where V is the central atom's valence-electron count. Mapping: SN 4 → sp³, 5 → sp³d, 6 → sp³d², 7 → sp³d³ an anionic charge adds one electron to the central atom's tally, a cationic charge removes one.
Solution
1. PF5 — P (5 valence e⁻) forms 5 bonds, 0 lone pairs ⇒ SN 5 ⇒ sp³d, trigonal bipyramidal. ✗
2. BrF5 — Br (7) forms 5 bonds, leaving 2 e⁻ = 1 lone pair ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ sp³d², square pyramidal. ✓
3. PCl3 — P (5) forms 3 bonds, leaving 2 e⁻ = 1 lone pair ⇒ SN 4 ⇒ sp³, pyramidal. ✗
4. SF6 — S (6) forms 6 bonds, 0 lone pairs ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ sp³d², regular octahedral. ✓
5. ICl4− — I (7) plus 1 for the negative charge = 8; four are used in bonds, leaving 4 e⁻ = 2 lone pairs ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ sp³d², square planar (the two lone pairs take the trans axial sites). ✓The shape is square planar, not tetrahedral — this is the option students most often miscount, because the visible geometry hides the axial lone pairs.
6. ClF3 — Cl (7) forms 3 bonds, leaving 4 e⁻ = 2 lone pairs ⇒ SN 5 ⇒ sp³d, T-shaped. ✗
7. IF5 — I (7) forms 5 bonds, leaving 2 e⁻ = 1 lone pair ⇒ SN 6 ⇒ sp³d², square pyramidal. ✓
8. Count of sp³d² species = 4 (BrF5, SF6, ICl4−, IF5).
 species   central   valence e-   bonds   lone pairs   SN   hybridisation   shape
 PF5         P           5          5         0         5      sp3d        trig. bipyramidal
 BrF5        Br          7          5         1         6      sp3d2       square pyramidal   <--
 PCl3        P           5          3         1         4      sp3         pyramidal
 SF6         S           6          6         0         6      sp3d2       octahedral         <--
 ICl4-       I         7+1=8        4         2         6      sp3d2       square planar      <--
 ClF3        Cl          7          3         2         5      sp3d        T-shaped
 IF5         I           7          5         1         6      sp3d2       square pyramidal   <--
 count = 4                                                            <-- answer 4
⚡ JEE Trick Use the one-line formula SN = ½ [V + M − C + A] (V = central atom's valence electrons, M = monovalent atoms attached, C = cationic charge, A = anionic charge) and read the hybridisation straight off SN. For ICl4−: ½[7 + 4 + 1] = 6 ⇒ sp³d², done in three seconds without a Lewis structure. The trap in this list is the pair ClF3 (SN 5) and BrF5 (SN 6): both are interhalogens with lone pairs, both "look" similar, but only one of them reaches six.
Answer: 4 — BrF5, SF6, ICl4− and IF5.
111Amongst SF4, XeF4, CF4 and H2O, the number of species with two lone pairs of electrons is ____ (numerical value) (JEE Main 2022, 26 Jun Shift 2 — Level-2)Lone-pair countingFull worked solution to this exact SF4 / XeF4 / CF4 / H2O lone-pair count🟢 Exact2XeF4 and H2O
Concept Every lone-pair count is the same two-step piece of bookkeeping: start from the central atom's own valence electrons, hand one out to each σ bond it forms, and pair up whatever is left over. Whatever remains after the bonds are paid for stays on the central atom as non-bonding pairs. The reason the four species here give four different answers is that the central atoms come from four different groups — S (6 valence e⁻), Xe (8), C (4) and O (6) — while forming 4, 4, 4 and 2 bonds respectively. Notice that lone pairs are the quantity that controls both the hybridisation and the distortion of the shape, so getting this count right is the gateway to almost every VSEPR question.
Key relation lone pairs = ½ [V − (number of σ bonds to monovalent atoms)] where V is the central atom's valence-electron count. SN = σ bonds + lone pairs then fixes the geometry for neutral molecules with only monovalent outer atoms this shortcut needs no Lewis structure at all.
Solution
1. SF4 — S has V = 6, forms 4 bonds ⇒ lone pairs = ½(6 − 4) = 1. SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d, see-saw. ✗
2. XeF4 — Xe has V = 8, forms 4 bonds ⇒ lone pairs = ½(8 − 4) = 2. SN = 6 ⇒ sp³d², square planar with the two lone pairs trans to each other on the axis. ✓
3. CF4 — C has V = 4, forms 4 bonds ⇒ lone pairs = ½(4 − 4) = 0. SN = 4 ⇒ sp³, regular tetrahedral. ✗
4. H2O — O has V = 6, forms 2 bonds ⇒ lone pairs = ½(6 − 2) = 2. SN = 4 ⇒ sp³, bent (104.5°, squeezed below 109.5° by the two lone pairs). ✓The two lone pairs on oxygen are exactly why the H–O–H angle is the smallest of the sp³ family — lone-pair/lone-pair repulsion > lone-pair/bond-pair > bond-pair/bond-pair.
5. Species with exactly two lone pairs: XeF4 and H2O ⇒ count = 2.
 species   central   V    sigma bonds   lone pairs   SN   hybrid   shape
 SF4          S      6         4            1         5   sp3d     see-saw
 XeF4         Xe     8         4            2         6   sp3d2    square planar   <-- counts
 CF4          C      4         4            0         4   sp3      tetrahedral
 H2O          O      6         2            2         4   sp3      bent            <-- counts
 number with exactly two lone pairs = 2                        <-- answer 2
⚡ JEE Trick Commit the fluorides of the third row onwards to memory as a lone-pair ladder: XF2 → 3 lp, XF4 → 2 lp, XF6 → 1 lp for xenon; SF4 → 1 lp, SF6 → 0. Then XeF2/XeF4/XeF6 and the sulfur fluorides are instant. The trap here is SF4: it is superficially "the same" as XeF4 — four fluorines on a p-block centre — but sulfur brings two fewer valence electrons, so it has one lone pair, not two, and is see-saw rather than square planar.
Answer: 2 — XeF4 and H2O.
112Number of lone pair(s) of electrons on the central atom and the shape of BrF3 molecule respectively, are: (a) 0, triangular planar (b) 1, pyramidal (c) 2, bent T-shape (d) 1, bent T-shape (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jun Shift 2 — Level-2)BrF3 — VSEPRFull worked solution — lone pairs on Br and the shape of BrF3🟢 Exactc2 lone pairs, bent T-shape
Concept BrF3 is the standard AX3E2 case and it shows off two VSEPR rules at once. First, the steric number of 5 forces a trigonal bipyramidal electron geometry with sp³d hybridisation. Second — and this is what makes the shape "bent T" rather than a neat trigonal plane — the two lone pairs must choose between axial and equatorial sites, and they always take the equatorial ones, because an equatorial position has only two neighbours at 90° whereas an axial position has three. With both lone pairs sitting equatorially, the three fluorines occupy the two axial positions and the one remaining equatorial position, giving a T. The T is bent because the bulky lone pairs squeeze the two axial Br–F bonds slightly towards the equatorial fluorine, closing the F–Br–F angle from 90° to about 86°.
Key relation lone pairs = ½ (V − bonds) = ½ (7 − 3) = 2; SN = 3 + 2 = 5 ⇒ sp³d ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal electron geometry; AX3E2 ⇒ T-shaped molecular shape repulsion order lp–lp > lp–bp > bp–bp forces both lone pairs equatorial, where each faces only two 90° neighbours instead of three.
Solution
1. Bromine's valence configuration is 4s²4p⁵ ⇒ V = 7 electrons. Three of them form the three Br–F σ bonds.
2. Electrons left over = 7 − 3 = 4 ⇒ 2 lone pairs. That already eliminates (a), (b) and (d), each of which quotes the wrong lone-pair count.
3. SN = 3 bond pairs + 2 lone pairs = 5 ⇒ sp³d hybridisation ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal electron geometry.
4. Place the lone pairs. Axial sites have three 90° neighbours; equatorial sites have only two. Since lp–lp and lp–bp repulsions are the most costly, both lone pairs go equatorial, 120° apart from each other.
5. The three fluorines therefore occupy the two axial sites plus the single remaining equatorial site ⇒ molecular shape is T-shaped, described in this paper as "bent T-shape" ⇒ option (c).The measured Fax–Br–Feq angle in BrF3 is ≈86.2°, not 90° — direct experimental evidence that the equatorial lone pairs push the axial bonds away, which is exactly what "bent" T records.
6. Check the rejected options: (a) 0 lone pairs with a triangular plane would be BF3; (b) 1 lone pair with a pyramid would be NH3 or PCl3; (d) has the right shape but the wrong lone-pair number.
 step                                        result
 valence electrons on Br                       7
 electrons used in three Br-F bonds            3
 electrons left  = 7 - 3 = 4                 -> 2 LONE PAIRS
 steric number = 3 bp + 2 lp                   5   -> sp3d
 electron geometry                             trigonal bipyramidal
 lone pairs occupy                             both EQUATORIAL sites
 molecular shape (AX3E2)                       T-shaped / bent T   <-- answer (c)
 F(ax)-Br-F(eq) angle                          about 86 deg, not 90
⚡ JEE Trick For every sp³d species just count the lone pairs and read the shape off a three-line table: 0 lp → trigonal bipyramidal (PCl5), 1 lp → see-saw (SF4), 2 lp → T-shaped (ClF3, BrF3), 3 lp → linear (XeF2, I3−). Lone pairs always go equatorial, without exception. The trap is option (d), which offers the right shape with the wrong lone-pair count — read both halves of the option before ticking it.
Answer: (c) 2 lone pairs, bent T-shape.
113Match List-I (Compound) with List-II (Shape). List-I: (A) BrF5  (B) CrF63−  (C) O3  (D) PCl5. List-II: (I) bent  (II) square pyramidal  (III) trigonal bipyramidal  (IV) octahedral. (a) (A)−(I), (B)−(II), (C)−(III), (D)−(IV) (b) (A)−(IV), (B)−(III), (C)−(II), (D)−(I) (c) (A)−(II), (B)−(IV), (C)−(I), (D)−(III) (d) (A)−(III), (B)−(IV), (C)−(II), (D)−(I) (JEE Main 2022, 26 Jul Shift 1 — Level-2)Shapes — matchingMatching NH3, BrF5, XeF4 and SF6 to pyramidal, square pyramidal, square planar and octahedral🟡 SimilarcA−II, B−IV, C−I, D−III
Concept Four different structural situations are deliberately mixed here, so use a different tool for each. BrF5 and PCl5 are ordinary main-group VSEPR problems solved by the steric number. CrF63− is a transition-metal complex, where VSEPR does not apply at all — with six monodentate ligands around a d-block ion the geometry is octahedral essentially by coordination number alone (Cr³⁺ is d³, so it takes the d²sp³ inner-orbital octahedral arrangement). O3 is a resonance-stabilised molecule whose central oxygen carries one lone pair, which bends it. Recognising which framework applies to which species is the real skill being tested.
Key relation SN = σ bonds + lone pairs ⇒ SN 6 with 1 lp → square pyramidal; SN 5 with 0 lp → trigonal bipyramidal; SN 3 with 1 lp → bent; for a coordination complex C.N. 6 ⇒ octahedral (d²sp³ or sp³d²) the shape of a complex ion is set by the number of ligands, not by lone pairs on the metal.
Solution
1. (A) BrF5 — Br: V = 7, 5 bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair; SN = 6 ⇒ sp³d², octahedral electron geometry with one vertex taken by the lone pair ⇒ square pyramidal ⇒ II.
2. (B) CrF63− — hexafluoridochromate(III); Cr³⁺ is d³ and six F⁻ ligands surround it ⇒ coordination number 6 ⇒ octahedral (d²sp³) ⇒ IV.Fluoride is a weak-field ligand, but with a d³ ion the t2g³ configuration is the same either way, so the octahedron is unambiguous — no Jahn–Teller distortion to worry about.
3. (C) O3 — the central oxygen has one σ bond to each terminal O, one delocalised π contribution and one lone pair; SN = 3 ⇒ sp², and the lone pair bends the molecule to ≈117° ⇒ bent ⇒ I.
4. (D) PCl5 — P: V = 5, 5 bonds ⇒ 0 lone pairs; SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d ⇒ trigonal bipyramidal ⇒ III.
5. Pattern A−II, B−IV, C−I, D−III ⇒ option (c). Options (a), (b) and (d) each misplace at least two entries — (b) in particular is the exact reversal of the list, a favourite NTA distractor.
 compound    centre   sigma bonds   lone pairs   SN / C.N.   hybrid    shape                 List-II
 BrF5         Br           5            1            6       sp3d2     square pyramidal        II
 CrF6 3-      Cr(III)      6 ligands    -            6       d2sp3     octahedral              IV
 O3           O(central)   2            1            3       sp2       bent (~117 deg)          I
 PCl5         P            5            0            5       sp3d      trigonal bipyramidal   III
 A-II, B-IV, C-I, D-III                                                     <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick In a mixed match-list, find the one entry you are certain of and eliminate: PCl5 is trigonal bipyramidal in every textbook, and only option (c) pairs D with III, so the answer is settled without checking the other three. The planted trap is CrF63− — students try to apply VSEPR to a transition metal and get lost; for complexes the rule is simply C.N. 6 → octahedral, C.N. 4 → tetrahedral or square planar.
Answer: (c) A−II, B−IV, C−I, D−III.
114The total number of Mn=O bonds in Mn2O7 is (a) 4  (b) 5  (c) 6  (d) 3 (JEE Main 2022, 27 Jul Shift 1 — Level-2)Structure of Mn2O7Full worked solution — drawing Mn2O7 and counting its Mn=O bonds🟢 Exactc6
Concept Every "X2On" anhydride of a high-oxidation-state element is built the same way: two tetrahedral (or pyramidal) units joined through one bridging oxygen. The bridging oxygen forms two single bonds, one to each metal, while all the remaining terminal oxygens form double bonds. So the count is mechanical — subtract the single bridging O from the total, and every oxygen that is left is a terminal, doubly bonded one. Mn2O7 is the anhydride of permanganic acid HMnO4, and it is literally two MnO4 tetrahedra sharing a corner: O3Mn–O–MnO3. Manganese is in its highest oxidation state, +7, so it uses all seven of its 3d⁵4s² electrons and has nothing left for a lone pair.
Key relation Mn2O7 = O3Mn–O–MnO3; terminal O = 7 − 1 (bridge) = 6, each doubly bonded; oxidation state check 2x + 7(−2) = 0 ⇒ x = +7 the +7 state exactly exhausts Mn's 3d⁵4s² valence electrons, leaving no lone pair and forcing every terminal oxygen into a double bond.
Solution
1. Confirm the oxidation state: 2x + 7(−2) = 0 ⇒ x = +7 for each manganese — the highest oxidation state manganese can reach.
2. Draw the skeleton. Mn2O7 is the anhydride formed by dehydrating two HMnO4 molecules, so it is two MnO4 tetrahedra sharing one corner oxygen: O3Mn–O–MnO3.
3. Classify the seven oxygens: one is the bridge (two Mn–O single bonds, giving the characteristic Mn–O–Mn linkage) and six are terminal, three on each manganese.
4. Each manganese must reach +7. Three terminal oxygens taking two electrons each (3 × 2 = 6) plus one share of the bridging oxygen (1) accounts for exactly 7 ⇒ each terminal Mn–O is a double bond.The same bookkeeping gives Cl2O7 = O3Cl–O–ClO3 with six Cl=O, and P4O10 with four terminal P=O and six bridging P–O–P — one structural motif, three standard questions.
5. Total Mn=O double bonds = 3 + 3 = 6 ⇒ option (c). Options (d) 3 and (a) 4 come from counting only one manganese or from mistaking the bridge for a double bond; (b) 5 corresponds to no sensible structure at all.
 structure :        O           O
                    ||          ||
               O = Mn - O - Mn = O
                    ||          ||
                    O           O
 oxygen inventory      count   bond type      contributes to Mn=O ?
 bridging O              1     Mn-O-Mn single         no
 terminal O on Mn(1)     3     Mn=O double           yes  (3)
 terminal O on Mn(2)     3     Mn=O double           yes  (3)
 total O = 1 + 3 + 3 = 7  (matches Mn2O7)
 total Mn=O bonds = 6                                  <-- answer (c)
⚡ JEE Trick For any symmetric anhydride X2On, the answer is (n − 1) terminal X=O bonds and exactly 1 bridging X–O–X. Mn2O7 → 6 double bonds; Cl2O7 → 6; S2O72− (pyrosulfate) → 6; P2O74− → 2 terminal P=O plus 6 P–O⁻ around the bridge. Memorise the motif, not the individual molecules. The trap is answering 3 — the count for a single MnO3 half — when the question asks for the total in the whole dimer.
Answer: (c) 6.
115Based upon VSEPR theory, match the shape (geometry) of the molecules in List-I with the molecules in List-II and select the most appropriate option. List-I (Shape): (A) T-shaped  (B) Trigonal planar  (C) Square planar  (D) See-saw. List-II (Molecules): (i) XeF4  (ii) SF4  (iii) ClF3  (iv) BF3. (a) (A)−(I), (B)−(II), (C)−(III), (D)−(IV) (b) (A)−(III), (B)−(IV), (C)−(I), (D)−(II) (c) (A)−(III), (B)−(IV), (C)−(II), (D)−(I) (d) (A)−(IV), (B)−(III), (C)−(I), (D)−(II) (JEE Main 2022, 27 Jun Shift 1 — Level-1)VSEPR shapes — matchingTrick to remember every VSEPR shape — T-shaped, see-saw, square planar and trigonal planar🟡 SimilarbA−III, B−IV, C−I, D−II
Concept All four molecules are AXnEm species and the whole match falls out of the steric number plus lone-pair count. The key insight is that the electron geometry is set by SN, while the molecular shape is what remains once you make the lone pairs invisible. SN 5 gives a trigonal bipyramid: strip one vertex (1 lp) and you get a see-saw; strip two (2 lp) and you get a T. SN 6 gives an octahedron: strip two opposite vertices (2 lp) and you get a square plane. SN 3 with no lone pair is simply a trigonal plane. Four molecules, four different (SN, lp) combinations — so there is exactly one consistent pairing and no ambiguity.
Key relation SN 3, 0 lp → trigonal planar; SN 5, 1 lp → see-saw; SN 5, 2 lp → T-shaped; SN 6, 2 lp → square planar in a trigonal bipyramid lone pairs take equatorial sites; in an octahedron two lone pairs go trans (axial), which is what flattens it into a square.
Solution
1. (iii) ClF3 — Cl: V = 7, 3 bonds ⇒ 2 lone pairs; SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d, both lone pairs equatorial ⇒ T-shaped ⇒ matches (A).
2. (iv) BF3 — B: V = 3, 3 bonds ⇒ 0 lone pairs; SN = 3 ⇒ sp², 120° angles ⇒ trigonal planar ⇒ matches (B).Boron's incomplete octet is what leaves it with no lone pair at all — the same feature that makes BF3 a strong Lewis acid.
3. (i) XeF4 — Xe: V = 8, 4 bonds ⇒ 2 lone pairs; SN = 6 ⇒ sp³d², the two lone pairs trans on the axis ⇒ square planar ⇒ matches (C).
4. (ii) SF4 — S: V = 6, 4 bonds ⇒ 1 lone pair; SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d, the lone pair equatorial ⇒ see-saw (distorted tetrahedral) ⇒ matches (D).
5. Pattern A−(iii), B−(iv), C−(i), D−(ii), written in the options as A−III, B−IV, C−I, D−II ⇒ option (b). Option (c) swaps XeF4 and SF4; (d) swaps ClF3 and BF3; (a) is a straight sequential guess.
 molecule   centre   V    bonds   lone pairs   SN   hybrid    electron geometry      SHAPE            List-I
 ClF3        Cl      7      3         2         5   sp3d      trig. bipyramidal      T-shaped            A
 BF3         B       3      3         0         3   sp2       trigonal planar        trigonal planar     B
 XeF4        Xe      8      4         2         6   sp3d2     octahedral             square planar       C
 SF4         S       6      4         1         5   sp3d      trig. bipyramidal      see-saw             D
 A-III, B-IV, C-I, D-II                                                          <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the two "strip the vertices" families as pictures: from a trigonal bipyramid, removing 1, 2, 3 equatorial vertices gives see-saw → T-shaped → linear; from an octahedron, removing 1 then 2 (trans) vertices gives square pyramidal → square planar. Every shape in every VSEPR match-list comes from one of these two ladders. The trap in this set is the SF4 / XeF4 pair — same "F4" formula, completely different shapes, because sulfur has one lone pair and xenon has two.
Answer: (b) A−III (ClF3), B−IV (BF3), C−I (XeF4), D−II (SF4).
116Which of the following pair of molecules contain an odd electron molecule and an expanded octet molecule? (a) BCl3 and SF6 (b) NO and H2SO4 (c) SF6 and H2SO4 (d) BCl3 and NO (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jul Shift 1 — Level-1)Exceptions to the octet ruleFull worked solution to this exact odd-electron / expanded-octet pairing question🟢 ExactbNO and H2SO4
Concept There are exactly three recognised exceptions to the octet rule and this question needs two of them held apart cleanly. (i) Incomplete octet — the central atom finishes with fewer than eight electrons, as in BCl3, BeCl2, LiCl; these are the electron-deficient species. (ii) Odd electron (hypovalent) molecules — the molecule has an odd total number of valence electrons, so at least one electron must remain unpaired and no Lewis structure can pair everything up; NO, NO2 and ClO2 are the standard examples and they are all paramagnetic free radicals. (iii) Expanded octet — a third-period-or-lower central atom uses its empty d orbitals to hold more than eight electrons, as in SF6, PCl5, H2SO4, H3PO4. The question asks for a pair containing one of each from categories (ii) and (iii), so both halves of the chosen option must be checked.
Key relation odd electron ⇔ total valence electrons is odd ⇒ ≥ 1 unpaired e⁻ ⇒ paramagnetic; expanded octet ⇔ central atom of period ≥ 3 with more than 8 electrons around it boron and beryllium can never expand — they have no d orbitals in the second period — which is why BCl3 belongs to a different exception entirely.
Solution
1. NO — valence electrons = 5 (N) + 6 (O) = 11, an odd number. One electron cannot be paired ⇒ genuine odd-electron molecule, paramagnetic, and (as MO theory shows) its unpaired electron sits in a π* orbital. ✓ odd electron
2. H2SO4 — sulfur is in period 3 and, in the conventional Lewis structure, forms two S=O double bonds and two S–OH single bonds ⇒ 12 electrons around S ⇒ expanded octet. ✓ expanded octet
3. So option (b) supplies exactly one of each ⇒ correct.
4. BCl3 — boron carries only 6 electrons ⇒ this is an incomplete octet, the third exception, not an odd-electron molecule (its valence-electron total 3 + 21 = 24 is even). That kills options (a) and (d).Students often blur "electron deficient" with "odd electron" — but a radical has an unpaired spin, while BCl3 has all its electrons paired and simply has an empty orbital.
5. SF6 — 12 electrons around S ⇒ expanded octet, but not odd electron (48 valence electrons, all paired). So option (c), SF6 + H2SO4, gives two expanded octets and no odd-electron molecule ⇒ rejected.
 molecule   valence e- total   odd?    e- around central atom   category
 NO             5 + 6 = 11     ODD             -               ODD-ELECTRON (radical)  <--
 H2SO4       2+6+32 = 32       even           12               EXPANDED OCTET          <--
 BCl3         3 + 21 = 24      even            6               incomplete octet
 SF6          6 + 42 = 48      even           12               expanded octet
 option (a) BCl3 + SF6      : incomplete + expanded   -> no odd electron
 option (b) NO   + H2SO4    : ODD-ELECTRON + EXPANDED -> correct    <-- answer (b)
 option (c) SF6  + H2SO4    : expanded + expanded     -> no odd electron
 option (d) BCl3 + NO       : incomplete + odd        -> no expanded octet
⚡ JEE Trick Test for an odd-electron molecule by adding up the valence electrons and checking the parity — odd total ⇒ radical ⇒ paramagnetic ⇒ coloured/reactive (NO, NO2, ClO2). Test for an expanded octet by asking whether the central atom is period 3 or below; B, Be, C, N and O can never expand. The deliberate trap is BCl3, planted in two of the four options to catch anyone who treats "electron deficient" and "odd electron" as the same exception.
Answer: (b) NO and H2SO4.
117Number of lone pairs of electrons in the central atom of SCl2, O3, ClF3 and SF6, respectively, are (a) 0, 1, 2 & 2 (b) 2, 1, 2 & 0 (c) 1, 2, 2 & 0 (d) None of these (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jul Shift 1 — Level-1)Lone pairs on the central atomFull worked solution — lone pairs on the central atom of SCl2, O3, ClF3 and SF6🟢 Exactb2, 1, 2 & 0
Concept The routine is always the same — take the central atom's valence electrons, spend one per σ bond, and pair up the remainder — but three of the four species here carry a wrinkle worth understanding. In SCl2 and ClF3 the arithmetic is direct. In SF6 sulfur spends all six of its valence electrons on bonds, so nothing is left over — the expanded octet uses up the entire valence shell. O3 is the one that catches people: you must count on the central oxygen only, and that atom is not a normal two-lone-pair oxygen. In the resonance picture the central O forms one single bond and one double bond (delocalised into an equivalent 3-centre π system), so it has spent four of its six valence electrons and retains just one lone pair — which is precisely why ozone is bent at ≈117° rather than at the ≈104.5° of water.
Key relation lone pairs on central atom = ½ [V − (electrons used in σ and π bonding)]; equivalently lp = SN − (σ bonds) where SN = ½ [V + monovalent atoms − cation charge + anion charge] π bonds do not change the steric number, but they do consume valence electrons on the central atom — which is exactly what reduces ozone's central oxygen from two lone pairs to one.
Solution
1. SCl2 — S: V = 6, two σ bonds ⇒ 6 − 2 = 4 e⁻ left ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 4 ⇒ sp³, bent (≈103°).
2. O3 — central O: V = 6; it uses two electrons for the two σ bonds and two more for the delocalised π bond ⇒ 6 − 4 = 2 e⁻ left ⇒ 1 lone pair. SN = 2 σ + 1 lp = 3 ⇒ sp², bent at ≈117°.Contrast the two terminal oxygens, which carry two lone pairs each — the question asks only about the central atom, and this is the whole point of including ozone in the list.
3. ClF3 — Cl: V = 7, three σ bonds ⇒ 7 − 3 = 4 e⁻ left ⇒ 2 lone pairs. SN = 5 ⇒ sp³d, T-shaped (the same AX3E2 analysis as BrF3 in Q112).
4. SF6 — S: V = 6, six σ bonds ⇒ 6 − 6 = 0 e⁻ left ⇒ 0 lone pairs. SN = 6 ⇒ sp³d², regular octahedral.
5. Sequence = 2, 1, 2 and 0 ⇒ option (b). Option (a) wrongly gives SCl2 zero and SF6 two — an exact swap of the first and last entries; option (c) treats ozone's central oxygen as though it were water's oxygen (2 lone pairs) and SCl2 as though sulfur had only one; (d) is redundant once (b) checks out.
 species   central   V   sigma bonds   pi bonds   e- left   LONE PAIRS   SN   hybrid   shape
 SCl2         S      6        2            0         4          2         4   sp3      bent ~103
 O3           O      6        2            1         2          1         3   sp2      bent ~117
 ClF3         Cl     7        3            0         4          2         5   sp3d     T-shaped
 SF6          S      6        6            0         0          0         6   sp3d2    octahedral
 sequence : 2 , 1 , 2 , 0                                             <-- answer (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Remember the single line that decides this question: the central atom of ozone has ONE lone pair, not two — it is sp², bent at 117°, and isoelectronic with SO2 and NO2−. Every distractor in items like this is built on the assumption that you will treat O3 like H2O. Second shortcut: any central atom that uses all its valence electrons in bonds (SF6, PCl5, CH4, BF3) automatically has zero lone pairs — no calculation needed.
Answer: (b) 2, 1, 2 & 0.
